PREFACE 

ENERAL BUSINESS TRAINING in its first edition pioneered in emphasizing the general, non-technical values in junior business education. These values are now commonly recognized as being of greatest importance. The recognition of their worth has been accelerated in recent years through the observations of teachers and business men, and as the result of several surveys which have been made by those interested in business education. 

One of these surveys reported the business activities of 686 persons in Des Moines representing all vocations. It indicated an almost universal need for information about a great variety of business activities. Its findings have been confirmed by other surveys, notably by that made by Cecil 

C. Wrisley in Los Angeles.¹

When the individuals whose business activities were studied were classified by occupations, each group was found to need business information with only a small variation in degree. Several groups, including the professional group, were found to use a greater variety of business information than was required by strictly clerical workers. This justifies not only the teaching of fundamental business activities, but also the teaching of these activities to all pupils regardless of the occupations they expect to follow in the future. 

The objectives of the second edition of GENERAL BUSI- NESS TRAINING may be briefly summarized as follows: 

1. To aid in developing good citizens through teaching the fundamental business practices which everyone should understand and which so greatly affect the financial success of individuals in all walks of life. Obviously, it is impossible to give a mastery of all business methods. The ones included were those which surveys showed to be used most commonly, to be most valuable, or to be the common sources of errors. 

1 Balance Sheet, Feb. 1931, p. 190. 3

PREFACE 

2. To encourage pupils to continue in school as long as possible. Various fields of knowledge were opened and the value of continuing their study was suggested. 

3. To aid in discovering aptitudes, abilities, and interests which will help pupils in planning a school program and finally in selecting a vocation. 

4. To provide a background for the study of commercial subjects. Although the content of the text is of general interest, yet those who elect to continue the study of business subjects will find that they have a background which will aid them in efficiently mastering the advanced courses. 

Time will be saved especially in bookkeeping courses because of the elimination of the need for a study of ordinary business papers and simple bookkeeping methods. 

5. To give the general information which will be most useful to those entering business. Narrowly vocational information, valuable in only a few positions and to only a limited number in each class, was avoided. On the other hand, the general information of value to everyone entering business was emphasized.

The purpose of the revision was twofold: first, the including of information which was not in the first edition but which surveys showed to be of value; second, the simplifying of the material by the breaking up of all chapters into short sections and the gradual approach to business problems through a consideration of simple activities readily understood by pupils.

In planning and completing the revision, the authors were aided by suggestions from a great number of teachers, especially from teachers of junior business training in the city of Des Moines. Acknowledgment is also due Mr. Irving 

R. Garbutt, Director, Department of Commercial Education, Cincinnati Public Schools; Mr. C. M. Yoder, President,

State Teachers College, Whitewater, Wisconsin; and the representatives of various banks, transportation companies, and other businesses, who carefully read the manuscript and made many important criticisms and valuable suggestions for its improvement.

TO THE PUPIL 

VERYONE comes in contact with business to some EVE extent. Many earn their livelihood in business positions. Others, such as doctors, lawyers, and mechanics, complete many business transactions. For example, a doctor must maintain his office, hire a nurse or nurses, buy supplies, and act in many other ways as a business man, In addition, he should know how to use his income in a skillful manner.

Even the pupils in your school have many contacts with business. A pupil may buy lunches, street car tickets, and books. He may spend money for picture shows and ball games. He may earn money or it may be given to him. If he saves part of his income, he may place it in a bank. All of these transactions bring him into contact with business organizations and require that he have some knowledge of business methods. Throughout his entire life, he will be completing similar transactions. Many of them may be of great importance to him and may involve the spending of large sums of money.

It is apparent that much of the success of those who earn their livelihood in business positions depends upon their skill in handling business affairs. It is equally true, however, that since everyone completes business transactions, everyone's success depends in part on his knowledge of business.

It is the purpose of this book to provide the information about business which everyone should have. As the book was being planned, studies were made to find what business information was most commonly used and what mistakes were most frequently made. Material was then

included in this text to provide the information most commonly needed in handling business affairs and in avoiding mistakes. 

As you master your lessons in the coming weeks, you

I will obtain various kinds of valuable information. 

First, you will learn how to manage your own business affairs more satisfactorily. This is important, for you will surely have many contacts with business. Much of your financial success will depend on how skillfully you complete transactions relating to your personal business. 

Second, you will learn something about different fields. of business. This will help you to decide whether you wish to make business your life work. If you decide to enter business, it will also help you to decide the type of work in which you will be most interested. 

Third, you will get much information which will aid you in obtaining and occupying a business position. You should remember, however, that this course is not a complete business training. One who has studied this text will be qualified for a minor business position and gradual promotion. 

But one whose training is more complete is qualified for a better position and can expect more rapid promotion. You should therefore plan to continue your training as long as possible. 

Fourth, you will obtain an excellent foundation for the study of advanced business subjects, and will thus be aided in preparing for more desirable business positions than are available for persons of limited training.

CHAPTER I 

MONEY

Section I. USE OF MONEY Importance of Correctly Handling Money. Money is used in many business transactions. If you buy a book, you may pay for it with money; if you work for some one, you may receive money for your services; if you save a part of your income and deposit it in the bank, the deposit may be in the form of money. These examples illustrate the frequent use of money in business. Because you handle money so frequently, it is important that you understand what money is, why it is needed, and how it should be handled. 

What Is Money? Money is anything which passes currently from hand to hand as a means of payment, and for which people will readily exchange their products or their services. We commonly think of money as con- 

Figure 1-Egyptian Ring Money sisting only of the paper cur Many centuries ago Egyptians carried money in the form of bracelets or rings worn about the arm. The figures accompanying the ring in the illustration are typical of the period in which this form of money was used. rency or the metal coins which we use. But in different countries and in different ages, many other things have been used as money. Among the American Indians, and to some extent among the early Colonists, wampum was a common form of money. Wampum consisted of beads made of shells strung together to form belts or sashes. In ancient 

England and in other parts of the world, iron bars were once used for money. The early Egyptians used bracelets or rings. Small shells known as cowrie shells have been used as money in many parts of the world. 

A great many other things which were valuable as ornaments or for other reasons, and which could be readily

[ch. 1 

passed from one person to another, have been used as money. In practically all parts of the world, however, money 

Figure 2-Chinese Shirt Money 

Coins such as this were used in 

China about 2600 years ago. Like other early people, the Chinese made their first metal coins to represent articles of value which had previously been used as mediums of exchange. 

The street scene at the right of the coin is typical of the period in which this coin was used. similar to ours is now used because it is more convenient than the more primitive types. 

Why Is Money Needed? 

Before money was used, a person could get those things which he wished by exchanging articles with someone else. An Indian who owned furs might trade them for ornaments, weapons, or other things he wished. Exchanging goods for goods in this way is known as barter.

The exchange of goods between individuals may be satisfactory among primitive people who seldom engage in commerce. But it would be very difficult for a person living in this country to trade something for everything that he needed. What, except money, can you give a merchant for a book, or the manager of a cafeteria for a lunch? You may not have any goods that these people care for, but both of them will gladly accept money for your purchase. They, in turn, can spend this money for what they wish. Money is therefore called a medium of exchange because it aids in the exchange of goods between different individuals. 

Figure 3-Barter

Early colonists frequently traded beads and other ornaments for furs owned by the Indians. 

The value of different goods or articles is usually expressed in terms of money. that a pencil costs five cents, a notebook thirty cents, and 

For example, you may say

ch. 1] 

MONEY 

a book one dollar. In each case you have given the value of the article in money. Since money is used to measure the value of things, it is called a standard of value. Money is therefore said to be a medium of exchange and a standard of value. 

Money in the United States.

The basis of the system of money in this country, and in almost all others, is gold. A Figure 4-Money of Croesus 

A gold coin issued by Croesus 

2500 years ago. Croesus had such a great reputation for wealth that we still use the expression "rich as Croesus." Chariots, such as the one illustrated here, were used at the time this coin was in circulation. gold dollar contains a certain amount of pure gold (13.71 grains). All other forms of money issued by the United 

States Government are worth their face value because that value has been established in relation to gold. The term face value means the amount printed on a piece of money, either coin or bill, indicating the value placed on it by the government of the United States. 

Figure 5-Cowrie Shell 

Since the value of money depends upon the amount of gold that can be obtained for it, the actual value of the material of which it is made is not important. It does not cost the same to make one ten-dollar bill as it does to make ten one-dollar bills, ten silver dollars, one hundred dimes, or one thousand pennies. 

Nevertheless, a ten-dollar bill passes from one person to another at the same value as these other forms of money because the same amount of gold can be obtained for it. In other words, the money which we commonly use has certain values because it is issued by the United States Government, and its value in gold is fixed by the government. 

Small shells similar to the one illustrated here have been used for money by primitive people in many parts of the world. They constituted one of the earliest known forms of money and are still used for money in isolated communities in Africa, India, and the South 

Seas. This form of money is as primitive as the scene which is shown at its right.

MONEY Section II. HANDLING MONEY 

Making Change. If you purchase a pencil for five cents, it is not necessary that you offer the clerk five pennies or a nickel. You may give him a dime, a quarter, a larger coin, or a bill, and he will return the difference. This is called making change. 

Suppose you give a clerk a five-dollar bill in payment for a pencil costing five cents. If he makes change properly, he will leave this bill in plain sight on the counter or cash register so that it can be seen while he is making change. This will prevent a mistake about the amount given him. He will then take the change from the cash drawer or cash register and count it as follows: 

To indicate the amount of the purchase, he will say "five cents." 

He will then give you two dimes, saying "twenty-five cents." 

He will then give you a quarter, saying "fifty cents." 

He will then give you a half-dollar, saying "one dollar." 

Finally, he will give you four dollars, one at a time, saying "two, three, four, five dollars." 

The clerk may abbreviate the counting by saying "five, twenty-five, fifty, one dollar, two, three, four, five dollars."

This method of counting out change increases accuracy and prevents misunderstandings. For this reason you 00000 00000 00000 00000 Co 

Courtesy National Cash Register Co. 

Figure 6-Making Change 

Note that the bill is left in sight while change is being made. 

The coins are given to the customer one at a time.

[ch. 1 

should follow this method when you make change. You should also use the same method when you take the change from a cash drawer, or from your pocket or purse. You will then have in your hand the pieces of money needed for change and can count them out quickly.

In making change, one should use the smallest number of coins possible. If a customer gives a clerk one dollar for a fifty-cent purchase, the clerk should return a half-dollar in change, and not five dimes or ten nickels. This method leaves as many pieces of change as possible for future transactions. It also is a convenience for the customer as he usually does not care to have a large number of small coins. 

Counting Coins. When you count money, you will find the work easier if you first separate the coins and bills so that each denomination is by itself. It is then helpful to divide the coins of one denomination into stacks containing equal amounts, such as stacks of fifty pennies, forty nickels, or fifty dimes. If you are going to give this money to anyone else who will also have to count it, you should wrap the stacks of coins separately in small papers made for this purpose and furnished by banks. These wrappers are provided for the following amounts:

NUMBER OF COINS 

50 40 50 40 20 20 

DENOMINATION OF COINS 

pennies nickels dimes quarters 

TOTAL AMOUNT 

$ .50 2.00 5.00 10.00 10.00 20.00 half dollars silver dollars

One who wraps coins should stamp or write his name on the package so that responsibility for errors can be placed. Banks ordinarily will not accept wrapped coins unless each package is identified in this manner. An individual or business receiving wrapped coins should verify the amount of each package; otherwise loss may arise from the mistakes of others.

Smaller quantities of any one denomination than the number given above are kept loose, as they could not be

ch. 1] 

MONEY 

easily counted if they were wrapped separately or with coins of another denomination. For example, if one has ninety nickels, he can make two packages of forty coins each. He should not, however, wrap the additional ten nickels separately or in one of the packages, as they could not then be counted promptly. 

Figure 7-Wrapping Coins 

When large numbers of coins are deposited in the bank, the bank usually requires that they be wrapped. Even if wrapping is not required, it is desirable, as it enables the bank clerk to count the money faster and thus to take care of all customers more promptly. If you were to go into a bank, you would not wish to wait for attention until a clerk had counted a large number of small coins for several customers.

Counting Paper Money. When paper money is to be counted, it should first be sorted according to denominations and should be right side up. The right side is the one on which appears the picture of some great American, such as Washington, Lincoln, or Hamilton. Arranging money in this way makes it easier to count and aids in securing accuracy.

[ch. 1 

The bills should be smoothed and placed in neat bundles. 

This makes them easier to handle and therefore easier to count accurately. It also improves their condition so that they can be used more satisfactorily by others.

MONEY Section III. MACHINES FOR HANDLING COINS 

Introduction. Machines of various kinds are used to increase the ease and accuracy with which businesses can handle large numbers of coins. The machines most commonly used are cash registers, counting and wrapping machines, and change-making machines. These machines are of many sizes and styles to fit different business conditions. 100 

Courtesy National Cash Register Co. 

Figure 8-Cash Register 

Cash Register. A cash register is a machine which records cash receipts. It is provided with one or more cash drawers in which cash received is placed. Cash registers are commonly used in retail stores, especially in those selling largely for cash.

The clerk operating the register presses one or more keys to record the amount of the sale. This also opens a cash drawer so that the amount received can be deposited and change can be returned to the customer, if that is necessary.

Cash receipts are added by the machine as they are recorded. This enables the merchant to know at the end of the day exactly how much money he received. If there are several clerks, provision can be made for adding the sales of each clerk separately, and a separate cash drawer can be used

[ch. 1 

by each. This enables the merchant to know the amount of the sales of each clerk and to hold the clerk responsible for the change given him in the morning and the cash received during the day.

At the time each amount is received and recorded in the cash register, the register prints a receipt which is given to the customer. 

This shows the amount of the customer's purchase. If the receipt does not show the same amount as the customer was charged, it is evident that a mistake was made. 

JAN 10 016 00.08 00.30 00.40 00.30 00.19 00.50 $001.77CaB

THANK YOU 

Figure 9 Cash Register

Counting and Wrapping Machines. When many coins are handled, it is difficult to count and wrap them by hand. Machines have therefore been developed for these purposes. One type of a counting machine counts only one kind of coin at a time. In another type, coins of all denominations from 

Figure 10-Coin-Sorting and 

Counting Machine

The coins in the tray are pushed by the operator into the machine. The machine sorts and counts them, and drops them into the containers. The numbers of coins of the various denominations are shown on the small registers at the top of the machine. Receipt pennies to half-dollars are sorted and counted. 

Wrapping machines wrap coins in small packages similar to those described on page 14 and illustrated in 

Figure 7 on page 15. Sorting, counting, and wrapping machines are useful in banks, and wherever large numbers of small coins are handled, as in the cafeteria of a large city school.

Change-Making Ma- chines. In many businesses it is necessary for a cashier or a clerk to make change quickly and accurately for customers making small purchases. For example, those buying tickets for a moving

ch. 1] 

MONEY 

picture show or paying for a lunch in a cafeteria seldom give the cashier the exact amount required to pay for the ticket or the lunch. Some change must therefore be given to almost every customer. Counting change hurriedly may lead to mistakes. Change-making machines are therefore used in many instances. 

Figure 11-Change-Making Machine 

There are change-making machines of two general styles. 

Both have one key for each amount from one cent to one dollar. In using one style, the clerk pushes the key for the amount desired. If he wishes sixty-three cents, he pushes the key for sixty-three cents and this amount falls out below. This machine is ordinarily used when people call for a certain amount of cash, as in a bank. 

When the other style of change-making machine is used, the difference between one dollar and the amount printed on a key can be secured by pressing the key. A customer may make a purchase amounting to sixty-three cents and may give the clerk one dollar. The clerk then presses the key for sixty-three cents, and the difference between one dollar and sixty-three cents, or thirty-seven cents, drops out. This saves two operations for the clerk.

First, it makes it unnecessary for him to subtract to find the amount of the change; and, second, it makes it unnecessary for him to count out the change.

[ch. 1 

If an amount less than a dollar is given to pay for a purchase, change can also be easily secured from a changemaking machine. Suppose a quarter and a half-dollar are given to pay a bill of sixty-three cents. A special key for the amount received, seventy-five cents, is pressed, and then the key for sixty-three cents. The difference between seventy-five cents and sixty-three cents drops out. 

Verifying Change. Whenever a person receives change, he should count it immediately. If an error has been made, it should be called to the attention of the clerk before the money is taken out of his sight, or before it is mixed with other money. Errors are not probable when a machine is used, but even with a machine errors are possible. The amount of the change should therefore be verified, regardless of the method which the clerk uses in securing it. 

1. Name several stores in which you have seen cash registers. 

2. How is change made in stores not using cash registers? If possible, give illustrations of those stores with which you are familiar. 

3. If you pay a clerk ninety cents for a purchase, do you care whether he gives you a cash register receipt? Why? 

4. If you paid a clerk $1.75 for a purchase and you observed that he rang up only $1.25 on the cash register, what would you do? 

5. If you paid a clerk $1.75 for a purchase and you observed that he rang up $2.00 on the cash register, what would you do? 

6. Explain how a person uses a change-making machine such as that used in a bank. 

7. Explain how a person uses a change-making machine such as that used in a moving picture show. 

8. A cashier makes change for you by using a changemaking machine. If you believe the machine to be accurate, should you count the change? 

9. Which is more likely to need a coin-sorting machine, a shoe store selling shoes for five dollars, or a five-and-tencent store? Why? 

10. The Brown Grocery Company uses a cash register with four cash drawers, one for each clerk. What is the advantage of having several cash drawers?

CHAPTER II BANK CHECKS Section I. FORM OF CHECKS 

Need for a Substitute for Money. For many transactions money is a satisfactory medium of exchange. If a person makes a small purchase in a retail store, he may give the clerk a coin or a bill in payment. But the situation may be different if he buys an overcoat for thirty dollars. 

He may not have this amount with him. Many people do not wish to carry much money since there is always some danger that it may be lost or stolen.

The situation is also different if a person buys something which costs a large amount, such as a house or an automobile. Obviously, he would not carry with him enough money to pay for such a purchase, because the danger of loss would be too great. 

Use of Bank Checks. The foregoing examples show that there is a need for something which can be used as a substitute for money. This need has been met by the use of bank checks. A bank accepts deposits of money from its customers (those doing business with it). A customer may then direct the bank to pay to another person or his order, an amount not larger than the amount deposited. If he deposits $200, he may direct the bank to pay $200 for him to one person, or he may have payments made to several persons, the total not exceeding $200. This makes it unnecessary for him to keep large sums of money on hand. 

The order directing the bank to make payment for the depositor must be in writing. Such a written order is called a bank check, or simply a check. It is necessary that the check be written so that the bank will have a record showing that the depositor ordered it to pay out his money. 

Furthermore, the use of written orders is convenient for the depositor since he does not have to go to the bank to direct that payment be made. He writes a check and gives 24

ch. 2] 

BANK CHECKS 

it to the one who is to receive payment. This person may then obtain the money from the bank. 

Mellon National Bank, Pittsburgh 

Figure 12-Lobby of a Bank

Form of Checks. A check does not have to be written in any special form. A letter signed by the depositor directing a bank to pay some one a definite sum of money is a valid check. If Robert McKee has money deposited in the 

Merchants National Bank of Columbus and wishes to pay 

George Phillips $7.32, he may write the letter shown at the top of the next page, to the bank. 

Although the form illustrated here is sufficient, it is not commonly used because it is not convenient. It requires that everything in the check be written by hand. When

[ch. 2 

October 18, 193 

Merchants National Bank 

Columbus, Ohio 

Please pay to George Phillips seven dollars and thirty-two cents and charge. this payment to me. 

Robert McKee one writes many checks, as some people do, this method is troublesome and requires much time. 

The hand-written form might be written on a sheet of paper of any size. Some checks might be written on large sheets, 812 x 11 inches in size, and others, on small pieces of paper, possibly about two inches square. Furthermore, the information on different checks might be arranged in various ways. Bank clerks would find it difficult to handle checks of many different sizes and different arrangements. 

It is much more convenient if all checks are about the same size as paper money, and are similar in form. 

For these reasons, banks furnish printed checks to their customers. The checks used by different banks are usually about the same size; on each is printed the information common to all checks. The one writing a check has only to fill in the blanks. This can be done much more quickly than the entire check can be written.

Robert McKee's check, shown at the top of this page, appears as in Figure 13 when it is written on a printed form.

Figure 13-Bank Check

DOLLARS

ch. 2] 

BANK CHECKS 

The printed parts of the check include the name and the address of the bank, "193_," "No.-," "Pay to the order of," "$," and "Dollars." The name and the address of the bank are given to show which bank is directed to make payment. The figures "193_" are given as a part of the date.

The month and day are written in the space before these figures, and the figure indicating the exact year directly after them. "No." is the abbreviation for "number." It is customary to number checks for convenience in referring to them. 

The words "Pay to the order of" precede the space in which is written the name of the one to whom payment is to be made. The words "Pay to" could be used, but in that case the bank could make payment to George Phillips only. 

Mr. Phillips might not wish to go to the Merchants National

Bank of Columbus for his money but might wish to transfer the check to some one else. When the words "Pay to the order of" are used, the check is payable to George 

Phillips or to any one to whom he properly transfers it. (The methods of transferring checks from one person to another are discussed in Chapter III.) This part of the check might also be written "Pay to George Phillips or order."

The amount is written in figures after the symbol, "$," and in words before the word, "Dollars." Writing the amount twice lessens the chance of error and makes it more difficult for anyone to alter the check.

Bank Numbers. Numbers are printed on checks to indicate the location of the bank on which the check is drawn. 

The use of these numbers is discussed in Chapter IV, Section II.

1. L. C. Holmes has $285 on deposit in the Front Street 

Bank of Blair, Kentucky. He gives the following letter to 

O. C. Klinger: 

The Front Street Bank of Blair, Kentucky: 

Please pay $78.75 to O. C. Klinger or his order and charge this amount to me. 

L. C. Holmes

(a) Will the bank pay Mr. Klinger the amount mentioned in this letter? (b) Is this a bank check? (c) Would the bank prefer that Mr. Holmes use a different form? 

2. Why are checks seldom written for amounts less than one dollar? 

3. C. J. Heckert has a bank account against which he can write checks. One afternoon he makes purchases in four stores. In one, he purchases a radio; in another, a pair of shoes; in another, a book; and in another, a pencil. For which purchase or purchases is he most likely to write checks? 

4. A person deposited $200 in a bank. Before he made any additional deposits, he wrote checks for $100, $50, $75, and $30. Would his bank pay all of these checks? 

5. The Harvey Variety Store is a retail store, selling small articles valued at twenty-five cents and less. All sales are for cash. Should the store pay for the goods it buys in cash or by check? 

6. Why is it desirable for all checks to be approximately the same size? 

7. Why is it necessary for a check to show the name and the address of the bank on which it is drawn? 

8. One check reads, "Pay to the order of L. C. Holmes." 

Another reads, "Pay to L. C. Holmes." Does this difference in wording make any difference in the ways in which 

Holmes can dispose of the checks? 

9. L. C. Holmes telephones his bank and asks it to pay twenty dollars to L. M. Adams. Will the bank make the payment for him? 

10. (a) How many times is the amount written on a check? (b) Why is it written more than once? 

11. Under what circumstances would you prefer another to give you a check instead of money? 

12. Why would you prefer to receive a check from a person who has money in a bank rather than from a person who does not?

BANK CHECKS Section II. CHECK STUB AND CHECK REGISTER 

The Check Stub. Checks are usually bound in books, each sheet in the book consisting of two parts, the check itself and the check stub. The check stub and the check are separated by a perforated line. After the check is written, it is torn out on this line. The stubs are retained in the book where they are used by the depositor as his record of deposits and checks.

Figure 14-Bank Checks With Stubs

Filling Out the Check Stub. Figure 14 shows stubs properly filled out. The number of the check is written on the first line of the stub. This is desirable so that the stub corresponding to a certain check can be located quickly. The date is entered on the second line, and the name of the one in whose favor the check is written, on the third. The fourth is frequently left blank, but it may be used for a brief explanation of the purpose of the check if the name given on the preceding line is not sufficient explanation. 

In Figure 14 the explanation "club dues" is entered on the first stub, as otherwise the writer might not know why a check had been given to Dean Long. The reason for the second check was not shown on the stub as the writer probably gave checks to the Johnson Shoe Store only in payment

[ch. 2 

for shoes.

He would therefore know the reason for this payment of $5.85.

The foregoing information and the amount of the check should be written on each check stub before the check is filled out. If this is not done, one may delay filling out the stub until he has forgotten the purpose of the check and its amount.

The lower part of the check stubs in Figure 14 is used as a record of deposits and checks, and shows the balance on hand after each check is written. This information is needed, for banks are not supposed to cash (to pay) checks for more than the sum of the deposits, and usually they will refuse to do so. A person should therefore be careful not to write checks for more than the amounts deposited, for a refusal by his bank to cash his checks will tend to injure his reputation. Also, writing checks for more than one has in the bank is sometimes forbidden by law.

If a bank, through an error or as an accommodation to the depositor, pays an amount greater than the depositor's balance, the latter's account is said to be overdrawn. The depositor is notified at once and is directed to deposit funds to make up the shortage.

The first line of the lower half of each stub in the illustration is for the balance on hand after the preceding check has been written. The next is for deposits. Usually one entry is made on this line as a person ordinarily draws checks more frequently than he makes deposits. Two deposits may be entered on one line, if necessary. The third line shows the total of the original balance and the deposit. 

This is the total in the bank against which checks may be drawn. The following line is for the amount of the check. 

When the amount of the check is subtracted from the amount available, the difference is the balance against which other checks may be written. This new balance is then entered on the next check stub.

Check Register. Those who issue a large number of checks do not always keep the information about the balances on the check stubs. They may find it more convenient

ch. 2] 

BANK CHECKS 

to enter this information in a separate book known as a check register. Even if the register is used, the check number, the date, the name of the one to whom payment is made, the purpose, and the amount of the check are ordinarily entered on the stub. The entry in the register is then made from these data. 

Figure 15 shows a typical check register. The first line shows the first deposit, the first check, and the balance after the first check was written. Additional checks and deposits

BANK CHECKS Section III. WRITING CHECKS 

Need for Care in Writing Checks. A check directs a bank to pay money for the depositor and to deduct this amount from his balance. Great care must therefore be taken in writing checks. Otherwise, errors which will cause the depositor serious loss are likely to occur. 

Use of Ink. A bank can pay any amount called for by a check which is signed by the depositor and can deduct that amount from the depositor's balance. If the depositor writes a check for $10, but someone changes the amount to $100, the bank can charge the depositor for $100. The depositor thus loses $90, the difference between the original amount of the check and the amount paid by the bank. 

A check should therefore be written in ink because checks so written cannot be changed easily. This is especially true because many banks print their checks on paper manufactured with waved lines so that ink cannot be erased without the erasure showing plainly. The check in Figure 

17 was written on this kind of paper. 

TODD Check-Writing Machines. The writer of a check may further guard against possible changes in the amount by using a check-writing machine. A machine of this kind stamps the amount on the proper line in such a way that it cannot be altered easily. 

When a machine is used, all information except the amount should be written with a pen. Check-writing machines provide the greatest security against loss, but a check carefully written in ink is reasonably safe.

PROTECTOGRAPH KEEP MACHINE WELL INRED 

Figure 16-Check-Writing Machine 

Date. The date of a check is entered in the space provided on the check, the same as it is entered on the stub.

[ch. 2 

 KING COUNTY BANK PAY TO THE ORDER OF.

ROZ+THIRTY EIGHT DOLLARS SEVENTY FIVE CENTS COZOY DOLLARS

Figure 17-Check Written with a Check-Writing Machine 

It includes the month, the day of the month, and the year. 

Ordinarily the date is the day on which the check is written, but occasionally a check is dated with a future date. 

For example, a check written on October 1 may be dated

October 15. A check dated ahead in this way is known as a post-dated check. The bank will not pay it before the day corresponding with the date.

Since a post-dated check will not be accepted by the bank before its date, such a check is usually written when the drawer wishes to issue a check but does not wish the amount deducted from his account at once. Obviously, this check is less desirable for the one to whom it is given than a check with a current date, for money cannot be gotten for it until the date of the check. For this reason a post-dated check is not issued except by special agreement. 

Number. Each check in a check book and the corresponding check stub are numbered consecutively, beginning with "1" or the next number higher than the last one in the preceding check book. Space is provided on both the stub and the check for the number. Since each check has the same number as the corresponding stub, the depositor can conveniently compare the checks with the stubs after the checks are paid and returned to him by the bank. The method of making this comparison and its value are dis cussed in Chapter IV. 

Payee. Each check directs the bank to make a payment for the depositor. The one to whom the payment is to be

ch. 35 

BANK CHECKS 

made is known as the payee. Thus, in Figure 17 the payee is A. B. Conover.

Usually a check is made payable to an individual or an organization, but it may be made payable to "Cash" or "Bearer." The check in Figure 18 is payable to "Cash."

Figure 18-Check Payable to "Cash" 

A check payable to "Cash" or "Bearer" can be cashed by anyone. For this reason a person should not ordinarily write such checks, for the safety provided by a check is largely due to the fact that it can be cashed only on the order of the payee. A check may safely be made payable to "Cash" if the writer cashes it immediately after writing it. 

In this case there is obviously no danger of loss. A check may also be made payable to "Bearer" when the maker wishes to enable someone to cash it without first being identified. Such instances occur rarely. Usually the name of a person or an organization is entered as the payee. 

Amount. The amount of the check is written in figures as close to the dollar sign as possible to prevent the amount being changed by the insertion of another figure. The cents are written in smaller figures slightly higher than the other figures and are underlined. This prevents their being confused with the dollars. The cents are written close to the dollars so that another figure cannot be inserted to change the amount.

The dollars in the amount are written in words at the left end of the line provided for this purpose. This makes

This check illustrates the method used in writing the amount of a check in figures and in words when the amount is large. it impossible to change the amount by the addition of a word at the beginning of the line. These words are followed by the cents written as the fraction of a dollar, or as so many hundredths of a dollar. (See Figure 19.) If the words and the fraction do not fill the space, a waved line is usually drawn from the fraction to the word "Dollars," as this makes it more difficult to alter the amount. 

Checks are seldom written for amounts less than one dollar because it is usually more convenient to make such small payments with money. A check may, however, be written for any amount. If a check is written for less than one dollar, the form used in Figure 20 should be followed in writing the amount in figures and in words. 19.3

ch. 2] 

BANK CHECKS 

If the amount in figures is not the same as the amount in words, the amount written in words is considered to be the amount of the check. When there is a material difference between the two amounts, the bank probably will investigate before making payment to see which is correct. 

It is extremely important that the writer of a check enter the amount in figures and in words with care. If he is careless and leaves spaces in which additional figures or words may be added, the amount of the check may be increased. (A check on which the amount has been increased is known as a raised check.) The bank is not expected to know the amount for which the check was originally written; hence, it may pay the amount called for and charge that amount to the depositor. It is therefore the depositor's responsibility to write checks so that they cannot be altered. without the changes being readily observable.

[ch. 2 

 Section IV. WRITING CHECKS-Concluded 

Signature. When a person opens an account at a bank, and before he begins to write checks, he must place his signature on file at the bank. The bank clerk can then compare this signature with that on any check written by the customer. The customer must sign all checks with the same signature as that which is on file at the bank. If he does not do this, the bank will not pay the checks. The one signing the check is commonly known as the drawer. 

The bank may subtract from the depositor's balance only the amounts of checks which he actually signs. If some one else signs the depositor's name and the bank pays the check, it is the bank's loss and not the depositor's. The bank is supposed to know the depositor's signature and to make payment only on his order. This situation is different from that occurring when a check is raised. As was explained in the preceding section, a loss resulting from a raised check falls on the depositor.

When a person signs a check for a company or an organization, he should clearly indicate this fact. If a check is written by James Lucas for the Lucas Paint Company, the signature should be:

LUCAS PAINT CO. 

By James Lucas 

A check signed in this way is subtracted from the bal ance of the Lucas Paint Company's account. Mr. Lucas' 

name is added to show that someone with authority wrote the check for the company.

Similarly, if a check is written by R. O. Green for the 

School Athletic Fund, the signature should be:

School Athletic Fund 

By ROGreen In some instances the funds of an organization are deposited in the bank in the rame of the treasurer, as "R. O.

ch. 2] 

BANK CHECKS 

Green, Treasurer." In such cases the person's name and his title are used as the signature of the checks of the organization. Mr. Green should not sign his name only, as this would cause the checks to be charged to him personally. 

Address. When a check is sent to a firm that may have several customers with the same name or with similar names, the drawer may write his address under his signature. Figure 21 shows a check which Mrs. L. A. Kent gave to C. A. Brown & Son, on which she wrote her address. As 

PAY TO THE ORDER OF. 

HUNTINGTON, WEST VA... April 17193 No. 11 The First National Bank 

C. A Brown & Son - 

Eightun ice- 

FOR cash purchase 50 100 1850 

DOLLARS 

Mrs. LA Kent 

270 Gilbert Ave, City 

Figure 21-Bank Check

Note that this check is written on a form on which the name of the bank is not printed. The purpose of the check is indicated in the lower left-hand corner. The address of the drawer is written directly below her name. this store probably has a large number of customers, Mrs. 

Kent thought some other person with a similar name might be credited for her payment if her address were not given. 

The address following the signature may be required by a bank if the bank has two or more customers with the same name and needs a means of identifying the checks of each. 

Purpose of Check. If a person wishes his check to show exactly why payment was made, hẻ may indicate the purpose of the check on the check itself. This information may be useful if there is a dispute about why the check was issued. Ordinarily, if the purpose of the check is given, it is written in the lower left-hand corner, as in Figure 21. 

Checks Written on Special Forms. One usually writes checks on forms supplied by his own bank. These forms show the name of the bank and its location. Sometimes it

[ch. 2 

is necessary for a person to write a check when he does not have a blank check of his own bank. In such cases he may be able to secure from some other bank or from a store a check form which does not show the name or the name and the address of a bank. The check may be written on this form in the usual way, except that the name or the name and the address of the drawer's bank must be written in. 

Figure 21 shows such a check.

Similarly, a check can be written on the ordinary form of a bank other than the one in which a person has a deposit. 

The name and the address of that bank must be crossed out, and the name and the address of the drawer's bank must be written in. This method is to be avoided if possible, as changing the name of the bank may cause confusion.

CHAPTER III  Section I. INDORSEMENT OF CHECKS 

Introduction. In previous chapters we have observed that a person can avoid handling large sums of money by depositing his money in a bank and making his payments by check. The payee of a check can transfer it to another person, can deposit it in his bank, or can get money for it from a bank. Before another person or a bank accepts a check from the payee, the latter is required to write his name on the back of the check. A signature of this kind is called an indorsement; the act of signing in this manner is called indorsing.

Need for Indorsements. We have already learned that a check is usually made payable to the order of a person or a business. This means that a bank will pay a depositor's money only upon the order of the one in whose favor a check is written. It is not possible for the bank to make payment upon an oral request, for then it could not prove that payment had been ordered by the proper person. For this reason, the indorsement is required. The indorsement of the payee provides written proof that the payment was made to the one in whose favor the check was written, or on his order.

Not only is the indorsement of checks desirable from the point of view of the bank, but it is also useful to the depositor. Suppose you pay a merchant twenty dollars by check. When he cashes the check, he will be required to indorse it. At the end of the month the bank will return to you all the checks which have been paid and deducted from your balance. If the merchant forgets to give you credit for the payment and tries to collect a second time, you can show him your check with his indorsement and thus prove that you do not owe him this debt. 45

[ch. 3 

Position of Indorsement. An indorsement should always be placed on the back of a check near the left end. The check will not be refused by the bank if the indorsement is ABConover 

OUNTY BANK 

19 26 

June 5, 19.3 No629 $3875 

DOLLARS 

Charles M. Burke

Figure 22-Bank Check Showing Indorsement placed elsewhere, but the usual practice should be followed. 

It is much more convenient for everyone to handle checks when they are all indorsed in the same position. 

Form of Indorsement. In indorsing a check, the payee should sign his name exactly as it was written on the face by the drawer. If Charles Selden receives a check for ten dollars made payable to "C. R. Selden," he must indorse it "C. R. Selden." It will not be satisfactory for him to sign his name "Charles Selden" or even "Charles R. Selden."

If a person's name is not given correctly on a check, he should first indorse it exactly as the name is given and then write his name correctly as a second indorsement. If, in the case mentioned in the preceding paragraph, the check were made payable to Charles Seldom, Mr. Selden could indorse it as follows: 

Charles Seldom 

Charles Selden

Responsibility of the Indorser. When one indorses a check, his indorsement serves as a guarantee to subsequent holders that the check will be paid. If you receive ten dollars from a bank in exchange for a check signed by another person and later it is found that the signature was forged,

ch. 3] 

you will be required by the bank to refund the amount of the check. Or, if a check is properly signed but the drawer does not have ten dollars in the bank, you will be required to pay the bank this amount. 

A check is sometimes transferred several times by indorsement. Each indorser guarantees the payment of the check to the succeeding holders. If, therefore, you hold a check indorsed by three persons and if it is refused by the bank, you can collect the amount of the check from the drawer or from any one of the three indorsers. 

1. Is a check indorsed by the payee or the drawer? 

2. What are the purposes of an indorsement? 

3. If you have a checking account, your bank will require that those cashing your checks indorse them. Is this an advantage to you? 

4. M. R. Haynes indorses a check by writing his name on the back near the middle. Is this a valid indorsement? 

5. Describe a method of indorsing a check which is preferable to that given in Problem 4. 

6. John Smith receives a check intended for him, but with his name written as "J. R. Smith." If his middle initial is "R," how should he indorse the check for transfer? 

7. If Mr. Smith, in Problem 6, has no middle initial, how should he indorse the check?

Later the bank returned it to him saying that it was not good because the drawer had no money in the bank against which he could write checks. Did Mr. Anderson lose the value of the check? 

9. A check is indorsed in succession by S. M. Hanson, E. J. Elliott, and F. R. Cole. Mr. Cole deposits the check in his bank. Later it is found that the check is not good, and the original drawer cannot be located. Who loses the value of the check? 

10. E. J. Elliott received a check made payable to his order. The check was cashed by another person without 

Mr. Elliott's indorsement. Who lost the value of the check?

[ch. 3 

 Section II. KINDS OF INDORSEMENTS 

Introduction. There are several forms of indorsements that are used for different purposes. The more common are indorsement in blank, indorsement in full, and restrictive indorsement. These forms of indorsements will be discussed in the following paragraphs. 

Indorsement in Blank. When the payee of a check writes only his name on the back of a check, he is said to indorse it in blank. The effect of this indorsement is to make the check payable to any holder. Any one receiving such a check can get money for it. Also, a check so indorsed can be transferred an unlimited number of times without further indorsement. 

EL. Miller 

Figure 23-Indorsement in Blank 

Since an indorsement guarantees that a check will be paid, few people will accept a check unless it is indorsed by the one transferring it, even though it may have been indorsed in blank by a previous holder. The indorsement of the one transferring a check is required, as this gives an additional guarantee that the check will be paid. Furthermore, if the check is not good, it probably will be easier for the one receiving the check to collect from the one who gave it to him rather than from a previous indorser or the drawer.

Indorsement in Full. An indorsement in blank may always be used, but sometimes other indorsements are preferable. Suppose you have a check for ten dollars made payable to yourself, which you wish to make payable to 

C. R. Anderson in another city. If you simply sign your name on the back of the check, that is, indorse it in blank, C. R. Anderson can cash the check when he receives it. If, however, it is lost in the mail and is found by someone, that person can cash it because the indorsement in blank makes the check payable to bearer.

To avoid this possibility of loss, you may indorse the check by placing these words before your signature:

ch. 3] 

"Pay to the order of C. R. Anderson," as in Figure 24.

An indorsement of this kind is called an indorsement in full. 

The bank will not cash this check until it is indorsed by Mr. Anderson, so you can send it to him by mail or by messenger with the assurance that it will not be cashed by anyone else. 

Pay to the order of C. R. Anderson 

EL. Miller 

Figure 24-Indorsement in Full 

Restrictive Indorsement. You may have several checks which you wish deposited in the bank at a time when it is not convenient for you to go to the bank. If you indorse these checks in blank and send them to the bank by messenger or by mail, they may be lost and cashed by anyone who finds them. This danger is avoided by an indorsement which restricts the use of the check and which is therefore known as a restrictive indorsement. If you place the words "For deposit" above your signature, as in Figure 25, you have restricted or limited the use of the check so that it can only be deposited to your account. If a check so 

For Deposit 

E.L. Miller indorsed should be lost, it could not be cashed by the finder. The drawer would therefore be willing to give you another copy of the check, for the first one could not be deducted from

Figure 25-Restrictive Indorsement his bank balance. 

Use of Ink. Indorsements should always be written in ink so that there will be no possibility of their being altered. 

This is especially true when indorsements are in full or are restrictive. If an indorsement in full were written with a pencil, all of the indorsement except the signature might be erased and the holder would then have a check indorsed in blank. 

Rubber-Stamp Indorsements. An indorsement is usually written by hand, but it may be made by a rubber stamp if

[ch. 3 

the indorsement is restrictive and the amount is being deposited in the indorser's account. The reason for this is obvious. It makes no difference who indorses a check which is deposited in the account of the true owner, for, regardless of who indorses the check, only the owner secures the benefit from it.

Rubber-stamp indorsements are also satisfactory for indorsements by banks as they are, in fact, indorsements for deposit. In Figure 27, page 52, the bank indorsements were all made with rubber stamps. 

Richard Lange

 Section III. PROCEDURES IN TRANSFERRING CHECKS 

Transfer of Checks Between Banks. Banks have a system whereby checks are transferred from one bank to another. This makes it unnecessary for one to go to the bank on which a check is drawn in order to get his money. For example, if you receive a check drawn on the Merchants National Bank of Columbus, you need not go to that bank in order to get the amount of the check. You can take the check to any bank and either deposit it or secure money for it. That bank will then transfer the check to the bank on which it was drawn. A check can be handled in this way regardless of whether it was drawn on a bank in the payee's city or in another city. 

Figure 27-Back of Check with Indorsements

Transfer of Checks Between Individuals. It is not even necessary that one take a check which he has received to a bank in order to get value for it. His indorsement is his order to the bank to make payment. For this reason, a properly indorsed check can be passed from one person to another in much the same way as money. 

Identification of Drawer. A check is valuable only when it is drawn on a bank in which the drawer has money on deposit. For this reason, if you accept a check, you should be sure that the drawer has money on deposit in the bank on which the check is drawn. If you receive a check from

ch. 3] 

someone you know, you can be reasonably certain that he does have money on deposit. If, by mistake, the drawer has overdrawn his account, you can locate him and collect from him the amount of the check.

On the other hand, if a check is offered to you by a stranger, you have no way of knowing whether he actually has money in the bank on which the check is drawn. If his check proves to be of no value, you probably will not be able to locate the drawer and to make collection from him. For this reason, you should not accept the check of a stranger until he has in some way definitely proved that he is a responsible person and has an account in the bank on which his check is drawn.

Identification of Indorser. As has been explained, a bank is supposed to pay a check only on the order of the payee. 

If it makes payment without his order in the form of an indorsement, it cannot deduct the amount of the check from the depositor's balance. For this reason, a bank will not cash a check for a stranger, even though the check is drawn by a depositor of that bank. The one wishing to cash the check must be identified by a personal introduction or by the presentation of letters or papers which show that he actually is the payee. Similarly, an individual should not accept a check indorsed by a stranger, for if the indorsement does not prove to be that of the payee, the amount of the check cannot be collected from the bank. 

A check made payable to "Cash" or "Bearer" may be paid to anyone who presents it to the bank on which it is drawn. Many banks, however, will not cash such a check for a stranger as it is possible that this person may have stolen the check. The same identification is required as if the check were made payable to a certain person. This is a protection to the drawer since it makes it difficult for anyone to steal his checks made payable to "Cash" or "Bearer” and to get money for them. Of course, the drawer should not need this protection frequently as he should not make checks payable to "Cash" or "Bearer" unless he plans to cash them immediately.

[ch. 3 

Time of Cashing Checks. Suppose a person receives a check and deposits it in his bank, or cashes it within a reasonable length of time. (A reasonable length of time is usually considered to be any time during the following business day.) If the bank on which the check was drawn fails and is unable to pay checks drawn on it by its depositors, the payee will not lose anything. The drawer of the check will have to pay him again in money, or give him a check on another bank.

If, on the other hand, the payee holds the check a month before attempting to cash it and in the meantime the bank fails, the loss will be the payee's and the drawer will not have to pay him again. The reason for this is that the loss was caused by the payee's negligence in not cashing the check within a reasonable length of time. 

As a result, checks are seldom held long or transferred from one person to another many times. They usually are cashed at a bank or deposited as soon as convenient after being received.

Stopping Payment on a Check. The checks which a depositor writes are not charged to his account until they have been paid by the bank. If, before a check is paid, the drawer directs the bank not to pay it, the bank will refuse to honor it when it is presented for payment. This is called stopping payment on a check.

The value of the right to stop payment on a check is illustrated by the following example: Suppose Joseph Field loses a check which you have given him. He probably will ask you for another copy of this check. Before you give him the new copy, you should stop payment of the original. 

Then there will be no danger of the original, as well as the duplicate, being cashed.

When a bank is asked to stop payment on a check, it should be given full information about the check. In an emergency a bank will temporarily stop payment on a check

ch. 3] 

in answer to a telephone call.

It requires, however, that a written notice to stop payment be given as soon as possible. This written notice includes the date, the number, and the amount of the check, and the name of the payee. 

Usually, the information is recorded on a form similar to that in Figure 28. This form is then kept by the bank and compared with checks received so that payment will not be made at some future time. 

DENVER, July 22, 193

WESTERN NATIONAL BANK 

Number 102

CHAPTER IV  Section I. ESTABLISHING A BANK ACCOUNT 

Introduction. A bank keeps a record of the deposits made by each customer and the checks written by him. 

This is necessary so that it may at all times know the customer's balance and whether he actually has funds against which checks may be drawn. The bank's record of the deposits and checks of one person is known as that person's account.

Opening an Account. If a person who has a good reputation offers to open an account in a bank where he is acquainted, the banker will accept his deposit and will provide him with a check book. He will not hesitate to do so, for he will feel sure that the depositor will use the account properly.

On the other hand, if a stranger goes into a bank and asks to open an account, the banker will probably ask him to be introduced by someone or to provide some identification. This is necessary, for the banker always wants to be sure that a depositor will use his account honestly. Obviously, he can hold those whom he knows responsible for their acts more easily than strangers whom he might not be able to locate if any question arose regarding their accounts. 

If, at any time, you wish to open an account in a bank in which you are not known, you should ask some one to introduce you to the banker. If you cannot arrange for a per- . sonal introduction, you probably can show the banker some papers or letters to indicate that you are a dependable person and will use your account properly. 

Signature Card. We have observed in an earlier chapter that a bank will cash a check only if it is signed by a depositor. It is therefore necessary for the banker to have the signature of each depositor, with which the signatures on 58

ch. 4] 

checks can be compared. For this reason a depositor is asked to fill out a signature card at the time he makes his first deposit and before he is provided with a check book. Date

Figure 29-Signature Card for an Individual 

Figure 29 shows the signature card of James Logan. 

You will observe that, in addition to Mr. Logan's signature, the telephone number and the address are given. This information may be desired at some time by the bank, as it may wish to write him or telephone him about his account. 

On the signature card in the illustration there are four lines for signatures. Several lines are needed because a depositor may wish to have several signatures on file so that he can use different signatures on his checks. If he wishes to sign checks in different ways, it is necessary for him to have the different signatures on file because the bank is supposed to cash checks only when they are signed exactly like the signature, or one of the signatures, on the signature card.

Several lines may also be necessary for the signature card for a business or company account. A business must authorize one or more persons to sign checks for it. Usually the signature includes the name of the company and the name of a person authorized to sign. The signature card in 

Figure 30 shows the authorized signatures for the Vance 

Builders' Supply Company. Both Frank C. Vance and Her-

Joint Account. When two persons use one bank account, it is known as a joint account. Such an account is frequently used by a man and his wife. When a joint account is used, both persons may make deposits and write checks. Of course, the signatures of both must be on the signature card. Date

Joint accounts are sometimes troublesome. Each person may not know how many checks the other has written. 

As a result the account may be overdrawn. To prevent this,

ch. 4] 

only a part of the bank balance may be shown on the check stubs of each of the two depositors. For example, if the total balance is $200, each may enter only $100 on his check stub, and write checks against that amount only. This will reduce the danger of overdrawing the account.

[ch. 4 

 Section II. USING A BANK ACCOUNT 

Deposit Ticket. We have observed that when a customer directs a bank to make payments for him, he uses a printed form known as a check. Similarly, when a deposit is made, the items deposited are listed on a printed form. This is known as a deposit ticket or a deposit slip. This form is filled out by the customer and is given to the bank clerk with the deposit.

The deposit ticket provides spaces for the name of the depositor, the date, the items deposited, and the total. The items of the deposit are given for the convenience of the bank clerk in observing errors.

The items are classified as currency, silver, and checks. 

Under the title "Currency" is included all paper money. 

Metal money, including silver coins, nickels, and pennies, is listed as silver. Checks are listed separately to assist the clerk in verifying the amounts. 

Sometimes deposit tickets provide a space for gold, but gold is used as coin to such a small extent that this heading may not be given. 

Figure 32 shows two deposit tickets made out by Carl

L. Merton, a depositor in the Merchants National Bank. His first deposit consisted of seventeen dollars of currency and five checks. You will observe that in listing the first four checks he gave the name of the bank, but in listing the fifth check he gave the name of the city. When checks are drawn on banks in the city in which the deposit is being made, the names of the banks are given on the deposit ticket; when they are drawn on banks in other cities, the names of the cities are given.

Bank Numbers. The deposit ticket at the right in Figure 32 is similar to the other, but there are two points of difference. First, the deposit includes silver amounting to $3.58. Second, the banks on which the checks are drawn are indicated by numbers.

The American Bankers' Association has given a number to each bank in the United States. This number identifies all checks and helps in the forwarding of checks from one

Figure 32-Deposit Tickets bank to another. The larger cities, known as reserve centers, are numbered from one to forty-nine. The states are also assigned numbers from fifty to ninety-nine. Each bank is also given a number. Any bank can then be identified by two numbers, the city or state number and the bank number. These are known as American Bankers' Association numbers. They are also known by the abbreviation "A. 

B. A." numbers.

In the illustration of a check on page 65, the A. B. A. numbers are 13-1. The checks listed on the second deposit ticket in Figure 32 were drawn on banks Nos. 13-31, 12-16, 9-21, and 25-8. 

Some banks prefer that depositors use the names of banks or cities on deposit tickets while others prefer that they use the A. B. A. numbers. The depositor should follow the plan preferred by the bank with which he is doing business.

A bank's checks may also show the district of the Federal Reserve System in which the bank is located. This

[ch. 4 

number is shown in the lower part of the check illustrated on page 65. The Federal Reserve System number is not used by the depositor and is not to be confused with the 

A. B. A. numbers.

Bank Book. A bank furnishes each customer with a book in which deposits may be entered as they are made. 

This is known as a bank book or a pass book. The customer presents the bank book to the bank clerk together with the deposit ticket and the deposit. An entry for the amount of the deposit is then made by the clerk. This serves as a receipt for the amount deposited. When a customer does not have his book at the time a deposit is made, he can make out the deposit slip in duplicate. One of the deposit slips will then be receipted by the clerk and returned to the depositor. The amount of the deposit can later be entered in the pass book when the book is taken to the bank. 

Some banks prefer that the bank books be left with them at intervals for balancing. This is usually made unnecessary, however, by the use of bank statements, discussed in later paragraphs. The customer should find out from his bank whether it prefers to have the bank book left with it for balancing.

Deposits by Mail. When one cannot conveniently visit his bank to make a deposit, he can send the deposit through the mail. Deposits sent through the mail should not include money as the money might be lost. They should include only checks and similar forms, to be discussed later, which can be replaced without cost if they are lost. The indorsements should restrict the use of the checks with the words "For Deposit." Then, if the checks are lost in the mail or are stolen, they cannot be cashed. 

Withdrawing Money. To withdraw money from the bank, the depositor may write a check payable to himself and then indorse it as he would indorse the check of another. 

He may also write a check payable to "Cash." If he personally presents this check to the bank, an indorsement usually is not required.

ch. 4] 

The checks which a person writes and gives to others decrease his bank balance. They therefore result in a withdrawal of funds the same as checks which he writes and gives to the bank in exchange for money. 

Canceled Checks. When a bank has paid a check, it cancels the check with a rubber stamp or a canceling machine. 

The purpose of the cancellation is to show that the check has been paid and to avoid the possibility of its being paid a second time.

PAY TO THE ORDER OF.

Figure 33-Canceled Check 

The canceled checks are returned to the depositor periodically, usually at the end of each month. These canceled checks should be kept by the depositor because they are evidence that he has made payment to the payee of each check. If the purpose for which a check is issued is written on the front of a check, the receipt is more valuable as it not only shows that payment was made, but also gives the reason why it was made. 

Bank Statements. When a bank returns canceled checks to a customer, it also gives him a statement of his account known as a bank statement. This shows the depositor's balance at the end of the preceding month, the deposits during the month, the checks paid, and the balance at the end of the month. Figure 34 is a bank statement. The balance at the end of the previous month was $147.35; ten checks were paid and four deposits were made. The balance at the end of the month is $172.68.

Reconciliation of Bank Balance. Some of the checks which were written by the depositor may not have been presented to the bank when the statement was made. If that is the case, the final balance on the statement will be larger than the balance shown on the check stub. The accuracy of the bank statement and of the record on the check stubs can be proved by comparing the balance on the latest check stub with the balance on the statement less the outstanding checks. The outstanding checks can be determined by comparing the check stubs with the canceled checks returned. If the balance on the check stubs is not equal to the balance on the bank statement less the outstanding checks, there is an error which should be located at once. 

In Figure 34, Carl L. Merton's bank statement shows a balance of $172.68. His check stubs show a balance of

ch. 4] 

$148.18 at the end of the month. In comparing the canceled checks with the check stubs, he finds that the following checks have not been paid: 

No. 26 

No. 28 

No. 29 $ 5.00 10.50 9.00 $24.50

Total Checks Outstanding 

He then proves the accuracy of the bank statement and check stubs by subtracting the amount of the outstanding checks from the bank balance, as follows: 

Bank Balance, March 31 

Checks Outstanding, March 31

Balance in Check Book 

$172.68 24.50 $148.18

This last balance should be the same as that shown on the check stub. If it is not, an error has been made by the bank or the depositor. The depositor should then compare the checks returned with the checks listed on the bank's statement and with the amounts given on the check stubs. 

He should then carefully go over the calculations on his check stubs. If he does not find an error in his records, he should take the matter up with the bank. 

Comparing the bank statement with the check stubs to prove the accuracy of both records is known as reconciling the bank balance.

The Cost of a Bank Account. A bank has the right to use a depositor's money until it is withdrawn. As all depositors will not wish to withdraw their funds at the same time, a bank can loan a part of the deposits and earn interest. For this reason a bank does not ordinarily make a charge for handling an account. If, however, the depositor's balance is so small that little can be earned from it, a small monthly charge may be made. Whenever one opens an account he should inquire about the bank's rules in regard to charging for handling accounts. This may enable him to avoid unnecessary expense.

CHAPTER V  Section I. NOTES 

Introduction. Have you ever borrowed money from anyone or lent money to another? If so, you have completed a transaction that is common in business. Many people find it necessary to borrow money to use in carrying on a business or to pay for goods which they purchase, such as automobiles or houses.

When an individual needs to borrow money, he usually goes to a bank as banks make it a part of their business to lend money. Although a bank is glad to make a loan, it cannot lend a person money unless he can assure it that the loan will be repaid when it is due.

If the borrower owns property, a bank is more willing to lend to him than it otherwise would be, as this gives some assurance that the borrower will have the money with which to repay the loan. If the property is in a form that can be left with the bank until the loan is repaid, the bank looks on the loan most favorably. 

A bank considers not only the amount of property which a borrower has but also his reputation for honesty and for prompt paying of obligations. If a person has built up an excellent reputation, he can frequently borrow from a bank, even though he has little property to offer as security for the loan.

Notes. When a person borrows money, he is usually required to sign a promise to repay it at a certain time. A written promise of this kind is called a promissory note, or more briefly, a note. 

A promissory note does not have to be in any special form. It may be written in any style and on any kind of paper. Usually, however, a printed form is used for the same reason that a printed form is used for a check. A typical note is shown in Figure 35. 73

[ch. 5 

$12.0000 

Waynesville Mass, March 10, 193Sixty days after date I promise to pay to the order of merchants National Bank 

One thousand two hundred no, ee-Dollars 

Payable at Merchants National Banh 

Value received 

No 81 

Due May 9.193Harry Simmer 

Figure 35-Promissory Note 

Terms Peculiar to a Note. The one who promises to pay the amount stated in a note is known as the maker; the one to whom it is to be paid, as the payee; the years, months, or days before the note is due, as the time; the due date, as the date of maturity; and the amount, as the face. Thus, the maker of the note in Figure 35 is Harry Simmer; the payee is the Merchants National Bank; the time is sixty days; the date of maturity is May 9; and the face is $1200.

Writing Notes. Notes should always be written in ink to prevent alterations. Whether a printed form is used or the entire note is written, the amount should be written twice, once in figures and once in words. This renders it more difficult to change the amount, and thus affords greater protection to the maker.

Figure 35 shows the usual position of the date, the time, the name of the payee, the amount, and the signature of the maker. Observe that the amount is written carefully in figures and in words so that it cannot be altered easily. 

The method of writing the amount in words and in figures is similar to that used in writing the amount of a check. 

The place where the note is payable is shown on the line below the amount expressed in words. If this line is left blank, it is assumed that the note is payable at the maker's place of business or residence.

ch. 5] 

The number of the note is given in one corner. In the illustration it is in the lower left-hand corner. The number is not required but it is desirable in identifying a note, especially when a person or business issues many notes. 

Signature. When the maker of a note is a person, he will sign the note in much the same way as he signs his checks. If the maker is a business, the names of both the business and the person signing are required. The person indicates that he is not personally responsible for the note, but is only signing for the business, by writing the word "By" before his name. For example, if C. M. McLeod, the 

President of The McLeod Manufacturing Company, signs. a note for the company, he will sign as follows: 

The Mc Leod Manufacturing Co.. 

By C. M. Mc Leod

Two or more persons or businesses may wish to make themselves jointly responsible for the payment of a note. 

In such a case both will sign. For example, if both B. B. 

Litton and O. L. Wells are to be responsible for the payment of a note, they will sign as follows: 

13. 13. Litton OL. Wells 

Note Stubs. Notes are usually bound in books with stubs. In general, the form of a note book is similar to that of a check book. Figure 36 shows a note with its stub.

The stub is filled out by the maker of the note so that he will have full information about each note which he issues. Amount Date Time 2575 July 199
