[ch. 5 

Indorsements. Notes may be indorsed for transfer in much the same way as checks are indorsed. In addition, there are three types of indorsements which are peculiar to notes. These are indorsement for security, indorsement for partial payment, and indorsement for collection. 

Indorsement for Security. The maker of a note may not have sufficient property or a sufficiently good reputation to make it possible for him to borrow money on his note. If someone else indorses his note, this second person becomes liable for its payment. For this reason the maker may get someone who has property or a reputation for promptly paying his obligations to indorse the note. The bank will then lend money on the note since not only the maker but also the indorser is responsible for payment. 

Indorsement for Partial Payment. If a note is paid in installments, a record of each payment may be made on the back of the note. It is desirable to have the record on the note itself since this record shows that the maker no longer owes the face value, but only the difference between the face value and the payment. An indorsement acknowledging the receipt of a partial payment is shown in Figure 37. 

Nov. 30, 193-. Received $100° in part payment of note 

Figure 37

Indorsement Acknowledging the 

Receipt of Partial Payment 

Indorsement for Collection. When the one who owns a note wishes to have it collected by a bank, he should indorse it "For Collection" as in Figure 38. This indorsement shows 

For Collection 

D.L. Miller 

Figure 38 

Indorsement "For Collection" that the note is the property of the indorser and in the hands of the bank for collection only. If the owner of the note indorsed it in blank, he would have nothing to show that he had not sold the note to the bank.

ch. 5] 

In fact, his indorsement would make him responsible to the bank for its payment if the maker did not pay the note at maturity.

 Section II. INTEREST AND DISCOUNT 

Interest. Suppose you borrow $100 from a friend. If you repay this amount at the end of a year, your friend will at that time have his original sum. But during the year he has not had the use of his money. It is probable that he has wished to buy things for which he might have used the amount lent you. It is only fair that he be paid for waiting a year to make his purchases. In paying him, you might give him an amount in addition to the sum borrowed, perhaps $5. A payment of this kind for the use of money is interest. The money on which interest is calculated is the principal. The sum of the principal and the interest is the amount.

Interest is usually expressed in percentages or hundredths. For example, you may borrow $100 with the understanding that you are to pay interest at the rate of 5%. This means that you are to pay interest at the rate of 5% or 

5100 of $100 a year. If you borrow the money at 5% for only a half-year, the interest is 1/2 X 5100 X $100. Interest is commonly expressed in terms of one year. If a loan for three months is said to bear interest at the rate of 6%, it is meant that the rate is 6% a year and not 6% for each three months.

Calculating Interest. When a loan is made for a certain number of days, such as 30 days, 60 days, or 90 days, the interest is computed by days. In order to make the calculating of interest easier it is customary to use 360 days as a year. If a loan of $100 is made for 60 days at 6%, the interest is found as follows: 6360 X 100 X $100 - $1 When businesses or individuals borrow from each other, a month is usually considered to be one-twelfth of a year, regardless of the number of days in the month. Thus, interest on a loan of $100 for two months at 6% is: 212 X 100 X $100 = $1 Banks usually follow a slightly different method. When they loan money for a certain number of months, interest

ch. 5] 

is charged for the exact number of days but 360 days are considered to be a year. If a loan is made on April 1 and is due in one month, it must be repaid on May 1. Interest is charged for 30 days because there are 30 days in April. If a loan is made on April 1 and is due in two months, it must be repaid on June 1. Interest is charged for 61 days, the sum of the 30 days in April and the 31 days in May.

If a loan becomes due on a holiday or on Sunday, it need not be paid until the following day but interest is charged for the extra day.

Sixty-Day Method of Calculating Interest. You prob ably have observed in the calculations given on page 78 that 60 days are 660 or % of a year. If interest is calculated at 

6%, the interest for 60 days is % of 6%, or 1%. The interest for 60 days at 6% is, then, 1% of the principal. For example, it is $1 for $100, $2.50 for $250, and $3.66 for $365.50.

This is the basis of a method frequently used in calculating interest even when the time is not 60 days and the interest rate is not 6%. If, on a loan of $1000, the rate is 6% and the time is 30 days, the interest may be found as follows: $10, interest for 60 days $5, interest for 30 days 

1/100 X $1000 12 X $10 

On a similar loan for 

90 days, the interest is found as 

100 $1000 $10, interest for 60 days 

112 X $10 -$15, interest for 90 days

If interest at the rate of 5% is charged on a loan of $1200 for 60 days, the amount of the interest may be found 

1/100 X $1200 = $12, interest for 60 days at 6% 

5% X $12 - $10, interest for 60 days at 5%

The sixty-day method is frequently a convenient method for use in calculating interest, but in all cases one of the following methods can be used:

When the interest is to be calculated by months, Months 12 

X rate X principal interest.

[ch. 5 

When the loan is for a number of days, Days 360 

X rate X principal interest. Bank Loans. When money is borrowed from a bank, the bank usually deducts the interest in advance. For example, if you borrow $100 from your bank for 60 days at 6%, the bank will first find the interest on $100 for 60 days at 6%. 

This is $1. You will then sign a note payable in 60 days for $100, but you will receive only $99, $100 minus the interest, $1. The note which you sign will not mention interest because the note is $1 larger than the amount you actually receive. When interest is taken in advance by a bank, it is called bank discount. The amount received (the principal less the bank discount) is the proceeds. A note which does not bear interest is called a non-interest-bearing note. 

Interest-Bearing Notes. When loans are obtained from individuals or businesses, the interest is usually paid at the time the note is due. In such a case one borrows $1000, receives this sum, and pays it with interest when the loan is due. When a note includes a promise to pay the amount of the loan with interest, it is referred to as an interestbearing note.

When an interest-bearing note is issued, the rate is shown on the note. At the time C. K. Arnold pays the note in Figure 39, he will have to pay the principal, $1000, plus the interest, $10. The interest is found as follows: 

60360 X 100 X $1000 - $10

ch. 5] 

Discounting Notes. S. M. Moon, who has recently purchased a home, needs an additional $1000 to complete his payments. He might borrow this amount from his bank. 

He prefers, however, to dispose of a note for $1000 which he received from A. L. Starr when he sold Mr. Starr his former home. He therefore sells this note to his bank. This is called discounting the note. 

The note signed by A. L. Starr is dated May 1, and is due in 90 days from date. It does not bear interest. Mr.

Moon discounts it on June 30. In determining the amount which it will pay Mr. Moon for the note, the bank first considers the time which will elapse before the note is due. 

Since the note is discounted on June 30 but is not due until 

July 30, the bank will have to wait 30 days for the amount of the note. It therefore charges the same interest as if Mr. 

Moon borrowed $1000 on his own note for 30 days. If the rate is 6%, the interest or discount is: 

3%60 X 100 X $1000 – $5

Mr. Moon will then receive $1000 $5, or $995. 

Suppose Mr. Moon has another 90-day note signed by

W. R. Snow. This note is also for $1000 and is due in 30 days, but it bears interest at the rate of 6%. When it is due, it will be worth the sum of the principal and the interest. 

The interest is: 960 X 100 X $1000 = $15. The value on the due date is: $1000 + $15-$1015. 

If this note is discounted for 30 days at 6%, the bank will figure the discount on the value at maturity. The discount and proceeds are then computed as follows: 

3360 X 100 X $1015 — $5.08, discount $1015 $5.08 = $1009.92, proceeds

Mr. Moon therefore receives $1009.92 for the note signed by Mr. Snow. 

Time of Discount. When a note is discounted, the discount is calculated on the exact number of days from the date of discount to the date on which the note is due. For example, a 60-day note, dated July 2, is due on August 31. 

If it is discounted on July 15, discount is taken for 47 days, the number of days from July 15 to August 31.

[ch. 5 

Similarly, a note for three months, dated April 9, is due on July 9. If it is discounted on June 2, discount is taken for 37 days, the number of days from June 2 to July 9.

CHAPTER VI METHODS OF TRANSMITTING PAYMENTS Section I. MONEY ORDERS 

Introduction. As was explained in Chapter II, a substitute for money is especially needed when remittances are sent from one locality to another. Checks are most commonly used for this purpose. They can be written easily; they can be sent through the mail without danger of loss; they can be cashed readily by those receiving them; and each canceled check becomes a receipt proving that payment was actually made. 

Need for Additional Methods. Although checks are ordinarily satisfactory for remittances, there are some cases in which they cannot be used conveniently. Examples of such cases are:

First, the one sending the remittance may not have a bank account and may therefore be unable to write checks. 

Second, the one to whom the remittance is to be sent may be located where he cannot conveniently cash a check. 

For example, James Alden is spending his summer vacation at a camp about fifty miles from his home. His parents wish to send money to him but they do not know whether it would be convenient for him to have a check cashed. 

Third, the one to whom the remittance is to be sent may not know the sender and for this reason may not wish to accept his check. Suppose you order some merchandise from a merchant in another city, with whom you had not dealt previously, and ask that he ship it at once. If you enclose your check in payment, he may not wish to make shipment at once as he may not be sure that your check is good.

The three illustrations given above show that in some cases checks are not entirely satisfactory for use in making remittances. Some of the forms which may be used instead of checks are discussed in the following paragraphs. 86

ch. METHODS OF TRANSMITTING PAYMENTS 

87 Postal Money Order. Persons who do not wish to, or who cannot send remittances through the mail in the form of checks, frequently use postal money orders. A postal money order is a form issued by one post office directing another post office to pay money to a certain person or to his order. 

Such an order can be sent through the mail without danger of loss since it is payable only to a certain person or his order. No one else can cash it. If it is lost, a duplicate can be obtained without cost.

Application for a Postal Money Order. Before one pur chases a postal money order, he is required to fill out an application blank like the one shown in Figure 40. This application was written by Thomas Sackville, 3214 Main

Street, for a money order to be sent to Harrington and Company, 1625 Grantwood Street, Handelburg, Pennsylvania.

The application is for a money order for $6.00. Mr. 

Sackville will have to pay $6.10 for the order, $6.00, the face value, and 10 cents, the fee which the post office charges for this service. The fees for money orders are not large and vary according to the amounts of the orders. For an order of $2.50 or less, the fee is 5 cents. The charge gradually increases for larger orders until it becomes 22 cents for a money order for $100. The charge for money orders is printed in detail on the back of each application blank.

Indorsing Postal Money Orders. Postal money orders are indorsed in much the same way as checks, except that the signature of the one cashing an order is placed on the front of the paper. A special line is provided for this purpose. Postal money orders can be transferred only once by an indorsement on the back. Full information about the indorsing of these money orders is given on the back of each order. 

Cashing Postal Money Orders. Since a money order is payable only on the order of the payee, the bank or post office cashing a money order must know that it is indorsed by the payee. For this reason, money orders are not cashed for strangers. If a stranger wishes to cash such an order, he must first be identified. This may be accomplished by a personal introduction or by the presentation of letters, receipts, or other business papers which indicate who he is. 

Advantages of Postal Money Orders. Postal money orders have a number of advantages. First, they are convenient as they can be purchased and cashed at post offices which are located in almost every community and neighborhood. They can also be cashed at banks, the same as checks. Second, they are safe, for they can be cashed only by those to whom they are made payable or on their order. Third, the cost of purchasing postal money orders is not large. 

Express Money Orders. Money orders may also be purchased from express companies. Orders issued by these

89 companies are known as express money orders. (See Figure 

42.) An express money order is very similar to a postal money order, except that it is purchased from an express company instead of from a post office. The cost is about the same as for a postal money order.

Express money orders are reasonably convenient as they can be purchased at any express office. Express offices are located in almost every city or town served by a railroad. 

Express money orders can be cashed at an express office or a bank. Complete safety is provided because an express order, like a postal money order and a check, can be cashed only by the payee or on his order. Express money orders can be transferred by indorsement the same as checks. 

The maximum amount for which an express money order is drawn is $50. A person can, however, buy as many orders as he wishes. Express companies also sell another form, known as an unlimited check, which can be used in sending large remittances. 

No written application is required in buying an express money order. A receipt which serves the same purpose as the receipt given with a postal money order is given the buyer. This receipt should be retained as long as the buyer may need proof that the money order was purchased. 

Remittances to Foreign Countries. Both postal and express money orders may be used in sending remittances to foreign countries. The charge for remittances to be sent

[ch. 6 

outside the United States is usually slightly higher than that for remittances to be sent to places within the United States. Full information about the rates to foreign countries can be obtained from a post office or an express office.

Section II. ADDITIONAL METHODS OF TRANSMITTING PAYMENTS 

Bank Draft. Banks usually deposit a part of their funds in other banks in large cities. These deposits are subject to check, the same as the deposits of an individual. A check which a bank draws on a deposit in another bank is known as a bank draft. (See Figure 43.)

DOLLARS 

Figure 43-Bank Draft

A person who wishes to send a remittance through the mail may purchase a bank draft from a bank. Since the draft is the check of a bank, it can be sent through the mail as safely as any other check. It is readily accepted by the payee since he feels sure that a bank will have funds on deposit from which its drafts will be paid. 

Many banks do not charge their regular depositors a fee for drafts, in which case the cost of the draft is its face value. The fee ordinarily charged to others is larger than that for a postal or express money order if the amount of the draft is small. If the amount is large, the fee is less than that for money orders. For this reason drafts are not frequently used for small amounts, but they are commonly used when large remittances are sent. 

Drafts are also commonly used for large amounts since they can be purchased for any sum. For example, A. J.

Lang wishes to send $500 through the mail. If he uses postal money orders, he will have to send five orders. Obviously, this is not so convenient as sending one draft.

[ch. 6 

Cashier's Check. A check which a bank draws on its own funds is known as a cashier's check. It is frequently used by a bank in paying its own expenses, such as rent and salaries. It may also be purchased by an individual instead of a bank draft as it is accepted as readily by other banks and persons. Banks ordinarily offer both drafts and cashier's checks for sale at the same rate and recommend the use of one or the other according to the destination of the remittance.

Certified Check. When a bank guarantees the payment of a check drawn by a depositor, the check is said to be certified. Suppose Merle Lane wished to send a remittance in the form of a check, but the remittance was to go to some one who did not know him and who might hesitate to accept his check. In such a case he might have his bank certify the check, as in Figure 45. This check would then be accepted readily since payment of it would be guaranteed by the bank.

DOLLARS 

Merle Lane 

Figure 45-Certified Check

ch. 6] 

METHODS OF TRANSMITTING PAYMENTS 

In certifying a check, a bank writes its guarantee across the face of the paper. The amount of the check is immediately deducted from the depositor's account. This makes it impossible for the depositor to draw out the money or to use it for other checks. The bank can therefore safely guarantee that it will make payment when the check is presented with the proper indorsement. 

A check may be certified at the request of either the drawer or the payee. Ordinarily, however, a bank will certify a check for the payee only when it is acquainted with him. The bank has the right to refuse to certify a check for the payee as its only obligation is to the drawer or depositor.

A certified check is exactly like any other check, except that its payment is guaranteed by a bank. For this reason, it can be indorsed and transferred the same as other checks. 

Registered Letter. At a post office one can register a letter for a small fee. The post office then guarantees its delivery and, in case it is lost, is responsible to the sender for the value of the letter up to $1000. The amount of the fee charged depends on the value placed on the letter. For a small additional fee a return receipt signed by the one to whom the letter is addressed will be furnished the sender.

Any important letter may be registered. This service is especially desirable if one wishes to send a remittance through the mail but does not wish to use a check, draft, or money order. He can place currency in the letter and then have the letter registered. If the letter is lost, the value placed on it can be recovered from the government. 

The charge for registering a letter is greater than the charge for a small draft or money order. Furthermore, a receipt for a registered letter does not prove that the letter contained a certain amount of money. It is therefore less desirable as a receipt for a payment than a canceled check, draft, or money order. For these reasons, money is not commonly sent in registered letters, except when it is believed that the one receiving the remittance could not conveniently have a check, draft, or money order cashed. 

Sending Payments by Telegraph. Remittances may also be sent by telegraph. The use of the telegraph for this purpose is discussed in Chapter IX, in which telegraph services are considered.

CHAPTER VII 

CREDIT 

Section I. ESTABLISHING CREDIT 

Introduction. As we have observed in earlier chapters, a person may borrow money from a bank provided he can assure the banker that the amount borrowed will be repaid at the agreed time. Similarly, a person may obtain merchandise from retail dealers without paying for it provided he can assure the dealers that he will make payment at a definite time in the future. In this chapter we shall consider the reasons for such transactions and the methods of completing them.

Credit. The word credit is derived from the Latin word credo, meaning believe or trust. The word is used in business in different ways, but it is used in this chapter in the sense that involves belief or trust in someone else. If a person buys goods with the understanding that he will pay for them later, he is said to buy on credit, for the seller believes that he is trustworthy and that he will pay what he owes when it is due. When one sells to another on credit, the seller is known as the creditor, and the buyer is known as the debtor, or charge customer.

Need for Credit. Modern business could not be carried on without credit. Millions of dollars' worth of merchandise are bought and sold every day. The majority of these purchases and sales require credit. Without credit, business as we know it could not function, for a great many concerns would not have sufficient money to pay for all the materials they need.

Credit is also needed by individuals either as a convenience or because of a shortage of funds. For example, your mother may need groceries at a time when it is not convenient for her to go to the store. She may order them over the telephone and have them delivered. Payment may be 97

[ch. 7 

made to the delivery boy, but frequently it is more convenient to buy on credit and to pay at a later time. 

Also, a person may wish to buy a suit of clothes at a time when he does not have money to pay for it. He may then buy on credit and pay for the suit after he receives his wages at the end of the week, or possibly at the first of the following month. 

These illustrations show two of the reasons for buying on credit. There may be others, but usually purchases are made on credit either because that is more convenient or because the buyers do not have funds with which to make immediate payment.

Terms of Credit. The time given the purchaser for making payment is known as the terms. If a purchase is made with the terms "cash," cash must be paid by the time the goods are delivered to the buyer. If the terms are "thirty days," payment does not have to be made until thirty days after the purchase.

When one business buys from another, the terms of credit are usually fixed by agreement or by the custom of the seller. They may be ten days, thirty days, sixty days, or any other time agreed upon by the buyer and the seller. 

When an individual buys from a retail store, the terms are usually the first of the following month. For example, all purchases made during the month of January are due on the first of February, and all purchases made during February are due on the first of March. Merchants usually send to all credit customers statements showing the amounts owed at the first of each month. Ordinarily a customer who buys on credit is not expected to make payment until he receives this statement.

Basis of Credit. When a merchant grants credit, he delivers to the buyer some of his merchandise and trusts him to make payment at a later date. The buyer therefore should not expect the merchant to give him credit unless he can assure the merchant that payment will be made promptly.

ch. 7] 

CREDIT 

Before he issues credit, a merchant usually considers at least two things: first, the amount of the individual's income or property; and, second, the individual's reputation for honesty and for prompt paying of debts. 

If a person has considerable property or if he is earning a satisfactory income, the merchant feels that he will be able to pay his debts according to his agreement. A person who does not have property or a reasonable income should not ask for credit, for it is not fair to ask the merchant to have faith in him when there is little chance of his being able to pay for his purchases.

A more important element which is considered by those issuing credit is the buyer's reputation for honesty and for paying his debts promptly. If a person is known to be honest, and if he always pays his debts when they are due, a merchant is not greatly concerned with the amount of his property. The merchant knows that this person will not promise to pay unless he will be able to do so and that payment will not be delayed past the time agreed upon.

Method of Obtaining Credit. When one obtains credit, he should expect to furnish the merchant with information regarding his property or income and his reputation for paying his debts. For this reason, he should not resent being questioned by the merchant and should give all the information needed.

A merchant probably will not ask a person seeking credit whether he pays his bills promptly, but he will ask for the names of other firms with which the person has dealt. After these names have been given, he will get in touch with those businesses and find out whether the individual has paid his bills promptly.

A business may have a form which is filled out for each credit customer, to provide the information needed before the account is opened. A typical form is shown in Figure 48. 

Credit Rating. If one pays his debts promptly and in this way establishes a good reputation, he is said to have a good credit rating. On the other hand, if he does not pay

[ch. 7 

as he promises and thus gets a poor reputation, he is said to have a poor credit rating.

Note the type of information which the merchant wishes before opening an account.

The young man or woman entering the business world should endeavor to establish a good credit rating through paying all obligations as promised. A good credit rating is desirable because one may at some time wish to make an important purchase on credit for which a good rating is necessary. Also, positions of responsibility are more likely to be offered to those with good credit ratings. Business men

ch. 7] 101 

CREDIT 

feel that a person who does not pay his debts promptly may not be careful in taking care of his duties if he is entrusted with important work.

The merchants in each city or community usually exchange information about credits through an organization known as a credit association. Through this association all merchants soon secure information about the credit rating of each person in the community. This is an additional reason for one's being careful about taking care of all of his obligations. Any failure on his part will be generally known among the merchants and will unfavorably affect his credit rating.

[ch. 7 

 Section II. USING CREDIT Purchases on Open Account. When the buyer takes possession of the things purchased on credit, and the merchant accepts his oral statement that payment will be made within the designated time, the purchase is said to be on open account or on account. The merchant records the articles sold to the buyer and provides the buyer with a copy of this record. This permits each to know the amount to be paid at the time agreed upon. The form used to give this information is known as a sales ticket. Figure 49 is a form commonly used.

This sales ticket is used by the Henderson Stationery Company. It is for the sale of a loose-leaf binder, a package of paper, and a ream of typewriting paper.

DEPT 

HENDERSON STATIONERY CO. 

Fourth and Main Streets DETROIT Ela Epply 

1211 Burn Sta

SOLD BY 

8.1 1 #11 Binder 

DATE 

3-1- 

1 Package Paper. 

Ism. Twp. paper, 501-15 AM'T RECO 75 40 180 195 

Figure 49-Sales Ticket 

A person should retain all the sales tickets received during the month for purchases on account so that he will know how much he owes each merchant. The merchant will send him a statement showing the amount owed, but the individual should prove the correctness of this statement by comparing it with the sales tickets. He may, because of an error on the part of the merchant, be charged for goods which he has not purchased, or be charged more than the price agreed upon. If he does not have his sales tickets, he may not observe this error and thus may pay more than he should. 

Installment Purchases. If the value of an article purchased is large, and if it is not convenient for the buyer to

ch. 7] 103 

CREDIT 

pay for it at one time, the merchant may arrange for a part of the purchase price to be paid each month. In this way one may extend the payment over several months. 

When purchases are made on this basis, they are referred to as installment purchases by the one who buys, and installment sales by the one who sells. 

Installment purchases differ from purchases on open account in that only a part of the purchase price of the installment purchase is paid each month. When the purchase is on open account, the entire amount is usually paid in one sum. 

The two kinds of purchases may also differ in cost to the buyer. When a store sells for cash or on account, the same price is usually charged, regardless of whether cash is paid or whether credit is given. When goods are sold on installments, however, the merchant may charge an additional fee for this extra service. This is only fair since the merchant has to wait a considerable time for a part of his money. He may also be put to some additional expense in handling the papers and records required by the installment sale.

A purchase on open account is based on an oral order given the merchant. Usually in the case of an installment purchase, a written contract is signed by the buyer. The purpose of this is to provide additional protection for the merchant until all payments are made. In some instances the ownership to the goods may remain with the merchant until all installments are paid. This makes it easier for the merchant to reclaim the goods if the buyer fails to make his payments.

Finance Companies. In some lines of business the merchant does not care to sell on the installment plan, but he will arrange for the buyer to make payments to a company organized for the purpose of financing sales and making collections. Such a company is known as a finance company. Finance companies are especially common when the article sold is expensive, such as an automobile. From the point of view of the buyer, purchasing through a finance company is the same as purchasing on the installment plan.

[ch. 7 

The payments are spread out over a number of months to suit the buyer's convenience. For this service he pays a reasonable fee. This makes the cost somewhat greater than if the purchase were made for cash.

Purchases Secured by Notes. When the amount of a purchase is large and payments are made at longer intervals than a month, the purchaser may be required to give a note for each installment. Thus, if B. J. Knapp, a farmer, buys farm machinery on the installment plan, the use of notes may be convenient. Mr. Knapp does not receive a monthly salary but gets his income from the sale of his crops. The notes may be made payable at the time of year when Mr. 

Knapp receives his income, and in this way the payments may be continued over two or three years. 

Even though the sale is secured by notes, the merchant will not wish to make the sale unless the buyer has a good credit rating. If the credit rating of the buyer is not firstclass, he may be permitted to take possession of the property, but the ownership may remain with the merchant until all payments are made. 

Surety Bond. A good credit rating is useful not only when one wishes to buy on account or on installments, but also when one wishes a position of responsibility. This is especially true if the position requires that one handle large sums of money. Individuals and businesses are always more willing to trust the handling of their money to persons who are careful in the use of their own money and who pay all debts promptly. It is thought that such persons will be most careful in handling the funds of others. 

When a person is responsible for a large sum of money, even a good credit rating may not be considered to be sufficient. In such cases a surety bond may be required. This is an agreement on the part of a company, known as a surety company, to be responsible for any losses caused by an individual in handling money or in doing anything for which he is responsible.

When one gets a surety bond, he ordinarily fills out an application blank similar to that shown in Figure 50.

Each person, upon entering the business world, must establish his own reputation. Unless his reputation is favorable, he cannot expect others to have confidence in him and to give him responsibility over matters involving large sums of money.

CHAPTER VIII THE TELEPHONE Section I. THE TELEPHONE DIRECTORY 

Introduction. The telephone is one of the most commonly used business devices. You probably use it frequently. But possibly you do not always use it correctly or do not know all the services that telephone companies render. If not, the following discussion will help you in making better use of this common service. 

Telephone Directory. A telephone company furnishes a directory for use with each telephone. This directory contains the telephone numbers and general information about the telephone services. It is the property of the telephone company and must be surrendered to the company on demand. As telephone numbers are sometimes changed, and as new numbers are added frequently, new directories are issued at intervals, usually quarterly or semi-annually. 

Locating Numbers. When the telephone number which one wishes to call is not known by the one placing the call, it should be located in the directory if possible. The directory usually has two main divisions giving the telephone numbers. In the first, known as the alphabetic section, the names of the subscribers are arranged alphabetically. In the second, known as the classified section, the names of businesses and professional persons are classified according to occupations.

In the alphabetic section, the names are listed according to letter sequence throughout the entire name. In Figure 

51, "Smith, J. E." precedes "Smith, J. F." An initial is considered a whole word and is placed at the beginning of its letter group. For example, "Smith, J. F." precedes "Smith, J. Frank" or "Smith, James F." Hyphens, dashes, apostrophes, and the symbol "&" are disregarded. "Smith 

Kasson Co., The" would appear at the same place if the 107

[ch. 8 

name were "Smith & Kasson." The word "The" in a firm name is placed last. 336 SMITH

Names can be located in the alphabetic section of the directory, but sometimes it is more convenient to use the classified section. In this section the classifications are alphabetically arranged and the names under each classification are in alphabetic order. If a person wishes to locate the name of a business, he may be able to find it more quickly in the classified section than in the alphabetic section. 

For example, suppose a person wishes to secure the telephone number of a dentist by the name of Stephens. 

He can first find the classification "Dentists" and then the name desired. The advantage of the classified section is more apparent, however, when one is not certain about the exact spelling of the name desired. If a person wishes to call a dentist but is not sure whether he spells his name "Stephens" or "Stevens," he can examine the names under the classification of dentists much more quickly than he

Figure 52-Part of a Page of the Classified Section of a Telephone 

Directory could examine all the similar names in the alphabetic-section.

Information. If the name for which a telephone number is desired cannot be located in the directory, one should call "Information" at the central office. The name and, if possible, the address of the one to be called should be given the information operator. If the person has a telephone, the operator will first locate and then report the number. 

Central Offices. In large cities, telephone companies have so many subscribers that it is impossible to carry all lines through one switchboard or central office. Separate central offices are therefore provided for each part of the city. The name of the central office is given in the directory before the number of the phone. Some of the central offices in Figure 52 are EAst, PArkway, Woodburn, and CHerry. 

Party Lines. When two or more subscribers use one line, it is referred to as a party line. Only one of the subscribers can talk over the line at one time, but this is satisfactory when calls are not made too frequently. The purpose of party lines is to reduce the cost of telephone service to the subscriber.

[ch. 8 

A party line may be indicated by a letter after a telephone number. For example, in Figure 51 the letter "J" used with the first number in the second column indicates a party line. 

Extensions. For the convenience of resident subscribers, telephone companies provide one or more extra telephones, located in different parts of the home, at a small additional cost. These are known as extension telephones. Extension telephones are on the same line, ring at the same time, and 

Figure 53-Private Switchboard

ch. 8] 111 

THE TELEPHONE 

are called by the same number. It is therefore unnecessary for them to be indicated in any way in the directory. 

Private Branch Exchange. In Figure 51 the star before the number for The Smith-Kasson Company indicates that the company has a private branch exchange. When a company has a private branch exchange, two or more lines connect the central office with a private switchboard operated by an employee of the business. A person calls the company number in the same way as he does that for any other company. When he receives an answer from the operator of the private exchange, he states the name of the person or department desired. The connection is then completed by the operator.

The use of the private switchboard is desirable for a business as it enables many departments to have connections with the central office without there being a separate outside line for each. It also makes possible the connection of two telephones in the building without there being a connection through the central telephone office. For example, a shipping clerk on one floor may talk to a billing clerk on another by having the connection made through the private switchboard.

THE TELEPHONE Section II. RECEIVING AND PLACING CALLS 

Receiving Calls. When the telephone rings, it should be answered promptly. It is discourteous to keep the one who calls waiting longer than necessary. Furthermore, if there is a long delay, the one who is calling may hang up his receiver, with the result that no connection will be made when the telephone is finally answered.

When one answers the telephone, he should give his name or number, or the name or number of the business for which he answers. For example, if you are answering for a firm with the name Jordan and Harris, you should answer the telephone by saying, "Jordan and Harris." This makes it unnecessary for the one calling to ask the name, and thus quickens service.

TELEPHONE CALL Name Mr. Leslie Graham. 

You were called by.. Mr. Charles Dow 

Daw. Nov. 2.5.19.3 Time 10:30 A. M. 

He will call again.

A person receiving a call should have a pad of paper and a pencil at hand so that he can make notes about the call if they are needed. The one calling may wish to give him several names, figures, or similar data which he might not be able to remernber accurately. He should therefore be able to write them without delay. He may also receive a call for another person who is not then present. 

A complete note should be made of this so that when the one called returns, he will know who called him, the time of the call, and whether that person will call again. In some offices a printed form is frequently Such a form properly filled 'Time)

Talking Over a Telephone. A person using the telephone should speak distinctly, slowly, and directly into the transmitter, as shown in Figure 55. He should have the lips about

[ch. 8 

an inch from the mouthpiece, and should use an ordinary, conversational tone of voice. 

Types of Telephones. Telephones are classified as desk and hand telephones, and as manual and dial telephones. 

The desk telephone is illustrated in Figure 55. Its receiver and mouthpiece are separate units. The hand telephone, which is also known by the name continental telephone, has the receiver and mouthpiece in one unit, as is shown in Figure 

56. This style is more convenient when one holds the telephone while talking, as it requires the use of only one hand. Figure 55 Using a Telephone 

The classification, manual and dial, refers to the methods of making the connection with the number called. 

When the manual telephone is used, the number is given to the central operator who makes the connection by hand. In the dial system one makes his own connection by operating the dial. 

Figure 55 shows a manual telephone, and Figure 56 shows a dial telephone. 

Placing Calls on a Manual Telephone. When one places a call on a manual telephone, he should pronounce the number slowly and

Busy and Ringing Signals. If the line which one calls is in use, the central operator may report, "The line is busy." 

In some cities a busy line is not reported by the operator, but is indicated by a busy signal which comes to the one calling as a rapid "buzz-buzz-buzz" sound. This is not to be confused with the "burr-burr-rr-ing" sound at intervals, which indicates that the telephone desired is being rung. 

If no answer is received or the line is busy, the one calling should hang up the receiver and call at another time.

Making Emergency Calls. It is not necessary for a person to have the telephone number of the fire department or the police station in order to report a fire or to call the police. If he gives the central operator his address, she will connect him with the proper police or fire station. 

When he is connected with either department, he should be careful to state clearly the reason for his call and to report his exact address. If he does not, there may be a delay in his receiving the aid desired.

Placing a Call on a Dial Telephone. When a dial telephone is used, the one placing a call must make his own connection. He first carefully removes the receiver from the hook. This is done carefully, for if the hook is jiggled the desired connection may not be made. He then listens for the steady humming sound, known as the dial tone, which indicates that the line is ready for use. If the dial tone is not heard, the receiver is replaced on the hook and the call is made again a little later.

Each figure in the telephone number is dialed exactly as it is shown in the latest directory. This is done by placing a finger in the hole of the dial through which the first number or letter is seen and moving the finger clockwise to the

[ch. 8 

Figure 57-Central Office

With dial telephones, the subscribers make their own connections by dialing. Fewer operators are then required, though several are needed to complete emergency calls and other calls which the subscriber cannot complete himself.

dial stop. The dial is then permitted to return to its original position. Each succeeding number or letter is then dialed in the same way until the number is completed. 

When a city has several central offices, the office must be dialed before the line number. The office may be indicated by either a number or a name. If a name is used, only the two or three letters printed in capitals should be dialed. For example, in securing the first number given on page 115, one dials A-V-0-4-7-8. Figure 58 shows the dial in the natural position. The person using the telephone has placed his finger in the second dial hole preparatory to dialing the letter A, B, or C, or the figure 2. Figure 59 shows the dial after it has been moved clockwise until the operator's finger strikes the dial stop. When the finger is removed, the dial will return to its original position so that additional letters and figures may be dialed.

When the connection has been completed, the ringing or busy signal will be heard the same as when a manual telephone is used.

If an error is made in dialing, the person making the call will not get the correct number. If the error is discovered

[ch. 8 

before the dialing is completed, he should hang up the receiver at once and then dial again in a few moments. 

Party Lines and Extensions. When a party or an extension line is used, two or more persons may attempt to place calls at the same time. If the process of dialing a number is interrupted, it must be started again. For this reason, if you hear another party remove the receiver while you are dialing, you should tell him that the line is in use. 

You should then hang up the receiver for a few moments, after which you may dial again. Similarly, if you should remove the receiver while some other person is dialing, you should tell him of the interruption as soon as the dialing is finished so that he can dial again. It is not necessary to re-dial the number if the interruption occurs after the dialing has been finished.

When one using a party line wishes to talk to the other party on the same line, he should call the operator. The operator will then make the connection for him. 

Calling the Operator. If one wishes to call the operator, he should place his finger in the last opening in the dial, marked "Operator," and should turn the dial clockwise as far as possible. 

Emergency Calls. A fire or police station is secured most quickly if the number of the station is dialed, but if this number is not readily available, the operator may be called and asked to make the proper connection. 

Broken Connections. If the connection is broken when either a dial or a manual telephone is used, the one who called should call again. If both parties attempt to call, both will hear busy signals and will be unable to complete the connection.

Courtesy. Courtesy is always desirable. It adds to the pleasure with which everyone performs his work and aids in completing business transactions. It is therefore demanded of all employees by most business houses. 

But courtesy is especially necessary when one is talking over the telephone. The other party is more likely to mis-

ch. 8] 119 

THE TELEPHONE 

understand one's meaning and to be irritated by any seeming lack of consideration than if the two persons were talking face to face. Courtesy and a pleasant voice add much to the effectiveness of one's telephone calls and are real business assets.

[ch. 8 

 Section III. TOLL CALLS 

Placing a Call at a Pay Station. Telephone companies maintain telephones in many places for the use of the public. A person using one of these telephones pays for his call before the connection is completed. Usually one removes the receiver from the hook, deposits a nickel, and then gives the number to the operator or dials his number. 

If the call is not completed, the coin is returned. As some pay stations use slightly different methods, one should read the directions given on or beside the telephone before placing a call.

Long-Distance Calls. All calls in one city or through one exchange are known as local calls. All calls to other towns and cities are long-distance calls. 

In placing a long-distance call on a manual telephone, one first asks for the long-distance operator. If a dial telephone is used, he dials either the operator or the long-distance operator, according to the instructions given in the directory. 

After the connection is made with the operator, the one placing the call tells the operator the town to be called, the person's name or telephone number, and the class of service desired. The operator then completes the connection or requests the caller's number and name and tells him that he will be called back to the telephone within a few minutes. 

The charge for a long-distance call depends on the distance between the cities and the type of service desired. 

The different types of service which are available are discussed in the following paragraphs. 

Appointment Call. In an appointment call, communication is to be established with a particular person at a specified time. It is used when a person does not want to wait for his call to be completed, but wishes to be able to talk at a definite time. An appointment call is the most expensive type of service since the telephone company is required not only to locate the party called, but also to arrange for him to talk at a certain time with the one making the call. 

Person to Person. The person-to-person call is one in which the connection is to be made with a particular person

ch. 8] 121 

THE TELEPHONE 

whenever he can be located. It is easier for the telephone company to complete a call of this kind than it is to complete an appointment call, since the connection is made as soon as the party is located and the company does not have to make an appointment for a later time. The charge for a person-to-person call is therefore usually a little less than that for an appointment call.

When the telephone company is unable to locate the person desired in an appointment or person-to-person call, a small charge which is known as a report charge is made. 

This is about one-sixth the rate for a person-to-person call. 

Station to Station. Frequently one making a call does not care to talk to a certain person, but simply wishes to talk to anyone at a given business or telephone number. 

This type of a call is known as a station-to-station or telephone-to-telephone call. It is completed most easily by the telephone company since the company does not have to locate a particular person, but merely has to connect the one placing the call with a certain firm or telephone. The charge is therefore about two-thirds of that for the person-to-person call. 

Messenger Calls. When one making a call wishes to talk to a person who does not have a telephone, the telephone company will call that person to a connecting telephone. A call on which this service is rendered is known as a messenger call. The charge is the same as for an appointment call plus any charge which may be made for messenger service. 

Evening and Night Rates. During the evening and night, fewer long-distance calls are made than at other times. The telephone company offers reduced rates on station-to-station calls during these hours to encourage people to place some of their calls in the dull period. For example, in some localities the regular station-to-station rate, referred to above, is in force from 4:30 a. m. to 7 p. m. This is known as the day rate. An evening rate is in force from 7 p. m. to 8:30 p. m.; and a night rate, from 8:30 p. m. to 4:30 a. m. The evening rate is about three-fourths of the day rate, and the night rate is about one-half of the day rate. The evening

[ch. 8 

and night rates are in force between all stations except those that are so close together that the charge for all station-to-station calls would be practically the same. 

Minimum and Additional Time. The charge for longdistance calls is usually based on the minimum charge for three or five minutes. When the minimum time is three minutes, the charge for each additional minute thereafter is about one-third of the minimum. When the minimum time is five minutes, the charge for each additional minute thereafter is about one-fifth of the minimum. 

Collect Calls. A collect call is one on which the charges are to be reversed, that is, billed against the called station. 

Reversing the charges is desirable under several circumstances. Another person may ask you to telephone him by long distance and to have the call charged to him. You can then place the call and ask the operator to have the charges reversed. Similarly, if you are away from home and wish to place a long-distance call to your home from a friend's telephone, you may have the charges reversed. In this way they will be charged against your telephone and not against the telephone of your friend.

No additional charge is made for collect calls except in a few cases where the usual rate is very low. In such cases a slight additional charge may be made.

CHAPTER IX THE TELEGRAPH Section I. SENDING TELEGRAMS 

Introduction. As we learned in the preceding chapter, the telephone is frequently used as a means of communication between different towns and cities. Another means of rapid communication is that offered by telegraph companies. The discussion in this chapter relates to the services of these companies.

A telegraph company sends messages by wire from one telegraph station to another. The service is quick, and is cheaper for a few words than the telephone, especially when a message is sent a long distance. All messages sent by telegraph companies are commonly known as telegrams. BELE 

CABLES 

Postal Tel. Co. 

Figure 60-Call Box for Summoning a Telegraph Messenger 

A messenger is summoned when the handle of the call box is turned clockwise and then released. 

This name is also given to one type of message; other names are given to other messages, as will be discussed in later paragraphs. 

Delivering Messages to a Telegraph Company. A message which is to be sent by telegraph is delivered to the local telegraph office in writing or by telephone. 

Business messages are preferably delivered in writing. 

This lessens the chance of error and provides a permanent record of the message which may be desired for future reference. In cities, telegraph companies cooperate with businesses by collecting telegrams in certain business districts by means of messengers. A messenger may be summoned by 127

[ch. 9 

Western Union Tel. Co. 

Figure 61

Machine for Sending Telegrams 

A telegram written upon this machine is immediately reproduced in the office which is connected with this machine by wire. telephone. If a business sends telegrams frequently, a telegraph company will install a device known as a call box for use in summoning messengers. 

When a business sends many telegrams, a telegraph company will install a machine on which an employee of the business can type a message which will be immediately reproduced in the office of the telegraph company. 

This provides extremely rapid service with great accuracy.

Writing Telegrams. A tele gram should be brief because the charge for it is based upon the number of words in the message. It should never be so brief, however, that it cannot be fully understood. It is better not to send a telegram than to send one in which the meaning is not entirely clear. 

When a telegram has been written, it should be re-read by the writer to make certain that the only possible interpretation is the one he intends.

The writer should remember that the reader may not be thinking about the same thing as he, and may not readily understand the purpose of the brief message. 

Western Union Tel. Co. 

Figure 62

Machine for Receiving Telegrams 

When a telegram is written on the machine in Figure 61, it is reproduced and is printed on a strip of paper which moves out from the left side of the machine. The strip of paper is divided up into sections and is pasted on a telegraph blank for delivery.

ch. 9] 129 

THE TELEGRAPH 

After a telegram has been sent, a letter confirming the statement in the telegram may be mailed. This is desirable for it provides an additional written record of the message and thus helps to avoid misunderstandings. But as a letter is not delivered as soon as a telegram, writing a letter of confirmation does not lessen the need for clearness in the telegram itself.

Telegraph Blanks. Blanks on which telegrams may be written are furnished by the telegraph companies. On the back of these blanks, the service rendered by the telegraph companies is fully described. A person who is not familiar with the classes of the messages, the terms on which they are handled, and the responsibility of the telegraph company, can get this information from the blanks. 

On the face of a blank there is space in which the one sending the message is requested to check the class of service desired. Several classes of service are available, as will be explained later. If the class is not indicated, the message is sent in the quickest and most expensive manner. 

Codes. Two offices or persons may frequently exchange telegrams which cannot be condensed into a few words. In such cases they agree that one word in a telegram will mean several other words. The words which have the special meanings in the telegrams are known as a code. Of course, it is necessary for both the sender and the receiver to have a copy of the code with the meaning of each word. 

For example, suppose a manufacturer of furniture made a large number of products, all listed in his catalog by stock numbers. It would be difficult to give complete descriptions of these products in a telegram, and it might also be difficult to give the stock numbers on account of the length of the numbers. A certain group of tables might be listed in the catalog as follows:

STOCK NO. 

51062. 51063. 51064. 51065. 

Walnut gate-leg table, 48x48. 

Walnut veneer gate-leg table, 48x48. 

Walnut gate-leg table, 48x60.. 

Walnut veneer gate-leg table, 48x60.

CODE WORD 

Sand ., Gravel .Shell .Dust

[ch. 9 

In each case the short word at the end of the line is the code word. If a dealer wished the manufacturer to ship him one each of these four items, he could make his telegram read as follows: "Ship by freight one each sand gravel shell dust." The dealer would know the meanings of the code words as they would be given in the catalog or on the order blank of the manufacturer.

Codes are also used when secret messages are sent, but they are most frequently used as a means of reducing the number of words.

The use of codes is quite common and can be highly developed when messages are sent frequently between the same persons. In the event that codes are desired, much information about them and their use can be obtained from the telegraph companies.

1. If you were in an office in the business section of a city during the day, how would you deliver a message to a telegraph company? 

2. The Henry Miller Company of Boston has branch offices in Chicago, St. Louis, Denver, and Atlanta. It frequently finds it necessary to send fast day telegrams to the branch offices, but is usually unable to condense the telegrams into a few words. This makes the telegrams very expensive. Suggest a method by which the expense may be reduced. 

3. Why do many firms use codes when sending telegrams? 

4. If you were sending a telegram to a friend, would you use a code? 

5. The Board of Education of Harrison sends Mabel 

Breaker a telegram offering her a position as a teacher in the Harrison High School. It also sends her a letter confirming the telegram. Of what value is this letter? 

6. What information is given on a telegraph blank?.

ch.131 

THE TELEGRAPH Section II. COST OF TELEGRAMS 

Classes of Messages. Telegraph companies have established four classes of service for which they make different charges. The different classes or types of messages are desirable, as at one time a person may need very quick service for which he is willing to pay a high rate, while at another time he may find a slightly slower service at a lower rate more satisfactory. The four types of messages accepted by the telegraph companies are: 

(1) Telegram or Fast Day Telegram. 

(2) Day Letter. 

(3) Night Message or Night Telegram. 

(4) Night Letter or Lettergram. 

The Fast Day Telegram. The fast day telegram is the fastest type of service rendered the public by the telegraph companies. (See Figure 63.) It is sent immediately in the day or night, and is delivered to the addressee as soon as it is received at the destination office. It is the most expensive

C. L. GRANT & COMPANY WESTERN UNION MESSENGERS ARE AVAILABLE FOR THE DELIVERY OF NOTES AND PACKAGES 

Figure 63-Telegram

[ch. 9 

type of service rendered by the telegraph companies since it insures the quickest service. The sender of such a telegram knows that his message will usually be delivered within a very few minutes, because other classes of messages will be held until his is sent. The minimum rate is the charge for ten words, no charge being made for the address or signature. There is an additional charge for each additional word. 

The Day Letter. When a person wishes to send a message which he cannot condense into a few words, he may find a fast day telegram to be too expensive. For such messages, telegraph companies have established the class of service known as the day letter.

The rate for a day letter is based upon a minimum charge for fifty words, with an extra charge for each additional ten words or for any additional words less than ten. 

The rate for fifty words is only one and one-half times the rate for a ten-word telegram. The telegraph company can afford to send a day letter more cheaply than a telegram since it may hold the letter until sometime during the day when it is not busy sending fast day telegrams.

Immediate delivery of a day letter is not guaranteed to the sender, but usually a person can expect a day letter to be delivered the same day that it is sent. For long messages which do not require immediate service, day letters are satisfactory.

The Night Telegram. During the night the telegraph company has less call for its service than during the day. 

It therefore offers a reduced rate for night telegrams to encourage people to send their messages at night when possible. A night telegram is similar to a fast day telegram in that its cost is based on a minimum charge for ten words. 

The rate varies from one-half to about three-fourths of that for a fast day telegram. Night telegrams are accepted up to 2:00 a. m. to be delivered the following morning.

The Night Letter. The night letter (Figure 64) is simi lar to the day letter but the rate is less, for the same reason that the night telegram is sent at a cheaper rate than the fast day telegram. As in the day letter, the rate for a night

ch. 9] 133 

THE TELEGRAPH 

letter is based upon a minimum charge for fifty words, with an extra charge for each additional ten words. The rate

Figure 64-Night Letter for fifty words is the same as that for a ten-word day telegram. Night letters are accepted up to 2:00 a. m. to be delivered the next morning.

Counting Chargeable Words. The amount charged for a telegram is based on the number of words in the message. 

The address and the signature are not counted, but each punctuation mark and each figure in a number are counted as words.

Punctuation should be omitted unless it is necessary for clearness. When it is used, the name of each mark is written as a part of the message. If the name of a mark is not written, the mark may be omitted by the telegraph company. Frequently the word "stop" is used in a telegram instead of "period." The common abbreviations, such as a.m., p.m., c.o.d., and f.o.b., are counted as one word each. 

Numbers of more than one digit should be written out because each digit is charged as a word. For example, when figures are used, "10" is counted as two words, and "200"

[ch. 9 

as three. If they are written out, the word "ten" is counted as one word and "two hundred" as two words. The telegram, "Send two hundred dollars to James Moran before June ten," may be sent as ten words. If the telegram were written, "Send $200 to James Moran before June 10," the charge would be for twelve words.

Cost of Additional Words. As was explained in previous paragraphs, the minimum charge for fast day telegrams and night messages is the charge for ten words; for day and night letters, the minimum charge is the charge for fifty words. When additional words are sent, an additional charge is made. The charge for each extra word in a fast day telegram or night message is slightly less than onetenth the minimum charge. For each additional ten words in a day letter or night letter the charge is about one-fifth of the minimum charge.

Liability of the Telegraph Company. If an error is made in the transmission of an ordinary message, the telegraph company is liable to the sender for damages not greater than five hundred dollars. For a small additional charge, the telegraph company will assume a greater liability for any errors it may make. 

Paying Charges. Telegrams may be prepaid by the sender or may be sent collect. When they are sent collect, the charge is paid by the one receiving the message. Telegrams are frequently sent collect when they are sent upon the request of, or as a favor to, the one receiving the message.

A message may be sent prepaid even though it is telephoned to the telegraph office. If the person sending the telegram has a telephone in his name, the cost of the telegram will be charged to his account. In some cities this charge is included in the telephone bill; in other cities a separate bill is sent by the telegraph company.

 Section III. DELIVERY OF TELEGRAMS-SPECIAL 

SERVICES Delivery of Telegrams. From the telegraph office at which the telegram is received, the message is delivered by messenger or is telephoned to the addressee. Telegrams are delivered without charge within one-half mile of the telegraph office in towns of a population of five thousand or less, and within one mile in larger towns or cities. Outside of this free delivery zone, an extra charge is made for delivery by messenger. Where free delivery service is not maintained, it is customary to telephone the message to the addressee. A copy of the telegram is then mailed to him if he requests it. 

Time of Delivery. There are four different standard times in the United States: eastern time, used chiefly in the states along the Atlantic Coast; central time, used in almost all of the Mississippi Valley; mountain time, used in almost all of the Rocky Mountain district; and Pacific time, CENTRAL TIME

used in the states bordering on the Pacific Ocean, and in adjoining states. (See Figure 65.)

When a person sends a telegram a long distance east or west, he should remember that the time in the city in which the telegram is to be delivered is different from the time in his city. For example, if it requires ten minutes to transmit a telegram from New York to San Francisco, a message sent from New York at twelve o'clock noon will be delivered in San Francisco at 9:10 a. m. A similar message sent from 

San Francisco at twelve o'clock noon will be delivered in 

New York at 3:10 p. m.

Some cities move their clocks one hour forward during the summer months and use daylight saving time, which is one hour faster than the standard time for that section of the country. The possibility of this should also be considered when telegrams are sent during those months.

Delivery of Telegrams on Trains. S. M. Ferris is going from Birmingham to St. Louis by train. The company for which he works wishes to send him a telegram asking him to telephone to a certain person as soon as he arrives in St. Louis. There may be no address in St. Louis to which

[ch. 9 

a telegram could be sent which would insure its delivery as soon as Mr. Ferris arrived in that city. His company therefore sends him a message to be delivered on the train. 

A telegraph company will accept messages at regular rates to be delivered on a train. The message should be addressed in care of the conductor of a certain train, or of the Pullman conductor if the passenger is known to be in a Pullman car. It should be addressed to some city where the train will stop, and should specify the time the train is due. Obviously, the message should be sent to a city far enough in advance so that delivery can be made before the train passes that point. In deciding where to send the message, a person will probably wish to consult a railroad time table.

Sending Money by Telegraph. Suppose a person is away from home and loses his pocketbook containing all his money. He might be in a difficult situation if it were not for the service rendered by the telegraph company. 

To get money at once, he can go to the nearest telegraph station and send a collect telegram to his home or to

ch. 9] 139 

THE TELEGRAPH 

the company for which he works, asking that money be sent him. A return message can be sent ordering the telegraph company to pay him a certain amount. If the return message is sent promptly, he should have the money within a few minutes after the first telegram was sent. Figure 67 shows the form in which a message is written when money is sent by telegraph.

Identification. A person who sends money by wire can require positive identification of the payee before payment is made. If this is done, payment will be made only after the payee has been identified by someone known at the paying office, or by some convincing documentary evidence. 

If the one who sends money by wire believes that it would not be convenient or possible for the payee to be personally identified, he can waive positive identification. 

The telegraph company is then required to use only reasonable care in establishing the identity of the payee. It may require that he produce letters or papers which will indicate that he is the person to whom the money was sent. 

As a further means of identification of the payee, a question may be included in the order, which probably could not be answered by a stranger. (Note the question in the lower part of Figure 67.) This question will be asked the payee by the paying office before payment is made, and payment will not be made if an incorrect answer is given.

The charge for telegraphing money is relatively small. 

Usually the expense is not important when compared to the convenience of having money delivered promptly. 

Cablegrams and Radiograms. Messages known as cablegrams are sent from one continent to another by wires laid on the bottom of the ocean. Radiograms are messages sent by wireless telegraphy, and are used principally in sending messages to and from ships. 

Various classes of service are offered for both cablegrams and radiograms. Between certain points, the fastest class of service is most expensive, and slower classes are less expensive. Information regarding cablegrams and radiograms can be obtained from telegraph offices.

[ch. 9

THE TELEGRAPH LABORATORY PROBLEMS 

1. James Snyder of Kalamazoo has received a letter from his brother, Henry, who lives in Dayton, asking him (James) to meet Henry at the Prince George Hotel, Detroit, on Monday of the following week. James finds that he cannot be in Detroit until Tuesday. He writes the following message to be sent as a day letter: "I am unable to meet you on Monday as you requested, but I can meet you Tuesday morning at the Prince George 

Hotel, Detroit. Will that be satisfactory? Wire your reply."

After writing this day letter, he decides that his message should be sent as a fast day telegram in order to insure prompt delivery. Rewrite his message, condensing it into as few words as possible. 

2. The Ady Book Store wishes to order fifty-five dictionaries. The manager of the store writes the following letter to the publisher: "Please send us at once fifty-five dictionaries, edited by

E. S. Hilmer. The book is listed in your catalog as Stock 

Number 74. We prefer to have the books sent by prepaid express." 

After writing the letter, he decides to send a fast day telegram instead of the letter. He finds that the code word in the publisher's catalog for this book is "High." Write the telegram that the manager should send. 

3. A basket-ball game was to be played in Sheridan between the high schools of Winterset and Sheridan. The gymnasium of the high school at Sheridan was damaged by fire. The manager of the Sheridan basket-ball team now wishes to telegraph to the manager of the basket-ball team of the high school at Winterset, asking if the game can be played in Winterset instead of in Sheridan. Write the message for him.

Explain briefly the type of telegram which should be used for this message.

Friday night. On Thursday morning the costumes have not yet been received. As the secretary of the senior class, wire the Moore Costume Company, saying that if the costumes are not delivered by Friday morning, they cannot be accepted.

CHAPTER X MAIL Section I. FIRST-CLASS MAIL 

Introduction. The government conducts a mail service which carries both messages and merchandise from one person to another. The messages in the form of letters or postal cards are not delivered so quickly as telegrams, nor do they provide the rapid service offered by the telephone. 

But letters are used for the majority of messages sent from one locality to another. This is true chiefly because the cost is low. Letters are ordinarily delivered any place in the 

United States and in some foreign countries for three cents. 

The service rendered by letters is reasonably rapid. For example, ordinary letters posted in Chicago one day may be delivered in Buffalo, Memphis, or Omaha the next morning. As will be explained later, special types of mail service provide for even more rapid delivery of mail. 

First-Class Mail. The postal department groups mail into four classes. The first class consists of all material which is sealed in envelopes or packages so that it cannot be inspected, and all other matter which is wholly or partly in writing. Letters and postal cards are the most common representatives of this class.

First-Class Rates. The usual rate for first-class matter is three cents an ounce or fraction thereof. This applies to letters and sealed packages. Stamped postal cards can be purchased from the post office at one cent each and mailed without additional postage. Post cards, which are cards not printed by the government, are also carried for one cent each if they are approximately the same size as government postal cards. If post cards are much larger or smaller than government postal cards, the usual rate for written or printed matter applies. 144

ch. 10] 145 MAIL 

Air Mail. To provide more rapid service than that of ordinary mail, the post-office department sends mail by airplane. This greatly speeds delivery, especially for long distances. For example, mail carried by train usually requires about five days to cross the continent, but when carried by airplane, it may be delivered in about thirty hours. 

The rates for air mail are eight cents for the first ounce and thirteen cents for each additional ounce. These rates do not add much to the cost of sending a letter, but they are rather high for heavy packages. For this reason, air mail is not commonly used for packages. 

Special air-mail envelopes and stamps may be used, but their use is not required. If ordinary envelopes and stamps are used, the words "Air Mail" should be written prominently on the envelope. 

US MAIL

Wide World Photos 

Figure 68-Loading a Passenger and Mail Airplane

Special Delivery. The post office offers a service known as special delivery for mail which should be delivered as promptly as possible. For a small charge in addition to the usual postage, the post-office department will deliver mail by special carrier in cities and towns as soon as the mail is received at the local post office. The charge in addition to the regular postage is ten cents for first-class matter weighing not more than two pounds. A higher charge is made for heavier packages.

[ch. 10 

Special delivery stamps may be obtained for special delivery letters and packages, but ordinary stamps may be used. When ordinary stamps are used, the words "Special 

Delivery" should be written directly below the stamps.

Registered Mail. As was explained in Chapter VI, letters may be registered at a small cost in addition to the regular postage. The amount charged depends upon the value placed on the letter or package. It varies from fifteen cents for an article valued at five dollars or less, to one dollar for an article valued at not over one thousand dollars.

Registry service is desired most frequently for letters, but packages may also be registered. When packages are registered, they must be sealed and sent as first-class mail. 

Securing Indemnity for the Loss of Registered Mail. If a registered letter or package is lost, the application for compensation or payment for the loss must be made at the post office where the package was mailed or at the office to which it was addressed. The one making the application should present the receipt for the package and should be prepared to give the additional information which will be requested. This will include a complete list of the contents of the envelope or package and the value of each item. If a registered parcel is delivered but some of its original contents are missing, the envelope or wrapper must be given to the post office with the application.

For the reasons given above, a person who sends a registered letter or package should keep a list of the contents and the value of each item. One receiving a registered package should also examine it before he destroys the wrapper or envelope to make certain that the contents are complete. Otherwise, it may not be possible to collect an indemnity in case of loss. 

Foreign Mail. First-class matter may be sent to foreign countries, but the postage rates are not in all cases the same. If one is sending mail to a foreign country, for which he does not have the postage rates, he should get the rates from his local post office.

ch. 10] 147 

MAIL 

Forwarding Mail. When a person plans to change his address, he should notify his local post office so that his mail may be forwarded. He may notify the office personally or by mail. In either case he should give the post office his present address, his new address, and the time during which mail should be forwarded, if the change is temporary. 

If he notifies the office personally, he may be asked to fill out a form giving forwarding instructions. (See Figure 69.)

IN DIRECTORY 

If a person fails to notify the post office to forward his mail, and as a result his mail is delivered to his old address, the first-class mail may be forwarded to him by anyone without additional postage. The one who forwards it should cross out the incorrect address, write the new address, and then post the letter. This method cannot be followed when mail is returned to the sender because it was incorrectly addressed. If a letter is remailed with a new address, postage equal to the original postage must be added. 

Other classes than first-class mail are not forwarded without additional postage; but if one has left a forwarding address, he will be notified when other classes of mail addressed to him at his old address are received, and will be told the amount of postage required for forwarding.
