MAIL Section II. SECOND-, THIRD-, AND FOURTH-CLASS MAIL Second-Class. Second-class matter consists of ordinary newspapers and periodicals. The rate for this matter when it is mailed by the general public is one cent for two ounces or fraction thereof.

Third-Class. Third-class matter is miscellaneous mer chandise weighing not more than one-half pound. It is, with a few exceptions, carried for one and one-half cents for each two ounces.

Fourth-Class. Fourth-class matter, which is commonly known by the name parcel post, is the same as third-class except that the weight of the package is over one-half pound.

The rates for fourth-class mail, or parcel post, differ from the rates for the other classes in that they vary according to the distance the package is to be sent, as well as according to the weight. The following table shows the charges for packages of different weights:

To Illustrate 

The charges for packages mailed to points in the first zone are: 

1 pound 8c 

2 pounds 10c 

3 pounds 11c 

4 pounds 12c 

10 pounds 18c 

11 pounds 19c 

12 pounds 21c

It will be observed that the rates are given by zones. 

The postage required is the same for a given weight for all points in one zone. The first zone includes all points within fifty miles from the place of mailing; the second, points

[ch. 10 

within one hundred and fifty miles; etc. The pound is the smallest unit considered; the same charge is made for a fraction of a pound as for a full pound. The postage rate from any post office to any other point can be determined from the postal guide published by the post-office department. If packages are sent infrequently, it is more convenient to get the amount of postage for each package from a postal clerk than to refer to one of the guides. 

All written matter is first-class mail, so parcel post, as well as second- and third-class mail, must contain no writing. Also, sealed matter is first-class mail, so the other classes ordinarily must not be sealed. An exception is made, however, in the case of parcel post. A parcel-post package may be sealed if it bears a label in printing giving the postmaster permission to open it for inspection. This label must be in printing; it is not satisfactory if written by hand. It must also meet definite requirements in regard to wording. 

The correct wording is used in Figure 71.

Limitation on Size and Weight. The post office will not accept a parcel-post package weighing over seventy pounds. 

The regulations as to size state that the combined length and girth must not be over one hundred inches. This means

ch. 10] 151 

MAIL 

that the length, plus the distance around the thickest portion, must not be greater than eight and one-third feet. A package two feet long, one foot wide, and one foot thick is acceptable because its combined length and girth are only six feet. If the package were two feet long, two feet wide, and two feet thick, it would be unacceptable, for then the combined length and girth would be ten feet. 

Insurance. Third- and fourth-class (parcel post) matter may be insured for its actual value up to two hundred dollars. A small charge, in addition to the regular postage, is made for this service. As government insurance of packages is limited to two hundred dollars, very valuable merchandise is seldom sent by parcel post. It may, however, be sent as first-class matter and registered. This makes it possible to have a value as high as one thousand dollars placed on the package.

Securing Indemnity. When a package is insured, a receipt is issued by the post office. This should be retained as it will be needed if a claim for loss is made. The one sending the package should also keep a list of its contents and the value of each item as this information will also be needed in the event a loss occurs.

The application for indemnity may be made either at the post office where the package was mailed or at the office of the person addressed. 

C. O. D. Mail. Packages may be sent by mail collect on delivery. When a package is sent c.o.d. (collect on delivery), the usual postage plus the additional postage which is charged for the c.o.d. service must be placed on the package. The post office will collect the amount of the c.o.d. charges from the addressee before delivering the package to him. The amount collected will then be returned in the form of a money order to the one who mailed the package. 

The amount which will be collected may equal the value of the package and the postage, up to two hundred dollars. 

A receipt is given the one mailing a c.o.d. package. If the package is lost or destroyed before it is delivered, its

[ch. 10 

value can be collected from the government the same as if it were insured.

Special Handling. The rates for parcel post are much less than those for first-class mail. It is therefore necessary for the post office to handle parcel post as inexpensively as possible. For this reason, a package may not be delivered so quickly as first-class mail, but it is usually delivered within a day or two after a first-class letter.

If a person wishes a package to be sent as quickly as first-class mail, he can secure a service known as special handling. For this service he is charged a minimum of ten cents for packages weighing two pounds or less, and a maximum of twenty cents for packages weighing over ten pounds. A package marked "Special Handling" and with the proper additional postage will be delivered as quickly, or approximately so, as first-class mail. 

Special Delivery. Special delivery can also be secured for packages the same as for first-class mail. When the packages are sent special delivery, the special handling service is also given. The rates vary from a minimum of fifteen cents for packages weighing two pounds or less to thirty-five cents for packages weighing over ten pounds. 

Mailing Packages to Foreign Countries. The regula tions regarding the sending of packages to foreign countries are not the same for all countries. Before one sends a package outside of the United States, he should get information from his local post office about the postal regulations which will apply to his shipment.

[ch. 10 

 Section III. ADDRESSING ENVELOPES AND PACKAGES Importance of Correct Address. Carelessness and in accuracy are always costly. Nowhere is this more evident than in the addressing of letters and packages. Obviously, if a person goes to the trouble and expense of writing and mailing a letter, or if he mails a valuable package, he wishes prompt delivery to the addressee. 

Nevertheless, largely because of carelessness or a lack of attention to details, tremendous numbers of letters and packages are incorrectly addressed and are therefore greatly delayed or lost. In a recent year 22,685,940 letters and 511,687 packages had to be sent to the dead letter division of the post office, where undeliverable mail is handled. 

A few of these were eventually delivered to the addressees or were returned to the senders, but the great majority were destroyed.

Not only did the senders of these letters and packages lose much in the postage paid but there also was great loss because of the value of the packages and the inclosures in the letters. The packages sent to the dead letter office were valued at many thousands of dollars; the inclosures in the letters were valued at over $5,000,000. 

The loss to those sending the letters and packages was only a part of the loss caused by incorrect addresses. The salaries of the employees in the dead letter division of the post office were over a quarter of a million dollars. In addition, the efforts of the post office to deliver partially addressed mail cost many thousands of dollars, more than $500 a day in one large city.

All of this expense would be avoided if letters and packages were correctly and clearly addressed. If those mailing letters and packages were careful, they would not only save themselves tremendous sums but would also save the post office great expense.

The Address. The name of the person to whom the letter is sent, the street address, the city, and the state should

ch. 10] 155 

MAIL 

E.B.Radcliffe 

1310 E. Ridge Ave 

Buffalo, New York. Mr. 

Richard L.Jennings 

617 Mitchell Avenue Indianapolis Indiana

Figure 72-An Addressed Envelope be written or typed on separate lines as in Figure 72. The ordinary address on a letter should therefore have at least four lines. If the name of a company or a business is given in addition to the name of the individual, the address may have five or more lines. 

The Return Address. The address of the sender should be placed in the upper left-hand corner so that the post office can return the letter to him if the one to whom it is addressed cannot be located. This address is usually printed on the stationery used by businesses, but if it is not printed, it should be written. The words "After five days return to" may accompany the address of the writer, but they are not necessary.

Addressing Packages. A parcel-post package should be plainly addressed in a form similar to that used on a letter. 

The return address of the sender should also be written clearly in the upper left-hand corner. 

Wrapping Packages. All packages should be securely wrapped and tied so that they can be handled without danger of breaking. Many articles are damaged in the mails because proper care was not taken by those wrapping them. 

A package containing an article easily broken should be marked "Fragile." If the merchandise is perishable, such

CHAPTER XI EXPRESS AND FREIGHT Section I. EXPRESS 

Introduction. In an earlier chapter, we observed that express companies sell money orders which may be used in sending remittances from one place to another. This is an important service, but transporting packages from one town or city to another is an even more important and commonly used service which is rendered by these companies. 

The service of an express company in handling packages is similar to government parcel-post service. A person who has packages to ship usually has the choice of sending them by express or parcel post. The services of the two agencies will therefore be compared in the following paragraphs. 

Weight and Size of Packages Accepted. Express com panies will accept packages of almost any size or weight. 

This service is quite different from parcel post in which both the size and the weight are limited. Large or heavy packages are therefore commonly sent by express. As will be explained later in this chapter, they may also be sent by freight. Freight shipments, however, move much more slowly than express and are not so satisfactory if quick delivery is needed.

Written Material. Written material containing new in formation, such as letters or original manuscripts, is not acceptable for shipment by express. This limitation on express service is similar to that on parcel post. 

Cost. In some instances express rates are cheaper than parcel-post rates, while in other instances parcel-post rates are cheaper. Express rates between the same points are not the same for all shipments of the same weight, but vary according to the classification in which the express com. pany places each kind of merchandise. 159

[ch. 11 

For any particular shipment, rates can be obtained from the post office and the express office so that a comparison can be made. Reference books which provide information needed in comparing express and parcel-post rates are used in business offices. 

Insurance. Express rates include insurance for the value of a package weighing one hundred pounds or less, up to fifty dollars. Packages weighing over one hundred pounds are insured for their value up to fifty cents a pound. Additional insurance may be obtained for a reasonable fee. The amount of the insurance is not limited to two hundred dollars as in the case of parcel post, but may be equal to the value of the package.

When one ships by express, he is given a receipt showing the value at which the package is insured. This receipt should be retained and should be presented to the company if there is a claim for loss of, or damage to, a package. 

UNIFORM EXPRESS RECEIPT-NON-NEGOTIABLE 

The Company will not pay over 850, in case of loss, or 60 cents per pound, actual weight, for any shipment in excess of 100 pounds, unles a greater value is declared and charges for such greater value paid.

Figure 74-Receipt for an Express Package

An express company requires the one receiving a package to sign his name on a form provided for this purpose at the time the package is delivered. This is valuable to the shipper as it enables him to prove whether the package was delivered and who received it. 

Collection and Delivery. In the collection of packages, express companies render a greater service than the post

ch. 11] 161 

EXPRESS AND FREIGHT 

office. They collect packages for shipment in business districts and in many residential districts of cities. On the other hand, packages to be sent by parcel post must be delivered to a post office. Usually, however, delivering a package to the post office is not difficult since the post office or a branch post office is ordinarily easily accessible from any part of a town or city.

In the delivery of packages, the post office renders a somewhat wider service than express companies. Parcel post is delivered to almost any address in cities and in the country. Express, on the other hand, is not delivered in the country or in certain outlying residential districts of cities. When one is deciding whether to send a package by express or parcel post, he should consider the location of the one to whom it is to be sent. If he lives in the country or in a residential district where there is no express delivery, parcel post may be more convenient. 

Time of Delivery. Express and parcel-post packages are ordinarily delivered in about the same length of time. Both are carried on fast trains and require only a little more time than first-class mail. 

Collect and C.O.D. Shipments. Shipments may be sent by express, transportation charges collect. In this case, the sender does not have to pay any charges. 

Packages may also be sent c.o.d. by express much the same as by parcel post. The chief difference is that for c.o.d. express packages no charges are paid in advance. When parcel post is sent c.o.d., the postage must be paid in advance, although this amount can be included in the amount to be collected from the person to whom the package is sent. 

Air Express. Express companies will accept packages to be shipped by airplane between certain cities. This is a rapid service, but it is also expensive. It is used only when the need for quick delivery is great enough to offset the comparatively great expense. If one wishes to have a package sent a considerable distance as quickly as possible, he should investigate the possibilities of air express. Full information about it can be obtained from an express agent.

[ch. 11

ch. 11] 163 

EXPRESS AND FREIGHT Section II. FREIGHT 

Freight. Parcel post and express are carried on trains but the shipper deals with the government and express companies. On the other hand, when goods are sent by freight, the shipper delivers them directly to the railroad agent, and the railroad company or companies over whose lines the shipment is sent are responsible for its delivery. 

Ordinarily, railroad companies handle freight in large units and do not deliver it as promptly as parcel-post or express packages are delivered. This makes it possible for. them to handle freight cheaply and to offer rates much below those charged by these other transportation agencies. 

The minimum charge for a freight shipment is the charge for one hundred pounds. Small, light packages are therefore seldom shipped by freight, although they could be shipped by this means. 

Bill of Lading. Each package shipped by freight must show the address of the consignee (the one to whom the shipment is being sent). In addition, when the shipment is sent, a form known as a bill of lading must be filled out. 

This is a contract, prepared in triplicate, between the shipper and the railroad. The printed forms provide space for entering the following information: the name of the railroad receiving the shipment (unless this name is printed), the name of the city, the date, the name of the shipper, the name of the consignee and his address, the name of the railroad or railroads over which the shipment is to be sent, a list of the packages or cases shipped, the signature of the shipper and that of the railroad agent. (See Figure 75.)

All three copies of the bill of lading are made at one time by the use of carbon paper. The original is sent to the consignee as evidence that the goods have been accepted by the railroad company for delivery to him. If the consignee is not known to the local agent, he will be required to present his copy before the shipment will be turned over to him. He will also need it if he asks for an adjustment because goods have been damaged.

Figure 75-Bill of Lading 

One of the carbon copies of the bill of lading, known as the shipping order, is retained by the railroad; the other, known as the memorandum copy, is retained by the shipper. 

Thus, each has a record of the shipment. If the shipment is not delivered promptly, the shipper may need to refer

ch. 11] 165 

EXPRESS AND FREIGHT 

to his copy when he asks the transportation company to locate the goods. His copy also provides the information concerning the number and weight of the cases shipped. 

This may be useful if there is a disagreement between him and the consignee regarding the contents of the shipment. 

Prepaid, Collect, and C. O. D. Shipments. The charges for freight shipments may be prepaid by the shipper or may be collected from the consignee. Freight shipments may also be sent c.o.d. Detailed information about the procedure required for c.o.d. shipments can be obtained from any freight agent.

Responsibility of the Railroad. A railroad is responsible for the safe delivery of freight shipments. If the goods are lost or damaged, the shipper or the consignee may make a claim to the railroad company and get compensation for the loss.

Trucks. Freight is carried by trucks on much the same terms as it is carried by train. Regular truck lines are operated between many cities. They offer rapid service at a moderate cost.

Truck service is not available between all points, but the possibility of using this means of shipment should be investigated by one sending a large shipment or by one shipping rather heavy packages frequently. It is especially attractive for shipments of household goods which can be loaded on a truck from a house in one city and delivered to a house in another city without reloading.

CHAPTER XII TRAVEL Section I. METHODS-THE TIME TABLE 

Introduction. When George Washington was to assume command of the Continental Army in 1775, it required twelve days for him to travel from Philadelphia to Boston.

Now this trip can be completed by train in about eight hours. 

As recently as 1857 the trip from St. Louis to San Francisco, a distance of about twenty-three hundred miles, required twenty-five days. Now a train goes over approximately the same route in less than three days. 

A hundred years ago a train was small, crude, and slowmoving. A complete train, consisting of a locomotive and three passenger cars, was approximately equal in length to a modern locomotive. (Figure 76.) 

Courtesy of the New York Central Lines 

Figure 76

The DeWitt Clinton train, in use about one hundred years ago, contrasted with a modern locomotive. 

These examples emphasize the great improvement made in transportation in recent years. Formerly a trip of even a few hundred miles was long and arduous, but now we can travel hundreds of miles a day by automobile; we can cross the continent in less than four days by train; airplanes will 170

ch. 12] 171 

TRAVEL 

carry us on long or short trips at the rate of more than one hundred miles an hour. Travel has become so rapid and convenient that almost everyone travels to some extent. 

For this reason, everyone should know something about travel services and how they should be used. The following paragraphs provide the information which a person is most likely to need.

Selecting the Method. If one is planning either a long or a short trip, he probably will have a choice of several methods. Trains and busses operate between towns and cities in all parts of the United States. Airplanes are also being used more extensively from year to year and are constantly extending their services. Steamships ply between cities on the coast. In many instances a traveler has his choice of all of these methods. The choice will depend, to a large extent, on the speed required, the cost, and the personal preference of the traveler.

Airplanes offer the most rapid service, but in some instances the better connections made by trains or busses offset this advantage. For long trips, train service is usually more rapid than bus service.

In addition to the difference in speed, there is also a dif- 1241 

VANES 

FOKKER 

Underwood & Underwood 

Figure 77-Passenger and Mail Airplane

A tri-motored plane weighing, when loaded, 22,500 pounds and capable of carrying thirty-two passengers.

[ch. 12 

ference in cost. Usually the most rapid means of transportation is also the most expensive. 

Since the different types of service between all points are not entirely uniform, one should, before he starts on a trip, ascertain the different methods available, the time required, and the cost. The different transportation companies will be glad to give complete information through their local offices. After one has this information, he can select the method which best suits his needs, or which he prefers. 

The Time Table. The various transportation companies print and distribute pamphlets containing time tables. A typical time table of a railroad is illustrated on page 173. 

The time tables published by bus and airplane transportation companies are similar in arrangement and purpose. 

You will observe that the time table contains a list of towns and cities, the distance in miles between stations, and the time schedules for different trains. Some of the trains, like the first three given in the columns headed "59," "29," and "79" are called through trains as they do not stop at all stations. Those stopping at all, or almost all stations, are known as local trains. 

Trains are numbered and are frequently referred to by their numbers. The figures given at the top of the columns in Figure 78 indicate the train numbers. The better trains are also known by special names, such as the Liberty Limited, and the Broadway Limited. As is explained by the notes in the columns, these trains do not carry accommodations for coach passengers but have Pullman cars only. 

Coaches are the cars operated entirely by the railroad. 

One buying an ordinary railroad ticket may ride in any coach, as all are of the same class. Pullman cars are cars owned by the Pullman Company. An additional ticket is required for them. The use of the Pullman cars will be discussed later.

Several symbols are given in the various columns before the railroad time. These are "A" "f," "g," "¶," "j," and "x." These symbols refer to the notes at the right side of the page. You will observe that the letter "j," which is

Figure 79-Railroad Coach 

A modern coach with comfortable individual seats. found several times in the column for Train 9, indicates that this train stops regularly at certain stations only on week days.

The symbol "¶", which is found in the column for Train 23, opposite Bucyrus and Dunkirk, refers to the note which states that the train stops to discharge passengers from Harrisburg and points beyond. This means that Train 23 will not stop at these two stations for passengers traveling only short distances.

The notes about the trains, given in the upper right corner, refer to the trains by numbers and briefly describe the service given by each train. For example, Train 5 carries

Pullman cars, club cars, and observation cars which are open to Pullman passengers. It also carries a dining car between Fort Wayne and Chicago. Similarly, Train 9 carries sleeping cars, and a cafe car from Pittsburgh to Chicago.

ch. 12] 175 

TRAVEL 

The illustration is only a partial time table. Because of the lack of space on the page, it does not show the stations between Pittsburgh and Orrville, nor between Columbia

City and Chicago. Of course, these stations are included in the time table published by the railroad. 

In some pamphlets containing time tables, the description of each train and the notes referred to by the special symbols in the columns are not given on the same page with the time table. In such cases, however, there is a note on the page indicating where this information can be found. 

The time from 12:01 a. m. to 12:00 o'clock noon, inclusive, is indicated by light face type; from 12:01 p. m. to 

12:00 o'clock midnight, by dark face type. Note the change of type in the columns for Trains 23, 135, and 9. As was explained on page 136, there are different standard times for different parts of the country. The time used is indicated on the time table. You will observe that Eastern time is used from Pittsburgh to Lima, and Central time from Elida to Chicago.

The table in the illustration shows trains moving westward only. When there are many trains on one line, the trains moving in one direction are commonly shown on one page and those moving in the opposite direction, on the following page. On lines where there are fewer trains so that all may be shown on one page, the list of stations is given in the center, the trains moving in one direction on the left side, and those moving in the opposite direction on the right side.

Official Railway Guide. Suppose a person is not acquainted with the railroads serving Pittsburgh and therefore does not know which road he should use in going to Perrysville. In this case it may be necessary for him to use an official railway guide. This is a large book containing the time tables for all railroads. Copies are kept in all railroad offices, in hotels, and in many business offices.

In the back of the official railway guide there is an index of stations, giving all towns in the United States and Canada and the roads by which each is served. errysville, Ind., Chicago and E. Ill., 3

The index, parts of which are illustrated here, is that of the official railway guide. It shows the roads serving each city.

Figure 81 shows the parts of the index in which Pittsburgh and Perrysville are located. This index does not show table numbers for large cities, as this would require too much space because of the many roads serving these cities. The index does show the roads serving all cities and the table numbers for the small cities. As Perrysville is served by only one railroad, and as 

Pittsburgh is on this same railroad, it is evident that the tables for this railroad should be used. One can quickly learn whether the two cities are on the same line by referring to the tables indicated by the numbers after the name, Perrysville.

As the tables for each railroad are numbered beginning with one, it is first necessary to locate the part of the official railway guide assigned to each road. This can be done readily for there is an index of railroads in the front of the guide.

For large cities the general index shows only the names of roads serving the cities, but after the road to be used is learned, the table number can be found easily. The section of the guide assigned to one railroad is the same as the pamphlet of time tables issued by the road. It includes a map, an index of stations, and the various tables for the trains of that railroad.

If two towns are not served by the same railroad or are not on the same line, planning a trip is somewhat more difficult. For example, suppose a person wishes to go from 

Perrysville, Ohio, to Los Angeles, California. This trip cannot be planned from one table as there is not a continuous line connecting these two cities. In this case it is necessary

ch. 12] 179 TRAVEL to consult one time table for the road from Perrysville to Chicago, and another for that from Chicago to Los Angeles. 

Similarly, whenever a trip is planned between any two towns which are not on the same line, it is necessary to locate on a map the city which is a probable junction point. 

In doing this one must first locate in the official railway guide the names of the roads serving the two cities. Then, he can refer to the maps for these roads and readily determine which point is the most satisfactory junction for the two roads. The time tables for the roads from the first city to the junction point, and from the junction point to the destination city, can then be located from the index to time tables for the two railroads.

When a long trip is planned, it is advisable to consult the railroad passenger agent as he can give much help. He may save the traveler's time, and also call attention to special trains and services which will make the trip more pleasant.

Since the time tables for busses and airplanes are similar in principle to those for trains, it is not necessary to discuss or illustrate them here.

 Section III. RAILROAD SERVICES 

Purchasing Railroad Tickets. After a person has selected the route to be used, it is necessary to purchase a ticket. 

The ticket to the destination and, if one wishes, for the return trip also, may be purchased from the local railway ticket office. 

N-NEWS-COSA Courtesy of the New York Central Lines Figure 82-Railroad Station 

A section of a modern railway station, showing a part of the waiting room. The passage to trains is at the left. 

If one is planning a long trip, he should ask at the ticket office whether there are to be special excursions for which low rates will be offered. Frequently, in order to increase travel, railroads offer low rates for certain trips. If a person is able to take advantage of these offers, he may make a material saving. In case an excursion ticket is purchased, one should ask the agent about the trains on which it may be used, and what stop-over privileges are permitted. The agent can give this information and thus any misunderstanding about the terms may be avoided. 

If a traveler buys a ticket several days before he plans to use it, he should tell the agent the date that the ticket is to be used. The reason for this is that the ticket is stamped with a date, after which the conductor may not accept it

ch. 12] 181 

TRAVEL 

for a passage. If the ticket is not to be used for several days, the agent will date it in advance. 

Redeeming Tickets. If a ticket is purchased but is not used, it can be returned to the railroad and the amount paid for it will be refunded. When the return is made within a short time, usually thirty days, the refund can be obtained from the office where the ticket was purchased. At any time, full information about the steps to be taken to get a refund can be obtained from the local office. 

Lost Tickets. If a ticket is lost and is not used by anyone, the railroad will refund the purchase price. A ticket which is lost by one person may be found by some other person and used by him, in which case a refund cannot be secured. The railroad keeps a record of tickets sold and of tickets collected by conductors on trains, and can therefore tell whether each ticket sold was used.

Immediately upon discovering that a ticket has been lost, a person should get in touch with the agent at the 

Courtesy of the Pullman Company

Figure 83-Chair Car 

Chair cars are operated by the Pullman Company for day-time traveling.

[ch. 12 

ticket office and should inquire what should be done to secure a refund. If he is on a train when he discovers the loss, he should pay his fare, take a receipt for it, and consult the conductor about the best way to make a claim for the refund.

Pullman Cars. As was explained on page 172, trains usually carry two general types of passenger cars: coaches, which are open to all ticket holders, and Pullman cars, for which an extra ticket is required. Pullman cars are of two general classes-sleeping cars and chair or parlor cars. 

The sleeping cars are planned for night and day travel. The chair cars are similar to the coaches but are somewhat more comfortable and permit travelers the privilege of reserving seats in advance.

Inn nnn n 

Courtesy of the Pullman Company

Figure 84-Diagram of a Standard Sleeping Car 

This car has twelve sections, each with a lower and an upper berth, and a drawing room containing one section with a lower and an upper berth and a sofa bed. The car provides separate accommodations for twenty-seven persons.

When one purchases a Pullman ticket, he buys the right to use a certain space or seat. This space is reserved for him regardless of the number of others who wish to ride in the same car. Since the space is limited, it is advisable for one to make a reservation in advance, as otherwise there may be no space available for him. This can be done by telephoning or calling at a ticket office, and telling the agent the date and the train for which a reservation is desired. 

The Pullman ticket can then be purchased at the time the railroad ticket is purchased, or later if the traveler prefers.

Many times it is satisfactory to make a Pullman reservation a few hours in advance, but at times of the year when there is much travel, it is advisable to make a reservation at least a day or two in advance. 

Pullman reservations for an entire trip may be made through a local ticket office without extra charge. This is

ch. 12] 183 

TRAVEL 

35127. 6360. 4-21-30 KV 

Courtesy of the Pullman Company

Figure 85-Interior of a Standard Pullman Car true even though at the time the reservation is made a person may not have purchased a ticket for the entire trip. 

A reservation of Pullman space may be canceled at any time. Also, if one has purchased a Pullman ticket, he can return it and get a refund any time before the train, for which the ticket was purchased, leaves. Unlike an ordinary railroad ticket, however, a Pullman ticket can not be returned to the company for a refund after the time when it should have been used. The reason for this is that the Pullman Company reserved a certain space for the buyer and should therefore receive pay for it. 

Dining Cars. Cars on which meals are served during certain hours of the day are carried by almost all through

[ch. 12 

Courtesy of the B. & O. Railroad

Figure 86-Dining Car trains and by some local trains. If dining cars are not carried, stops are made at certain points sufficiently long for one to get meals.

Time tables indicate whether certain trains carry dining cars. One can, however, ordinarily depend on all through trains having satisfactory accommodations for meals. 

Tourist Sleepers. Some trains running west from Chicago carry cars known as tourist sleepers. These are similar to Pullman cars, but are somewhat less elaborate and therefore less expensive. If one is planning a western trip and wishes to make it as economically as possible, he should investigate the services rendered by tourist sleepers. 

Baggage. One traveling by train may take with him into the passenger coach his ordinary hand baggage. The railroad will also carry additional baggage for him in the baggage car. One hundred and fifty pounds will be carried in the baggage car without extra charge. A small additional charge is made for additional weight. 

To get baggage service, one delivers his trunk, box, or suit case to the baggage office at the depot and gives his

Figure 87-Baggage Check

The left-hand section of this baggage check is tied to the baggage. 

The right-hand section is given to the traveler. ticket to the agent. The agent punches the ticket to show that the baggage has been accepted, and makes out two baggage checks. One of these is given to the traveler together with his ticket; the other is fastened to the baggage. 

After he arrives at his destination, the traveler can secure his baggage by presenting his check to the agent. 

Baggage must be packed in a trunk, a box with handles, a traveling bag, or a suitcase. The size of a box or trunk must not be more than forty-five inches in any dimension. 

If baggage is lost or damaged, the railroad company is responsible for the loss up to one hundred dollars, unless the traveler has valued it at a greater amount. In case one wishes to place a greater value on his baggage, he is charged a small extra fee.

The railroad company cannot afford to store baggage indefinitely for its passengers, so a fee is charged for each day that baggage is left with the company after the first twenty-four hours. The first twenty-four hours do not include a Sunday or holiday.

[ch. 12 

Parcel Room. If a person wishes to leave a depot for a short time and does not wish to be bothered with heavy parcels, he may check (deposit) his bags and packages at the parcel room in the station. The fee for checking service is small-usually ten cents for each bag or package for each twenty-four hours or fraction thereof.

Ordinarily, when a station does not have a parcel room, it has a number of metal cases in which one can place baggage. If a traveler deposits a dime in a slot on one of these cases, it is possible for him to lock his parcels in a compartment of the case. Full information about the use of this checking device is given on the front of each case. If in a small station no other checking service is available, the station agent may check parcels.

Information. In small depots one may get information from the ticket agent about the service of railroads. In larger depots it is not desirable for those selling tickets to delay their work while they are giving information to travelers. A separate department is therefore operated for this purpose. This is located conveniently and is indicated by the sign "Information." This department is maintained for the service of travelers. Anyone may therefore feel free to ask it for any information desired about travel. 

Travelers' Aid. In large depots the Travelers' Aid Asso ciation maintains offices to give information and help. Travelers' Aid differs from the information department of the railroad. The information department gives information about trains, while Travelers' Aid is maintained to give travelers any other help that may be needed. If at any time you arrive in a strange city and need information relative to hotels, or boarding houses, or information of a similar kind, you may call on Travelers' Aid for it.

 Section IV. FOREIGN TRAVEL 

Foreign Travel. Travel in foreign countries differs from travel in the United States in many details. Furthermore, conditions of travel are not exactly the same in all foreign countries. For example, in some European countries one taking a trip by railroad will wish to ride first class; in other countries second class may be satisfactory; and in still others, fair service may be secured in the third class. 

Courtesy of the U. S. Lines 

Figure 88-Passenger Steamship

The Leviathan, the world's largest ship, 950 feet in length, with accommodations for 3,397 passengers.

Because of the many details about travel in foreign countries which are peculiar to each country, it is impossible for anyone except the experienced traveler to keep fully informed about travel in all parts of the world. For the average person, however, this information is unnecessary. If a person plans a trip to a certain country or countries, he can get information about the conditions and requirements of travel in those countries. This information is distributed by travel bureaus, and agents for steamship companies and similar organizations. Remarkably complete books which contain information about travel in all parts of the world are published for free distribution. 

Although it is not necessary for everyone to be fully informed about the conditions of travel in all parts of the

ch. 12] 189 

TRAVEL 

world, there are certain fundamental facts with which a person should be familiar. These are, first, the sources of information; second, the passport; and, third, the time when the trip should be planned.

Sources of Information. Much more printed matter is available in convenient form for foreign travel than for travel in the United States. One reason for this is that fewer people in this country are familiar with travel conditions in other parts of the world. Another reason is that transportation companies have found the distribution of travel information to be a profitable form of advertising.

Ocean and foreign inland transportation companies co-operate in their efforts to induce people to make trips to foreign countries.

If one is contemplating a trip to a foreign country, he can secure information in printed form from banks or other financial institutions as well as transportation companies. 

The transportation companies are glad to give the information since they may sell the tickets required for travel.

Financial institutions, such as banks, are especially interested in foreign travel since, as will be explained later, they have special forms for travelers' funds which they wish to sell to travelers.

If one is not certain where he can get the information he wishes, he may consult his banker, or the agents of local transportation companies. He can also get from the classified telephone directory the addresses of all the travel bureaus in the city in which he lives or in a neighboring city. These bureaus will be glad to give him full information if he calls personally at their offices or if he requests the information by mail.

The Passport. Foreign travel differs especially from travel in the United States in that a passport is required. 

A passport is a document showing the country of which a person is a citizen and indicating the reason for his traveling in foreign countries. It is required, with few exceptions, when a person goes from one country to another. A passport can be obtained from the Passport Bureau in the

[ch. 12 

Federal Building in a few large cities. Applications may also be made to the clerk of any United States District Court, or by mail to the Bureau of Passports, State Department, Washington, D. C.

Those issuing passports can give full information about how they are obtained. The prospective traveler should remember, however, that it may take several weeks to get a passport, so he should not fail to make his application some time before he plans to start on the trip. If he is consulting a travel bureau and securing information from it, he may also wish to consult it about the most convenient way of obtaining a passport, as this may save him much time and trouble.

PASSPORT APPLICATION 

The legal fee for a passport is nine dollars, in currency or postal money order, and one dollar for execution of application.

The total fee is $10.00. It is unnecessary to pay any other fee to any person in connection with the filling out or execution of this application or for obtaining the passport. All necessary information and guid ance will be given applicant by the clerk of court or Passport Agent before whom this application must be executed.

ch. 12] 191 

TRAVEL 

The passport should be approved or visaed by the consul of the country to which one is first going after leaving the United States. The transportation company through which one secures his ticket can give information about how the visa can be obtained most conveniently. 

Planning the Trip. Travel from the United States to foreign countries with the exception of Canada and Mexico requires a steamship voyage. Steamship accommodations vary greatly. The cost depends upon the speed and the type of the ship and the class of the ticket. Steamships ordinarily offer two or three classes of tickets for which different charges are made. The choice depends largely upon the amount one wishes to pay and the amount of luxury desired, for the service rendered in each class is usually quite satisfactory.

It is customary for steamship companies to assign reservations in each class in the order in which the applications are received. As some locations in each class are more desirable than others, it is best to plan a trip and make reservations as far as possible in advance, in order that suitable accommodations may be procured.

States travel without taking an ocean voyage?

 Section V. THE TRAVELER'S MONEY 

The Traveler's Money. It is dangerous for one who is traveling to carry a large sum of money as it may be lost or stolen. It is also undesirable for a person to attempt to pay his traveling expenses by check, since he will be dealing largely with strangers who will not know whether his checks are good. Even money orders and bank drafts may not be entirely satisfactory as it may not be possible for the traveler to get the identification which is necessary in cashing such forms. As was explained in Chapter VI, no one should cash a money order or a bank draft unless he knows the indorser, or unless the indorser has been identified to him. 

Travelers' Checks. Special forms have been designed to take care of the needs of travelers. These are known as travelers' checks. They can be purchased from an express company, a bank, or certain transportation companies. The checks are sold for an amount slightly in excess of the face value. They can be bought in convenient denominations, such as $10, $20, $50, and $100. A typical travelers' check is shown in Figure 90.

[ch. 12 

there is little danger of its not being signed by the right person. The checks are therefore very convenient for the traveler. They are also entirely safe, for they can be cashed only when the traveler's signature has been inserted a second time. If they are lost, duplicates can be obtained from the company which issued them. 

Letters of Credit. Travelers' checks are satisfactory for ordinary traveling, but one may be traveling so far that he does not wish to carry with him travelers' checks for the entire trip. For example, if a person going to Europe for an extended trip carried his money in the form of travelers' checks, he would have a rather large bundle of checks. 

To take care of such situations, banks and express companies issue instruments known as letters of credit. A letter of credit gives the one in whose favor it is made out the right to call on another bank or institution, mentioned in the letter, for funds. The letter of credit specifies the maximum amount which is to be paid the traveler. Whenever he uses the letter of credit to obtain funds, the amount which he receives is entered on the letter so that it will show how much is yet due him.

1. Suppose you were planning a six-weeks' trip in the 

United States. Would you wish to carry with you enough money for the entire trip? 

2. If you did not wish to carry enough money for a long trip, would it be convenient for you to pay your expenses by check? 

3. Money orders and bank drafts are entirely satisfactory as means of sending remittances through the mail. Are they equally safe and convenient as means of carrying money for use in traveling?

CHAPTER XIII 

THRIFT 

What Is Thrift? You probably have heard the term thrift used many times. Perhaps this is a word which you use. What do you think it means? Does it mean the same as saving?

No, thrift and saving are not exactly the same. A person who is thrifty usually saves a part of his income, but a person who saves may not be entirely thrifty. One who practices thrift is a person who uses care and wisdom in the management of all his resources. 

What are your resources? Two are of great importance, time and money. Time is of importance because the amount you learn and accomplish and the money you earn depend on how you use your time. If you are thrifty, you will use your time wisely. You will study, work, and play, but you will never waste time in idleness. Money is also of importance, for your financial progress depends largely on how you use the money you have or will receive. You should therefore use your money wisely. Part of it you should spend for needful things; the rest you should save for the future. 

Why Is Thrift Needed? Why is it necessary for one to plan for the future? Is there not always plenty of time and cannot one make as much money at one time as at another?

Let us see just what the record is. Look at the chart on the following page. This shows the average number of men out of one thousand who are dependent on others for support at different ages. Note that only a few are dependent on others at the ages of thirty-five and forty-five, but that the number rapidly increases until about six hundred out of every one thousand are dependent on others at the age of seventy-five. 

Why are so many dependent at these advanced ages?

Some have lost their money through misfortune or through unwise investments. Others have been handicapped by sick- 196

ch. 13] 197 THRIFT 25 600 500 400 300 200 100 35 45 55 65 75 

From Devney's Economic Table. Copyright, Jos. J. Devney, Cleveland Figure 92-Dependency Table 

This table shows the average number of men out of 1000 who are dependent at ten-year periods. The figures at the left indicate the number of men; those at the top, their ages. The great increase in the number of those who are dependent after forty-five years of age could have been avoided if each person had saved a part of his income during his youth. ness, or for various reasons have not been able to earn a satisfactory income. But probably the majority are in need of help because they have not been thrifty. They have not used their time well to learn and earn as much as possible, and they have not spent and saved their income wisely. 

Thrift is necessary if a person wishes to avoid poverty in his old age.

Another reason for thrift is that opportunities come to a person who is thrifty. Probably you have heard people say that they have had certain opportunities but have been unable to grasp them. They may have had the chance to accept important positions, but they were unable to do so because they did not have enough training. Or they may have had the chance to invest in very profitable businesses, but they did not have the money required. 

In many cases the inability to grasp an opportunity has come from lack of thrift. The person may not have used his

[ch. 13 

time wisely and therefore may not have been well trained. 

Or he may not have saved his money and therefore may have had no funds when they were needed. If you are to be able to grasp opportunities, you must be thrifty as to both time and money.

Why Consider Thrift Now? Do we not have a whole lifetime before us in which to save? Is it necessary to begin to plan for the future at the age of fourteen or fifteen years when one will have so many years in which to earn? 

It is obvious that thrift in time is necessary now, for there is no other time so satisfactory as the present for securing the training which will bring success. But it is equally true that it is desirable to begin to use and save money wisely at an early age. This is true because of the income which money will earn. 

Suppose you have ten dollars you wish to save for the future. If you place it in a safe place in your home or lock it in a safe, you will have ten dollars whenever you wish to spend it, whether that time be one or fifty years hence. 

But observe how different the situation is if you invest the money so that it will earn an income. If the rate of return is 6 per cent, you will receive 60 cents at the end of the first year. This is not a great amount, but it can be invested with the original ten dollars and the income will be greater the following year. If the income is reinvested each year, $10 will grow to $20 in twelve years; to $40 in twenty-four years; to $80 in thirty-six years; to $160 in forty-eight years; and to $320 in sixty years.

In the preceding example, note that the investment increased from $10 to $20 in twelve years, but that later, in the same length of time, it increased from $160 to $320. 

This is because each year the income may be reinvested to increase the income for the next year. It is clear then that one should begin saving early because the longer savings are invested, the more rapidly they increase. 

The table on the opposite page shows the rate at which one dollar will increase when it is invested at different rates of interest. You will observe that the per cent of increase

ch. 13] 199 THRIFT is the same each year, but that the amount of increase gradually becomes larger because the income is constantly reinvested. If you are to secure the greatest benefit from your savings, you must begin to save early. The earlier you begin, the longer your savings can be invested, and the greater will be your gain.

Figure 93-Compound Interest Table 

This table shows what $1.00 will amount to at compound interest at various rates per cent for periods from one to forty years. For example, $1.00 invested at 4 per cent, will amount to $1.04 in one year, $1.08 in two years, $1.12 in three years, $1.17 in four years, $1.48 in ten years, and

[ch. 13 

$2.03 in eighteen years. This assumes, of course, that the interest earned is invested at the same rate as the original $1.00. 

If $1.00 will amount to $2.03 in eighteen years, the interest earned is found as follows: $2.03, sum of principal and interest 

1.00, original capital $1.03, interest.

If the original capital is invested at the rate of 4 per cent but the interest is not reinvested, the interest for eighteen years is found as follows: $1.00 .04 $.04, annual interest $.04 18$.72, interest for eighteen years.

You will observe that when the interest is reinvested as it is earned, the total interest on one dollar in eighteen years is $1.03, while if the interest is not reinvested, the total is $.72. The gain from investing the interest is then: 

$1.03 — $.72 — $.31, gain from investing interest. This is a small sum, but it must be remembered that it is the result of the investment of only a dollar. If $1000 were invested, the gain would be: $.31 X 1000 = $310. 

The problems given above illustrate the importance of investing all funds as soon as possible after they are secured. Delays in investing even small sums greatly decrease the amount of interest that might be earned.

CHAPTER XIV PERSONAL BUDGETS AND RECORDS Section I. BUDGETS 

Introduction. In the previous chapter we studied the need for thrift. But thrift does not take care of itself. One cannot simply determine to be thrifty, and then without effort on his part properly use his time, save a satisfactory part of his income, and economically use the balance. A definite plan must be made, and then followed. Such a plan is known as a budget. 

Need for a Budget of Time. Have you ever observed people who do not seem to have enough time for their work? 

Their lessons are never fully prepared, and their tasks are never completely done. Probably the reason is that they do not make a budget of their time.

If such people divided their time according to a definite plan, the results would be much better. A certain amount of time each day could be set aside for study, another period for recreation, another for rest, and so on until the twenty-four hours were completely accounted for. If a plan were carefully followed, lessons would be prepared on time, work would be done, and more time would be available for recreation. The reason for this is that time would not be wasted, but would be used to good advantage. 

A Budget of Time. A budget of time made by a high school pupil is illustrated on the following page. When the budget was made up, only the six week days were considered and Sundays were not included. 

Need for a Budget of Money. A budget of receipts and expenditures of money is even more necessary than a budget of time. For example, Francis Conard, a high school pupil, had an income of five dollars a week. One dollar of this amount was an allowance from his parents; the rest was his wages for part-time work in a grocery store. Francis 203

He always expected the situation to get better, but whenever he had any money there was something else he wanted. 

In fact, it seemed that his wants increased more rapidly than his income and that he was never able to buy all that he desired. Francis therefore decided that he would make a budget of his money and would try to follow it closely. 

A Budget of Money. In a budget of time the total time available for distribution is always known. In a budget of money, the situation may be different as it may be necessary to estimate the amount of the income. Francis, however, did not have to make an estimate of his income as he knew exactly how much it would be. One dollar was given him each week by his parents and four dollars were earned through part-time work in a grocery store. Since he knew the exact amount of his income, Francis' chief task was preparing a budget for expenditures so that he would use his income thriftily.

In preparing the budget, Francis first decided the amount which he wished to save. He did this because he knew he could find plenty of things for which to spend

ch. 14] 205 

PERSONAL BUDGETS AND RECORDS 

money. If he first decided how to spend his money, he probably would have nothing left for savings. He therefore thought it better to deduct the amount to be saved and distribute the balance among possible expenditures. 

Francis estimated that he should save $1.00 a week and that his weekly expenses would be as follows: lunch at the school cafeteria, $1.10; street-car fare, at the special school rate, 50 cents; personal expenses, including barber work, pressing of clothes, and purchase of small articles of clothing such as neckties and collars, which he commonly bought, $1.20; education, including all supplies purchased for use in school, 20 cents; charity and benevolence, 25 cents; and entertainment, 75 cents.

The budget based on these estimates which Francis drew up appeared as follows:

ch. 14] 207 

PERSONAL BUDGETS AND RECORDS Section II. RECORDS 

Need for Records. In the preceding section we observed the budget prepared by Francis Conard. During the three months after this budget was prepared, Francis saved one dollar each week and placed it in a savings bank. As his savings were increasing according to the budget, he thought that he was following his budget plan. He kept no records of his expenses, so he did not observe that his personal expenses were small because he was not buying the small articles of clothing which he ordinarily purchased. He also did not observe that his entertainment expenses were too large. 

At the end of three months Francis needed several items of clothing, including neckties, socks, and collars; a suit had to be cleaned and pressed, and another suit had to be pressed. The total cost was $9.50. Francis had not accumulated any funds except his savings, so it was necessary for him to withdraw over half of his savings in order to take care of these expenses.

A definite plan for spending had been made, and it was thought that this plan was being followed. What then was the cause of the trouble? It arose largely from the fact that no records were kept to show the amount of the expenses. 

If such records had been kept, it would have been apparent that the entertainment expenses were too great and that the personal expenses were small. 

Francis knew that over a period of several months he had to pay about a certain amount for clothes, although he did not spend the same amount each week. If he had known how small his personal expenses were, he would have realized that he was not spending the usual amount for the care of his clothes, and that the weeks in which he was spending little would have to be balanced by weeks in which he spent more. From this he would have learned that he should accumulate money in addition to his savings to take care of the personal expenses which would be incurred later. He would also have learned that if he did not do so, he would not be following his budget and later would have to spend a part of his savings for his personal expenses.

[ch. 14 

Memorandum Book. From his experience during the three months, Francis realized that if he was to keep his expenses within the estimates, he must keep a record of them. Only in this way could he examine his expenses from time to time, observe whether they were higher or lower than the estimates, and then make plans for the future. 

Francis decided to record the amount spent, in a small memorandum book which provided a space for each day's record. He selected this book because it was small and could be carried with him. This made it possible for him to enter each amount very shortly after it was spent and thus to prevent his forgetting different items. SUN APRIL, 1930

Figure 96-Memorandum Book 

4.00 108

This memorandum book, Figure 96, made a convenient means of entering each expense, but did not classify them for easy comparison with the budget. Hence, the amounts were transferred each day to a larger book ruled with several columns, called a personal income and expense record.

ch. 14] 209 

PERSONAL BUDGETS AND RECORDS Personal Income and Expense Record. The first column of the personal income and expense record was for the date. 

The year was written directly under the word "Date" in the space at the top. The first of the two columns under the word "Date" was used for the month, and the second for the day of the month. The name of the month was written only at the beginning of each month, and each time the book was balanced. The explanation column was used to explain any unusual entry which otherwise might not be understood. This column was not frequently used as 

Francis planned to keep his memorandum book permanently. 

He was therefore able to refer to it for explanations of unusual entries.

In the "Receipts" column, the balance on hand and all additional receipts were entered. The total expenditures for each day were entered in the "Payments" column. This amount included savings, as well as expenditures, for savings were kept entirely separate from the balance which was kept on hand for current expenses. (The disposition of savings is discussed in a later chapter.) The difference between the total of the "Receipts" column and that of the "Payments" column at any given time was the cash on hand. The accuracy of the work could therefore be proved by a comparison of the balance as shown by the record with the money actually on hand. 

The last seven columns were used for the distribution of savings and expenses. The total of all entries made in these columns for any one day had to equal the amount entered in the "Payments" column for that day. The only purpose of the analysis was to show the distribution of the money spent so that Francis could determine whether the budget had been closely followed.

Francis proved his record each week as he wished to make certain that he was keeping it accurately. In proving the record, he used the following procedure: (1) He added all columns and entered the total in small pencil figures close to the line above. Small pencil figures were used as he wanted to prove the accuracy of his figures before entering them in ink.

[ch. 14

Comparison of the Record with the Budget. The per sonal income and expense record enabled Francis to compare the amounts saved and spent each week with the budget. But this record did not provide a very convenient means of comparing the amounts spent over a period of several weeks with the budget. The expenses of one kind for a week might sometimes be more and sometimes less than the budget. It was only by adding the amounts for several weeks and comparing them with the total budget allowances for those weeks that he could accurately compare the expenses with the allowances.

After the columns were totaled, it was possible to compare the amount actually spent for each item with the budget total and to observe whether the budget plan was followed. The amounts for lunches, personal expenses, and entertainment were less than the budget allowances. The expenses for education were much more than the budget, but they were for special items which probably would not be purchased each week.

Francis continued to enter his expenses in this form from week to week, thus closely watching each item. This provided an excellent basis for real thrift and enabled him to follow the plan of his budget.

CHAPTER XV  Section I. BUDGETS 

Need for Budgets and Records. We have observed that a person needs a budget and a record of income and expenses if he is to save reasonable amounts and is to spend according to a plan. It is even more necessary that a family make a budget and keep records. This is true for two reasons: first, savings are necessary, for there are dependents in most families who are not able to earn their own living; and, second, the different members of a family have so many wants that it is difficult to save. 

Making the Budget. When the budget is being made, the amount to be saved should be determined first, and the balance distributed between necessities and luxuries. It might seem more reasonable first to determine the amount required for necessities and then to distribute the balance between savings and luxuries. This is not advisable, however, on account of the great variety of prices at which necessities can be purchased. A family can spend practically any amount its income will permit for rent, food, and clothing. The amount to be allotted to these and other necessary expenses should therefore be determined after the amount to be saved has been deducted from the total income.

It is comparatively easy to fix the amounts to be allotted to regular expenses, such as rent, clothing, and food. 

Certain irregular expenses are much more difficult to estimate, but an effort should be made to estimate them accurately so that sufficient provision can be made for them. For example, expenses for medical and dental services may not be incurred frequently, but when they are incurred, may be large. If provision is not made for them, such irregular expenses may at times require the using of a considerable part of the savings. 217

[ch. 15 

Standard Budgets. In making a family budget, one may be guided to some extent by budgets which others have prepared. Such budgets are printed in many books and magazines. None of them may be accurate for a particular family, but they do show the different types of expenses that may be incurred and the amounts that can be allotted to savings, necessities, and luxuries for various incomes. 

They are chiefly useful to a family that wishes to make up a budget and does not have any records showing past expenditures, on which to base the estimates. They are also useful in checking the expenditures at the end of the year to see if any of them seem to have been excessive. Suggested budgets for an average family of four are given in 

Figure 99.

BUDGETS FOR AN AVERAGE FAMILY OF FOUR

Figure 99-Standard Budgets 

In the standard budgets, the items of savings, food, clothing, and recreation and entertainment need no explanation. Household expenses include rent (or interest if the property is owned but only partially paid for), taxes, fire insurance, gas, electricity, coal, and similar expenses that are necessary in operating the home. "Family Welfare" includes such expenses as medical and dental services, life insurance premiums, and books. Under "Benevolence" are classified all gifts to religious and charitable organizations. 

Budget of E. S. Thompson. E. S. Thompson, the head of a family of four, has an income of $2400 a year, or $200 a month. As he is paying for a home, he wishes to save as much as possible. He believes that $30 a month will be reasonable and decides to make that his goal.

As he has added $10 to the amount to be saved, it is necessary that he deduct a like amount from the allowances for the expenses. He therefore makes the allowance for household $60, for clothing $30, for recreation and entertainment $8.50, for family welfare $9.50, and for benevolence $12. ch. 15] 221 

 Section II. RECORDS 

The Household Income and Expense Record. After he establishes his budget, it is necessary for E. S. Thompson to plan a record of income and expenses so that he will know whether he is adhering to his budget. A form similar to the personal income and expense record discussed in Chapter 

XIV might be used.

Mr. Thompson prefers to use a complete record which will make it easy for him to compare the expenses with the estimates given in his budget and to continue the comparison over a long period, a year at least. This is desirable since some of the expenses, such as those for medical service and education, included in family welfare, will be quite irregular. Mr. Thompson therefore decides to use the form shown in Figure 101.

In the household income and expense record (Figure 

101), the "Date" and "Explanation" columns are used in the same way as the similar columns in the personal income and expense record (Figure 97). The columns for total receipts and total payments are also similar to the first two money columns in the personal income and expense record. The difference between the receipts or income and the payments is the balance on hand. This amount can be verified by an actual count of the cash on hand.

There are two columns for savings and for each type of expense. The purpose of this is to make easy the comparison of the savings and expenses with the budget allowances. 

The first of the two columns is for the estimate, and the second for the amount actually saved or spent. When the income is received, it is entered in the first column, that is, the "Receipts" column under "Totals." It is also distributed to the various "Budget" columns according to the budget which has been prepared. As Mr. Thompson receives onehalf of his income on the first and one-half on the fifteenth of the month, the amounts entered in the "Budget" columns on these dates are one-half the allowances for the month. 

Entries for the actual payments are made at the end of each day. Each amount spent may be entered in a memo-

[ch. 15 

randum book to insure that it will not be forgotten at the end of the day. Explanations of unusual expenditures may also be put down in the memorandum book for future reference. 

HOUSEHOLD INCOME

Figure 101-Household Income and Expense Record 

At the end of the day, the total amount spent is entered in the "Payments" column. Savings are included in this total, for savings are kept entirely separate from current funds and are not counted as a part of the balance on hand. 

The amounts spent for the different items in the budget are then distributed to the "Actual" columns. If there are several amounts spent for items which are classified together in the budget, they are added and entered in one amount. For example, all amounts spent for food on May 1, as shown by the memorandum book, are added and entered in the "Actual" column under the heading "Food." This sum will later be added to the other entries in the same column so that the total can be compared with the budget.

ch. 15] 223 

At the end of the month, all columns are totaled. The totals are first entered in small pencil figures. After the accuracy of the work is proved, the totals are written in ink, AND EXPENSE RECORD AND ACTUAL EXPENSES HOUSEHOLD CLOTHING ENTERTAINMENT

Figure 101-Household Income and Expense Record as in Figure 101. The accuracy of the work may be proved easily as the difference between the total of the "Receipts" column and the total of the "Payments" column should be the balance of cash on hand. The sum of the totals of all "Budget" columns should also equal the total of the "Receipts" column, and the sum of the totals of the "Actual" savings and expense columns should equal the total of the "Payments" column.

The difference between the total of each "Budget" column and the total of the corresponding "Actual" column is the amount by which the actual savings or expense fails to equal the estimate, or the amount by which the actual sayings or expense exceeds the estimate.

[ch. 15 

For example, during the month of May (Figure 101) the budget for food was $50, but only $43.20 was spent. The actual expense therefore was $6.80 less than the allowance. 

The household budget was $60 and the actual expense was $40.90. The actual expense was therefore $19.10 less than the allowance. The clothing budget was $30 and the actual expense was $23.60. The actual expense was then $6.40 less than the allowance. The budget for entertainment was $8.50, but the actual expense was $9.00. The expense was therefore 50 cents greater than the budget allowance. Also, the family welfare expense was 50 cents greater than the allowance, and benevolences were $2.00 less than the allowance. 

Balancing and Ruling the Record. It is not desirable to carry forward into the next month the totals for the month of May as this would make the figures too large to be handled conveniently. The balance of the cash on hand, which is the difference between the receipts and payments, is therefore carried forward to the next month; and the balance in each classification of expenses, which is the difference between the budget allowance and the actual expense, is carried forward. Each balance is proved correct by adding it to the smaller of the column totals in the comparison of budget and expenses. The totals are entered on the next line. The page is then ruled with a double line under the totals to show that a new month is being started. The balances are entered on the first line below the double ruling. 

In each case the balance is entered in the column which was larger during the previous month. For example, the total receipts are greater than the total payments so the balance of $33.30 is entered in the "Receipts" column. The budget for food is greater than the amount actually spent for food so the balance of $6.80 is entered in the "Budget" column under the heading "Food." The actual expense for entertainment is 50 cents greater than the allowance so the balance of 50 cents is entered in the "Actual" column.

There is no balance to be brought down for the "Savings" columns as the amount actually saved was the same as the budget allowance.

ch. 15] 225 

During June the entries are made in the household income and expense record the same as they were during the month of May. At the end of June, the totals and balances are again secured. This makes it possible for Mr. Thompson to see how his budget compares with actual savings and expenses throughout the period of two months. As the balances at the end of June are carried forward to the next month, the comparisons may be continued indefinitely. 

When a complete page of the record is filled, the book is ruled and balanced the same as at the end of the month, and the balances are carried forward to the next page.
