CHAPTER XVI INVESTMENTS Section I. QUALITIES OF GOOD INVESTMENTS 

Need for Care in Investing. In preceding chapters we observed that money which is saved and invested increases rapidly. In the course of a number of years, the income received from the investment is much more than the investment itself.

But this is true only if the money is wisely invested. 

Suppose you lend $10 to an acquaintance with the understanding that at the end of the year this amount is to be repaid, together with 6 per cent interest. If payment is made, you will receive $10.60 at the end of the year. On the other hand, if the borrower has no money at the end of the year, you may not receive anything. You may lose both the 

60 cents interest and the $10 principal.

Safety. The preceding example illustrates that investments are satisfactory only when the safety of the principal and the interest is assured. There is no purpose in saving money if a person carelessly invests it. One had better spend his money and enjoy what it will buy, rather than save it and then lose it through an unwise investment. 

Types of investments which are reasonably safe will be discussed in this chapter. 

The same degree of safety is not required by all investors. Suppose a person has enough money to make one hundred different investments. If he loses one of them, he still has the other ninety-nine, and his loss is not relatively important. On the other hand, suppose a person has only a small amount of money and is able to make only one investment. If he should lose this, the loss would be very serious to him. A person with small funds should therefore be exceptionally careful to secure investments which will be entirely safe. 230

ch. 16] 231 

INVESTMENTS 

Rate of Return. The second characteristic of a good investment is a satisfactory rate of return. For example, if you get 4 per cent interest, you are better off than if you get only 312 per cent or 3 per cent. In making an investment, you should therefore always consider the amount of income which it will yield. 

One should remember, however, that a high rate of interest usually indicates a great risk of loss. Loans to the

United States Government are most secure as no one doubts the ability of the government to pay its debts. The government usually pays from 3 to 4 per cent when it borrows money. If investors prefer to lend to individuals or businesses where there is some risk of loss, they do so largely because they are paid higher rates of interest than the government pays. For example, at a time when the government is paying 3 per cent interest, one business may be paying 

5 per cent and another, 8 per cent.

The offer of a low rate of interest does not guarantee safety, for investors may be mistaken regarding the risk involved. Similarly, the offer of a high rate of interest does not mean that loss is certain to occur. It does mean, however, that investors believe that the loan involves considerable risk, for the borrower would not offer a high interest rate if his credit were sufficiently good to make it possible for him to obtain a loan for less. A person making an investment should seek a fair return, but he should not let a high interest rate blind him to a danger of loss.

Availability. When an investment can be turned into cash readily, it is said to be liquid. This feature of an investment is desirable, for one frequently needs money to take care of emergencies. 

Suppose, for example, that you have one thousand dollars on deposit in a bank. If you need money at once, you can obtain it from the bank. On the other hand, suppose you have a piece of land which you purchased with this money. The land may be a safe investment and may return a satisfactory yield; but if you need some money at once, you may not be able to obtain it. No one may want

[ch. 16 

the land at this time, and as a result you may be unable to sell it. If you do sell it, you may be able to get only a part of the price you paid, perhaps seven hundred dollars.

You will then lose three hundred dollars, not because the investment was unsafe, but because it could not be easily turned into cash.

It is not necessary that a person making a number of investments have all in a liquid form, that is, in a form that can be turned into cash readily. He will seldom need all of his money at one time. A person should, however, have enough of his investments in a liquid form so that money, which may be needed to take care of an emergency, can be obtained at any time.

A good investment, then, is one that is safe, has a fair yield or rate of return, and is reasonably available or liquid. 

Different types of investments which have these characteristics to a larger or smaller degree will be discussed in the following sections.

1. Harold Hanson has five hundred dollars which he wishes to invest. What do you understand to be the meaning of this statement? 

2. Mr. Hanson lends his money to Mr. Andrews, who is to pay interest at the rate of 6 per cent a year for its use. 

How much does Mr. Andrews have to pay Mr. Hanson each year?

4. Are semi-annual payments of interest better for the lender than annual payments?

He knows of Mr. Belden's investment. As the latter has a reputation for being a good business man, Mr. Hogan decides to make a similar invest-

ch. 16] 233 

INVESTMENTS 

ment. Is the fact that the investment may be a good one for Mr. Belden sufficient indication that it is desirable for 

Mr. Hogan?

(a) Why should one of these three pay a higher rate of interest than either of the others? (b) Which paid the highest rate? (c) Which paid the lowest rate?

[ch. 16 

 Section II. SAVINGS ACCOUNTS 

Introduction. We have observed that savings must be invested if they are to earn interest and thus increase in amount. Usually, however, a person does not receive at one time a large amount of money which he may invest. In fact, he may accumulate his savings a dollar or two at a time. He may wait until he has saved a considerable sum, perhaps a hundred dollars, and then invest the entire amount at once. This plan is not usually followed, for if it were, the investor would secure no interest while the sum was being accumulated.

There are several institutions which enable a person to invest his funds as soon as they have been saved. They accept deposits as the depositor wishes to make them, and pay interest at regular intervals. Typical institutions which accept deposits of savings are the post office, savings banks, and cooperative associations. 

Rapidity with Which Savings Increase. Depositing only a few dollars at a time may not seem to be very important, for the interest on a few dollars is not great. But if this interest is added to the principal and invested so that it in turn earns an additional income, the increase during a number of years is surprisingly large. This is particularly true when additional savings are also deposited regularly. 

For example, Alva Green finds that he can save $10 a week. This will amount to $520 in a year, or $5200 in ten years. His attention, however, is called to the fact that he can deposit his money in a savings bank and receive a return of 4 per cent. He is told by the bank that if he does so, he will have at the end of ten years not $5200, but $6390.97, or $1190.97 more than the amount deposited. 

This increase of almost $1200 in his savings in ten years interests Alva, so he investigates further to see how much the increase will be if he continues to save for a longer period. He finds that at the rate of $10 a week he can save $9100 in seventeen and a half years, even if he does not receive any interest. If his savings are invested at 4 per cent, they will amount to $13,098, a gain of $3998. If the total

ch. 16] 235 

INVESTMENTS 

amount is left in the bank, the interest received from it will amount to more than $10 a week.

This means that if Alva saves $10 a week and deposits it so that it will earn 4 per cent for a period of seventeen and a half years, he can at that time discontinue saving and can withdraw $10 a week from his investment without decreasing the investment. It is apparent that Alva and his heirs may withdraw $10 a week indefinitely from this fund in which deposits of the same amount have been made for only seventeen and one-half years.

The table shown below illustrates the rapidity with which monthly savings increase. The first column of the table shows the amount saved each month. The following columns show the total amount of the savings at the end of different periods.

School Savings Banks. Savings banks are frequently operated in connection with schools. When they are available, they offer an exceptionally attractive means of investing small sums. If your school has a banking system, you can obtain complete information about its services from your teacher. 

Postal Savings. Any person over ten years of age may open a savings account at a post office. Almost all postal stations accept deposits. If a station does not accept deposits, full information can be obtained from it about how deposits should be made at a station where deposits are accepted. Deposits are accepted in even dollars only. When a person makes a deposit, he is given a postal savings certi-

[ch. 16 

ficate which serves as a receipt. If the certificate is lost, stolen, or destroyed, a new certificate is issued. 

Advantages. Postal savings have two important advantages. They are entirely safe, and the depositor can get his money at any time he may need it.

The safety of postal savings arises from the fact that they are guaranteed by the government. The depositor in the postal savings system never has to worry about the possible loss of his principal or interest.

When a person makes deposits in the postal savings system, his savings earn interest for him, but at the same time they are available if they are needed at any time. A depositor may withdraw all or any part of his postal savings deposits with any interest payable thereon, whenever he wishes. The withdrawals may be made in person, through a representative, or by mail.

Disadvantage. Postal savings accumulate interest at the rate of 2 per cent for each year. This rate of interest is frequently considered to be a disadvantage since a higher rate can be obtained from other investments. After a person has accumulated as much as twenty dollars of postal savings, he can have the savings exchanged for a postal savings bond which bears interest at the rate of 212 per cent. 

This is a slightly higher rate than is given on the regular postal savings, but is lower than the rate paid on investments of other types. 

Summary. Postal savings are recommended for those who wish to invest in such a way that they can get their savings at any time, and that complete safety will be assured. This type of investment is less attractive than others to those who are especially interested in the higher rates of interest which the savings may earn. 

1. Suppose you have a dollar a week which you wish to invest. Should you wait until the end of a year and invest fifty-two dollars at that time, or should you invest one dollar each week?

 Section III. SAVINGS BANKS AND COOPERATIVE ASSOCIATIONS 

Savings Banks. A great number of people invest small amounts by making deposits in savings banks. From the depositor's point of view, a savings bank is much the same as a commercial bank. The chief difference is that deposits in a commercial bank are subject to check and do not usually draw interest, while deposits in a savings bank are not subject to check and do draw interest.

When one opens an account in a savings bank, he is required to sign a signature card similar to that required by a 3-0-10

Advantages of Savings Banks. There are a number of features which make savings banks attractive to investors of small amounts. Among the most important are: (1) Small deposits are accepted. An original deposit of at least one dollar is usually required. Further deposits of practically any amount may be made. This makes it easy for one to save regularly, particularly when the income is small. (2) A great degree of safety is secured. Banks are carefully regulated by the government to prevent their being mismanaged. Furthermore, the owners have a considerable amount of their own resources invested in the bank, which, in case of its failure, may be taken to pay depositors. In

[ch. 16 

those instances where savings banks have failed, the depositors usually have lost little, if any, of their savings. (3) The interest rate is reasonable, usually from 3 to 4 per cent. Considering the safety of the investment and the fact that the money is available and may be withdrawn whenever needed, this rate of return is about as large as may be expected.

Deposits may be withdrawn at any time, but usually only those which have been in the bank a month or more on an interest-bearing date are entitled to interest. (4) Deposits may be made conveniently. Practically every commercial bank has a savings department. Many savings banks, particularly in suburban districts, are open evenings to accommodate those who can not make deposits during the day. (5) Deposits can be withdrawn at any time they are needed. Some savings banks require that sixty days' notice be given before withdrawals are made, but almost without exception the banks are able to give a person his money at any time it is desired.

Disadvantage. The interest paid on deposits in savings banks is considerably higher than the interest paid on postal savings. In some cases, however, even this rate of interest is considered rather low since some other investments pay higher rates. 

Summary. Savings banks are recommended for those who wish to secure a high degree of safety, a reasonable rate of interest, and a convenient means of making deposits and withdrawals. Because of the safety of the investment, savings banks are especially attractive to those who have limited sums to invest and for whom safety is therefore especially important. Those who have larger sums of money to invest may wish to place part of them in investments which offer a higher rate of interest. But even such persons may wish to deposit part of their funds in savings banks because of the availability and safety of such investments.

Cooperative Associations. There are two kinds of cooperative associations which are commonly used by inves-

ch. 16] 241 

INVESTMENTS 

tors-cooperative savings banks and building and loan associations. 

Cooperative Savings Banks. A person may use a cooperative savings bank in several ways as a means of investment. The two most important are: First, an investor may deposit funds in a savings account. Such deposits are the same as the savings banks deposits previously discussed. 

Second, the investor may buy an interest in the bank.

The ownership of a cooperative savings bank is divided into shares. These shares can be purchased in small installments. A person who is saving small amounts regularly can buy one or more shares and pay for them over a considerable period of time.

The procedure followed in buying a share is about the same as that followed in making savings deposits. Usually a deposit ticket is prepared for each payment and the amount paid is entered in a pass book, the same as if a deposit were being made.

The income received by an owner from shares in a cooperative savings bank is known as dividends. The dividends usually amount to more than the interest on a savings deposit of the same amount.

Such an investment is reasonably safe, for savings banks are usually carefully managed and supervised by the state. But an investment in shares is not quite so safe as a savings deposit. A loss suffered by the bank is borne entirely by the owners before the depositors lose anything. 

For example, suppose a cooperative savings bank has ownership shares amounting to $500,000 and deposits of the same amount. If, for any reason, the bank should lose $100,000, this loss would be borne entirely by the owners who would lose one-fifth of their investment. The depositors, on the other hand, would lose nothing. ✓ Building and Loan Associations. Building and loan associations are also cooperative organizations. The ownership is represented by shares which can be purchased in small installments. The payment of an installment is handled much the same as a deposit in a savings bank.

[ch. 16 

Building and loan associations pay shareholders dividends greater than the interest paid on ordinary savings deposits. As in cooperative savings banks, an investment in shares is slightly less safe than a deposit in a savings bank. 

The funds of a building and loan association, as the name suggests, are largely loaned to those who wish to build or buy homes. Usually those borrowing from a building and loan association pay for shares by installments in order to accumulate funds to pay off the loan. 

Summary. Shares in cooperative savings banks and building and loan associations can be purchased in small installments. They offer a higher rate of income than ordinary savings deposits. Investments in shares are reasonably safe, but they are not quite so safe as deposits in savings banks. They are therefore recommended for those who wish a comparatively high rate of interest and who are willing to assume the additional risk.

1. Name at least three types of savings accounts. If you know of any local institutions in which savings may be invested, give their names and locations. 

2. What are the principal differences between a savings account in a bank and a checking account? 

3. J. C. Ruhl has both a savings account and a checking account in the Williamstown Exchange Bank. Why is it desirable for Mr. Ruhl to have the two accounts? 

4. Mr. Ruhl withdraws fifty dollars from his savings account. He is required to bring his pass book to the bank at the time this amount is withdrawn. On the same day he withdraws fifty dollars from his checking account. He is not required to bring his pass book at the time this withdrawal is made. Why is there this difference in procedure?

7. Name several advantages of depositing money in a building and loan association.

ch. 16] 243 

INVESTMENTS Section IV. STOCKS 

Introduction. In the preceding sections we have discussed satisfactory methods for one to use in investing small amounts as they are saved. Money which has been accumulated may be invested in the same ways, but there are also other methods which may be used for such amounts. 

Some of them will be discussed in this chapter. 

Investing in a Business. Two common methods of investing funds are, first, buying an interest in a business and, second, loaning to a business. 

A person may buy an interest in a business with the intention of being active in its management. The desirability of this kind of an investment depends on the person's skill and experience. If he is familiar with the type of business and is able to operate it successfully, a purchase of all or a part of the ownership may be very profitable.

Many people wish to share in the profits of business concerns, but do not wish to take part in the management of those businesses. Provision has been made for such investors. Businesses which are organized according to a special legal form and are known as corporations, divide their ownership into shares. These shares are offered for sale to the general public. A person may buy one or more shares as he wishes. His interest in the business is shown by a document known as a stock certificate; he is known as a stockholder. The stockholders do not directly manage the business, but they select those who are to manage it. 

The purchase of stock is available to everyone who has funds to invest. The number of shares available is tremendous as there are thousands of corporations and many of them have thousands of shares. For example, the ownership of the General Motors Corporation is divided into about 45,000,000 shares, and that of the American Telephone and 

Telegraph Company into about 13,000,000 shares. Stock can be purchased through many stock brokers and banks.

Value of Stocks. The value of a business depends on the amount of the profits it earns. As each share in a corpora-

[ch. 16 

tion represents a part of the total ownership, its value is therefore determined by the amount of the profits. Of course, the sale or market value of the stock is also affected by what people expect the company to earn in the future. CN335 100

Figure 106-Stock Certificate 

Par Value. When stock is issued, it is frequently given a value called par value. This is usually $100. Par value is sometimes confused with the actual value, although the two are generally very different. The par value is simply the value placed on the stock at one time and is seldom the same as the actual value. One stock with a par value of $100 may be worth $250, while another with the same par value may be worth only $2.50. The difference is caused by the difference in the earning power of the companies. 

Classes of Stocks. The stocks of a corporation may be divided into a number of classes, such as preferred, common, Class A, Class B, etc. These classes differ simply in details. 

All stocks represent a part of the ownership of the business, and all are valuable only as the business is profitable.

Desirability of Stocks for Investments. When a person buys a small business of which he is to be the sole owner,

ch. 16] 245 

INVESTMENTS 

he should know how much the business has earned in the past and how much it probably will earn for him. Similarly, when a person buys a share of stock, he should know how much the business is making, what his share of the earnings will be, and how likely it is that these earnings will be continued. If he does not have all this information, he should not buy the stock.

If stock is purchased, it is better to buy a few shares in a number of companies rather than a considerable number of shares in one company. Then, if one company is unsuccessful, the investor's income and capital will not be seriously affected.

1. Alva Green has four thousand dollars to invest. He has the opportunity to buy a half interest in a small retail business. What should he take into consideration before he makes this investment? 

2. What is a share of stock? 

3. Alva Green tells you he has a stock certificate. What does he mean by the term stock certificate? 

4. If Alva owns a stock certificate, is he a stockholder? 

5. Alva Green says his stock is worth seventy-five dollars a share. What determines this value? 

6. What is meant by the term par value? 

7. May the stock referred to in Problem 5 have a par value of one hundred dollars? 

8. If the par value of all the stock of a corporation is $100,000 and there are one thousand shares outstanding, what is the par value of each share? 

9. If the par value of a share of stock is fifty dollars and there are thirty thousand shares, what is the total par value of all stock? 

10. Is the owner of common stock the part owner of a business? Is the owner of preferred stock the part owner of a business? 

11. What information should one have about stock before he buys it? 

12. If a friend of yours had ten thousand dollars to invest in stock, would you advise him to buy ten thousand dollars' worth of stock in one corporation or one thousand dollars' worth of stock in ten corporations? Why?

[ch. 16 

 Section V. BONDS 

Introduction. If a person does not wish to buy an interest in a business, he may loan his funds to someone who will use them and will pay him interest for their use. 

As was explained in Chapter V, when a loan is made to an individual or a business, a note is usually given the lender by the borrower. The lender may want the note secured by a mortgage. A mortgage is a document which gives the lender the right to take over property of the borrower in case the principal or the interest or both are not paid when due.

Bonds. Large businesses may wish to borrow so much money that they cannot secure the entire amount from a few individuals. In such cases the loan is divided into a number of different parts. Each part is represented by a bond, a document which gives information about the loan and which shows the amount borrowed and the rate of interest to be paid. A bond is therefore much the same as a long-term note. 

For example, if the Danville Electric Light Company wishes to borrow $250,000 to make an addition to its plant, it may divide the loan into 250 parts, or bonds. In this case each bond will have the face value of $1000 and will represent one two hundred and fiftieth part of the entire loan. If the company prefers, the loan may be divided into a greater number of divisions, in which case each bond will have a smaller face value. For example, the loan of $250,000 may be divided into 2500 parts, in which case each bond will have a face value of $100.

Businesses that issue bonds usually do not sell them direct to investors. The bonds are ordinarily sold through banks, or companies which specialize in the sale of investment securities.

Information Given on a Bond. A bond is a written promise to repay a loan at a certain time and to pay interest on the loan until maturity. For example, each bond of the 

Danville Electric Light Company may state that one thou-

sand dollars will be paid to the holder in twenty years and in the meantime interest at 6 per cent will be paid semiannually.

In addition, the security back of a bond is usually described, at least in general terms. For example, the bonds of the Danville Electric Light Company state that they are secured by a first mortgage on the entire plant of the company. This means that if either the interest or the principal of the bonds is not paid, the bondholders may take over the plant. The individual bondholders do not have to act in such a case, as a trustee is appointed to act for them. 

Value of a Bond. The par value, or the amount which will be repaid the bondholder when the bond is due, may be almost any sum, but it usually is $1000, $500, $100, or $50. 

The face value or par value of the bond and the actual value are usually not exactly the same. Two things cause the actual value to differ from the face value. They are the interest rate and the safety.

For example, if the United States Government issues certain bonds bearing 414 per cent interest and others bearing only 4 per cent interest, the former sell at a higher price, for, obviously, everyone prefers to have the higher rate of interest. Also, if the government issues bonds at 

4 per cent and a business issues bonds at the same rate, the United States Government bonds are likely to sell at a higher price because people feel more confident that the government will repay its debts.

Selecting Bonds. The making of satisfactory investments in bonds is extremely difficult. Tremendous sums are lost each year because people buy securities which are later found to be of little or no value. This is usually caused by the fact that individuals buy the securities without having full information about them. When one buys a bond, he is lending a business money. He should therefore know as much about the loan and about the possibility of its being repaid as if he were loaning money to some individual who would use it in operating a business.

ch. 16] 249 

INVESTMENTS 

Many people are not in a position to obtain full information about bonds for themselves. If they do not know a bond dealer from whom they can obtain reliable information, they probably should not buy bonds but should invest their funds in a safer manner.

CHAPTER XVII  Section I. FIRE INSURANCE 

Reason for Insurance. In preceding chapters we have observed that a thrifty person seeks a safe investment for his savings. He wishes a fair rate of income, but he is most anxious for an investment which will be safe.

But a person usually does not deposit all his money in savings banks or similar institutions. Ordinarily, he purchases some property. For example, he may buy a home or an automobile. Can such purchases be made as safe as the investments discussed in the preceding chapter? The home may be destroyed by fire or a windstorm; the automobile may be stolen, burned, or destroyed in a collision. How, then, can one safely own property of these kinds? 

To avoid loss, one usually insures his property with a company organized for the purpose of distributing risk. If the property is damaged or destroyed according to the terms of the agreement, the company reimburses the insured for the loss. This removes the risk from the ownership of property and makes an investment in property as safe as the investments discussed heretofore. 

Principle of Insurance. Suppose the head of each family in your city owned a house. If one building burned, the owner would lose its value while all the others would lose nothing. As it is possible that any building may burn, each person would be in danger of losing his entire investment. 

The danger of a great loss for one person might be lessened if all agreed to pay a share of the loss when any building burned. Each one would then have a greater risk of losing something, but would be freed from the risk of losing the entire value of his building. The agreement would, then, distribute the loss, not prevent it. 252

Figure 108-Fire Insurance Policy

[ch. 17 

Similarly, insurance distributes loss. A company insures the property of a great number of people and agrees to pay each person if his property is damaged or destroyed. 

To show that the property is insured, the company gives the insured a contract known as a policy. It collects from him a certain amount each year which is known as the premium. The premiums are based on past experience and are made high enough so that the company can pay all losses. 

A person does not avoid expense by carrying an insurance policy, for each premium is an expense, but he does avoid the danger of a great loss at one time.

Fire Insurance. One of the most common forms of property insurance is fire insurance. Policies providing protection from loss by fire are written by a large number of companies. It is not necessary for you to be familiar with the details of these policies, but you should be familiar with three features of the policies: first, the need for an insurable interest; second, the necessity of proof of loss; and, third, the coinsurance clause. 

Insurable Interest. Only a person or persons who own property which might be damaged by fire can insure the property against fire. The interest which can be protected by insurance is known as the insurable interest. 

The insurable interest must exist at the time of loss or the policy is of no value. Suppose Samuel Storm owned a house which was insured for ten thousand dollars under a policy expiring on August 1. If he sold the property on 

June 1, his insurable interest ceased at that time. If the property were destroyed by fire on July 1, Mr. Storm could not collect any insurance.

When a person's insurable interest ceases before a policy expires, or if for any reason he wishes to cancel this policy, he may be able to secure a refund from the insurance company for a part of the premium paid. 

Proof of Loss. If insured property is damaged or destroyed by fire, the insurance company reimburses the

ch. 17] 255 

policyholder for the loss up to the amount of the policy. 

This requires that the amount of the loss be proved in some way. Ordinarily a sworn statement of the insured is accepted as proof of the loss.

But if the insured is to prove the amount of the loss, he must have some method of determining how much the loss actually is. He must know the value of each article damaged. This requires that he know, first, the cost of the article and, second, the time that it has been owned. The time is important, for property is ordinarily less valuable the older it becomes because it gradually wears out. Its value at the time it is injured by fire is estimated by deducting from the original value the estimated decrease in value because of time or use. This decrease is known as depreciation.

The value of the goods damaged is found most easily if a list is kept, showing all items, the cost of each, and the time each was purchased. Such a list is known as an inventory. In Figure 109 is shown part of an inventory of household goods. This covers the furniture in two rooms. 

The complete inventory would have a similar list for each room. It would also include clothing and everything else of value covered by the policy. An inventory of this kind should be revised occasionally, perhaps once a year, so it will be up to date and will include new items purchased. 

The need for an inventory is shown by the following example. Oliver Copper insured his household goods for four thousand dollars. One night a fire completely destroyed his home and all of the furniture. Mr. Copper was required by the insurance company to make a statement showing the amount of his loss. If he had had no inventory, he probably would have completely forgotten many small items which were destroyed. He would not have known the exact cost of other items and might not have remembered when they were purchased. It would then have been very difficult for him to make a correct statement. Perhaps, because of the many items forgotten, Mr. Copper's statement would not have covered his entire loss.

Figure 109-Part of an Inventory of Household Goods 

Since Mr. Copper had an inventory of his household goods, it was easy for him to give an exact statement of his loss and to secure a satisfactory settlement from the insurance company.

ch. 17] 257 

 Coinsurance Clause. The ordinary fire insurance policy provides protection for an amount not greater than the face value of the policy. If a house worth $10,000 is insured for $5000, the insurance company pays the owner for any loss not greater than $5000.

Fire insurance policies for ordinary business property, but not for homes and their furnishings, may be secured at a lower rate if they contain a clause known as a coinsurance clause. This clause specifies that if property is not insured up to a certain per cent of its value, the owner of the property is considered to be bearing a part of the insurance and loses his share in case of fire. The 90 per cent coinsurance clause is common. When the policy contains this clause, the policyholder agrees to maintain insurance for at least 90 per cent of the value of the property. If he fails to do so, he is considered to be a coinsurer for the difference between the amount of the policy and 90 per cent of the value of the property.

Under the 90 per cent coinsurance clause if property valued at $10,000 is insured at $9000, or 90 per cent of its value, it is insured for the full amount agreed by the policyholder. If it is damaged by fire to any amount not exceeding $9000, the insurance company pays the owner the full amount of the loss.

On the other hand, if property worth $10,000 is insured for only $4500, the insured is considered to be carrying half of the insurance himself, since $4500 is one-half of 90 per cent of the value of the property. In this case, if the property is damaged to the extent of $5000, the policyholder receives only $2500 from the insurance company.

8. H. H. McKay has an office in a large office building. 

He carries insurance on his office furniture purchased two years ago for $950. A fire destroys the building in which 

Mr. McKay's office is located and his furniture is lost. Although he has a list of the articles of furniture and can prove how much he paid for each, he receives only $750 from the insurance company. Why does he not receive the full cost price? 

9. On January 2, H. H. McKay took out an insurance policy on his home for twelve thousand dollars. The policy was to run for one year. 

On December 1 Mr. McKay sold his home. On December 29, four days before the expiration of his policy, the house burned. Could Mr. McKay collect the insurance? 

10. What is meant by the term coinsurance clause? 

11. Ira Foreman insures his household goods for three thousand dollars. His home is destroyed by fire. He does not remember the exact cost of any of his goods nor can he remember all of the different items. He feels sure, however, that the total cost was about three thousand dollars. 

Does he need additional information? Why? 

12. Ira Foreman has a building worth twenty thousand dollars insured for nine thousand dollars under a policy containing a 90 per cent coinsurance clause. The building is damaged by fire to the extent of one thousand dollars. How much insurance can he collect?

ch. 17] 259 

 Section II. AUTOMOBILE INSURANCE 

Introduction. Another common type of insurance is automobile insurance. This insurance provides reimbursement for loss by fire, theft, or damage to one's own automobile; damage to the automobiles or property of others; or injury to other persons. 

Fire and Theft Insurance. Fire and theft insurance provides reimbursement for loss if an automobile is damaged or destroyed by fire or lightning, or if it is stolen. As losses frequently occur for these reasons, these types of automobile insurance are very common. 

If a car is damaged by fire or is stolen, the amount of reimbursement that is paid to the insured is not necessarily the cost of the car. It is rather the estimated value at the time of the loss.

For example, suppose that a new automobile costing $1,000 is insured against loss from fire and theft. If it is stolen within a short time, the insured may receive almost as much as the cost value, perhaps $900. If, on the other hand, it is stolen ten months after its purchase the amount received may be much less, perhaps $600, since the insurance company will pay only the original value less the estimated decrease in value since the purchase. 

Collision Insurance. Collision insurance provides reimbursement for damage to one's car by collision with other objects or by an upset. Various types of policies are available, some of which provide complete protection, and others, protection with certain deductions. If a person carries a policy of this kind, he should study it carefully so that he will know the exact extent of his protection and the claims which he can make for reimbursement. 

Property Damage Insurance. This type of insurance pro tects one from claims arising from damage the car may cause to the property of other people. This insurance not only is valuable as a protection, but it also is a great convenience in relieving a person of the necessity of making adjust-

ch. 17] 261 

ments with another person if there is an accident in which the other person's car is damaged. If one who is protected by property damage insurance does injure another person's car, it is not necessary for him to negotiate with the other person and to reach an agreement with him concerning who is to blame for the accident. The matter must be turned over to the insurance company, which will reach an agreement with the other person and will pay the claim, if that is necessary.

Public Liability Insurance. This type of insurance pro tects one from claims arising from injury caused another person by his car. This type of protection is especially desirable for two reasons. (1) If a person driving a car injures another, the injured party may claim a large amount, perhaps thousands of dollars, as damages. If the driver has public liability insurance, the insurance company will take care of the negotiations and any possible law suit, and will pay the amount of the claim up to the face of the policy. Policies can be secured for practically any amount, so a person can be fully protected from loss. (2) If a person driving a car does not have a large amount of money, he may not be able to pay a damage claim if it is charged against him. In such a case, it is only fair that the person carry public liability insurance so that if he should injure any one, the injured party will be able to secure reimbursement for the damages. 

Other Insurance Policies. The insurance policies dis cussed in the preceding paragraphs are typical of the many kinds of policies that can be secured to protect a person from loss of property and claims for damage. They serve to illustrate the principle that property may be lost, destroyed, or stolen, but that the owner can carry insurance to make the loss less serious to him. A thrifty person will always see that his property is fully insured. If he is in doubt regarding the insurance to be carried, he will discuss this matter with a representative of an insurance company, in whom he has confidence.

 LABORATORY PROBLEMS 

1. Several buildings, each worth $10,000, are insured against fire under policies which do not contain coinsurance clauses. How much should the insurance company pay the owner of the property in each of the following situations? (a) The property is insured for $10,000; the loss is $3000. (b) The property is insured for $7000; the loss is $4000. (c) The property is insured for $6000; the loss is $10,000. 

2. Several buildings, each worth $10,000, are insured against fire under policies which contain a 90 per cent coinsurance clause. How much should the insurance company pay the owner of the property in each of the following situations? (a) The property is insured for $9000; the loss is $3000. (b) The property is insured for $6000; the loss is $3000. (c) The property is insured for $6000; the loss is $6000. (d) The property is insured for $5000; the loss is $10,000.

(b) The property is insured for $6000; the loss is $4000. (c) The property is insured for $6000; the loss is $8000. (d) The property is insured for $4000; the loss is $10,000.

(a) The property is insured for $6000 under a policy without a coinsurance clause and is damaged by fire to the extent of $8000. (b) The property is insured for $6000 under a policy without a coinsurance clause and is damaged by fire to the extent of $3000. (c) The property is insured for $6000 under a policy having a 90 per cent coinsurance clause and is damaged to the extent of $4000. (d) The property is insured for $6000 under a policy having an 80 per cent coinsurance clause and is damaged to the extent of $3000.

CHAPTER XVIII LIFE INSURANCE Section I. INTRODUCTION 

Need for Life Insurance. As we observed in the preceding chapter, property insurance divides the risk of loss of property among a great number of people. Similarly, life insurance divides among a great number the economic loss caused by death.

Insurance as a Means of Protection. James Howard, who is twenty-one years of age, earns thirty dollars a week. His parents are not dependent upon him for support, but within a few years they may be partially dependent upon him. If he lives, he will be able to give them any financial assistance needed; but if he dies within a few years, he will not leave them much money for he has been working only a short time. He therefore desires to take out an insurance policy made payable to his parents. This will be a protection for them, for in the event of his death it will provide them with the money which he otherwise would have earned and given them. 

Insurance as a Means of Saving. James finds that policies offered by standard insurance companies have a cash surrender value. In other words, after they have been carried for a few years, they can be surrendered by the insured and a cash refund will be made by the insurance company. 

This cash surrender value increases from year to year. 

Thus, an insurance policy provides protection for the dependents of the insured and at the same time assists him in accumulating savings.

The cash surrender value of an insurance policy may not equal the amount that would have been accumulated if the premiums had been invested in other ways. On the other hand, if an insurance policyholder should die 266

ch. 18] 267 

LIFE INSURANCE 

shortly after taking out a policy, the beneficiaries, those to whom the policy would be payable, would receive the full amount of the policy; if the same amounts had been deposited in a bank or invested in other ways, the sum accumulated would have been much smaller.

Determining the Amount of Life Insurance. The amount of insurance which a person takes out is usually determined by the income which he wishes to leave to his dependents. 

For example, James Howard may decide that he should provide an income of $10 a week, or $520 a year, for his parents. If he assumes that money can be invested to earn 6 per cent, he will need an insurance policy for $8666.67. This amount invested at 6 per cent will give an income of $520 a year. Policies are not usually written for odd sums, so 

James will probably take a policy for an even amount, such as $8500.

Insurable Interest. A person may insure his own life and the life of any other person in whom he has an insurable interest. To be an insurable interest, there must be some kind of advantage from the continued life of the insured. 

For example, you may have an insurable interest in the lives of your parents, upon whom you are depending for support, but you would not have an insurable interest in the life of a stranger.

The insurable interest must exist at the time the policy is taken out, but it need not exist at the time of loss. In the latter respect, it differs from fire insurance. For example, two partners in a business may have an insurable interest in each other because the success of the business may depend upon the continued life of each partner. Each can then take out a life insurance policy on the other. These policies continue in operation even though the partnership is discontinued and the former partners no longer have a financial interest in each other's life.

[ch. 18 

  

1. Do property insurance and life insurance prevent loss? If not, in what way do they furnish protection? 

2. David Brittling is about twenty-two years of age. He is earning a good salary, part of which he saves each week. 

He considers buying life insurance but decides not to. He has no one dependent on him for support, so he sees no need for insurance. Do you think he is making a wise decision? Why? 

3. Would the situation in the preceding problem be any different if three people were dependent on David Brittling for support? 

4. What is meant by cash surrender value? Is the cash surrender value of a policy greater or less after the policy has been carried ten years than it was when the policy had been carried five years? 

5. Each year Henry Bane places one hundred dollars in a savings bank where his deposits earn four per cent. His brother, George, takes out a life insurance policy on which the premium is one hundred dollars a year. (a) At the end of five years, will Henry's savings be more or less than the cash surrender value of George's insurance policy? (b) Which plan of saving do you recommend? Why? 

6. James Hall decides that he should take out an insurance policy. How should he determine the amount of insurance that he should purchase? 

7. James Hall wishes to take out insurance policies on the life of his father; on the life of his uncle, who is paying his college expenses; on the life of the grocer, from whom his family purchases groceries; and on the life of the mayor of his city. Can he take out these policies? 

8. L. J. Ross and R. E. Montgomery are partners in the operation of a retail store. They own the building used for store purposes. Each takes out a life insurance policy on the life of the other, and together they take out a fire insurance policy to protect their interest in the store. 

After two years Mr. Ross sells his interest in the building and in the store to Mr. Montgomery. May Mr. Ross continue the life insurance policy on the life of Mr. Montgomery? May he continue to have a share in the fire insurance policy on the store building?

ch. 18] 269 

LIFE INSURANCE Section II. CLASSES OF POLICIES 

Introduction. The life insurance policies which are commonly recommended are of four different classes; namely, term, ordinary life, limited-payment life, and endowment. 

Each of these policies has certain advantages and disadvantages. In each case a person gets what he pays for, and no more. The choice between the policies depends, then, upon the protection one needs and the benefits one wishes to procure from an insurance policy. 

Term Insurance Policies. Term insurance is insurance purchased for a limited period of time, such as one or five years. If the insured dies during that period, the beneficiary, the one to whom the policy is payable on the death of the insured, receives the amount of the policy. At the end of the period, the policy expires and has no cash value. 

The rates for term insurance vary according to the age of the insured; they increase as one becomes older. 

A term policy is chiefly recommended for one who knows that for some special reason he will need protection for a short time only. If a person expects to take out a policy which he will carry for a number of years, he will not be interested in term insurance as the increasing rates will make the cost too great in the future. Furthermore, since a term policy does not accumulate a cash surrender value, it is not recommended for anyone who wishes his insurance policy to increase his savings.

Ordinary Life Insurance Policies. In an ordinary life insurance policy, the premium is a fixed amount and is the same each year during the life of the insured. At his death the face of the policy is payable to his estate or to the beneficiary.

In considering the desirability of an ordinary life insurance policy, one must determine the cost and the returns. In the table given on page 271 the premiums of one insurance company for several policies are given for the ages eighteen to twenty-two. The premiums on each policy do not change from year to year, but the premiums on different

Twenty-Two on $1000 Policies policies vary according to the age when the person takes out the insurance. The younger a person is when he takes out his policy, the lower is the rate. The rates are about the same for all companies. They vary slightly according to the benefits offered by the different companies. 

It will be observed that the ordinary life insurance policies are cheaper than the other policies in this table. This is a decided advantage if one wishes to keep the cost as low as possible. There are, however, two offsetting disadvantages. First, this policy must be paid throughout the life of the insured. In the other policies, as will be explained later, the premiums are paid for a certain number of years only. Second, the cash surrender value of the policy is less than that of some of the other policies. A comparison of the amounts that may be secured on the surrender of different policies is made on page 276. 

Limited-Payment Life Insurance Policies. Limited-pay ment life insurance policies are the same as the ordinary life insurance policies, except that the premiums are paid for only a limited number of years. Since the premiums are paid for only a limited number of years and not during one's entire life, the premiums are somewhat higher than those of the ordinary life policy. Under this plan, policies which require payments for different numbers of years can be secured. Of course, the more premiums one contracts to pay, the smaller is each premium. A typical limited-payment

[ch. 18 

life policy is one requiring payments for twenty years. This is known as a twenty-payment life insurance policy. The rates for this policy are given in the table on page 271. 

Limited-payment life insurance policies are more expensive than ordinary life insurance policies, but they have the advantage of not requiring payments during one's old age. 

If one is uncertain about his future income, the limitedpayment life policy is preferable to the ordinary life policy. 

As will be explained later, the limited-payment life policy increases its cash surrender value more rapidly than the ordinary life policy.

Endowment Policies. Endowment life insurance policies are similar to limited-payment life insurance policies in that the premiums have to be paid for only a fixed number of years, such as ten, fifteen, or twenty years. They differ, however, in that the face of the limited-payment life insurance policy is payable only on the death of the insured, while the face value of the endowment policy is payable on the death of the insured or at the maturity of the policy. 

For example, if one secures a ten-year endowment policy for one thousand dollars, this amount is paid to the beneficiary in the event of the insured's death within ten years. 

If the person who secures the policy is living at the end of ten years, the face value of the policy is paid to him. 

The cost of an endowment policy is more than that of any other type discussed in this chapter, because the insurance company must, on the average, make payment at an earlier date. The table on page 271 shows the amounts charged for ten-year, fifteen-year, and twenty-year endowments.

In this group the ten-year endowment policies are the most expensive and the twenty-year policies the least expensive. 

Premiums for Different Ages. It will be observed that in each of the policies the amount of the premium depends on the age when the insurance policy was issued. For example, if a person secures an ordinary life insurance policy at the age of eighteen, he will, according to the table on page 271, pay only $12.93 each year for each thousand dol-

ch. 18] 273 

LIFE INSURANCE 

lars of the policy. If, on the other hand, he waits until he is nineteen, the cost will be $13.20 a thousand, or 27 cents higher. As the cost of insurance continues to increase, it is desirable for one to purchase his insurance policies as early as possible. 

Disability Insurance. Insurance companies have policies, in addition to the regular life insurance policies, which will provide a person almost any protection desired. Insurance protecting one from loss because of sickness, accident, or permanent disability can be secured. If a person does not have rather large resources, it is especially desirable for him to purchase insurance which will provide him a personal income in the event that he is disabled because of accident or sickness and cannot support himself in his regular occupation. A monthly income, payable to the insured in the event of permanent disability, is frequently made one of the provisions of a life insurance policy. 

Participating Insurance Policies. Many companies issue what are known as participating insurance policies. When one has a participating policy, he participates or shares in the profits of the company. The one insured under such a policy pays his premiums the same as the one insured under a non-participating policy. The difference between the policies lies in the fact that companies issuing participating policies distribute a part of the earnings, known as dividends, at the end of each year. Age

These dividends may be withdrawn by the insured, deducted from the future premiums, or left with the company. 

If they are left with the company, they will earn interest and the amount of both the dividends and the interest will be added to the policy and will be available when the policy is surrendered or the insured dies. The rates for dividends for one company issuing participating policies are given on page 273. While a fixed amount of dividends is not guaranteed, the past record of the company indicates that the previous returns probably will be continued. 

The premiums for participating policies are higher than those for non-participating policies, but this is offset by the dividends which are paid in the future. In general, the cost of the two kinds of insurance is about the same. 

1. John Helmund took out an insurance policy when he was twenty years of age. Four years later he took out another policy of the same kind with the same company. The premiums on the second policy were greater than those on the first. Can you give a reason why that was true?

(b) Is this kind of insurance cheaper than other kinds? (c) Under what circumstances would you recommend that Harold take out a term insurance policy?

Which is the less expensive? (c) Which policy will Harold probably prefer?

(a) In what way does a twenty-payment life insurance policy differ from an ordinary life policy? (b) Which of the two policies is more expensive? (c) Under what circumstances would you consider a twenty-payment life insurance policy preferable to an ordinary life insurance policy?

[ch. 18 

 Section III. INSURANCE AS A MEANS OF SAVING 

Increase in Value of Policies. As has been suggested, policies have a cash surrender value and are therefore a means of accumulating savings. For example, if an ordinary life insurance policy is taken out at the age of twentyone, it will, according to the table on page 271, cost $13.77 for each thousand dollars. In twenty years the policy will have cost $275.40 ($13.77 x 20 = $275.40). The cash surrender value at that time will be $181.94. If one surrenders his policy at that time, he will then receive all but $93.46 of the total premiums paid (total premiums, $275.40 cash surrender value, $181.94 — $93.46). 

The cost of the policy for twenty years appears to be only $93.46, but one must take into consideration the interest which might have been earned if the premiums had been invested instead of being paid to the insurance company. The total of this interest and the premiums is the real cost of protection.

Similarly, the cost of other insurance policies can be measured. The table given below shows the amount of the premiums paid on six different policies, the cash surrender value of each at the end of twenty years, and the difference between the total premiums and the cash surrender value. 

$1000 POLICIES, AGE TWENTY-ONE FOR A PERIOD OF TWENTY YEARS 

Premiums 

Cash Surrender 

Value at end of period Difference

NON-PARTICIPATING PARTICIPATING 

Ordinary 20-Paym't 20-Year Ordinary 20-Paym't 20-Year

Life Endowm't Life Life Endowm't 

Life $275.40 $421.20 $806.00 $350.40 $510.80 $904.20 

181.94 418.69 1,000.00 347.32 628.00 1,275.00 $93.46 $2.51 $194.00 $3.08 $117.20 $370.80

The table is for policies amounting to $1000, taken at the age of twenty-one, and carried for twenty years. Of course, different kinds of policies taken at different ages would have different amounts and would result in different costs and values. The ones given here, however, are typical. 

The cost of a non-participating ordinary life policy is the least of any of the six illustrated. The cash surrender

ch. 18] 277 

LIFE INSURANCE 

value is also small, making the net cost, as stated before, $93.46 plus the interest which might otherwise have been earned.

The non-participating twenty-payment life policy costs somewhat more than the ordinary life, but the cash surrender value at the end of twenty years is almost equal to the amount of the premiums. As indicated in the table, one will receive all but $2.51 of the amount paid in premiums if he cancels his policy at the end of twenty years. It appears, then, that the cost of the twenty-payment life policy is actually less than the cost of the ordinary life policy since the cash surrender value is so nearly the same as the amount of the premiums. The insured has, however, paid larger sums as premiums and has therefore lost more interest since these sums might have been invested to earn interest. 

The actual cost of the two kinds of insurance is therefore about the same.

In the twenty-year endowment, the value at the end of twenty years is $194 greater than the amount of the premiums. One has, then, not only been insured for twenty years, but his investment in the form of premiums has earned for him $194. It then appears that there has been no cost for insurance, but there actually has been some cost since the premiums, if invested for a period of twenty years, would have earned more interest than $194. 

In the participating policies given in the table, it is assumed that the dividends are not withdrawn but are added to the value of the policy. It will be observed that the cash surrender value of the ordinary life policy is about the same as the total premiums, and that the values of the twenty-payment life and twenty-year endowment policies are greater than the amounts of the premiums. In each case, however, there is an actual cost of insurance which arises from the interest that might have been earned. 

In the majority of cases, an endowment policy is withdrawn at its maturity. Such policies are ordinarily sold to those who wish to use insurance policies as a means of accumulating savings. It is not assumed nor recommended

[ch. 18 

that other policies be surrendered. It is rather supposed that these policies will be permitted to continue until the death of the insured. In the table given on page 276, however, the cash surrender values of the different policies have been indicated to illustrate that life insurance is a valuable means of accumulating savings.

CHAPTER XIX BUSINESS CORRESPONDENCE Introduction. 

Section I. OPENING MAIL 

Many modern businesses receive large quantities of mail daily. The volume varies, not only according to the size of the business, but also according to its nature. A successful mail-order house receives a tremendous number of letters because its orders for merchandise are received in that form. A department store with an equal volume of sales receives fewer letters, for practically all of its sales are made to those who visit its place of business and give their orders orally. 

Figure 114-Opening Mail 

In this office the mail of the Veterans Bureau, Washington, D. C., is opened and sorted. Thousands of letters are handled each day. 

Need for Care in Handling Mail. In the average business, important letters are received every day and in almost 281

[ch. 19 

every mail. One letter may contain an order for a large amount of merchandise; another may include a remittance of a large sum of money; and a third may contain a question about the merchandise or services of the company, which, when properly answered, may secure a new customer and thus increase the volume of sales. 

Since important letters are received frequently, a business usually establishes a definite procedure for handling incoming mail. This prevents the loss of letters and insures that each letter will be taken care of promptly and correctly. 

Opening Letters. When the mail is received from the post office, it is given to one person or department to be opened. In a large business, the incoming mail department may employ scores of people. In a small business one person may in a few minutes open all the mail received in a day. In both instances, however, one person or group of persons should be responsible for the opening of mail. The same procedure can then be followed in all cases, and if errors are made, their cause can be found and removed. 

Figure 115-Letter-Opening Machine 

Each letter should be opened carefully so that its contents will not be damaged. Machines can be purchased which cut a narrow strip off one side of the envelope. (See 

Figure 115.) In most businesses, however, the mail is opened

ch. 19] 283 

BUSINESS CORRESPONDENCE 

by hand. Each letter is laid flat on the desk with the address side down. Then a paper knife or letter opener is inserted under the top flap at one corner and the envelope is cut all the way across the top. In order to make certain that everything is removed from the envelope, some firms also have the envelopes cut at the two ends. 

If one is careful in opening envelopes, he will seldom injure any of their contents. When one of the inclosures is accidentally injured, it should be repaired with transparent tape which can be obtained for this purpose. 

Address and Signature. As soon as the contents are removed from an envelope, and before the envelope is thrown away, the one who opens the mail should examine the letter to make certain that the address and the signature of the writer are complete. Sometimes the address, the writer's name, or both are omitted from the letter but are given in the return address on the envelope. In such cases much time is saved if the omission is discovered while the envelope is on the clerk's desk.

PROBLEMS FOR CLASS DISCUSSION 

1. A certain business having a large amount of incoming mail has the mail opened by several different persons. 

Anyone may help with the work who happens to have spare time. Why is this not a good plan? 

2. Four businesses have the same volume of sales. List them in the order of the probable volume of incoming mail. 

They are: a department store; a chain store system selling for cash; a mail-order business; and a clothing factory which sells to retailers. 

3. Explain why great care should be taken in handling incoming mail. 

4. Describe how letters should be opened when they are opened by hand. 

5. Elsie Copeland, a mail clerk, is opening mail by hand. 

She accidentally cuts a check in two. How should the check be repaired? 

6. Explain why the address and the signature on a letter should be noted before the envelope is thrown away.

[ch. 19 

 Section II. READING AND SORTING MAIL 

Reading Incoming Mail. After an envelope is opened and the address and the signature are checked, the one who opens the mail should carefully but rapidly read the letter. As he reads, he should note (1) references to inclosures, (2) references to mail to be received under separate cover, and (3) the department or person who should reply. 

Inclosures. All references to inclosures in a letter should be carefully checked with the papers actually inclosed. If a letter refers to a paper or business form which is not inclosed, a note regarding this should be placed on the letter. 

Then the one answering the letter will know that the intended inclosure was omitted by mistake and will be able to write his letter properly. 

Check not inclosed.

With this letter we are inclosing our check for one hundred dollars. 

Will you please acknowledge receipt of this amount, and send us a statement showing the balance of our account after credit has been given for this remittance. 

M Black

JMD:LS ENC. 

Very truly yours,

DOLBEY AND SON 

XmDolbey

Figure 116-Letter with a Note to Indicate the Omission of an Intended Inclosure 

For example, suppose a letter is received in which the statement is made that a check for one hundred dollars is inclosed. If the check is not actually received with the letter, the opener should write a note on the letter calling attention to the omission. (See Figure 116.) The one replying can then call the error to the attention of the writer of the original letter, and the matter can be adjusted without difficulty. If, on the other hand, the one opening the letter does not observe that the check was omitted and makes no

ch. 19] 285 

BUSINESS CORRESPONDENCE 

note of the omission on the letter, the one replying to the letter will not know what action to take. He will not know whether the check was omitted by mistake, whether it was lost after the letter was opened, or whether it was referred to some other person in the office. He will be uncertain how he should repły, and valuable time will be lost while an investigation is being made.

Disposing of Inclosures. In many instances an inclosure and the letter are referred to the same person. In such cases the inclosure is simply pinned or clipped to the letter and is sent with the letter to the person who is to answer it. 

An inclosure may be intended for one person or department and the letter for another. A letter inquiring about certain new goods may be accompanied by an order for merchandise, and a payment on account. The letter may be referred to the sales department, the order to the order department, and the remittance to the accounting department. 

The one who answers the letter should know that the inclosures were received and properly taken care of so that he can acknowledge their receipt in his reply. The one who opens the mail should therefore write on the letter the disposition of each inclosure which is not left with the letter. 

This may be done in writing, or partly with a rubber stamp and partly in writing. (See Figure 117.)

Mail under Separate Cover. The one opening mail should observe, as he reads each letter, whether reference is made to a package which is being sent under a separate cover. 

Such a package may include a catalog, a book, a photograph, a sample of merchandise, or some other item which should be referred to the one who receives the letter. 

The package may be addressed to the company and thus may not have anything on it to indicate its purpose. It should, however, be referred to the same person who received the letter. A record should therefore be kept of packages which are expected. This should show the nature of the article, the name and address of the one mailing it, the date of the letter, the person or department to whom it should be referred, and the date on which the package was received. A satisfactory form is shown in Figure 118.

At the time the letter is received, an entry is made filling all spaces on one line except the space for the date on which the package is delivered. When the package is received, the date is entered. If the package is not received within a reasonable length of time, the person who opens the mail calls this to the attention of the one to whom the letter was referred. This person can then investigate to learn whether the package actually was not sent or whether it was lost.

Time of Receipt of Mail. In business, time is always important. Delays may greatly inconvenience customers and may cause serious losses. A business therefore fixes responsibility for delays in handling mail by having each 

Figure 119-Stamping Letters for Time letter stamped with the time it is received. A time stamp (Figure 119) usually prints the year, month, day, hour, and minute that the letter is opened. Then, if the letter is not answered for several days, the extent of the delay is known and the reason for it can be determined. 

Sorting and Distributing 

Mail. After the procedure described in the preceding paragraphs has been completed, the letter is referred to a department or to a person for reply. The division of the mail is governed largely by the organization of the business. Ordinarily there will be a sales department, an order department, an accounting department, and probably several others.

The mail clerk determines to whom each letter should be referred and then places it in a basket or sorting compart-

BUSINESS CORRESPONDENCE Section III. BUSINESS LETTERS 

Importance of Letters. Business letters are of great importance. In fact, success may largely depend on the quality and appearance of letters. 

If a person applies for a position by mail, whether he gets the position may depend almost entirely on his letter. 

Or, if a person attempts to sell merchandise by mail, to get information, to adjust a misunderstanding, or to accomplish any one of many different purposes, the quality of his letter will directly affect his success. Almost everyone completes many transactions by letter; frequently these transactions involve large sums of money. Special attention should therefore be given to this phase of business. 

Need for Care in Writing Letters. A successful letter is one that is carefully planned and written. Much thought is required if a letter is to be most effective. There are several reasons why this is true:

First, a letter usually has to compete with other letters. 

Suppose that you apply for a position by letter. Probably your application will be considered together with many others. If it is to be successful, it must catch the interest and attention of the reader and must convince him that you are qualified for the position.

Similarly, if a business solicits orders by mail, its letters will directly compete with the many letters written by other concerns. A letter which is not well written probably will not increase the profits of the business by increasing sales. 

On the other hand, writing such a letter will increase the expense of the business, for this letter requires valuable time in addition to the stationery and postage.

Second, it is difficult for one who is writing to make his meaning entirely clear. When he is talking to another, he makes his meaning clear not only by the words used, but also by his tone of voice and expression. If an explanation is not understood, additional information can be given. 

In a letter, on the other hand, success depends on the words used. Additional words cannot be added if the reader does not understand or if he is not convinced that he should
