Figure 122-Typewritten Business Letter 

Attractiveness. If a business letter is to be effective, it must have an attractive appearance. This requires that it

[ch. 19 

be written or typed on a grade of stationery appropriate for business correspondence.

Business stationery need not be expensive, though it should be of a reasonably good grade. It should be of standard business size, that is, 8½ x 11 inches. Colored stationery is sometimes used, but white is much more common. White is always recommended for the business letters of an individual. Distinctiveness may be given to stationery by the use of color, but usually this is considered undesirable, for the color selected may not be attractive to the one to whom the letter is written.

Good stationery alone is not enough to make the appearance satisfactory. The letter must also be neatly written or typed and properly arranged.

Arrangement. Custom has largely fixed the form of business letters. One can vary the form as he pleases but too much variation is likely to create an unfavorable impression. It is recommended that a person adopt a standard form and follow it in all letter-writing. This will make the arrangement largely a matter of habit and will leave one's attention free for the composition of the letter. 

Two satisfactory forms are shown in Figures 121 and 122 on pages 290 and 291. The first form is recommended for pen-written letters, and the second for typewritten. In general, the styles are the same. Comments on the differences are made as each part is discussed. 

Parts of a Letter. The parts of the business letter are: (1) the heading, (2) the introductory address, (3) the salutation, (4) the body of the letter, (5) the complimentary close, and (6) the signature.

The Heading. The headings of the two letters are: 

908 Center Street 

Baltimore, Maryland 

March 10, 193June 14, 193-

ch. 19] 293 

BUSINESS CORRESPONDENCE 

The first heading contains the complete address of the writer and the date, while the second contains the date only. The reason for this is that the second is written on a sheet of paper on which the name and the address of the company are printed. A sheet of paper printed in this way is known as a letterhead. If a letterhead were used for the pen-written letter, the address would not be written. 

No punctuation is needed at the end of a line in the heading unless the line ends with an abbreviation, in which case a period is used. A comma is placed between the name of the city and the name of the state, and between the day of the month and the year.

Each line after the first in the pen-written heading is slightly indented. If this letter had been typed, this indented style might have been used, or the lines after the first might have been written without indentation. 

The Introductory Address. The addresses of the two letters are: 

Mr. S. M. Holladay 

The Midland Refining Co. 

Des Moines, Sowa 

The C. J. Krehbiel Co. 

1032 Broadway 

Cincinnati, Ohio

The address is placed a little below the heading, at the left side of the page. In the pen-written letter, each line after the first is indented the same as in the heading. In the typewritten letter, the lines are not indented. A comma is required between the name of the city and the name of the state. Punctuation is required at the end of a line only when the line ends with an abbreviation. 

The Salutation. The salutations of the two letters are: 

Dear Sir: Gentlemen:

The salutation is always followed by a colon, as in the illustrations. The word "Gentlemen" is ordinarily used as a salutation when the letter is addressed to a company or firm, although "Dear Sirs" may be used. The salutation "Dear Madam" is common when the letter is addressed to a woman. When letters are addressed to individuals, saluta-

[ch. 19 

tions such as "Dear Mr. Holladay," "Dear Mrs. Martin," or "Dear Miss Hunting" may be used instead of "Dear Sir" or "Dear Madam." The former are usually preferred as they are slightly less formal. 

The Body of the Letter. The first line of each paragraph in the body of the letter is indented from one-half inch to an inch. The exact amount of indention is not always the same, but it should be the same for all paragraphs in one letter. If the letter is typed single space, as in Figure 122, an extra space is left between paragraphs. The ordinary rules of composition are followed in paragraphing and punctuating the body of the letter.

The Complimentary Close. The complimentary closes of the two illustrated letters are: 

Respectfully yours, Very truly yours,

The complimentary close is placed beneath the body of the letter, beginning about the middle of the line. Forms, other than those in the illustrations, which are commonly used are: "Yours truly," "Yours very truly," "Yours respectfully," and "Sincerely yours." A comma is ordinarily used after the complimentary close.

The Signature. The signatures of the two illustrated letters are: 

Robert Holmes

THE MANVILLE TIRE COMPANY 

Everett Trueblood Secretary

The first signature is the name of the writer himself; the second is the name of the company and that of the one who wrote for the company. Both of these signatures are commonly used on business letters. 

Reference Initials. When a letter is typed by some one other than the author, the initials of the dictator and the typist are placed in the lower left corner of the letter. The initials are written on a line with the left margin of the letter. If space permits, they are placed a little lower than the signature.

ch. 19] 295 

BUSINESS CORRESPONDENCE 

The reference initials in Figure 122 are "ET-ZO." Figure 

121 has no reference initials as the letter was written with a pen.

Margins. A letter should be so spaced on the page that all margins are approximately equal. One cannot always exactly estimate the length of a letter, but if the letter has been planned in advance, the space which it will require can be estimated rather closely. As far as possible, a letter should be arranged on the page so that it appears like a picture in a frame. This greatly increases attractiveness and makes the letter more effective.

Postal Cards. Postal cards are not used frequently for business correspondence. When they are used, the message is arranged in much the same general form as a business letter. If the message is comparatively long, so that space is at a premium, the introductory address may be omitted. (See Figure 123.) The extra space between paragraphs in typewritten material may also be omitted. 

Dear Sir: 

200 Seventh Avenue 

Atlanta, Georgia 

June 23, 193All hats in our stock, including many worth ten dollars, are being placed on sale for $4.50.

This sale offers a real chance for economical buying. It is being announced today to our customers.

It will be announced Thursday in the newspapers. 

Come at once and get first choice. 

Yours truly, E. B. Hammond

HAMMOND AND STREET. 

Figure 123-Message Written on a Postal Card 

Note that to save space the introductory address is omitted.

BUSINESS CORRESPONDENCE Section IV. PREPARING LETTERS FOR THE MAIL 

Need for Care in Preparing Outgoing Mail. It is not enough for a letter to be written in an attractive form. It must also be delivered to the addressee, and the condition in which it is delivered should meet with his approval. Much care should therefore be given to the preparation of letters for mailing.

The Envelope. The envelope used should match the stationery on which the letter was written. The standard size for business envelopes is 35% x 6½ inches. A larger size, 416 x 912 inches, is frequently used for letters written on two or more sheets or containing inclosures. 

Addressing the Envelope. Carelessness and inaccuracy in addressing envelopes are responsible for the loss or delay of many letters; they should be carefully avoided.

The form of the address on the envelope is similar to that used in the letter. The chief difference is that on the envelope the name of the state may be written on a line by itself, even though in the address in the letter it is written on the same line as the name of the city. Observe the illustrations of addressed envelopes in Figures 124 and 125 and compare them with the letters in Figures 121 and 122.

The address on the envelope should include the name of the city even though the letter is to be delivered in the same city in which it is mailed. Then, if the post office sends the letter out of the city by mistake, the letter will be returned promptly and delivered. A letter which does not show the name of the city may be delayed or lost.

The address should be placed on the envelope so that the lower and right margins are about equal. The upper and left margins should be greater. Proportions similar to those used in Figures 124 and 125 are satisfactory.

The Return Address. The name and address of the writer should be placed in the upper left-hand corner of the envelope. Then, if for any reason the letter cannot be delivered to the addressee, it will be returned to the writer.

[ch. 19 

When a person or business uses printed letterheads, the return address may be printed, as in Figure 125. If it is not printed, it may be written as in Figure 124. The return address may be accompanied by the words "Return in five days," or by similar words to indicate the length of time the post office should hold a letter which cannot be delivered. 

These words are unnecessary, for if they are not used, the post office will return a letter which cannot be delivered within a reasonable length of time. 

Robert Holmes 

908 Center Street 

Baltimore, Maryland 

Mr. S. M. Holladay 

The Midland Refining Co. 

Des Moines Sowa

The Manville Tire Company

Folding a Letter. When a letter is to be folded and inserted in a standard envelope, size 35% x 612 inches, the following method may be used: 1 2 

Figure 126-Folding a Business Letter and Inserting It in a Standard Envelope 

1. Lay the letter flat on the desk. 

2. Fold from bottom toward top, bringing the bottom to within one-half inch of the top. 

3. Fold from the right to the left a little more than onethird of the width of the sheet. 

4. Fold the remaining portion from the left to the right. 

5. Insert in the envelope with the crease downward.

[ch. 19 

For a large envelope, size 416 x 912 inches, a letter may be folded as follows: 1 2 

Figure 127-Folding a Business Letter and Inserting It in a Large Envelope 

1. Fold from bottom a little less than two-thirds of the way toward the top. 

2. Fold the top over the bottom, extending the top below the lower fold about one-half inch. 

3. Insert in the envelope with the crease downward.

Window Envelope. A business may use an envelope known as a window envelope for its communications. (Figure 128.) Such envelopes are seldom used for letters, but are frequently used for statements, invoices, and similar forms which a business sends out in large numbers. 

The address on the letter or form which is inserted in the window envelope may be read through the transparent window. In this way the labor of writing the address on the envelope is avoided. Also, the danger of sending the letter to the wrong address is lessened.

BUSINESS CORRESPONDENCE Section V. COMPOSITION OF A LETTER 

Planning a Letter. An effective letter is based on a plan. 

The writer studies his problem, decides exactly what he wants to say and how he wants to say it, and then starts to write. A rambling, incoherent letter which does not successfully convey its message is usually the result of a lack of planning. 

Clearness. A worthwhile letter is understood by the reader. If it is not understood, it is worse than useless.

When one speaks, his words may not clearly convey his meaning but his manner of expression, a smile or a gesture, may make his meaning plain. If his words are not readily understood, he probably will observe this and add a sentence or two of additional explanation.

But the situation is different when a letter is written. 

The letter succeeds or fails by itself. If the words do not successfully convey the writer's meaning, the reader can get no hint of the meaning from the facial expression or a gesture of the writer; nor can he immediately get additional explanations to clear up doubtful points. Delays, misunderstandings, and the entire failure of many letters arise from a lack of clearness.

If a letter is carefully planned, it will include definite statements in a logical order and will therefore be likely to be clear. The writer should, however, always remember that the reader may not interpret his words as he intended. He should therefore write as clearly as possible. After his letter has been completed, he should read it to make certain that no part of the letter can possibly be interpreted in a way which he does not intend. If a statement can be interpreted in more than one way, it should be changed. 

Conciseness. Another result of careful planning is conciseness, stating the message directly and in few words. 

Business men do not object to long letters if every sentence carries a message. They do object to letters which ramble and repeat and waste valuable time. A writer should

[ch. 19 

always plan for conciseness but not necessarily for brevity, for a letter that is too brief may not be clear. 

Paragraphing. The usual rules of composition apply to the paragraphing of a letter. But it has been found that short paragraphs are most quickly and accurately understood by the reader. The writer should therefore plan his letter so that he can divide it into short paragraphs, each presenting one idea.

Courtesy. A business letter should always be courteous. 

Even slight abruptness or rudeness, which might be overlooked were one talking, appears to be important in a letter. 

A successful letter never fails in courtesy, for a good business letter is written to get action, not to arouse anger. 

Style. A business letter may be slightly more formal than spoken words. For example, contractions such as "don't," "won't," and "can't" are used less frequently in letters than in speech. 

In general, however, one should write as he speakssimply, and directly to the point. One should never use formal or artificial expressions which he would not use if he were talking. 

Note the contrast between the following sentences used in similar letters: 

We are sorry to learn from your letter of 

June 2 that your order No. 1068 has been delayed.

If it has not been delivered by the time you receive this letter, please notify us by wire. 

Yours of June 2 received and contents noted and in reply would state that if the order has not been delivered by this time you should notify us by wire. 

We also wish to say that we regret the delay in the delivery of the order.

The first is simple and direct; it clearly expresses the writer's meaning. The second is awkward and wordy; it confuses its meaning with unnecessary and artificial phrases. 

Effective letters give the impression of naturalness, the feeling that two people are talking together. Modern busi-

ch. 19] 305 

BUSINESS CORRESPONDENCE 

ness demands such letters and is avoiding more and more the use of letters which seem formal or artificial.

CHAPTER XX FILING Section I. FILING DEVICES 

Need for Filing. In Chapter XIX we observed that a business may receive and write hundreds or even thousands of letters. No one could remember everything in them. The letters received and the copies of the letters written must therefore be kept, for the information which they contain may be needed at any time. Other business papers, such as carbon copies of invoices, cancelled checks, and receipted bills, must also be kept for reference. 

If thousands of letters and papers are kept, some definite system must be followed in storing them; otherwise, a person would not be able to find a certain one in a reasonable length of time. In handling his own affairs, an individual may not have as many papers to take care of as a business, but he may accumulate hundreds of items in the course of a year. He needs a system which will enable him to find quickly any item that he may want.

Placing papers, cards, or other records in a systematic arrangement so that each one can be located quickly is known as filing. Businesses and individuals are giving increasing attention to this subject. Filing methods and devices have been developed which make possible the quick finding of any item properly filed. 

What Should Be Filed? The original copies of letters received, carbon copies of letters written, catalogs, circulars, price lists, and, in fact, any record or paper to which reference may be made at some time in the future should be filed. 

Not everything which is filed will necessarily be kept the same length of time. A letter inquiring about the date an order was shipped may not be kept so long as a letter acknowledging the receipt of a payment of money. The 307

[ch. 20 

former may be of temporary interest only, but the latter may be valuable as long as there is any possibility that the one to whom payment was made will attempt to collect that amount again. Anything that is kept, however, should be filed carefully for there is no advantage in keeping a letter or paper if it cannot be located when it is wanted. 

Figure 132 

Spindle File. Very small businesses, having only a few papers to file, sometimes place each paper on a spindle or spike in the order in which it is received. This is not a system of filing, for one of the requirements of a filing system is that one be able to locate a paper quickly. Obviously, a paper on a spindle cannot be located unless one examines each paper separately. A spindle file is actually a substitute for a filing system, rather than such a system. It is, however, sometimes useful as a temporary method of disposing of papers which are to be more permanently filed later. 

Spindle File

Shannon File. Another filing device which is frequently used for temporary records is the Shannon file. This consists of a board, on one end of which is mounted a double arch. Each arm of the arch breaks near the top and swings outward. Papers to be filed are perforated and slipped up over the upright posts. Then the arch is closed, making the papers secure.

The Shannon file is particularly useful in factories and offices where a number of papers are to be temporarily filed. 

This is especially true when additional memoranda have to be added to these papers at frequent intervals. The Shannon file holds the papers flat so that entries can be made on them conveniently.

If many papers are placed on a Shannon file, they are ordinarily separated by an index similar to the one shown at the right of the file in Figure 133. All papers from departments or companies the names of which begin with A or B are placed back of the AB guide; those which begin

ch. 20] 309 

FILING 

with C or D back of the CD guide; and so on through the alphabet. This aids the one using the file in quickly locating the desired paper. 

LABOR-SAVING DEVICES 

TAMAN & ERSE MFG CO. and

Figure 133-Shannon File

Box File. When a person or a business has only a small number of letters and business papers to file, a device, known as a box file, may be used. This has a number of cardboard divisions which are alphabetically indexed. The filing is usually done by name. All letters written by people GLOBE 

SECURITY FILE REDO CHE BREED RED CODE LETTERS 

Figure 134-Box File having last names beginning with A, and by companies having names beginning with A, and carbon copies of letters written to them, are placed in one section; those for people and companies having names beginning with B, in another section; and so on through the alphabet.

[ch. 20 

A box file is recommended only when the volume of correspondence is quite small. Ordinarily, it is more likely to be used by an individual than by a business. 

Figure 135-Bellows File

Bellows File. Another device used for small collections of letters is known as a bellows file. This has divisions similar to those of the box file. It is sometimes preferred since its thickness is variable, depending on the quantity of material filed in it. The bellows file, like the box file, is used largely by individuals in filing business letters, tax receipts, leases, contracts, and other business papers. 

Vertical File. Although the box and the bellows files are satisfactory for persons having only a small amount of correspondence, they are not used extensively by persons or businesses having a large amount of correspondence. The small amount of space in such a file does not permit extensive filing, and the small number of divisions does not permit the classification of material into many groups. 

The device commonly used for extensive correspondence is known as a vertical file. (See Figure 136.) This file is made up of one or more drawers. In each drawer the letters stand on edge behind suitable guides. 

The vertical file is preferable to the other kinds of files discussed because of its much greater capacity and flexibility. The correspondence is also much more accessible since

ch. 20] 311 

FILING 

R-Z A-D E-K 

Figure 136-Vertical File the hundreds or thousands of letters in one drawer are available when that drawer is opened.

Folders. Obviously, if letters are placed on edge in a drawer, some means must be provided for holding them in position and for separating or classifying them. For this purpose, a device known as a folder is used. (See Figure

[ch. 20 

137.) This is a heavy sheet of paper folded once as shown in the illustration. The folder is a little larger than the standard letter size so that letters can be placed in it conveniently. 

A folder not only supports the letters and keeps them in good order, but it also aids a person in finding them. The letters of one person or firm, or those pertaining to one subject, are usually placed in one folder. The corresponding name is placed on the tab at the top where it is visible as soon as the drawer is opened.

The folders are arranged al- 

Figure 137-Folder phabetically, or in some other order as explained in Section III. The folder desired can then be found quickly.

FILING Section II. INDEXING 

Need for Indexing. A business may have hundreds or even thousands of folders, each containing a different group of correspondence. If folders are to be located quickly when they are wanted, they must be arranged according to a definite plan or index. All offices may not follow exactly the same plan, but one office should have a definite plan and should follow it in all cases.

Rules for Indexing. The alphabet is the basis of all filing systems. The files may be divided into sections according to some geographic grouping, or the folders may be numbered and arranged numerically. Nevertheless, some alphabetic arrangement of folders or reference cards is necessary if folders are to be located quickly. It is therefore important that one understand the following rules for indexing. (1) Names of individuals are transposed. The surnames are written first, then the given names, and finally the middle initial or name. The names are then arranged according to the alphabetic letter sequence of the entire name. 

THE NAMES 

Harry J. Brown 

Harry R. Brown 

C. J. Cody

ARE INDEXED AS 

Brown, Harry J. 

Brown, Harry R. 

Cody, C. J. (2) If one surname includes the same letters as another plus one or more additional letters, the shorter surname is indexed first regardless of the given name or initial. 

THE NAMES 

Harry J. Brown 

William R. Brown 

Amos A. Browne

ARE INDEXED AS 

Brown, Harry J. 

Brown, William R. 

Browne, Amos A. (3) When the surnames are the same, if one given name contains the same letters as another plus one or more addi tional letters, the shorter is placed first. 

THE NAMES 

Will Brown 

William Brown

ARE INDEXED AS 

Brown, Will 

Brown, William

[ch. 20 

(4) An initial is considered to be a whole word and precedes all names beginning with that initial.

THE NAMES 

B G Sandwich Shops, Inc.

J. W. Babb 

John Babb

ARE INDEXED AS 

B G Sandwich Shops, Inc. 

Babb, J. W. 

Babb, John (5) Abbreviations are indexed as if the names were spelled in full.

Wm. Brown 

Wilson Brown

ARE INDEXED AS

Brown, Wilson 

Brown, William (6) Surnames beginning with O', De, D', Mc, and Mac are considered as whole words.

J. S. O'Dell 

Owen Odell

ARE INDEXED AS 

MacMillan, William 

McMillan, Hugh 

Odell, Charles 

O'Dell, J. S. 

Odell, Owen (7) Titles such as Dr., Professor, Mrs., and Jr. are placed at the end in parentheses and are disregarded in indexing.

THE NAMES Dr. Mark S. Green 

Mrs. L. M. Scott 

Harry L. Smith, Jr.

ARE INDEXED AS 

Green, Mark S. (Dr.) 

Scott, L. M. (Mrs.) 

Smith, Harry L. (Jr.) (8) Firm names are indexed as written unless they include the full names of individuals. (See Rule 9.)

 Section III. FILING METHODS 

Need for Filing Methods. Figure 136 on page 311 illustrates a vertical file with four drawers, each having space for many folders. A large business may have dozens of such drawers with thousands of folders containing many thousands of letters and business forms. When the files are so extensive, efficiency in filing and finding material is important. For this reason various filing methods and time-saving devices have been developed to aid each company in using its files efficiently.

Labels and Guides. Each drawer in a vertical file is labeled so that the one using the files will know the folders it contains before he opens the drawer. Note the labels on the drawers in Figure 136. The name of the business, person, or subject for which correspondence is placed in one folder is placed on the tab at the top of the folder. These names can be seen as soon as the drawer is opened. It is therefore possible for one to select the folder he wishes without moving others. 

Figure 138 

Guides for a Vertical File 

To make the location of a folder still easier, the drawer is divided into sections by means of guides. Letters or numbers on each guide indicate the folders which are to be filed behind it. This enables a person to find quickly the section in which a folder is located, and then the folder itself. 

An extremely simple filing system might have one guide for each letter of the alphabet. Then all folders having names beginning with A would be placed behind the A guide; those having names beginning with B would be placed behind the B guide; and so on through the alphabet.

ch. 20] 317 

FILING 

Usually there are so many folders that more divisions are needed. The guides may then be labeled as follows: 

A, An, B, Bar, Be, etc. All names beginning with A with a second letter before n in the alphabetic order are filed in the first section. Other names beginning with A are filed in the second section. Each of the names Adams, Andrews, Baker, Barrows, and Berg are filed behind different guides if guides labeled A, An, B, Bar, Be, etc., are used.

Behind each guide the names are usually arranged alphabetically according to the rules for indexing discussed An

Alphabetic Filing. A filing method commonly used is known as alphabetic filing.

In this method the guides are arranged alphabetically according to the usual indexing plan. The folders, then, are in the same order as if they were arranged alphabetically without guides. The only purpose of the guides is to aid one in quickly finding the section of the files in which the folder desired is located. 

Geographic Filing. In some instances alphabetic filing is not entirely satisfactory. Suppose a company sells supplies to banks. It may have correspondence with many banks named The First National Bank. Or, suppose a company sells direct to individuals. It may have correspond-

[ch. 20 

ence with many people with frequently recurring names such as Charles Baker or John Brown.

In either of these cases it would be confusing to use alphabetic filing because so many names which are the same or are very similar might be found together. The files are therefore divided into geographic sections. Such filing is known as geographic filing.

Austin, THE. 

Tarlar, G. 7.

In geographic filing, the main divisions may be states, cities, or any other divisions desired. When the files are divided by states, the first main division is Alabama, the second is Arizona, and the third, Arkansas. The correspondence for persons and firms in each state is then filed alphabetically, When a business has correspondence with many people or businesses in one state, a further subdivision of the files may be desirable. The correspondence with those in 

Alabama may be first divided according to the names of the cities, such as Abbeville, Acton, Adger, Alabama City, Albany, etc. The correspondence for each city may then be filed alphabetically according to names of the persons or businesses.

For each city, correspondence is filed alphabetically according to the names of the persons, businesses, or subjects.

Figure 141 a lawyer has correspondence with several people about one subject or case. As all letters may deal with the same problem, it may be desirable to file all together. This would be confusing if the correspondence were filed under the name of any one person. 

The difficulty is avoided by having correspondence relating to one subject filed in one folder. This folder does not bear any person's name, but it is numbered. A card is then made for each person whose correspondence is filed in that folder. This card carries the name and the address of the person and the number of the folder.

The card in Figure 142 was prepared as a cross 

Drawer of a Numeric File reference for Folder 42 in Figure 141. This card shows only the name and the address of the person whose correspondence is filed in this folder and the number of the folder. All cards are filed alphabetically in the card case, and the folders are arranged numerically in the files. When the correspondence with Henry Barnes is desired, his card is first located in the card case. This shows that his correspondence is in Folder 42. This folder is then readily located in the numeric file. 

When correspondence is filed under the name of the subject, the card shows the name of the subject instead of the name of the person. More than one card may be prepared for one folder. The first may show the subject for

[ch. 20 

which correspondence is filed in one file; another may show the name of the person writing the letter. The folder can then be located regardless of whether the person looks in the card case under the name of the person or the name of the subject.

Numeric File When a folder is located in a numeric file, one must first refer to the card case, and then to the files. It ordinarily requires more time to locate a folder when it is filed numerically than when it is filed alphabetically or geographically. 

Numeric files are therefore used only when they are especially needed, as was explained on page 319.

10. Under what circumstances do you recommend the use of numeric filing?

12. The Jones Engineering Company has at all times a number of small construction jobs in different parts of one state. The correspondence relative to each job is carried on with a number of people. Do you recommend the company's using alphabetic, geographic, or numeric filing?

ch. 20] 321 

FILING Section IV. USING THE FILES 

Placing Copy in Folders. When material is properly and neatly filed, any paper desired can be located quickly. But when errors are made in filing, the location of a particular paper may be a long, tedious process. Proper filing of copy is therefore important. 

Before filing is started, the file clerk must understand the system that is used. This may be alphabetic, geographic, or numeric. The file clerk must also know the basis on which titles are assigned to folders. The title may be the name of an individual, a company, or a subject. 

The correspondence with each person with whom a company deals may relate to transactions with that person only. In such cases the correspondence for each individual may be placed in a separate folder, and the folder title may be the name of the individual.

Some businesses may not deal with individuals acting separately, but rather with other businesses or organizations. Correspondence may be carried on with several representatives of each organization, but all of it may relate to the same transactions. In that event, all correspondence for one organization may be filed together, and the folder title may be the company name.

In still other cases, the correspondence with several individuals or firms may relate to the same transaction. All of this correspondence will probably be needed if information is sought about a particular transaction. The folder title is then the subject, and all correspondence relating to that subject is filed together. 

Ordinarily, one basis of assigning folder titles is used in all files of one company, but occasionally some correspondence may be filed under the name of the individual; other correspondence, under the name of the company; and still other correspondence, under the name of the subject. 

When a paper is filed, the folder should be raised from the file. This helps the file clerk to make sure that the proper folder is selected, and also allows the material filed to fall

[ch. 20 

to the bottom of the folder. Each paper should be filed with its heading to the left so that all papers can be read naturally as if they were bound in book form. 

The correspondence in one folder should be arranged according to the dates, with the latest on top. This brings the most recent correspondence to the front of the folder. 

This is desirable because reference is made most frequently to correspondence of recent date.

Miscellaneous Folders. As far as possible a separate folder should be maintained for the correspondence with each person or firm, or for each subject. In some cases the correspondence with one person or about one subject is so limited that a separate folder does not seem to be necessary. 

Miscellaneous folders may be maintained for such correspondence in each section of the files. The miscellaneous folder should be placed back of the individual folders in that section, and should be indicated by a label different from that used on the individual folders. In Figure 139 on page 317 the tabs for miscellaneous folders appear in the same positions as the guides for the corresponding sections. 

The folder labeled "A 1," directly back of the guide "A 1," is for the miscellaneous correspondence which is to be indexed under "Aa" to "Ah" inclusive. The second miscellaneous folder, which may be seen directly back of the guide "Ai 2," is for miscellaneous correspondence which is to be indexed under “Ai” to “Al" inclusive. 

All correspondence placed in a miscellaneous folder should be arranged in strictly alphabetic sequence. When more than one paper is to be filed under one title, the papers should be arranged in date order, with the latest to the front. 

If a number of papers are filed under one title in a miscellaneous folder, a new folder for that title should be opened. 

Requisitioning Matter from the Files. You probably have observed that when you take a book from the library, the librarian makes a record of the withdrawal so that she will know who has the book and can call for it if it is not returned. Similarly, the one in charge of the files should keep

ch. 20] 323 

FILING 

a record of each folder that is taken out. This is especially true in a large business where there are a number of persons using the same files. 

There are various methods of keeping a record of folders which have been removed from the files. The one described below is ordinarily satisfactory.

Each person requesting a folder fills out a requisition slip. This shows the name of the folder, the address, the 

NAME OR SUBJECT ADDRESS

At the time the folder is returned, the out-guide is removed from the files and the requisition slip is destroyed. OUT

CHAPTER XXI PURCHASES RECORDS Section I. THE PURCHASING PROCEDURE AND THE PURCHASE REQUISITION 

Introduction. We have, in previous chapters, studied various business methods and forms. These methods and forms are used by both individuals and businesses. Other methods and forms which are used chiefly by businesses are discussed in this chapter. Although these forms are used by businesses, individuals should be familiar with them. 

Almost everyone has some contact with business and can better use its services if he understands its procedures. 

Furthermore, these are the methods and forms one is most likely to use if he engages in business activities. common.

Each business is in some respects different from all other businesses, but all businesses have some transactions in All buy and sell, and receive and pay out money. 

These transactions are completed by the corner news stand and the great department store, the small shoe repair shop and the great factory.

The forms and methods which we shall study are those which are most commonly used. One who understands them can more readily understand and use other forms and methods required in other types of transactions. 

Purchasing. We come in contact to the greatest extent with the selling departments of businesses. If we go into a large retail store, we see beautifully arranged sales rooms, and shelves and counters of attractive merchandise. Scores of clerks are willing to aid us in making our selections.

But before this merchandise was made available for us, it had to be purchased. Not only did the retail stores have to purchase these finished goods, but also the factories making the goods had to buy the raw materials used in the 327

[ch. 21 

manufacturing process. When we think of a retail store or a factory, we do not commonly think of its purchasing activities. Nevertheless, purchasing is important and is usually completed according to a definite procedure. 

Purchasing Department. In small businesses, the one selling or using the materials ordinarily buys them. Thus, the proprietor of a small news stand both buys and sells his merchandise. But this is not true in large organizations. In large retail stores the employees may be numbered by the hundreds; in large factories thousands of persons may be using materials in manufacturing. Obviously, in such organizations each person who sells or uses materials cannot aid in the purchasing. For this reason, one department, known as a purchasing department, is commonly responsible for buying for the entire organization. This department is usually in charge of a person known as the purchasing agent.

Purchasing Procedure. Whether the business is large or small, the same general buying procedure must be followed. Some person must learn for himself or must be told what to buy; the order must be placed; the invoice giving the cost of the goods must be received and approved; the cost of the goods must be recorded; and the goods must be received and approved as to quantity and quality. As this procedure is repeated with great frequency, a business form is commonly used in each step.

Purchase Requisition. The person who is responsible for keeping a supply of goods on hand, usually the stock clerk, notifies the purchasing agent when a purchase is to be made. To prevent mistakes, he usually gives this notice in writing on a form known as a purchase requisition. Each requisition is prepared in duplicate. The original is given to the purchasing agent. The duplicate is retained by the stock clerk as his record of the transaction.

Purchase requisitions differ in details but usually they show the date of the requisition, the name of the person or department for which the goods are desired, the name of

ch. 21] 329 

PURCHASES RECORDS 

the person to be notified when the goods arrive, the date the goods are needed, the quantity and description of the goods, and the signature of the one placing the requisition. 

Requisitions are numbered consecutively for the convenience of the stock clerk and the purchasing agent. The duplicate copy of each requisition has the same number as the original.

W. A. LARIMER COMPANY INDIANAPOLIS, INDIANA REQUISITION

Figure 145 illustrates a common form of a purchase requisition. On this form, A. L. Halden, the stock clerk of the W. A. Larimer Company, a dealer in electrical supplies, requests the purchase of several items of merchandise. 

The number of the requisition is 120; the date is March 1; the goods are required by March 15; the requisition is for merchandise for stock; and Mr. Halden is to be notified when the goods arrive. The two lines at the bottom of the requisition, under the heading "Purchasing Agent's Memorandum," are to be filled out by the purchasing agent at the time he places the purchase order.

A small business may not use a purchase requisition as the buyer may know what is needed. But even a small business, operated by one person, needs some record of stock requirements so that orders will be placed promptly.

PURCHASES, RECORDS Section II. THE PURCHASE ORDER AND INVOICE 

Purchase Order. When a business orders merchandise, it usually gives the order in a written form. This is more satisfactory than giving the order orally as it provides a permanent record of the transaction, which may be needed if the goods delivered are not exactly the same as those ordered, or if for any other reason a reference to the order is desired.

An order for goods may be contained in a letter, but a business ordinarily uses a form on which as much information as possible is printed. It is then necessary to insert only the information which is peculiar to the one order. 

This saves the time of the one preparing the order and also the time of the one receiving it. A written order of this kind is known as a purchase order.

A typical purchase order, such as is illustrated in Figure 146, contains space for the name of the company to

As many copies of the purchase order may be made as are needed. The purchase order shown in Figure 146 was prepared in triplicate. The original was sent to the seller, H. A. Benson and Company, as its authority for filling the order. One copy was sent to the stock clerk, A. L. Halden, to show him that the order had been placed. The third copy was retained by the purchasing agent, C. W. Davis, as his record of the transaction.

After the purchasing agent has placed the order, he enters on his copy of the requisition the purchase order number, its date, and the name of the company to which the order was issued. Figure 147 shows the entries made on the lower part of the purchase requisition in Figure 145. 

These entries are desirable as they show on the requisition itself what disposition was made of the request that goods be purchased.

In some cases it may be desirable to tell the sellers the name of the person to whom invoices are to be sent, the railroad over which freight shipments are to be carried, and similar information. 

Although purchase orders differ in form because of the difference in the buyers' instructions, they are in general much alike. One who understands the form illustrated in

Invoice. After the seller has received a purchase order, he sends the buyer a written form showing the items purchased and the charges for them. This form is known as an invoice. On the invoice the seller usually writes the name and the address of the buyer; the date; the method of ship-

[ch. 21 

ment; the terms; the quantity, description, and price of each item purchased; and the total amount charged for the goods. The name and the address of the seller are printed on the invoice. Headings and titles are also printed so that the information which the seller writes can be entered most conveniently.

Figure 148 is the invoice received by the W. A. Larimer 

Company from H. A. Benson and Company in response to the purchase order shown in Figure 146.

Terms. When goods are bought on credit, the seller usually fixes the time when payment is to be made. This time is known as the credit terms. The terms offered by 

H. A. Benson and Company, one of whose invoices is illustrated in Figure 148, are fifteen days. This means that the invoice must be paid within fifteen days after its date. The invoice is therefore due on March 18. Other companies may offer different terms. The terms are frequently given as thirty days or sixty days. The first of these means that the invoice must be paid within thirty days after its date, and the second, within sixty days after its date.

In order to encourage prompt payment, a business may offer special discounts to those making payment within a very short time. In such a case the terms on the invoice may be 2/10-n/30. These terms are read two per cent discount in ten days, net in thirty days. They are also read more briefly as two, ten; net, thirty. They mean that the net amount for the merchandise is due in thirty days but that a two per cent deduction may be made for payment within ten days of the date of the invoice. Slightly different terms are sometimes offered, such as 1/10-n/30, 3/10-n/60, and 3/15-n/60.

Approval of the Invoice by the Purchasing Agent. When the invoice is received, it is referred to the purchasing agent, or the purchasing department, and is compared with the purchase order to verify the quantities and prices. If errors are discovered, they are immediately called to the attention of the seller so that an adjustment can be made.

ch. 21] 335 

PURCHASES RECORDS 

When the purchasing agent approves the quantity and price of each item, he places a small check mark (V) at the right of each quantity and price to show that each is correct.

The check marks in the "Quantity" column and in the "Price" column on the purchase invoice in Figure 148 were inserted by the purchasing agent or by one of his assistants. 

The check marks in the "Amount" column were inserted in another department, as will be explained in the following section.

1. When a company orders merchandise, it might write a letter to the seller describing the goods desired. Why is the use of a printed purchase order form preferable? 

2. Why are purchase orders numbered? 

3. Ordinarily several carbon copies are made of each purchase order. Give some uses that may be made of these carbon copies.

Company sends ten thousand of each style of calendar, and charges for the total number shipped. How can the Hickman Manufacturing Company prove that this was the error of the Stone Company?

The purchase invoice, however, is referred to the purchasing agent for his approval. Why is it desirable to have him approve the invoice?

[ch. 21 

 Section III. RECORDING PURCHASES 

Approval of Calculations on the Invoice. A business will not accept an invoice for goods purchased and place it in the records until the calculations on it have been approved. Some person or department is therefore assigned the duty of approving the extensions and total of each invoice. When errors are found, the invoice is corrected or referred to the seller for correction. If the extensions and the total of the invoice are correct, a small check mark is placed at the right of each. In Figure 148 the check marks opposite the figures in the "Amount" column indicate that the calculations are correct.

In the verifying of the calculations on an invoice, a machine may be used. Figure 149 illustrates one of the many machines that may be obtained for this purpose. It can be used for addition, subtraction, multiplication, and division. MONROE 

Figure 149-Calculating Machine

Purchases Journal. After a purchase invoice has been approved, the purchase is usually recorded. Purchases of merchandise to be sold are recorded separate from purchases of supplies which are to be used by the business. This is desirable so that the manager of the business may know the cost of goods which are purchased for resale and can thus find the cost of goods sold, and the profit received from the sale of these goods.

ch. 21] 337 

PURCHASES RECORDS 

The book in which purchases of merchandise are recorded is known as the purchases journal. Figure 150 illustrates the purchases journal of the W. A. Larimer Company. The first entry in this book records a purchase from 

H. A. Benson and Company. The other entries are for similar purchases made during the same month. 

Description of the Purchases Journal. The columns in the purchases journal are used as follows: 

The first column, headed "Date," is for the date of the entry. Only the day of the month is entered in this column as the month and the year are written at the top of the column. 

The next column, headed "L. F.," is not used when the invoice is recorded. It is used later, when the entries in the purchases journal are transferred to other records. It will be further discussed in Chapter XXIII. 

The name and address of the seller are entered in the "Vendor" and "Address" columns.

The terms and the date of the invoice are entered in the "Terms" and "Invoice Date" columns. The date of the invoice will usually be earlier than the date of the entry because some time will elapse between the date the invoice is mailed and the date it is delivered. The terms refer to the date of the invoice and not to the date of the entry. To illustrate, the first invoice recorded in the purchases journal, March 1932

CHAPTER XXII SALES RECORDS Section I. WHOLESALE SALES 

Introduction. When merchandise is sold to individuals for their own use, the transactions are said to be retail sales. 

When merchandise is sold by one business to another for resale to individuals, the transactions are said to be wholesale sales. In this section we shall consider some of the problems and records involved in wholesale selling. 

Credit. One business usually sells to another on credit. 

This is more convenient for both the seller and the buyer than selling for cash. It is also an accommodation to the buyer as it gives him an opportunity to sell a part or all of the merchandise before paying for it, and thus lessens the amount of capital needed in operating his business. 

If a sale is made on credit, it is said to be on account. 

The seller is called the creditor, and the buyer, the debtor. 

When one business sells to another regularly, it knows whether the latter pays its bills promptly and is entitled to credit. But a business may receive an application for credit from a company that has not purchased from it before. 

The seller must then determine the buyer's credit standing. This may be done partly from statements sent by the buyer to show the amount of his resources, and partly from references supplied by the buyer. The seller may also consult credit rating books for further information about the buyer's credit standing. 

Credit Rating Books. Certain companies collect information about the credit standing of businesses in the United States and Canada. This information is published in books which can be secured by those who need credit information. 

The two most widely used credit rating books are published by R. G. Dun and Company and The Bradstreet Company. 347

[ch. 22 

The names in the credit rating books are classified, first, alphabetically by states; second, alphabetically by towns or cities; and, third, alphabetically by names. After each name the credit rating is indicated by a letter or a number, or a combination of the two. A key to these ratings is given on the inside cover of the book. 

The estimated wealth and the credit ratings of the businesses listed in Figure 156 can be found by comparing the symbols given after the company names with the key in Figure 157. The first company for which a credit rating is given is worth over $1,000,000 and has high credit. The next to the last company has an estimated wealth between $75,000 and $125,000 and has high credit. The last company has an estimated wealth between $125,000 and $200,000 and has high credit. Companies for which credit ratings are not given have not supplied the credit rating agency with the information necessary in establishing ratings. Those wishing to do business with such companies must secure information about their credit ratings elsewhere.

Invoice. After the credit standing of a company placing an order has been approved, the invoice for the goods sold is prepared. The process of preparing the invoice is commonly known as billing, and the clerk preparing it is known as the billing clerk.

ch. 22] 349 

SALES RECORDS 

The billing clerk enters on the invoice the name and address of the buyer, the date, the terms of the sale, the description of the items sold, the quantities, the prices, and the total of the sale. The sales invoice in Figure 158 was made out by a clerk of the W. A. Larimer Company after it had received a purchase order from W. P. Ryan. 

In writing the sales invoice, the clerk may use a machine known as a billing machine. This machine is similar to a typewriter with an attachment to hold the invoice in place and to facilitate the use of carbon paper. (See Figure 159.)

This form of invoice was adopted by a national conference held under the auspices of the Division of Simplified Practice, Department of Commerce, Washington, D. C.

A sales invoice from the viewpoint of the seller is a purchases invoice from the viewpoint of the buyer. For this reason, the invoice in Figure 158 is similar in arrangement and contains the same type of information as was found in the invoice in Figure 148, page 333. The invoice

[ch. 22 

on page 349 was prepared by the W. A. Larimer Company for one of its customers, while the invoice illustrated on page 333 was received by the W. A. Larimer Company from another merchant.

At the time the billing clerk makes the original invoice, he usually makes several copies through the use of carbon paper. The original is mailed to the one to whom the merchandise is sold. One copy may be used as a basis for charging the customer; another may be sent to the shipping 

Figure 159-Billing Machine room as authority for making the shipment; and a third may be filed with the correspondence of the customer, so that it will be available for reference. Other copies may be prepared if they are needed.

Sales Journal. It is necessary for a business to keep a record of its sales so that it will know whether their amount is satisfactory, and how much merchandise must be purchased to provide for future sales.

This record is often kept in a book known as a sales journal. A common form of sales journal is illustrated in 

Figure 160. The first entry was required to record the sale for which the sales invoice in Figure 158 was prepared. 

The columns of the sales journal are much the same as those of the purchases journal. The date of the entry is written in the column at the extreme left. The second col-

After the correctness of this amount is proved, the total is entered in ink opposite the words, "Sales Cr." and "Total Sales," which are written on the first line after the last entry. The book is then ruled as shown in Figure 160. 

Binder for Sales Invoices.

Many businesses making a large number of sales do not record each invoice in a sales journal. Instead, a copy of each invoice is kept in a binder. These copies provide the same information that is provided by the sales Figure 161-A Binder for Invoices journal. The form is less

[ch. 22 

convenient because the information which might be given on one page of the sales journal is given on many pages in the binder. The lack of convenience is offset by the time saved, because each sale does not have to be recorded in the sales journal. 

Credit Memorandum. Goods are sometimes returned to the seller for credit by the purchaser. They may be returned because they are defective, because they were sent by mis- Form

Figure 162-Credit Memorandum take, or because they are unsatisfactory. When a return is made, the merchant gives the customer a detailed account of the return on a form known as a credit memorandum (Figure 162). It is similar in arrangement to an invoice in that it shows the number of articles and their cost. 

If a customer is overcharged on an invoice or if the goods are not received in first-class condition, the merchant may also give him a credit memorandum for the difference between the actual value and the amount charged. In other words, a credit memorandum may be used whenever the amount charged by a merchant is reduced by an allowance or by a return of merchandise. 

When a credit memorandum is prepared, at least one copy is made with carbon paper. The original is sent to the customer and the carbon copy is kept by the business as its record of the transaction.

 Section II. RETAIL SALES 

Cash Sales. Sales by retail stores, unlike sales by wholesale concerns, are in the majority of cases made for cash. 

Cash sales may be recorded in a cash register, as was explained in Chapter I. The cash register shows the total of the cash sales and the total amount of cash received. 

A detailed record of the sale is not required, for the customer has paid for the goods received and therefore the business does not have to collect for them later. 

Credit Sales. When a sale is to be paid for at a later date, it is said to be a sale on credit or on account and is referred to as a credit sale or a charge sale. When such a sale is made, the clerk usually prepares a record of the transaction on a form known as a sales ticket. All sales tickets are not alike, but they usually contain about the same information. Figure 

163 shows a typical form.

As is shown in the illustration, the clerk records on the sales ticket the name of the customer, his address, the goods sold him on credit, their value, and the total of the sale. He also records the department in which he works, his name or initials, the date, and the amount received if a payment is made to apply on the sale. The clerk who prepared the sales ticket in 

Figure 163 worked in Department D and his initials were G. K. The date of the sale was March 1. M

Figure 163-Sales Ticket

Ordinarily, several copies of the sales ticket are made. 

One is retained by the business as its record of the sale so

ch. 22] 355 

SALES RECORDS 

that the customer can be charged the proper amount. Another is given to the customer. Others may be used for different purposes as the business wishes. For example, if the order is to be delivered, one copy may be sent to the shipping department as a shipping order. 

Recording Retail Sales. Retail sales on account may be entered in a sales journal similar to the one shown in Figure 160. Because of the great number of sales, a copy of the sales ticket is frequently used as a permanent record of each sale. All copies may be kept in a binder similar to the binder used for invoices (Figure 161), or they may be kept in some other conveniently arranged device. 

Use of Sales Tickets by Customers. A sales ticket is valuable to the customer as it helps him to verify the correctness of the records of the business. When the goods are delivered to him, the customer will wish to compare them with the sales ticket to make certain that he was not charged for goods which he did not receive. He will also wish to retain the sales ticket until the end of the month. 

At that time the business will send him a statement showing the different amounts charged to him during the month. 

If he has the sales tickets, it will be possible for him to know definitely whether the amounts shown on the statement are correct. If they are not, he can call the error to the attention of the merchant, and obtain a satisfactory adjustment. 

Credit Ticket. When goods sold by a retail store are returned by the purchaser for credit, a credit ticket or memorandum is issued similar to the credit memorandum issued by a wholesale business. A common form of credit ticket is shown in Figure 164. This was issued when E. C. Epply returned one binder which he had purchased from the Henderson Stationery Company. The sales ticket for the sale to him is illustrated on page 354.

CHAPTER XXIII FINANCIAL RECORDS Section I. CASH TRANSACTIONS 

Need for Records. If you buy an overcoat on credit, an employee of the store will make a record of the sale so the amount you owe will be known. If later you pay for the coat, a record of the receipt of cash will be made by the store so that it will not try to collect this amount from you again. 

In a like manner, a business makes many other records of the transactions which it completes so that it will know the amounts which it owes, the amounts owed it by others, and other facts which are needed in the control of its many operations.

In the preceding chapters we have studied the records used in recording purchases and sales. Other records are considered in this chapter. These are not the only records kept by businesses but they are presented here because they are typical of those which are commonly used. Furthermore, one who understands the use of these records will more quickly understand the other and more complicated records with which he comes in contact. 

Cash Receipts. Anyone who is responsible for handling cash should keep a careful record of all cash received. This is necessary because both the total amount received and the source of each item should be known. It makes no difference whether one handles cash for a social organization or for a business. In either case he is expected to be able to give an accurate accounting of all cash received.

The total amount received should be known so that the business or the organization may know how much cash is available. If the amount received is unknown, the amount which should be on hand at any time will also be unknown. 

In that case some of the receipts may be lost and the loss may never be discovered. 361

[ch. 23 

The source of each receipt of cash should be known for several reasons. For example, the sales journal, illustrated on page 351, shows the sales on account but not the cash sales. If the total sales of a business are to be known, the cash sales must also be recorded. Also, cash may be received from those charged for merchandise in the sales journal. 

Each receipt must be recorded so that the amount still owed by the customer will be known.

Similarly, in the cash records of a social organization, the cash received from each individual should be recorded so that the amount contributed by each will be known. 

Cash Payments. The reason for each cash payment and the amount of each should be recorded. If goods which were purchased on account are paid for, a record of the payment should be made. If this is not done, the same invoice may be paid again by mistake.

Furthermore, if a record of payments is not kept, no one will know whether the cash balance is correct. The balance is found by subtracting the cash payments from the cash receipts, or from the balance on hand at the beginning of the period plus the cash receipts. It can also be found by counting the cash actually on hand, but if there is no record of payments and receipts, it is impossible to know whether the correct amount is on hand. 18 

CASH RECEIPTS DATE

FINANCIAL RECORDS 

Cash Book. Cash receipts and payments are commonly recorded in a book known as a cash book. A typical form of a cash book is shown in Figure 166.

Cash receipts are entered on the left side of the cash book and cash payments on the right side. On each side the first two columns are for the date. The first is for the month and the second is for the day. The year is written above the month at the top of the column. The name of the month is repeated only at the top of each page or when the month is changed. 

The next column headed "L. F." is used when the entry in the cash book is transferred to another record. Its use will be explained later.

In the column Account Credited on the receipts side of the cash book, the source of each cash receipt is entered. 

The first receipt was for cash sales so the word Sales was entered. The second was a payment from W. P. Ryan so his name was entered on the second line.

It will be observed that when a sale is made for cash, the name of the purchaser is not entered. But when cash is received to apply on a purchase on account, the name is entered. In the latter case the individual's name is needed, for the business must record the amount received to apply on each sale on account so that it will know the amount owed by each customer. DATE

In the column Account Debited on the payments side of the cash book, the person or business, or the purpose for which cash was paid is entered. The payment of rent in the first entry on page 363 was indicated by the word Rent in the "Account Debited" column. The payment to H. A.

Benson and Company in the second entry was indicated by entering the creditor's name in the "Account Debited" column. When a person or business is paid in cash for goods previously bought on credit, the creditor's name must be recorded so that the business will know the amount paid and can find the amount still owed the creditor. 

In the “Explanation" column on each side of the cash book further explanations of the reasons for the receipts and payments are entered. Note how the entries in these columns in the illustration give information about the reasons for the receipts and payments.

The "Amount" columns are for the amount received or paid in each transaction. The total of the "Amount" column on the cash receipts side of the cash book is the total cash received. Similarly, the total of the "Amount" column on the cash payments side is the total cash paid. 

Proving Cash. At the beginning of a month an entry is made for the balance on hand, as is shown in the illustration of cash receipts on page 362. The amount of this balance is entered in the "Total" column. At the end of the month the "Amount" column on each side of the cash book is added, and the totals are entered in small pencil figures directly below the last entry. On a separate sheet of paper the balance on hand at the beginning of the month is added to the total cash receipts, and the total cash payments are subtracted. This gives the balance at that time. If this equals the cash actually on hand and in the bank, the balance is known to be correct.

Many businesses make it a practice to deposit all cash receipts in the bank and to make all payments by check. 

When this is done, the total cash receipts will equal the total of the deposits, and the total cash payments will equal the total of the checks. The balance as shown by the cash book

ch. 23] 365 

FINANCIAL RECORDS 

will equal the balance in the bank when the proper adjustment has been made for outstanding checks. This serves as a further proof of the accuracy of the cash record. 

A business may find it necessary to pay some amounts in cash instead of by check. In such cases a form known as a voucher should be filled out for each payment. This voucher should be signed by some person in authority to show that the payment was properly authorized. The total of the checks and the vouchers must then equal the total of the cash payments recorded in the cash book. 

A typical voucher for a cash payment is shown in Figure 167.

VOUCHER FOR CASH PAYMENT 

Voucher No. 106 

Amount $25 For

THE BARNES COMPANY 

Date May 6, 195Paid to Frank Wilmer

Delivery of merchandise 

Authorized by 

J. M. Barnes 

Figure 167-Voucher

Balancing and Ruling the Cash Book. It is desirable to record the cash receipts and payments for each month separately. For this reason the cash book is ordinarily balanced and ruled at the end of each month. 

After cash has been proved, the total of the cash receipts is extended into the "Total" column on the receipts side of the cash book so that it can be added to the original balance. 

The total of the cash payments is then extended into the "Total" column on the payments side of the cash book. The balance at the end of the month is entered on the next line so that it can be added to the payments. 

The "Total" column on each side of the cash book is then added, and the two sums are written on the same line of writing, regardless of the fact that more entries may have been made on one side of the cash book than on the other. 

These two sums must be the same, for the beginning balance

[ch. 23 

مار plus the receipts must equal the payments plus the final balance. The fact that the book has been balanced is indicated by the double ruled lines which are drawn across all columns except the "Account" and the "Explanation" columns. The new balance is then entered as of the first day of the following month on the receipts side of the cash book. Study carefully the method used in balancing and ruling the cash book illustrated on pages 362 and 363.

1. If you buy an overcoat on credit, will the sales clerk make a more detailed record of the transaction than if the purchase had been for cash? 

2. If later you pay for the coat which you bought on credit, will it make any difference to you whether the clerk who receives your money makes a record of the transaction? 

3. T. W. West operates a cash grocery. He does not keep a record of the cash received, but he counts his cash at the end of each day and thus finds out the amount received. Do you consider this a good plan?

ch. 23] 367 

FINANCIAL RECORDS Section II. CUSTOMERS' ACCOUNTS 

Need for Additional Records. We have seen that in business certain books are used to record business transactions. 

Among these are the cash book, the sales journal, and the purchases journal. These books completely record transactions but they do not classify them so that all desirable information can be obtained easily. 

For example, the amounts of sales to customers are recorded in a sales journal, and the amounts they pay are recorded in a cash book. But the balance which each customer owes cannot be found directly from either book. 

The Ledger and the Account. Businesses collect information about different classes of transactions in a book known as a ledger. Transactions of one kind or with one person are summarized on one page, known as an account. Figure 168 shows several customers' accounts from the ledger of the W. A. Larimer Company. They illustrate the purpose of accounts and the way in which entries are made in them. 

Title of an Account. The title of an account is a name which describes the information that is recorded in that account. In a customer's account it is most convenient to use the name of the customer as the title of the account. 

Businesses frequently find it desirable to give the address of the customer with the title. This address may be needed if the business has correspondence with the customer, or if it has two or more customers with the same or similar names. When the address is given, it is written to the right on the line below the title, as is shown in Figure 168. 

Form of the Account. An account has two equal sections. The left half is known as the debit side and the right half as the credit side. The abbreviations for debit and credit are dr. and cr.

The amounts which customers are charged for merchandise bought on account are entered on the left or debit side and are called debits. The amounts which they pay are en-

[ch. 23 

tered on the right side and are called credits. Amounts allowed customers for returned or defective merchandise are also credits.

The two sides of an account have the same vertical ruling. The first two columns are for the date, the first being for the month and the second for the day. The year is written above the month, as is shown in Figure 168. During one month the name of the month is entered only once in the date column.

The third column is for any explanation of the entry which the clerk wishes to record. Frequently this column is left blank.

The fourth column is known as the folio column. In it is entered the page of the book from which the entry was transferred. The letter to the left of this column indicates the name of the book, C standing for cash book, and S, for sales journal.

Posting. The book in which a transaction is first recorded is known as the book of original entry. It may be the cash book, the sales journal, the purchases journal, or one of the other books used by businesses in keeping a record of their transactions. 193March On account $ 15

FINANCIAL RECORDS 

The process of transferring entries from a book of original entry to a ledger is known as posting. At the time each entry is posted, the number of the page in the ledger to which the entry is transferred is entered in the folio column of the book of original entry. At the same time the number of the page of the book of original entry from which the entry is transferred is recorded in the folio column of the ledger. 

This makes it easier to trace an entry from the book of original entry to the ledger, or from the ledger to the book of original entry.

[ch. 23 

If a sales journal is not used but copies of the invoices or sales tickets are kept in a binder as a record of sales on account, the number of the invoice or ticket is entered in the folio column of the ledger. 

The difference between the debits and the credits of a customer's account is the amount which he owes the business. This is referred to as the balance of his account. 

Other Accounts. Accounts with customers are not the only accounts which a business keeps. It summarizes its transactions with creditors in accounts. In addition, accounts are used in collecting information about many of the activities of the business.

In this chapter we shall consider only the use of customers' accounts, for customers' accounts are usually more numerous than all other accounts. Furthermore, entries are made to these accounts and statements are prepared from them by office assistants who may not have studied bookkeeping. The other accounts are ordinarily used only by those having an understanding of bookkeeping and accounting principles.

Bookkeeping Machine. Entries in ledgers are frequently made with a machine known as a bookkeeping machine.

 Section III. STATEMENTS 

At intervals, usually at the end of each month, a business sends to each customer who has not paid for all his purchases a summary of his account. This summary is known as a statement of account or, more briefly, as a statement. 

This form shows: 

(1) The balance of the account when the last statement was mailed. If statements are sent monthly, this is the balance at the end of the previous month. (2) The amounts charged to the customer during the month. These amounts are found on the left side of the account. They arise from sales to the customer.

This is the sum of the original balance and the amounts charged the customer, less the amounts received from the customer.

A statement serves two purposes. First, it shows the customer how much he owes. Second, it gives the customer a complete record of his account in the merchant's ledger. 

He can prove the accuracy of the record by comparing the statement with his copies of sales tickets or invoices and credit memoranda, and with his record of payments. 

If an error is discovered, it can be called to the attention of the merchant at once so that it can be corrected.

FINANCIAL RECORDS Section IV. RECEIPTS 

Need for Proof of Payment. In our study of checks in 

Chapter III, we observed that when an indorsed check was returned to the drawer by the bank, it was valuable as a proof that payment had been made. Frequently a person needs some other method of proving that he has made payment. 

James Randolph, a high school pupil, paid Robert Tyler, the treasurer of a school club, twenty-five cents for dues. 

Robert forgot about this transaction and. tried to collect the amount from James a week later. James had nothing to prove that payment actually had been made. In fact, he was not absolutely certain that he had made payment, as it seemed possible to him that he had only intended to give the money to Robert, but had actually forgotten to do so. 

This example illustrates the need for a person's being able to prove that he has paid any amount which he was supposed to pay. Of course, he does not need to prove that payment was made when the complete transaction was for cash and no one made a record of it. If you have made a purchase in a cafeteria, you do not need anything to prove that payment was made since cash was paid immediately and the business did not record your name as a debtor. 

A written form acknowledging the receipt of money, which can be offered as proof that payment actually was made, is known as a receipt. A common form of receipt is shown in Figure 173. 

No. 10 

Date August 9, 193 To 

Hugh Davis 

For Dues 

Amount $2,0 

No. 10 

Received of Hugh Davis 

August 9, 193Two and 400

Dollars Dues 18200 

Woodward Athletic Club 

By G Fisher 

Figure 173-Receipt

This form provides spaces for the number of the receipt, the date, the name of the one from whom payment

[ch. 23 

was received, the amount in words, the reason for the payment, the amount in figures, and the signature of the one receiving payment. In the illustration, George Fisher, the treasurer of the Woodward Athletic Club, acknowledged the receipt of two dollars from Hugh Davis. 

Writing Receipts. Receipts may be bound in books with stubs, the same as checks. The stubs are filled out to show the number of the receipt, the date, the name of the payer, the reason for payment, and the amount. This information may be needed for reference by the one issuing the receipt. The receipt is written in ink with the same care as is used in writing checks. Each stub and receipt is numbered so that if there is a dispute about the amount paid, the receipt can easily be compared with the stub. 

Receipted Bills, Invoices, and Statements. Each month your electric light company sends to your home a bill for electricity used. If you take this bill to the office of the electric light company and pay the amount in cash, the clerk may write a receipt. It is more probable, however, that the
