FINANCIAL RECORDS 

Similarly, when invoices or statements are paid, the acknowledgment of the receipt of payment may be written or stamped on the invoice or statement and the form may be returned to the one making payment. Receipted bills, invoices, and statements are satisfactory as receipts from the point of view of the payer as they definitely show that payment has been made. Since these forms can be receipted more quickly than a receipt can be written, this method of acknowledging payments is frequently used. 

Canceled Drafts. When a remittance has been sent in the form of a draft, the one making payment does not receive the canceled draft with the signature of the payee. 

Pay to the order of 

John Hammond 

Nachaniel M. Mes Garland 

John Hammond

RUST COMPANY 

35-69 

JANUARY 29 

193No 51606 175.50

CFARLAND. 

175 AND 50CTS www.m HinHarris

Figure 175-Draft Showing Indorsement CASHIER 

The draft, which is the check of one bank on another bank, is returned to the issuing bank. One can, however, find out from this bank whether the draft has been indorsed by the payee and can thus prove whether the payee actually received the payment.

The value of the draft as a receipt is increased if the one buying the draft has it made payable to himself and then indorses it in full to the one to whom payment is to be made. The canceled draft then shows both the name of the

[ch. 23 

one making payment and the name of the one receiving payment.

The draft in Figure 175 was purchased by Nathaniel M. 

MacFarland to be sent to John Hammond. Mr. MacFarland had the draft made payable to himself and then indorsed it in full to Mr. Hammond. When Mr. Hammond cashed the draft, he indorsed it in blank. The indorsements show that 

Mr. MacFarland originally owned the draft, that he transferred it to Mr. Hammond, and that later Mr. Hammond cashed it. This increased the value of the canceled draft as a receipt for Mr. MacFarland.

Money Orders as Receipts. When a postal or express money order is purchased, the post office or express company gives the buyer a receipt together with the money order. The buyer sends the money order to the payee and retains the receipt. This receipt does not show that the payee actually received payment, but only that the order was purchased.

If there is a question regarding whether the payee received payment, the one holding the receipt can take it to the issuing office and can find out if the corresponding money order has been returned with the indorsement of the payee. If the money order has been returned with the indorsement of the payee, it is evident that he received payment. If the money order has not been returned, a duplicate can be obtained without extra charge. 

Keeping Receipts. Receipts, canceled checks, receipted bills, and all other forms showing that payments have been made should be kept carefully. If they are lost, the payer may have to pay again as he may not be able to prove that the debt was paid. In all cases, a receipt should be kept until the payer is sure that he will never have to prove that the payment referred to was made. Many people have caused themselves much trouble and expense by losing or destroying receipts.

CHAPTER XXIV SALESMANSHIP Section I. IMPORTANCE OF SALESMANSHIP 

Selling Merchandise. Business is directly dependent on salesmanship. Retail stores are organized and conducted solely for the purpose of selling merchandise. Almost all of their employees are salespeople. Unless merchandise is sold in large volume, such businesses cannot continue. 

In other than retail businesses, salesmanship is also of great importance. A factory may employ more mechanics than salesmen, but if its goods are not sold it cannot continue to operate. A mine may be chiefly concerned with the production of minerals, but some one must sell its product. 

The success of a grower of fruit may seem to depend upon the quantity and the quality of his product, but fruit growers through their associations are finding it necessary to give more and more attention to marketing. To produce excellent fruit in large quantities is not sufficient. The fruit also has to be sold profitably. 

Skill in salesmanship largely determines the success of all businesses producing or handling goods which must be sold. As practically all businesses are in this group, it is apparent that salesmanship is of fundamental importance. 

Selling Ideas. We most commonly think of salesmanship in connection with the selling of merchandise. But salesmanship is not of such a limited application. In many instances, the selling of an idea is tremendously important to an individual or a business.

If a person develops a new method of manufacturing a product, he must convince others that it is desirable before they will use it. Similarly, if a business executive plans a new campaign, develops a new product, or outlines a new operating procedure, he may have to convince his associates 385

[ch. 24 

and superiors that his ideas are valuable, before they will be accepted.

The development of business constantly requires new plans, new methods, and new products. The success of business is therefore largely controlled by the success which those interested in it have in selling their ideas to others. 

Selling Services. Success has always been achieved by those who render distinctive service. The merchant who best serves his customers secures the largest sales. The lawyer who best serves his clients has the greatest demand for his aid. The employee who best serves his employer secures most rapid advancement.

Although success comes to those who serve with distinction, it does not necessarily come to all those who are capable of rendering distinctive service. A merchant may have excellent merchandise reasonably priced, but if no one knows this, he may have but few customers. A lawyer may have great legal ability, but if this is not known by prospective clients, they may go elsewhere for legal aid. An employee may be able to do much more difficult work than he is doing, but the employer may not know this and may give the more important position to another. 

It is therefore evident that it is not enough for a person to be able to do important work. He must also be able to convince others that he can do this work and that they should engage him to do it for them.

Summary. A person or a business may have merchandise, ideas, or services to sell. The merchandise may be excellent, the ideas may be sound, or the services may be valuable. But success comes only when others are convinced that they should secure these goods, ideas, or services. Salesmanship is therefore of fundamental importance to every business and to every individual. Ț is important to an individual even if he is not engaged in business, for everyone has ideas which he wishes others to adopt or services which he wishes others to use.

ch. 24] 387 

SALESMANSHIP

[ch. 24 

 Section II. PERSONALITY 

Importance of Personality. Did you ever buy anything from a salesperson with whom you especially liked to deal? 

And, on the other hand, did you ever deal with a clerk who did not please you? If so, you have had very common experiences. Many people always look for one clerk in a store because they prefer to deal with him. In fact, they may go to that store rather than another because they wish that salesperson to serve them.

It is probable that one of the reasons why one clerk is preferred above others is that he has an attractive personality. What we mean by the term personality cannot be stated with exactness. It is obvious, however, that it includes all one's characteristics which are apparent to others. 

If a person has good health, is attractive in appearance, is fairly well educated, is pleasant and courteous, and is strictly honest in his dealings with others, we are likely to say that he has a good personality. If, however, a person is careless about his appearance, if he uses incorrect English, if he is discourteous or dishonest, we probably say that he has a poor personality. It may thus be seen that personality is the combination of the qualities that appeal to or repel others.

Since one's personality tends to attract or repel others, it is extremely important. This is true whether a person wishes to sell merchandise, ideas, or services. It is therefore true of practically everyone, both those engaged in business and those following other pursuits. 

Health. Health may have more to do with personality than any other one factor, for health affects one's appearance, enthusiasm, courage, disposition, and mental ability. 

Since good health is such an important part of personality, and since personality is such an important factor in selling, every person should give most careful attention to his health. This does not mean that he should be thinking of his health continually; but it does mean that he should take reasonable care of himself, that he should eat moder-

ch. 24] 389 

SALESMANSHIP 

ately, sleep regularly, and take a sufficient amount of exercise.

Personal Appearance. If one is to make a favorable impression on others, he should have a reasonably attractive appearance. This is especially true if his work requires that he frequently meet strangers, for one's first impression is largely the result of appearance. One's appearance is important in all cases, for it is noticed even by those with whom one associates most frequently. 

A satisfactory personal appearance does not depend upon whether one is tall or short, fat or lean, or whether one's features are handsome and his clothing is expensive. 

From the point of view of the salesman, one's appearance is judged largely by neatness and cleanliness. An inexpensive suit of clothes well pressed is more desirable than an expensive suit of which little care has been taken. A wrinkled necktie or shoes run down at the heel detract more from one's personal appearance than can be added by the expensiveness or the attractiveness of other articles of clothing. 

Not only is carelessness in one's dress undesirable because it is unattractive to others, but it is also thought by many to indicate careless habits of thought. It is believed that a person careless in one thing may also be careless in others, and that he is not to be trusted with important affairs.

Some phases of a good personality are hard to develop, but a reasonably attractive appearance can usually be secured with a moderate amount of effort. Attention to his appearance should therefore be given by every ambitious person.

Self-Confidence. If others are to have a good opinion of a person's ability, he must have confidence in himself. To a great extent, one's estimate of himself is accepted, particularly if his actions seem to indicate that the estimate is fair. 

To be self-confident one need not be boastful nor believe that he has unusual or marked ability. He does need to believe that he is as important as others, and that he can do at least some things as well as they.

[ch. 24 

As being self-assured is wholly a state of mind, one can do much to improve this phase of his personality. Selfassurance can be increased by developing the habit of acting as if one were confident.

When talking to another, a person should stand erect and look directly at him. Nervous movement and a twisting about indicate a lack of poise and confidence. One should always develop the habit of speaking clearly and distinctly, as if he considered what he had to say to be of value. Nothing indicates a lack of confidence more than a low, hesitating and indistinct voice.

A person can further develop his self-confidence by completing each task he undertakes. Many people start something, partly finish it, and then stop. They get in the habit of doubting their own ability for no reason except that they do not persevere until their work is done. 

In their school work, such persons study until they have partially mastered their lessons, but they do not put forth the last bit of effort required to complete those lessons. Because of their laziness, they eventually acquire the feeling that they do not have the ability of other people. Similarly, they do a piece of work assigned them sufficiently well for it to be accepted, but they do not continue working until the task is done as well as they can do it. Eventually they come to believe that they cannot do better work, and thus lose their self-confidence.

Enthusiasm. Enthusiasm and interest are also essential parts of a good personality. A pupil who is without interest in things about him and who has no enthusiasm about his school work will not be efficient. Others will also be inclined to doubt his effectiveness in getting work done and will hesitate to trust him with important duties. 

A person can develop an enthusiastic attitude by taking a real interest in all phases of his school and business life. 

The qualities of interest and enthusiasm should be developed, as employers are looking for assistants who will take an interest in their work. Such employees learn most rapidly and do their work most efficiently.

ch. 24] 391 

SALESMANSHIP 

Honesty. You like to associate and trade with those on whom you can depend. So do others. Some faults may be overlooked, but a person who is not dependable or who deceives will not be liked and others will not wish to associate or trade with him. On the other hand, a reputation for honesty and dependability adds greatly to one's standing. 

If a person has a reputation for complete honesty, his success as a salesman will be increased, for others will believe the statements he makes. It makes no difference whether one is selling merchandise, services, or ideas; he cannot do one thing which will do more to insure complete success than steadily building his reputation by being entirely honest at all times.

Cheerfulness. A seemingly minor characteristic which is of real importance is cheerfulness. One who is pleasant in his dealings with others is always more likely to be successful than one who is not. 

Cheerfulness or pleasantness does not require talkativeness or a continued and forced smile. A person can be serious and work hard and still be pleasant in his relations with others. He can speak pleasantly and refrain from showing irritation when things are not exactly as he would like them to be.

It is difficult for one to be pleasant if he does not feel well. This further emphasizes the necessity of maintaining one's health. A person who is in poor health may be handicapped in not being able to do his best work; he may be handicapped even more by not being able to get along well with others. In attempting to develop a habit of cheerfulness, one should give first attention to his health. This may affect his attitude to others and may control his success. 

Courtesy. If a person is pleasant, he has a good start towards courtesy, as courtesy requires that a person be pleasant in his dealings with others. Courtesy, however, is more than pleasantness. It includes a consideration of others which will make one respectful and helpful. A courteous person is always on the lookout for opportunities of

[ch. 24 

helping others. He respects the rights of others and will not do things which will interfere with their work or make them less happy. Courtesy as well as cheerfulness can be developed. It is worth while to make an effort to develop these qualities as they are valuable business assets. 

Summary. We frequently think of one's personality as being something over which he has no control. But a study of the characteristics of a good personality, given in the preceding paragraphs, indicates that one's personality is largely made up of factors which are completely under his control.

Since a good personality is so important, any one wishing to achieve success should give much thought to methods of improving his personality. By so doing, he can greatly increase his chance of achieving success.

1. What is meant by the term personality? 

2. Why is personality important? 

3. Is a good personality more necessary for a salesman than for other people? 

4. Some retail stores require clerks to wear simple clothing or uniforms. Give reasons for this. 

5. Charles Reed wishes to hire a bookkeeper. He will not consider the applications of those who are not careful about their personal appearance. The bookkeeper will never come in contact with those doing business with the company. Under these circumstances, why does the bookkeeper's personal appearance make any difference?

ch. 24] 393 

SALESMANSHIP Section III. KNOWLEDGE 

Importance of Knowledge. A person, however fine his personality, is likely to be limited in the success he achieves in proportion to the limitations of his knowledge. There are, of course, certain types of knowledge which are more useful in some circumstances than others. It is difficult to think of any kind of knowledge which might not be useful occasionally. Other kinds are of fundamental value and are useful with great frequency to almost any one. 

English. Language is probably the most important tool which a salesman uses. Only by means of written or oral words, can a person effectively describe what he has to sell, persuade another to buy, and make necessary terms of sale. 

Reports, letters, and personal interviews, all require the use of language.

A person is judged by his speech just as he is judged by his appearance. Probably you have dealt with others who spoke carelessly or ungrammatically. You may have observed that instead of paying attention to the thoughts they were trying to convey, you were considering the mistakes in grammar or the crudeness of the language. Surely you did not respect their knowledge and follow their suggestions as fully as if their use of English had been more accurate. A person who wishes to achieve success should therefore attain as complete a mastery of the English language as he can. He should not be satisfied until he has an understanding of grammar, a large vocabulary, and correct pronunciation. 

Knowledge of Goods. A salesman is expected to sell merchandise, services, or ideas. He must, then, convince others that they should purchase his goods, use his services, or accept his ideas. It is apparent that if he is to be successful he must have full information about what he wishes to sell. For how can he convince others that they should buy his merchandise if he does not know its values? Or how can he convince others that they should use his services if he does not know just what he can do?

[ch. 24 

You probably have often observed so-called salesmen who were simply order-takers. They could not point out the advantages of their goods for they did not know what they were. If questions were asked, they were not able to answer, or their answers were hesitating or inaccurate. The success of these persons was certain to be limited. 

A salesman also needs to know about competing goods. 

There is little advantage in his calling to the attention of his prospective customer advantages of his goods, which his competitors' goods have to an equal or greater degree. 

On the other hand, it is better for him to emphasize the features of his goods which are superior to those of his competitors. Obviously, he cannot do this if he does not know his own goods and the goods offered by others. 

General Knowledge. A salesman must always guide the thinking of others. It is impossible to tell in advance what questions will be asked, what subjects will be discussed, or what ideas will be of greatest influence. The broader the salesman's knowledge, the more surely he will be able to answer questions or discuss subjects which will be of interest to and which will influence prospective customers. To a salesman, all knowledge is useful.

Obtaining Knowledge. Much of the knowledge which one needs to be a successful salesman of goods, services, or ideas, can be obtained most easily or quickly by studying subjects offered in school. It is true that the training in school can be supplemented. General knowledge can be secured from magazines, newspapers, and books. A knowledge of one's firm may be obtained from the firm's manual for employees. A knowledge of goods can be secured from fellow salesmen, from manufacturers' bulletins describing the goods, from trade journals, and from books obtained from the public library.

Since much knowledge can be secured outside of school, the person who wishes to become a successful salesman never limits his gathering of knowledge to school only. But knowledge can be secured more quickly in school than out.

ch. 24] 395 

SALESMANSHIP 

If, therefore, a pupil who desires to become a salesman is to have the greatest success, he will continue in school as long as possible. This is especially true in recent years since competition is each year becoming keener and greater skill is needed if one is to attain success. 

1. Explain how a knowledge of general science or commercial geography may be valuable to a salesman. 

2. To which of the following is a mastery of English most important? Give reasons for your answer. (a) An attorney (b) A bookkeeper (c) An engineer (d) A salesman (e) A timekeeper 3. Carl Gurber, a salesman of fine rugs, is studying the manufacture and designing of rugs. In what way will this help him as a salesman?

This decreased his selfconfidence and made it difficult for him to obtain a reasonable number of sales. In what way might he increase his effectiveness as a salesman?

[ch. 24 

 Section IV. MAKING THE SALE 

Introduction. A person who has a good personality and who has information about what is to be sold has a good foundation for selling. But still the sale is to be made. The art of actually making the sale involves many aspects and might be discussed at great length. There are, however, a few fundamental principles which apply in practically all cases.

Selling One's Self. The first step in making a sale is to sell one's self thoroughly on the merits of his proposition. If a person is not thoroughly convinced that merchandise is desirable, that an idea is sound, or that a service is valuable, his chances of convincing others are slight. 

Perhaps you have noticed some one make a suggestion as if he were not sure that it was a good one, and have seen how little progress the suggestion made. Similarly, failure attends sales effort which is made half-heartedly or without conviction. If a person has any doubts about what he is selling, he should first convince himself. Then he will be in a position to convince others. 

Buyer's Viewpoint. A successful salesman is one who has his customers' interests at heart. The insurance salesman who is, in the long run, the most successful is the one who sells his customers the protection they actually need. 

The clothing merchant who is most successful is the one who sells his customers clothing that actually is attractive and durable. Likewise, other salesmen who are most successful consider what their customers really need. A person who feels that a salesman is not primarily interested in making a sale, but rather in securing for him goods or services that it is to his advantage to have, has confidence in the salesman and will buy from him most freely.

Information. A salesman may say that a pair of stockings will wear unusually well, that a necktie will give exceptional service, or that an overcoat is of the best style. Such general claims mean little to a prospective customer. A sale

ch. 24] 397 

SALESMANSHIP 

is more probable if the salesman tells why the stockings will wear well, why the necktie will give good service, or why the overcoat may be considered an exceptionally good style. Definite information helps a person to reach a decision and should always be given by the salesman. 

Method of Presentation. A salesman should be enthusiastic, for his enthusiasm is likely to be contagious and to inspire enthusiasm on the part of the customer. He should present his facts clearly, for the prospect does not know as much about the merchandise as he does and may therefore not understand its advantages readily. He must take sufficient time so that the customer does not feel that he is being hurried, and yet not so much time that the customer loses interest. He must present his facts in a non-argumentative style, for argument is liable to cause the customer to take the other side of the question and to become irritated. He must always remember to present his subject in as attractive a form as possible so that the subject itself will appear attractive.

Objections. The one to whom the sale is being made may have some objections. He may say that the price is too high, the quality poor, the style undesirable, or the suggestion unwise. A person's skill as a salesman is largely measured by his skill in meeting such objections. He may be tempted to argue the points raised. If he does argue, he may anger his prospect and thus entirely lose the chance of making the sale. He should avoid directly contradicting the prospect but should give additional information about the subject which will cause the prospect to change his mind or to overlook his previous objection. 

Positive Suggestions. The reaction a person secures to any proposition or suggestion depends much on the words he uses. If he suggests a negative answer, he probably will receive such an answer. If he says, "You wouldn't want to buy this, would you?" he probably will be answered "No," for that is the answer suggested. Similarly, if he says, "The price of this is not high," "You don't like this, do you?" or

[ch. 24 

lo "This proposition is not unattractive," he is less likely to secure a favorable response than if he said, "The price of this is quite reasonable," "Isn't this beautiful?" or "This proposition is unusually attractive."

A salesman should always remember that he must guide the thinking of the prospect so that the prospect will think as he does. He can do this most successfully if all of his suggestions are positive, that is, if he makes it easy for the prospect to agree with him. 

Summary. Success in selling is more than the work of a moment. A person must first develop a good personality and secure a complete knowledge about what he has to sell. 

Then if he presents his proposition clearly, enthusiastically and tactfully, he probably will be successful in securing agreement and the action desired. 

1. A salesman in a paint store believes that a neighboring store is selling a better grade of paint at a more reasonable price than that offered in his store. In what way will this affect his success as a salesman? 

2. What is meant by a salesman's having the "buyer's viewpoint"?

CHAPTER XXV  Section I. A DESIRABLE POSITION 

Introduction. When a person wants a position, any position that he can get may seem desirable. To a certain extent it is. Any job is better than no job at all. Yet it is apparent that some jobs are more desirable than others. 

Obviously, a person's chances of securing a desirable position are increased if he knows what a good position is. If he does know what a good position is, he may look for one of that type; otherwise, he will simply be looking for work and will be as likely to secure an undesirable position as a desirable one.

Characteristics of a Desirable Position. Short hours of work, good pay, and pleasant surroundings may seem to be the most important characteristics of a desirable position. 

But in the long run these are not of greatest importance. 

The desirability of a position can be measured, first, by the amount that can be learned in that position; second, by one's ability for, and interest in, the work required; third, by the possibility of financial return; and, fourth, by the opportunities for advancement. 

Amount to be Learned. A person who wishes to be an elevator operator can soon completely master all the details of this job. He may start in the morning to learn how to be an elevator operator and may become reasonably efficient before evening. He has then learned almost all there is to know about his position. Although he may be a good elevator operator, he cannot demand high wages since another person can be secured to take his place at any time. Only a few hours at the most will be required to train his successor. 

In a time of poor business, he may be discharged or his salary decreased. His small value to the firm makes it of 401

[ch. 25 

little importance whether or not he stays. If he holds his position for ten years, he will not be any more valuable at that time than before, as he will not have been learning anything new about his position. Furthermore, he will not be in any better position to secure a new job as his experience will not have taught him anything about any other kind of work. Such a job is commonly called a "blind alley job" as it does not lead anywhere. It is to be avoided the same as a street marked "no outlet" is to be avoided by one who wishes to travel across a city.

Many other positions are similar in that they give a person small chance to increase his ability through learning more about his work or the work done by others. Such jobs are not desirable; they pay little, offer irregular employment, and do not give one an opportunity to prepare himself for advancement.

A position may not pay very well and may require much hard work under conditions not altogether pleasant. It is desirable, however, if it offers one the opportunity to learn more and to increase his value to the firm. Learning the methods used by a business not only will make a person more valuable in his present position but also will fit him for better positions. If there is no opportunity for promotion in the office in which he happens to be located, increased knowledge about business methods will assist him in securing a position with a different firm. 

The position of elevator operator may be contrasted with that of general office assistant. Both positions may be filled by inexperienced persons with little training. They differ in that the elevator operator has little or no opportunity of increasing his value, while the general office assistant can learn much about office procedure and business methods, which will increase his ability and earning power.

The first measure, then, of the desirability of a position is how much can be learned which will be of value in other positions. A position not offering opportunity of this kind should not be taken except as a temporary means of

ch. 25] 403 

making a living while a more desirable position is being secured.

Ability and Interest. In selecting a position, one should also consider ability and interest. A person will never be happy doing work for which he is not fitted and which does not interest him. There is much drudgery about any type of work. Some people drift from position to position simply because they are lazy and do not care to work at anything. 

They mistake laziness for lack of suitability. A normal person should, however, secure some enjoyment from his work. 

A person may have to do work which does not particularly interest him in order to qualify for a better position. 

But if a position does not interest him and does not give any opportunity for promotion to more interesting work, it should be avoided as it will not prove desirable. Success depends largely on the energy and enthusiasm put into a job. It is difficult to work energetically at some job which is not attractive. Furthermore, so much of a person's time is spent at his work that a large part of his happiness depends on whether or not he enjoys his occupation. 

Possibility of Financial Return. A third measure of the desirability of a position is the amount of profit which is being made by the firm and the department in which the position is located. If the firm is prosperous, it is likely to be growing. When a firm is growing, new positions are continually being established and the opportunity for promotion is increased. Also, a firm which is making a satisfactory profit is usually able to pay its employees better wages than one which is not prosperous.

Similarly, in a large business, one department may be making good profits while another department may be unprofitable. The one which is earning profits is the one most likely to be expanded and to offer opportunities for advancement. If the less profitable department is continued, it is not probable that many new positions will be made in it or that wages will be greatly increased.

Even if the profits of departments are not recorded separately, one department may be more desirable than others

[ch. 25 

as it may be more important. In a factory, the production department is usually more important than the purchasing; in a retail store, the selling department is more important than the delivery. The more important department is likely to pay the better wages since the success of the business depends on it to the larger extent. 

Opportunities for Advancement. The fourth measure of the desirability of a position is the opportunities it offers for advancement. This depends, first, on whether the results of one's work can be seen, and second, on whether the position trains one for better positions. 

When one works under the supervision of a foreman who is not closely connected with the management, he is not likely to have much chance for promotion. The foreman does not have many positions under him to which he can make promotions. He also does not have the authority to make transfers to other types of work for which a person may be qualified. One is better off in a position directly under someone in authority, even if the pay is small, than he would be in a better paying position where his work would not be noticed. In the former his chances for promotion are much better than they would be in the latter. 

One's opportunities for advancement are also increased if the position he occupies trains him for more responsible positions. A person may do good work and this may be observed, but if he is not qualified for a better position, promotion can scarcely be granted. 

Summary. Before a person definitely accepts a permanent position, he should ask himself: If I take this job, what is the best that can possibly happen? What is the worst than can possibly happen? What can normally be expected to happen?

These three questions should be answered for each of the four standards by which a position can be judged, that is, the amount to be learned, the ability and interest one has in this kind of work, the possibility of financial return, and the opportunities for advancement. A study of this

ch. 25] 405 

kind will show the position which, in the long run, will be most satisfactory. Such a study is worth much more than the short time required for it, because one's progress is greatly influenced by the position he holds. 

1. George Taylor applied for a position in the Harris

Department Store, but it had nothing to offer him except a position as an elevator operator. He refused this position as he believed that it would give him no opportunity for advancement. Do you consider that he was justified in making this decision? 

2. Three months later George Taylor accepted a position as a cashier in the Smithfield Cafeteria. The amount charged each customer was registered on a small slip which was presented to George together with the payment. It was his duty to make change for customers and to place in the cash register the amounts received. What were the advantages and disadvantages of a position of this kind? 

3. Later George was offered a position as an assistant clerk in the office of the Rudolph Grocery Company which operated a chain of twenty-six grocery stores. The pay in this position was $2 a week less than that which he was receiving at the Smithfield Cafeteria. Do you believe that it was advisable for him to consider making a change? 

4. George Taylor was warned by a friend not to take "a blind alley job." What did his friend mean? What do you consider to be the characteristics of this kind of a job? 

5. Why should a person attempt to secure work which he finds interesting? 

6. William Ogden is offered a position in the office of the 

Hoyman Manufacturing Company and another in the office of the Brookhart Manufacturing Company. The two companies are in the same line of business and offer the same beginning wages. The Hoyman Manufacturing Company is not very old but is making rapid progress, and it seems probable that it will grow considerably in the future. The Brookhart Company is an old company with rather inefficient management. Its business has been gradually decreasing for several years. The plant of the Brookhart Company is nearer William's home, so he would find it more convenient to work there. Which position do you suggest that he accept? Why?

[ch. 25 

7. Hugh Kennedy is offered the position as a time keeper for a group of men doing construction work for Morrison and Company. He is also offered the position as stenographer for the superintendent of construction work. The two positions pay exactly the same and he is qualified to fill either. Which do you recommend that he accept? He 8. Arnold Smith is offered his choice of two positions by the Benedict Company. He can learn the work in one position in a few minutes. The pay is $15 a week. The other position requires a knowledge of the general office procedure which can not be well mastered in less than a month. is offered $10 a week for the first month, with the assurance that his pay will be raised to $12 for the second month and $15 for the third month, if he does the work satisfactorily. Which of these two positions do you recommend that Arnold take? 

9. Helen Reed learns of two positions, one as a typist and the other as an assistant bookkeeper. The position of typist will pay $15 a week and that of assistant bookkeeper, $12 a week. Helen believes that she is sufficiently skilled as a typist to fill either position, but that she has much more abil. ity in bookkeeping and accounting work than in typing. 

Which position should she seek? Give reasons for your answer. 

10. Thelma Moffett, a stenographer, learns of two positions for which she might apply. One is in a small office where she will do all of the stenographic and clerical work: the other is in a large office employing fifty stenographers. 

For which position should she apply (a) if the same salary is offered? (b) if the small office offers a salary of $2 less than the large office? (c) if the small office offers a salary of $2 more than the large office?

ch. 25] 407 

 Section II. QUALIFYING FOR A POSITION 

Introduction. After a person has decided whether a position is desirable, he is faced with the problem of securing the position. Employers are always looking for employees who can earn profits for the business. It is therefore the applicant's task to convince the employer that it would be to his, the employer's, advantage to hire the applicant. If the applicant can do this, he will have no difficulty in securing a position.

Education. One seeking a position is a salesman who wishes to sell his services. Obviously, the more he has to sell, that is, the more valuable his services are, the more likely he is to make a sale at a satisfactory price. 

For example, if you were to seek a position at the end of this school year, you would find that positions requiring much skill were not open to you. You could, of course, do certain kinds of clerical work, but the wages for such work would be low. On the other hand, if you continue in school for several years and obtain a training in bookkeeping and accounting, stenography, or salesmanship, the number of positions available for you will be greatly increased, and the wages offered will be much higher. The time spent in school will then bring you an increased income which will much more than offset the amount which might have been earned during those years.

Such courses as bookkeeping and accounting, stenography, and salesmanship are not the only ones which are valuable to one who wishes to qualify for a good position. 

It is true that these courses definitely train a person for desirable business work. Other courses, however, which are of a more general nature and which do not seem to train one for a specific job are also valuable.

It is generally recognized that a person well trained can learn the duties of a position more quickly and perform them more efficiently than an individual without an education. The services of such a person are therefore preferred. 

For this reason, one should continue in school as long as pos-

[ch. 25 

sible. A junior high school graduate has a better opportunity of securing a business position than a person who dropped out of school earlier. A senior high school graduate has a better opportunity of securing a desirable position than a junior high school graduate. And one who graduates from college has an even better opportunity than either of the two.

A general education is valuable as it increases a person's knowledge and gives a foundation which helps him in mastering new methods and principles. A definite training in business combined with a general education is even more valuable as it gives the information which is required in business and thus enables one to increase his value to the employer more rapidly.

Obtaining an Education Outside of School. A person who is not able to continue in school as long as he would like should not be discouraged as he is not permanently handicapped. He probably will not progress as rapidly as the one who has been in school longer. He will have greater difficulties to overcome. But he should be able to attain success eventually. In many cases he can attend a night school or a continuation school and thus continue his education. In every case he can make a study of the work he and his associates are doing and thus learn much which will increase his value to his employer.

Many people with limited education have succeeded in business. This has not been because of their lack of training but because they refused to let it become a permanent handicap. They applied themselves to the mastering of the details of the business with greater energy and thus qualified for advancement or the securing of a more desirable position. 

Personality. An employer is also interested in the prospective employee's personality. As was explained in the preceding chapter, an individual with a good personality will be most valuable to the organization because he will best serve its customers. He will also be able to work most satisfactorily with others in the organization. This will increase his value to the company, because a smoothly work-

ch. 25] 409 

ing organization can accomplish more than one which does not work without friction.

A person with a good personality will also be best able to present reasons why he should be hired. It is therefore apparent that everyone should carefully improve his personality according to the methods previously suggested. 

1. The office manager of Songer & Co. is planning to hire an assistant bookkeeper and a stenographer. The combined salaries of the two will be $45 a week. Why should 

Songer & Co. go to this expense? 

2. Why should an applicant for a position be interested in the amount of profit he can earn for his employer? 

3. Songer & Co. require each applicant to fill out a blank giving his training and experience. Why is this information desired? 

4. The work required of the assistant bookkeeper will at first be quite simple. The details can be learned in less than a day. But Songer & Co. will consider only the applications of those who are high school graduates. Suggest reasons for this. 

5. Two applicants are of about equal ability and training except that only one of them has had a thorough course in bookkeeping. The position of assistant bookkeeper, however, does not require a knowledge of bookkeeping principles, as the work done is limited to the recording of sales. 

Which of the two would you recommend that Songer & Co. hire? Why? 

6. John Jones wishes to work in the advertising department of a department store when he has finished high school. He is considering taking a business course in high school. His school offers only a one-semester course in advertising. Do you believe that he should enroll for the commercial course when such a small part of it deals with the subject in which he is interested? Why? 

7. George Hawthorn suggests that education is unnecessary for success. He believes this because he knows a number of men who have succeeded with only a small amount of school training. Do you agree with Mr. Hawthorn? Give reasons for your answer. 

8. Gerald Smith says that he has no chance to succeed in business as he was forced to leave school before he had completed the last two years of the high school course. Do you believe that this is true? Why?

[ch. 25 

 Section III. APPLYING FOR A POSITION 

Locating a Vacancy. Before one applies for a position, he must locate a place where his services may be used. 

One of the common ways of getting information about openings is through suggestions of friends and relatives. 

If a person lets his friends in business know that he desires a position, they can, in many cases, tell him of openings or where openings will occur a little later. Friends may be able to give personal recommendations which are of great value to an applicant. The one making the recommendation may not have much influence, but if he is known to the employer, his statements will be considered. 

Sometimes schools conduct employment departments through which the graduates are located in positions. When such a department is conducted, it is advisable for a student to cooperate with it as it will try to locate a position which will be most suitable for him. 

When neither of the methods given above can be followed, positions are frequently secured by placing applications with a number of representative firms which may have openings similar to the one desired. 

There are many changes among the employees of a large business. This makes possible the hiring of an additional person with desirable qualifications at almost any time. 

Newspapers provide another excellent means of getting in touch with desirable positions. Daily newspapers have columns of classified advertisements. One section

Figure 176 42 

Part of a "Help Wanted" Column from a Daily Newspaper

ch. 25] 411 

is for "Help Wanted." In this section employers enter small announcements of vacancies. They are classified and indexed to help a person locate the position in which he is most interested. An application for such a position should be made as soon as possible after the paper is issued, for many other persons may also make applications.

If one can find no suitable positions in the "Help Wanted" columns, he may advertise in the "Positions Wanted" columns. Such advertising is especially effective when one has unusual qualifications. 

Commercial employment agencies also help many people in locating positions. A charge is made for their services. 

An agency usually deals with only one general class of employees, such as unskilled labor, household servants, or office employees, so one should carefully choose the agency with which he registers. One should also select an agency with care since they are not all equal in standards or in the effectiveness of their services.

The Application. After a person has learned of an opening, his task is to convince the employer that he should be hired. This requires the use of the selling methods described in the preceding chapter. The applicant must first consider just what he has to offer, and then how the value of his services can be presented most convincingly. 

A Personal Application. When making a personal application, one should be very careful of his appearance. He will be observed more closely at this time than at any other. 

Poorly combed hair, unclean hands, and a general lack of care in appearance have prevented many people from securing positions for which they were well qualified. Extreme styles should also be avoided as they may not be attractive to the prospective employer.

A Letter of Application. Sometimes a written applica tion is necessary. Some positions are so far away from the applicant's home that a personal interview is not possible unless he is practically certain of securing the position. In other cases, those advertising positions to be filled require written applications and give personal interviews only to those whose letters seem to indicate the desired ability.

[ch. 25 

If a letter is written, particular care should be taken with its appearance. It will be examined carefully by the reader. Incorrect arrangement, misspelled words, a lack of neatness, or a lack of clearness will be noticed. As the employer will expect the letter of application to be the best work of the applicant, it will be judged accordingly. 

In writing the letter, the applicant should follow a standard form for a business letter, such as was shown in Chapter XIX. Plain white paper, business size, should be used. Unusual or individual stationery and a peculiar style may be used in personal correspondence and occasionally in business correspondence, but they should be avoided in a letter of application.

Address for the Letter of Application. When one knows the name of the firm or individual to whom a letter of application is to be sent, the letter should be addressed in the ordinary form used for a business letter. But occasionally the name of the firm or individual is not known. In the clipping from a help-wanted column on page 410, the names of the advertisers are not given in the first, third, and fifth advertisements. In these cases, those advertising do not care to be bothered by personal applications, but prefer to receive all applications through the mail. 

The addresses on the envelope and on the inside of the letter should correspond to the signature in the advertisement. In the first advertisement the signature is N 306,

Commercial Tribune. The newspaper in which the advertisement appeared was the Commercial Tribune. This signature therefore means that the application is to be addressed to N 306, Commercial Tribune. The newspaper will then forward the application to the proper person. 

If the application is not to go to a newspaper, the advertiser may give only a post-office box number. This was the method followed in the fifth advertisement on page 410.

In such a case the applicant will use only the box number and the names of the city and the state in the address. 

When a person applies for one of these positions, he does not know whether his letter is intended for a firm or

ch. 25] 413 

an individual. In such cases the salutation "Gentlemen" is considered to be satisfactory.

Content of the Letter of Application. A letter of appli cation is primarily a sales letter. It usually comes into competition with many other letters, some of which may be well written. If it is to be successful, it must catch the attention of the reader by its attractiveness, the clearness with which it is written, and any special features which are characteristic of the writer. It must hold the reader's attention by its interesting style. And the facts which it contains must be such as will convince the employer that the applicant should be hired. The letter should be reasonably brief, but not so brief that it does not fully carry its message.

A typical letter of application is shown in Figure 177. 

This letter was satisfactory for the applicant, but other applicants should not necessarily model their letters too closely after this, or after any other illustration. The best results are secured when a person's letter is somewhat individual, that is, when it is written in much the same way as he would talk in a personal interview.

Although an applicant should not follow any certain form in writing a letter of application, he should include certain essential information which is commonly desired. 

He should give complete information about: (a) His education. The employer will wish to know the .schools which the applicant has attended, whether he is a graduate, and something of his record in school. (b) His business experience. Information about the kinds of work done, the length of time in each position, and the names of previous employers is desirable. (c) His age. (d) His special qualifications for the position desired. (e) The names of those to whom the employer can write regarding the applicant's ability, industry, and character. 

It is much better to give the names of those to whom the employer can refer than to send copies of letters of recommention with the application.

[ch. 25

The letter of application need not be limited to a discussion of these subjects but can include any other information which the applicant thinks will influence the employer. The applicant should remember that his letter of application is a sales letter. In other words, he should write as effective a sales letter as possible and should include in it all statements which he believes will help to convince the employer that his services should be secured.

1. Lester Shaw is graduating from high school this year and wishes a position in the office of one of the insurance companies in his city. Suggest methods which he might use in locating a desirable position. 

2. Andrew Hagan wishes a position as a clerk or assistant bookkeeper in an office. Suggest methods which he might use in locating a desirable position.

4. The A. W. Hodge Company wishes to hire three stenographers. It advertises in the "Help Wanted" columns of the local newspapers. It does not give the company name but requests that written applications be sent to a certain number in care of the newspaper. Give reasons why this plan is followed.

7. What facts should be given in a letter of application?

[ch. 25 

 Section IV. QUALIFYING FOR PROMOTION 

Introduction. A person, no matter how well trained he may be, is of limited value to a business until he has learned, through experience, the details of that particular business. 

He ordinarily starts at a low salary in a comparatively unimportant position; then, as he becomes more proficient he may do more important work for which he should receive a better salary. Securing promotion is, therefore, at least as important as securing a position, and should be given as much thought.

Some employees believe they should be promoted because they have been with a company for a long time, or because they cannot live on the amount they are earning. These facts may be considered by a business, but they are not most important in fixing salaries. The night watchman may have been in the employ of the business longer than any other person, but others will be paid more; an inefficient clerk may find it hard to live on his salary, but he will not for this reason be paid more than those who do better work. 

The value of a person to his firm determines how rapidly and how greatly his responsibility and salary will be increased.

Knowledge. A thorough training is even more necessary to a person when he qualifies for promotion than it was when he applied for his first job. Many companies require that all new employees first do routine work of relatively little importance. A person who has had only a limited training may be able to do this work with almost as much skill as a person whose training is very complete, but promotion will not come with the same rapidity.

Two stenographers may begin working for a company at the same time and for the same salary. Both may be equally proficient in shorthand, but one may have a much better training in English, letter-writing, and the fundamentals of business than the other. Obviously, this person will more quickly be promoted to a secretarial or executive position. The other person may be able to write letters as

ch. 25] 417 

they are dictated, but her training will not permit her to undertake readily the duties of a more advanced position. 

Similarly, two boys may begin working about the same time and at the same salary in the accounting department of a large business. Both may keep simple clerical records which can be readily mastered by one with a slight amount of training. One may have barely enough training to enable him to do this work, while the other may have a complete training in bookkeeping and accounting. Obviously, the latter person will be able to gain promotion more readily, for he will understand the records and will be able to master quickly the duties of a more responsible position. 

These are two examples of the many ways in which a thorough training will aid a person in gaining promotion. 

For those who wish to succeed in business, the training can not be too complete.

Personality. A good personality is as essential in securing promotion as it is in securing a position. A person with a poor personality is not a desirable employee in an office as he may not favorably impress customers and may not cooperate with other employees. A person desirous of promotion should therefore attempt to improve his personality, as was explained in Chapter XXIV. 

To one who is seeking a position, the phases of his personality which are readily observable are extremely important. To one who is seeking promotion, other characteristics may be of equal or greater importance. 

Honesty. No one wants a dishonest employee. Small tricks to defraud the employer or a customer are sufficient to prevent a person from being given a promotion. Stealing money or goods is usually cause for dismissal. An employee may also steal from his employer by being late at his work, by wasting his own time and the time of others in needless conversation, and in many other ways. While these actions may not cause his dismissal, they certainly will lessen his chances for promotion. When an employer is seeking some one for a position of responsibility, he chooses a person

[ch. 25 

whom he feels to be honest. A person who wishes to advance regularly should be honest in all matters.

Courage. Many people are afraid to take responsibility and thereby make their promotion impossible. They are afraid that they will make mistakes, so they never decide anything for themselves; they continually ask questions which they should answer for themselves. Courage is similar to self-confidence, but it is more important because a person may be quite certain he is right and have confidence in his own judgment but be afraid to go ahead and act. A wise employer wants employees who are willing to act on their judgment and who do not need too close supervision. 

But if one

Courage cannot be acquired all at once. makes a habit of acting on his own decisions when he has reason to believe that they are sound, he will in time secure a freedom from timidity which will be valuable to him. 

Industry. Both the quantity and the quality of one's work are considered by an employer. Some people, who seem to be working most of the time and who do not actually steal time, fail to accomplish much. They may work slowly and stop to let their minds wander or they may give their work only part of their attention. This causes them to make errors frequently and thus decreases the quality and quantity of the work done. Haste and bustle should be avoided, as they lead to errors and lost motion. But the habit of applying one's self to one's job closely and putting out a commendable volume of work in a given time should be developed. 

Dependability. Some people do not advance rapidly because they are not dependable. At times they do their work in a highly satisfactory manner. At other times they are careless, inefficient, and indifferent. This is very irritating to an employer and causes him to have little confidence in the employee. When an employer knows that a person can always be depended upon to do his work efficiently and promptly, he will be much more favorably inclined toward promoting him.

ch. 25] 419 

Resourcefulness and Initiative. Many people do what they are told but make no attempt to do anything else. The result is that they are taught one kind of work and left to do that continually. Their chance for advancement is thus greatly lessened. Advancement comes to the one who can apply the information he has to different situations and can improve upon the methods he has been using. This can be done by one who thinks about his work and avoids doing it in a mechanical manner.

If one is to obtain advancement, it is also necessary for him to actually put into practice his new ideas. One is judged not by what he knows but by what he does. It is not enough, therefore, only to think about one's work. Plans or ideas must be put into operation or passed on to the one who can use them if they are to be of value or if they are to bring recognition and credit to the person who originated them.

Tact. When business transactions are completed between individuals, one is usually a buyer; the other, a seller. 

The buyer has the choice of buying from one of several persons, any of whom may be able to supply him with good merchandise. He usually buys from the one who best displays his merchandise and whom he likes best personally. 

Frequently this depends on the tact of the seller-the saying and doing of the right thing at the right time. Tact can be acquired by putting one's self in the place of the other and imagining what he would like to have said to himself under similar circumstances.

A tactful person avoids abrupt statements which will cause irritation. He suggests what he would like to have done. He is careful in everything he says not to give a suggestion contrary to the thought he has in mind. 

Those who are not actually selling merchandise need to be tactful also. They have to sell their services to an employer. Their ideas must be sold to their associates if they are to be put into operation. Differences of opinion are certain to arise and can be adjusted satisfactorily only when tact is used.

[ch. 25 

Ambition. One must really want to succeed if he is to attain success. Many people think that they want to succeed while they actually want other things more. These other things which they want are not very great things, but rather are a large number of small things. For example, they prefer to arrive at work late. They prefer to waste much time while they are supposed to be at work. They prefer to do simple routine work only and not to do the serious thinking that might qualify them for better positions. In other words, while they may want to succeed, they want other small things more and thereby make success impossible.

Determination. It is obvious that if one is to continue to give his work more thought than anything else, and if he is to prevent other things interfering with his success, he must have considerable determination. Fortunately the development of determination is comparatively simple. It requires only practice in doing what one has decided to do. 

A pupil can secure this practice by continuing the study of every lesson until he has fully mastered it and by finishing each piece of work started, regardless of the number of distracting things that come up. Determination will then be a matter of habit which requires no conscious effort. 

Summary. The qualities discussed in the preceding paragraphs are not the only ones necessary for securing promotion but they are among the most important. None of them are fixed qualities; on the other hand, they can be developed. A person's success, therefore, depends not on his inherited ability but on the amount of effort he is willing to put forth. This accounts for the success of many who seem to have mediocre ability and the failure of many of those who seem to have unusual ability. It places the responsibility for a person's progress largely with himself. 

1. John Doe asked his employer for an increase in salary, saying that he could not live on the amount he was

ch. 25] 421 

making. Can you suggest a better reason which might have been given? 

2. After continuing with the same firm for another year, John Doe asked again for an increase. The reason given this time was the fact that he had not had an increase in pay for a long time. What better reason might have been given? 

3. John Doe finally decided to look for a new position.

He did not consider his employer fair since the salaries of others were increased more rapidly. Suggest reasons why others may have been given preference. 

4. John Doe secured a new position with the Excelsior

Elevator Company but was discharged after a few weeks. 

An employee of this company said that the reason for the discharge of John was his poor personality. What do you think was meant by that statement?

CHAPTER XXVI BUSINESS ORGANIZATION Section I. INTRODUCTION 

Purpose of Business. Individuals want food, clothing, shelter, and many other things which they believe are necessary to their existence or will add to their happiness. Their wants never seem to be entirely satisfied. If they are well clothed, they want finer and more expensive clothing. If they live in comfortable houses, they want more pretentious or beautiful ones. It seems that the saying, "The more one has, the more he wants," is literally true.

It is impossible for an individual to make everything he wants or for a family to make everything desired by the group. Suppose that the only way for you to secure a pair of leather shoes was to make them and that you could not procure from others any of the needed supplies. It would first be necessary for you to prepare the leather. This would require that you kill an animal, remove the hide, and complete the long tanning process. It would then be necessary for you to cut out the leather according to a pattern that you had devised, and to sew the parts together. The sewing would have to be done with a substitute for thread and with a rude substitute for a needle, such as you could make. At best, the result would be a crude covering for your feet, inferior to Indian moccasins.

Although an almost unlimited amount of work would be required in making a poor substitute for shoes, a person can buy a pair for about the amount which he receives for a day's labor. Thus, business has enabled one to exchange a day's labor for something that he could not produce in weeks, if at all. The purpose of business, then, is to enable individuals to satisfy their wants more fully than otherwise would be possible. 424

ch. 26] 425 

BUSINESS ORGANIZATION 

Business and Profits. Businesses are organized and conducted to render services and thus enable people to satisfy their wants more fully than otherwise would be possible. 

A business is usually successful in proportion to the services that it renders its customers. One organizing a business must therefore consider how he can serve his customers, for if he cannot render a satisfactory service his venture will be a failure. Nevertheless, when a business is being organized, the primary purpose is not to render service, but rather to earn a profit.

Suppose a truck gardener rents a piece of ground, raises vegetables on it, and sells them in a nearby city. He renders a service in that he makes it possible for others to buy food from him. The reason he goes into this business, however, is that he believes he can sell the vegetables for more than they cost him in rent and labor. If, after operating his farm for a few years, he finds that he is not making a profit, he will change his occupation. Probably he will be forced to do so as otherwise he will not be able to make a living. 

Similarly, a business which makes shoes does so because it can sell them for more than the cost of production. An individual may pay for a pair of shoes only the income received for a day's labor. This is much less than the shoes would cost him if he made them himself. Even this amount, however, is more than they cost the manufacturer in materials, equipment, and labor. The use of machinery which can make thousands of articles exactly alike makes it possible to produce manufactured articles at very low cost. If manufactured goods could not be sold for more than they cost, there would be no incentive for anyone to build large factories and equip them with expensive machinery. 

These are examples of the fact that men engage in business in order to make a profit. Without the hope of profits there would be no incentive to organize and operate a business. If a business cannot be made profitable, it will in time be discontinued.

Elements Used by Business. Businesses are organized to earn profits by rendering services. In accomplishing this

[ch. 26

Figure 178--A Natural Resource 

Keystone View Co., Inc

One of our great natural resources is the copper ore of northern Michigan. The mine in this illustration is called an open-pit mine because all the earth over the ore is removed, thus making an open pit after the ore is mined. The mining is done with large steam shovels which load the ore directly on cars. purpose they use five elements: natural resources, labor, capital, knowledge, and organization. 

Natural Resources. Nature is the great storehouse from which we get all of the materials we use. Our food comes from the crops raised on farms; our lumber comes from the forests; our coal and minerals come from the mines; bricks are made from clay; and building stone is taken from quarries. These are only a few of the many examples of the fact that we get all materials from nature. The useful things supplied by nature are called natural resources. 

Labor. While everything is derived from natural resources, most of these resources are not in a form that

ch. 26] 427 

BUSINESS ORGANIZATION 

makes them directly usable. Before they can be used, labor must be applied to them. Trees must be cut up into lumber and made into houses or furniture; metal must be mined, purified, and made into machinery, railroads, or ships. In a like manner business must take our natural resources and apply labor to them to make them into the goods or commodities that we want.

Capital. More than labor is required in making the things we want from our natural resources. Robinson 

Crusoe, while on his lonely island, took forty-two days to cut out a board to be used as a shelf. So much time was required because he did not have the proper tools with which to work. The only tool he had was a small ax. With proper tools, the work could have been done in a much shorter time, and done much better.

Similarly, buildings, equipment, and supplies are used by business as it makes the many things we use. For example, in the production of automobiles, machinery valued at many thousands of dollars is housed in great and expensive buildings. The supplies used may be collected from all parts of the world. These buildings, equipment, and supplies are known as capital. This is one of the important elements business uses in making possible the satisfaction of our wants. 

Knowledge. Suppose that a plant equipped to produce automobiles were turned over to one thousand men collected at random from the streets of your city. You would not expect that any machines would be produced, as the men would have neither the technical knowledge nor the skill needed to operate such a plant. Thus, it is seen that knowledge is as essential as natural resources, labor, and capital. 

Organization. Gather together in a newspaper plant the supplies necessary for the production of a paper, and a number of people who can write. The result of this, alone, will not be one of our wonderfully complete daily newspapers. 

Some one must direct the gathering of news from all over the world, the editing of the news, the collecting and arranging of the advertising, and the multitude of other

This direction is possible only when there is a complete organization in which each individual has a certain share of the work to do.

1. In ancient times each family produced everything it used, ate or wore. Suggest some of the things which you would have to do without if your family were forced to make everything it used. 

2. "A business is organized to render services.” "A business is organized to earn profits." 

Which of the preceding statements do you consider to be the more accurate?

 Section II. COMMON TYPES OF ORGANIZATIONS 

Legal Form of Organization. Businesses are alike in that they render services to earn profits, but there are differences in the way they are organized. The three common types of business organizations are known as sole proprietorship, partnership, and corporation.

Sole Proprietorship. A sole proprietorship is a business which is entirely owned by one individual. This type of organization is commonly found in the small independent retail store and in similar businesses not requiring great amounts of capital.

Partnership. In a partnership, two or more persons combine their capital and skill in conducting a business. They usually have a definite agreement regarding the amount of capital each is to contribute, the work each is to do, and how the profits and losses are to be divided. If this agreement is in writing, the contract is known as the Articles of Copartnership. Typical articles are shown in Figure 180. 

Corporation. The third form of organization is known as the corporation. To establish a corporation a charter is secured from the state, permitting one or more persons to conduct a business in the name of the corporation. The business is then known by the corporate name and all transactions are completed in that name.

As was explained in Chapter XVI, the ownership of corporations is divided into parts known as shares or shares of stock. The ownership of each person investing in the corporation is represented by a stock certificate which shows the number of shares he owns. A certificate may be purchased and sold by different individuals, for, since the business is carried on in the name of the corporation, it makes no difference so far as the operation of the business is concerned who owns the certificates. 

Advantages of a Sole Proprietorship. The sole proprietor has great freedom. With but few exceptions, he can open

ch. 26] 431 

BUSINESS ORGANIZATION 

his business at any time or in any place, can close it whenever he desires, and can sell or manufacture any kind of 

ARTICLES OF COPARTNERSHIP 

This Contract, Made and entered into on the second day of July 19 by and between E. C. Cline of Little Rock, Ark., party of the first part, and J. G. Pipkin of the same city and state, party of the second part. 

WITNESSETII: That the said parties have this day formed a copartnership for the purpose of engaging in and conducting a retail wood, coal and ice under the following stipulations which are made a part of this contract: at three years

FIRST: The said copartnership is to continue for a term of 

SECOND: The business shall be conducted under the firm name of Cline & Pipkin 

212 South Main Street, Little Rock, Ark. business from date hereof. 

THIRD: The investments are as follows: Each partner invests $2,000.00 cash. 

FOURTH: All profils or losses arising from said business are to be shared as follows: Equally

FIFTH: A systematic record of all transactions is to be kept in a double entry set of books, which are to be open for the inspection of each partner. On 

June 30 and December 31 hereafter a statement of the business is to be made, the books closed and each partner credited with the amount of the gain. A statement may be made at such other time as the partners agree upon. 

SIXTH: Each partner is to devote his entire time and attention to the business and to engage in no other business enterprise without the written consent of the other. 

SEVENTH: Each partner is to have a salary of $ 100.00 per month, the same to be withdrawn at such time or times as he may elect. Neither partner is to withdraw from the business an amount in excess of his salary without the written consent of the other.

Cline is to have general supervision of the business, and have charge of credit and collections, all correspondence, and the accounting records. J. G. Pipkin is to have supervision of incoming and outgoing merchandise, and the merchandise stock. Each partner is to attend to such other duties as are deemed necessary for the successful operation of the business. 

NINTH: Neither partner is to become surety or bondsman for anyone without the written consent of the other. 

TENTH: In case of the death, incapacity, or withdrawal of either partner, the business is to be conducted for the remainder of the fiscal year by the surviving partner, the profits for the year allocated to the withdrawing partner to be determined by the ratio of the time he was a partner during the year to the whole year. 

ELEVENTH: In case of dissolution, the assets are to be divided in the ratio of the capital invested at the time of dissolution.
