Figure 180-Articles of Copartnership

[ch. 26 

merchandise. He is not forced to consult others nor to consider them when fixing his policies. 

A sole proporietor is not bothered with legal formalities. A partnership must have a contract between the partners; a corporation must have a charter from the state and must submit reports annually; but a sole proprietor is free from such obligations.

The sole proprietor secures all the profits made from his business. When one is successful in making a business profitable, he naturally does not wish to divide the profits with others as he must do in a partnership or corporation. 

Disadvantages of a Sole Proprietorship. A sole proprietor has certain disadvantages which in some cases make a partnership or corporation preferable. Some of the disadvantages are:

The sole proprietor does not have the assistance of others interested in the business. This is not essential in a small business, but in a large one it is highly desirable. One man cannot take care of everything nor even be an expert in directing all kinds of work. He can hire others to help him, but employees are seldom as efficient as those who have a financial interest in the business. 

The sole proprietor may not have sufficient capital to conduct his business most effectively.

A sole proprietor must bear all the losses sustained by the business. He, therefore, takes greater risks than if several persons were interested in the business, and the profits and losses were distributed among the group. 

Advantages of a Partnership. A partnership is preferable to a sole proprietorship in that it combines the resources and ability of two or more persons. The management should then be more capable and the resources should be greater than if one partner were in business by himself. 

The risk of loss is also divided among the partners, and the risk borne by each partner is therefore decreased. 

Disadvantages of a Partnership. Although the partnership may combine more ability than that possessed by a

ch. 26] 433 

BUSINESS ORGANIZATION 

sole proprietor, each partner may be able to accomplish less than if he were working alone, because it is necessary for him to consult his partner or partners before acting. Furthermore, in this kind of an organization each partner is bound by agreements or contracts made by the other partner or partners, even if he knows nothing about them and is not favorable to them. A poorly chosen partner may cause a business ruinous losses.

For example, Herbert Dow formed a partnership with 

Earl Harvey to operate a grocery store in Jasonville. It was understood that Mr. Dow was to make all purchases, but without consulting his partner, Mr. Harvey bought a delivery truck for $600, and equipment for a meat department for $2,100. These purchases were contrary to Mr. 

Dow's plan, since he did not believe it wise to make deliveries or to operate a meat department. It was necessary for him to pay for this equipment, however, since he was bound by the acts of his partner. Furthermore, it was necessary for him to pay the entire cost of the equipment and not simply a part of the cost, as his partner, Mr. Harvey, had no funds.

Regardless of the fact that partnerships have the disadvantages described above, they are quite common. Usually they are formed when individuals have entire confidence in each other and believe that the advantages of greater capital and increased skill in management more than offset the necessity for the division of profits and the danger of loss through the acts of one partner. 

Advantages of a Corporation. A member of a corporation can not act as the agent of the company unless he is given specific authority to do so. This eliminates the danger of loss through the unauthorized acts of one member of the organization and thus overcomes one of the disadvantages of the partnership.

Members have no liability for the debts of the corporation beyond their investments or beyond a fixed and limited amount in excess of their investments. This removes the

[ch. 26 

danger of the loss of one's private property, which cannot be avoided in a sole proprietorship or partnership. 

Authority is centralized. Since the owners of a corporation cannot act unless given specific authority, it is possible to center the responsibility and authority in one person or small group.

The proprietorship is represented by shares which are transferable. This makes it possible for an owner to sell all or part of his interest without affecting the corporation. 

The shares may be owned by any number of persons. A corporation can, therefore, secure its capital by selling stock or shares to a large number of people. This makes possible the accumulation of the great amount of capital required in the operation of many of the large corporations. 

Disadvantages of a Corporation. There are offsetting disadvantages to the corporate form. Taxes are frequently heavier than for partnerships or sole proprietorships; reports of operations must be given to the state and national governments; and the activities of the corporation are limited to those mentioned in its charter. Also, if those managing the corporation do not own a large number of shares, they may not be as interested in the welfare of the corporation and may not be as careful to manage it efficiently as if they were sole proprietors or partners.

Summary. From the point of view of those dealing with an organization, each form has some advantages. The corporation is more stable, that is, it is not dissolved on the death or withdrawal of the proprietor, or one of the proprietors, as is the case in a partnership. One can ordinarily make a long-time agreement with a well-established corporation with the assurance that the corporation will continue long enough to carry out its promises. On the other hand, the owners of a corporation are not responsible for an amount in excess of their investment if the corporation fails to carry out its agreements. The responsibility of a sole proprietor or partner is unlimited. This may make an agreement with either of the latter somewhat safer, especially if the proprietor or partners have any personal wealth.

 Section III. PRODUCTION AND DISTRIBUTION 

Introduction. Businesses differ not only in their form of organization, but also in the services they render. For example, one business may manufacture shoes; another may sell them in a retail store; while a third may insure both companies from loss because of fire. 

Classification According to Services. The three types of organizations mentioned in the preceding paragraph illustrate the three common groups when services are the basis of classification. One group manufactures or produces goods. In it are found the farmer, miner, and manufacturer. 

The second makes goods produced by others available for consumers. Retail stores make up a large part of this group. 

The third assists both the others in rendering their services. 

Insurance companies and banks are typical examples. Production, distribution, and financial organizations are the names by which the groups are commonly known. 

Production. The services rendered by businesses engaged in production are easily understood. A farmer takes his capital consisting of land, equipment, and live stock, and produces the food necessary for the maintenance of life. A flour milling company buys the farmer's wheat and, in a large factory called a mill, changes it into flour. It thereby renders a service, as flour 6758 cannot be made readily by Figure 181-One Step in the Proindividuals. A baker combines the flour with other ingredients in making bread duction of Lumber 

Six million feet of Oregon fir traveling on the Columbia River.

ch. 26] 437 

BUSINESS ORGANIZATION 

and pastries. A service is rendered since this saves individuals the time and expense required in doing their own baking. 

Similarly, coal and metals are produced from the mines; lumber is secured from the forests; the thousands of articles commonly used are made in factories. Companies engaged in such work obviously render a service for which they are entitled to charge, or from which they are entitled to make a profit.

Distribution. The shoes you wear may have been made in Lynn, Massachusetts; the watch you carry, in Cincinnati, Ohio; and the breakfast food you eat, in Cedar Rapids, Iowa. 

But to buy shoes, a watch, or breakfast food you do not go to 

Lynn, Cincinnati, or Cedar Rapids. Rather you go to one of your local retail stores. These stores collect goods from all parts of the world and carry them in stock until you are ready to buy them. Although they do not change them in form, they make them more valuable from your point of view as they make it possible for you to get them when they are needed. The stores, therefore, render you a service for which they are entitled to charge. They make this charge by selling the goods at a price greater than they pay. 

Sometimes manufacturers sell direct to consumers and thus do all the distributing themselves. Probably you have noticed advertisements of clothing, bicycles, and many other articles which manufacturers offer for sale. 

Although manufacturers may sell direct to consumers, they frequently find this to be too expensive. It requires that they advertise in every locality where sales are to be made, that catalogs be sent out, that small orders be packed and shipped, and that money be collected from a host of individuals.

Buying from a manufacturer may also be too inconvenient for the customer. Consider how much trouble it would be for you to send to a manufacturer in a distant city every time you wished a box of matches.

For these reasons manufacturers frequently sell to retail dealers. They can afford to sell to them at cheaper prices than to individuals since the dealers buy in large quanti-

[ch. 26 

ties and take care of much of the advertising as well as the retail selling. The retailers in turn sell to individuals. Their prices are higher than those charged them by the manufacturers, but usually they are about the same as those manufacturers would have to charge if they sold direct. 

Retail stores, such as hardware stores, often carry thousands of articles in stock. The great number of articles makes possible the purchasing of only a small quantity of each at a time. It is therefore difficult, if not impossible, for the dealer to buy direct from the many different manufacturers. To aid in distribution, wholesale companies are established. They buy in large quantities from many manufacturers and carry goods in stock so that retailers can secure them in smaller quantities. As they make it possible for the retail dealer to carry small stocks, they reduce his cost of doing business and therefore render him a service. 

Goods are then distributed according to one of the plans in the following chart. Note the three plans indicated. Manufacturer

Wholesaler Retailer Consumer

All of the plans involve similar expenses for advertising, carrying goods in stock, and handling the orders of individual consumers. Consumers usually buy from retail stores as this is the most convenient and frequently the cheapest method, but purchases may be made from manufacturers in some cases.

It will be observed that a part of the cost paid by the consumer for merchandise is the cost of distribution. This includes the operating expenses and profits of wholesale and retail dealers. For some products, these costs make up a large per cent of the total cost of the merchandise to the consumers. This does not necessarily mean that the goods

ch. 26] 439 

BUSINESS ORGANIZATION 

cost more than they would if the dealers were eliminated. 

Large factories can produce many types of goods in great quantities much more cheaply than they can produce them in small quantities. The cost of the merchandise produced in these factories is, therefore, cheaper to the distant consumer than if the goods were made in each community. For example, matches can be manufactured in large quantities, and sold first to a wholesaler, then to a retailer and finally to you, much more cheaply than they could be made in small quantities in your town and sold direct to you.

1. In your city there are many retail stores in which you can buy merchandise. These stores do not manufacture anything, but only buy and sell goods produced by others. Do these stores render you a valuable service? 

2. How do retail stores obtain a profit which justifies their continuing in business? 

3. The Hamilton Hardware Company is said to be engaged in production, while the Mason Hardware Company is said to be engaged in distribution. From this information how would you judge that the services rendered by these two companies differ? 

4. Do you consider businesses engaged in production more or less important than those engaged in distribution? 

Give reasons for your answer. 

5. What is a wholesale business? What service does such a business render? 

6. Henry Samson tells you that he can buy merchandise more cheaply from a manufacturer than from a local retailer as this eliminates the middleman's or retailer's profit.

He has a large catalog of a thousand pages in which the manufacturer lists the many articles offered for sale. He selects the merchandise from the catalog and sends his order by mail. The goods are then sent to him by mail or express. Does this method entirely eliminate the costs of distribution which are incurred when goods are bought from a retailer? 

7. Retail dealers wish to buy goods as cheaply as possible so they can sell them to their customers at a low price and thus increase their business. Why, then, do they frequently buy from wholesale dealers when manufacturers sell at lower prices?

 Section IV. FINANCIAL ORGANIZATIONS 

Introduction. The chief purpose of business is to produce goods which people want and to distribute these goods so that they are available when and where they are needed. 

Some organizations are engaged primarily in production; others, in distribution. Others, however, neither produce goods nor distribute them. But they are of value because they aid businesses engaged in production and distribution in rendering their services, and individuals in using these services. 

Services of Banks. As we have previously observed, some banks, known as commercial banks, accept deposits and permit customers to draw checks against their deposits. They serve in that they provide a convenient method of making payments and render it unnecessary for individuals and businesses to keep on hand large sums of money. 

Other banks, known as savings banks, accept deposits but do not offer their customers checking privileges. They do, however, pay interest on deposits which are left with the bank for a certain length of time. The rate of interest is usually about 3 or 4 per cent; that is, the depositor receives three or four dollars for each one hundred dollars deposited for a year.

A savings bank does not serve its customers in the same way as a commercial bank, but it does offer its customers a convenient means of investing savings as they are accumulated.

Savings banks are sometimes operated entirely separate from commercial banks, but most commercial banks have savings departments. If you deal with such a bank, you have the choice of depositing your money in the commercial department or in the savings department. Either department will render valuable service, although the service will not be exactly the same. 

Source of the Bank's Income. When a customer deposits money in a bank, the bank does not promise to return the same money, but simply an equal amount. Until the money

ch. 26] 441 

BUSINESS ORGANIZATION 

is withdrawn, the bank has a right to lend it to businesses or individuals who may need to borrow. 

The balance of each customer may not be large, but when all balances are added together, they may make an important sum. The bank can safely lend a considerable share of the total amount, for it is not probable that all depositors will wish to withdraw their funds at the same time. 

The bank charges interest on the money it lends. In this way it obtains the income needed to operate the bank, to keep a record of each depositor's account, to handle each depositor's checks, and to pay interest on savings. Earnings may be obtained from charges made for miscellaneous services, but the chief source of income is interest. 

Bank Services in Accumulating Funds. When banks collect the deposits of many people and thus make funds available for borrowers, they render valuable service to those in need of loans. For example, one business may need to borrow ten thousand dollars. If banks did not accumulate funds, it might be necessary for this business to borrow small amounts from a number of persons. Obviously, it is more convenient for the business to borrow the entire amount from one bank. It is probable that many businesses could not be adequately financed if banks did not serve the community by accumulating funds which can be lent. 

Safety Deposit Vaults. A further service rendered by banks is the providing of a safe place in which to store valuable articles. Banks ordinarily have vaults in which customers may rent small boxes known as safety deposit boxes. These boxes are well guarded and offer a maximum amount of safety. They are valuable for keeping jewelry, stocks, bonds, and other business papers. Rent is charged for the use of a safety deposit box since the bank does not have the right to use anything placed in the box, and thus has no source of income such as it has from money left on deposit.

Other Services of Banks. In addition to the major services of banks described in the foregoing paragraphs, banks render many other services. Many banks sell securities to

[ch. 26 

their customers and thus assist them in investing their funds. Other banks have travel departments to assist their customers in planning tours. Practically all banks are valuable sources of information about financial matters and are thus helpful to customers who are in need of such information. 

Figure 182-Safety Deposit Vault of a Bank

Services of Insurance Companies. As was explained in 

Chapters XVII and XVIII, insurance companies are conducted to assume risks which individuals would otherwise have to bear. For example, a person who owns an uninsured building must bear the risk of losing his investment as the result of a fire or a storm. For a reasonable com-

ch. 26] 443 

BUSINESS ORGANIZATION 

pensation, an insurance company will assume this risk. The company thus serves by removing the danger of great loss which the individual could not otherwise escape. 

Life insurance companies also serve in providing a means of investing funds. When holders of certain types of policies pay the premiums, they obtain protection and at the same time accumulate cash values with the insurance companies, which they can withdraw. For many persons the service of life insurance companies in providing a means of accumulating savings is almost as important as their service in providing a means of protection. 

Insurance companies also serve in accumulating funds which can be borrowed by individuals and businesses. These funds are accumulated by the companies to pay policies which will become due in the future. Until the policies are due, the funds may be lent to those who wish to borrow money and who can give satisfactory security for the repayment of the loans. Many of our businesses are financed by insurance companies. Many individuals who wish to buy homes, farms, or other property are able to borrow money from insurance companies for this purpose. The funds accumulated by these companies are therefore useful to industries and business men.

Summary. Many other illustrations of the services rendered by banks, insurance companies, and similar organizations might be given. The foregoing examples are, however, sufficient to illustrate the point in which we are interested; namely, that production and distribution are aided by financial organizations. These organizations do not produce goods which individuals use, nor do they engage directly in the distribution of such goods. Nevertheless, their services are valuable because they aid other businesses in producing and distributing. 

1. The Phillips Grocery Store has a large safe in which it keeps all of its records. It keeps only a comparatively small amount of money in its safe. Most of its cash receipts

[ch. 26 

are deposited each day in a bank. Suggest reasons why this is done. 

2. William Harrison has carried a bank account in the 

Merchants National Bank for several years. The bank has taken care of his deposits and paid the checks he has written. 

No charge has been made for these services. Explain how a bank can render Mr. Harrison so much service without charge. 

3. The balance in Mr. Harrison's account in the bank changes quite frequently and widely. At one time his balance may be several hundred dollars, and then a few days later, less than one hundred. Is it possible for the Merchants National Bank to make a profit from an account of this kind? 

4. The Merchants National Bank conducts two departments, a commercial department and a savings department. 

It pays interest on deposits in the latter department, but not in the former. (a) Since interest is paid on money deposited in the savings department, why do any customers of the bank place their money in the commercial department? (b) Why can a bank better afford to pay interest on deposits in the savings department than on those in the commercial department? 

5. Mr. Harrison carries an account in both the savings department and the commercial department of his bank. 

Why might he need the two accounts? 

6. A merchant borrows five thousand dollars from a bank. He contends that it is not fair for the bank to charge interest because the money lent is really the money of the depositors. Explain what services the bank renders which entitles it to charge interest on loans. 

7. (a) Explain three important services that are rendered by insurance companies. (b) Under what conditions would a person probably use each of these services?

CHAPTER XXVII  Section I. LAW AS A DEVICE TO CONTROL AND TO AID BUSINESS 

Laws as Rules of Business. There are many laws relating to business. The purpose of these laws is to guide, direct, protect, and aid those engaged in business activities. 

They therefore serve much the same purpose as the rules of games and athletic contests with which you are familiar. 

A basket ball game is being played. Two teams are striving for victory. Each team wishes to win, and yet the members of the two teams try to advance the ball and to make goals according to the rules of the game. Obviously, this is necessary, for if each team played in any way it pleased, all would be confusion. Under such conditions the game could not be continued. 

Similarly, laws are needed for business to guide and direct business men in performing the great variety of business transactions that are necessary in modern commerce. 

Without some system of laws our highly complex business organization would be impossible. Business men, like the players on the basket ball team, are trying to win, but they must proceed according to certain rules or laws. In this way the processes of business run smoothly and without confusion.

The rules of a basket ball game not only state how the game is to be played, but also fix penalties for their violation. The purpose of a penalty imposed upon a player or a team is not revenge, but rather the protection of each team from unfair actions on the part of the other. The punishment is for the purpose of restraining the person who broke the rule from repeating the offense, and for the purpose of restraining other players from doing the same thing. Before a person attempts to play basket ball or a similar game, he should know the rules of the game thor- 445

[ch. 27 

oughly. Knowing the rules, he will be able to play the game fairly and squarely, and will be able to insist upon the others doing likewise. It is also a matter of common prudence as a protection against costly penalties. A player is penalized for a breach of the rules, regardless of whether he did the act with or without a knowledge of the rules. 

In a like manner, punishments are imposed upon members of society for breaking laws. A traffic law may impose a fine for driving an automobile at more than thirty miles an hour on a certain street. Another law may require that a person who injures another's automobile make good the loss suffered, by paying damages. The primary purpose of these punishments, as in the case of the basket ball game, is not revenge. They are imposed to prevent such violations of the rules because such acts are contrary to the peace, security, and good order of the community. 

Laws of various kinds have been established for the protection and the welfare of residents of our country. 

Many of these laws specifically state the rights of individuals under certain circumstances and the penalties for a violation of these rights. It is desirable for everyone to have some appreciation of the most important rules of law so that he will understand what his rights are and will be able to assert them. It is also important that he know something of the rules of law so that he will understand the rights of others and will not, because of ignorance, infringe upon them.

Laws as Aids of Business. We have seen that laws are a device to control business; that the purpose of certain laws is to guide and to direct those engaged in business activities. There are many other instances in which laws are used as a device to aid in business transactions. Certain transactions give rise to relations which, under the rules of law, create corresponding rights and duties. It is by creating these relations that business enterprises can be carried out to completion. 

To illustrate, business men must know that they can depend upon the promises of others; otherwise our complex

ch. 27] 447 

and huge business ventures could not be undertaken. Laws have therefore been established as rules for the formation of enforceable promises. When an agreement has certain essential characteristics specified by law, each party is required to fulfill his promise or to pay damages for failing to do so. 

In a course in business law a great number of rules relating to business transactions and a number of ways in which laws control and aid business are studied. In this text it is not possible to consider all of the ways in which laws control and aid business, but in the following pages you will find discussions of a few of the most important principles with which you should be familiar.

1. Brooks, a public speaker, claims that all laws limit a person's liberty and that we would be more prosperous if we had no laws. Do you agree? 

2. Harms, another speaker, declares that some laws are necessary to guide businesses in their dealings, but that there should be no penalties for violations. Do you agree? 

3. Kenneth, a student in high school, says that everyone should know something about business law. Harry says that if one has legal troubles, he can consult a lawyer and that, therefore, each person does not need to know anything about law. With which do you agree? 

4. Describe some law with which you are familiar, which aids or controls business. 

5. Why would you not care to invest money in a business in a country where laws were not enforced? 

6. If everyone were honest, would there be any need for laws? 

7. "Laws have been established as rules for the formation of enforceable promises." Explain how such laws are similar to the rules of an athletic contest, and what useful purpose they serve. 

8. One law requires that promises made under certain conditions must be fulfilled. Another requires that advertising must be truthful. A third requires that food offered for sale must be wholesome. Does each of these laws aid or control business?

[ch. 27 

 Section II. CONTRACTS 

Law and the Assurance of Future Conduct. In the carrying on of business a great number of agreements are made. One person agrees to buy and another agrees to sell certain goods. An employee agrees to work for a certain wage, and the employer agrees to pay him regularly. One firm agrees to erect a building, and another firm agrees to pay for the building upon its completion. These are common examples of agreements which are made in great number during each hour of a business day.

Law aids business in specifying that agreements which have certain qualities are enforceable, and that damages can be collected from a person who fails to keep such an agreement. Such enforceable agreements are called contracts. 

Since they are made with great frequency by almost everyone, we shall in this and following sections observe some of the characteristics of contracts.

Agreement. A contract cannot exist unless there is an agreement between two or more parties. mean that all agreements are contracts. 

This does not 

If Harry and 

Robert agreed to meet at 3:00 p.m. to go to a football game, no contract has been formed. This is purely a social obligation and is not enforceable under the law. The agreement must contemplate a legal obligation, that is, one the courts will enforce, before it becomes a contract.

The parties must apparently agree to the same thing or there is no contract. To illustrate, Harry offers to sell 

Robert a book for one dollar. Robert says, "I will take the book for seventy-five cents." This does not result in a contract, for the two persons have not agreed to the same thing. 

Making an Agreement. Every agreement consists of two parts, an offer and an acceptance. One person offers to do or to refrain from doing something; another accepts. 

In most contracts the offer and the acceptance are stated briefly or are assumed from the actions of the parties. If a newsboy offers you a paper and you accept it, an agreement has been formed. Nothing may have been said about price,

ch. 27] 449 

but it is assumed that you will pay a reasonable amount. 

You have a right to keep the paper, and the newsboy has a right to demand payment from you. 

Your mother may telephone to the grocer and ask him to deliver certain goods. Nothing may be said about the cost of these goods nor the payment for them. Since it is assumed, however, that she will pay a reasonable amount, an agreement has been formed. The grocer delivers or agrees to deliver the goods, and your mother agrees to pay a reasonable amount for them.

Agreements Must be Voluntary. Agreements must be entered into voluntarily or they can be avoided by the unwilling party. Suppose Simmons agrees to sell Doe his house for $10,000 because Doe threatens to burn the house if the sale is not made. The agreement is not enforceable against Simmons if he chooses to avoid it.

Similarly, a contract may be avoided if one party agrees to it because another person, on whom he depends because of their relationship, brings too great influence to bear on him in making the agreement. A. M. Hartman had always depended on his uncle, his legal guardian, for advice about financial matters. Soon after Hartman became of age, the uncle persuaded the former to sell him property worth $10,000 for $5,000. Hartman, depending upon this advice, made the sale. He could avoid carrying out the contract because of the undue influence brought to bear on him by one on whom he had been accustomed to depend. 

Agreements Must be Honestly Obtained. An agreement is also unenforceable against the injured party if it was brought about by false representation and if the party making this representation knew that it was false at the time that it was made.

C. E. Collins was considering purchasing a piece of property. In order to make the sale, the seller told him that the house was built by a certain contractor who had the reputation of doing unusually good work. The seller knew that this statement was false. Collins depended on the information given him, and agreed to buy the property. He

[ch. 27 

could later avoid his contract because of the false representation which had induced him to make the agreement. 

A contract brought about by threat, by undue influence, or by fraud cannot be enforced against the injured party.

If, however, he elects to fulfill his obligations under such a contract, he can force the other party to carry out his part of the agreement.

1. James agrees to sell a ball glove to George for one dollar. He also agrees to walk home with George after the next game. May either or both of these agreements be the basis of an enforceable contract? 

2. Helen says, "I believe this book is worth seventy-five cents. I wonder if I could sell it for that amount." Jane says, "I will give you seventy-five cents for it." Is there an agreement? 

3. Mary goes into a store and points to a pencil in a tray on which there is a sign, "Five cents each." The clerk hands her the pencil. Has an agreement been formed? 

4. Mrs. Rae telephones a grocer, directing him to send her five pounds of sugar, ten pounds of potatoes, and ten pounds of flour. Nothing is said about the prices. Has an agreement been formed? 

5. Kane agrees to buy a book from a dealer for six hundred dollars, when the dealer assures him that it is several hundred years old. Kane discovers that the book is less than fifty years old and that the dealer knows its age. Is there an enforceable agreement? 

6. Doe threatens to burn Smith's automobile unless the latter sells it to him, Doe, for five hundred dollars. Smith therefore agrees with Doe to make the sale for that amount. 

Later Doe decides that the automobile is not worth as much as five hundred dollars, and that he does not wish to carry out the agreement. Can Smith enforce the agreement?

ch. 27] 451 

 Section III. PARTIES TO A CONTRACT 

Introduction. In general, any person can enter into an agreement which is enforceable under the law. At one time married women could not make enforceable promises, but in practically all states they now have the same right to contract as other persons.

There are two groups of persons who are especially protected by the laws relating to contracts: minors, and persons who are mentally deficient. 

Minors. Minors, who are known in law as infants, are ordinarily persons under twenty-one years of age. It is assumed that such persons are less skilled in making agreements than older persons or are lacking in judgment and may therefore be imposed upon if they are not given some special protection. Laws are therefore in force which give them special advantages and protection in the formation of contracts.

Minors who are dependent upon themselves may have to make certain contracts in order to live. For example, it may be necessary for them to make contracts for board and room, and for the purchase of clothing. Although contracts for such necessary things are not binding on the minor, the latter is liable for them at a reasonable value if they are actually supplied. If this were not true, people might hesitate to contract with minors, and thus the latter might suffer from a lack of necessary things. 

In order to be considered necessary, the article contracted for by a minor must be suitable to his station in life. If a minor with limited funds and dependent upon himself for support made a contract for board and lodging at an inexpensive boarding house, the contract would be considered to be for necessaries. If, on the other hand, he made a contract for a suite of rooms in an expensive hotel, the contract might not be considered to be for necessaries. 

Contracts with minors made for other than necessary things are ordinarily enforceable by the minor, but are not enforceable against the minor by the other person. The minor can claim all of the rights under the contract that

[ch. 27 

anyone could claim. If he claims these rights, he must fulfill his obligations arising from the contract. On the other hand, if he does not wish to carry out his part of the contract, he can disaffirm it, in which case the contract is no longer in force. He can also claim anything that he may have given the other person under the contract, even though he may not be able to return that which he received in exchange. 

If a minor carries out the terms of a contract which was not enforceable against him, he can, upon becoming of age, disaffirm the contract and demand the return of what he may have given the other party. This right must be exercised within a reasonable time after he attains his majority. 

If a minor makes a contract which is to be completed at a future date, he is not, upon becoming of age, bound by the contract unless he indicates his willingness to be bound. 

It is evident that laws relating to the contracts of minors are for the purpose of protecting them. It makes them liable on contracts for necessary goods actually supplied, but only for the reasonable value of such goods. It permits them to make other contracts, but to avoid carrying out such contracts if later they believe that these agreements are not to their advantage. If, then, an older person who, it is assumed, is better acquainted with business and law than a minor, makes a contract with a minor which is unfavorable to the latter, the latter may avoid carrying out his part.

Although a minor can avoid carrying out a contract, he is not relieved of the moral responsibility to keep his promise when it was honestly gained. Minors practically always do keep their promises and seldom find it necessary to avail themselves of the privilege of refusing to carry out the terms of an agreement. It is only when some unfair advantage has been taken that they should seek the protection of the law.

Mentally Deficient Persons. Persons who are not mentally responsible are also given special protection in the making of contracts. If an agreement is made by an insane

ch. 27] 453 

or feeble-minded person or by one so under the influence of liquor that he is not capable of knowing what he is doing, the agreement can be enforced on his part; but if the agreement is unfavorable to him, he, or some one acting for him, can, as a general rule, disaffirm it and thus avoid obligation. Of course, rights under a contract cannot be claimed, and obligations under the same contract avoided. 

It is necessary that the contract be either affirmed or disaffirmed as a whole.

If a contract is made during a lucid interval by a person who is sometimes insane, or if a contract is made by a person while he is sober, although he is at other times under the influence of liquor, the contract is enforceable. 

1 1. Gardner, a college student who was supporting himself, bought a suit of clothes for forty dollars, a fair price for that suit. After he had worn the suit a few times, he repented his bargain and sought to avoid making payment on the grounds that he was a minor when the contract was made. Was this a good defense? 

2. Gardner also agreed to buy an expensive automobile. 

He later decided that he could not afford the car. Could the dealer force him to pay for the car as he had agreed? 

3. Judson, a minor, purchased a suit of clothes for fifty dollars, the advertised price. He decided that the price was too high and that he would not make payment. The merchant then demanded the return of the suit. Judson refused to return the suit but offered to pay thirty-five dollars, which he considered to be a fair price. Could the merchant force the return of the suit? 

4. Harms, a minor, sold a watch which had belonged to his grandfather, for fifty dollars. He later wished to regain the watch, but he had spent the money and could not immediately return that amount. Had he a legal right to demand the return of the watch? 

5. A person, while sober, agreed to purchase an automobile. Later he attempted to avoid carrying out the contract. 

He claimed that he was sometimes under the influence of liquor, although he admitted that he was sober when this purchase was agreed upon. Could he be held to his agreement?

[ch. 27 

 Section IV. OTHER REQUISITES OF A CONTRACT 

Legality. An agreement is enforceable only when it involves a legal object. An agreement between two persons to rob a bank is not enforceable. If one person bets with another over the outcome of a football game, the winner cannot collect from the loser by legal procedure. 

In many states certain contracts made on Sunday are not enforceable. A person should investigate the law in his state if he considers making an important contract on Sunday. Ordinarily, the possibility of Sunday contracts not being enforceable is of no great importance, for contracts are usually made on business days. 

Consideration. To be enforceable an agreement must have consideration. This means that the promise of each person must be exchanged for something of value. The inducement which is given the other may consist of things such as money or goods; or it may consist of doing or refraining from doing something that the party would not otherwise have had to do or refrain from doing. 

Examples of consideration are:

A customer in a retail grocery purchased five dollars' worth of goods on credit. The grocer delivered the groceries and the customer promised to pay for them. The promise to pay money was the consideration given by the customer, and the groceries were the consideration given by the grocer. 

The Barnes Company hired James Barr, a watchman.

In the agreement the Barnes Company promised to pay Barr $125 a month for his services. The promise to pay the money was the consideration given by the Barnes Company, and the promise to watch the property was the consideration given by Barr.

A promise to do something that a person is already required to do is not consideration. If one person owes another ten dollars, a promise to pay this amount is not consideration for a new contract. The debtor was already legally obliged to make the payment. If, however, the

ch. 27] 455 

debtor promises to pay this amount before it is due, or in some place other than that mentioned in the contract, there will be consideration for a new contract.

Consideration need not have a money value; it is adequate if it has a value to the party to the contract, or if it requires that he do something that he otherwise would not be required to do. For example, Adams promised Webster five dollars to watch his automobile while Adams attended a ball game. Later Adams proved that there had been no danger of his car being stolen and no need for its being watched. He therefore contended that there was no consideration for the contract since Webster had not given him a valuable service. Webster could collect the money promised, however, for in accordance with the agreement he had done something that he was not legally obliged to do, and therefore had given sufficient consideration. 

Writing. A contract does not ordinarily need to be in writing, but there are a few agreements which must be in writing to be enforceable.

Among the most important which must be written are contracts involving the sale of real estate, contracts involving the sale of a large amount of personal property, contracts to pay the debts of another, and contracts which by their terms cannot be completed within a year from the time they are made.

All contracts involving the sale of real estate (land or property attached to land) must be in writing. This includes not only contracts to transfer titles, but also contracts to sell real estate at some time in the future. 

The laws regarding the sale of personal property (property other than real estate) are not the same in all states. 

Generally, however, a contract involving the sale of a large amount of personal property, unless the goods are partially delivered and accepted, or partly paid for, must be proved by some written note or memorandum properly signed. 

A contract to answer for the debt of another person must be written. John Doe promises to pay for purchases made by Richard Roe if the price cannot be collected from

[ch. 27 

the latter. This promise is not enforceable unless it is in writing, for this is Doe's promise to answer for Roe's debt. On the other hand, if Doe had told the merchant to give Roe certain merchandise and charge it to him, Doe, the contract would have been enforceable, for this would have been Doe's debt.

Contracts which by their terms cannot be completed within the period of one year from the time they are made must ordinarily be written. An agreement to support another person for life does not have to be in writing, for the other person may not live for a year, and the contract may therefore be completed in less than that length of time. 

On the other hand, a contract to cultivate a farm for two years would have to be in writing, for obviously this could not be completed in one year's time.

Although many contracts do not have to be in writing, it is advisable to have all important contracts written. The terms of unwritten contracts may be forgotten. Misunderstandings may arise because of a lack of clearness. Witnesses who might testify to the terms of contracts may move away or die. Written contracts are therefore more satisfactory because they permit the intention of the parties to be more easily proved. 

1. The speed limit for automobiles in a certain city is thirty-five miles an hour. Eden agrees to drive an automobile for the Standard Automobile Company up a steep grade at the rate of fifty miles an hour. Is this an enforceable contract? 

2. Barr promises to give a nephew one thousand dollars when the nephew becomes of age. Later he decides that he does not want to give the nephew this amount. When the latter becomes of age, can he collect from his uncle? 

3. If you board a street car, what consideration do you give the street-car company, and what consideration does the company give you, to provide a basis for a contract? 

4. Clark promises Davis ten dollars if the latter will attend a lecture for him. Davis attends the lecture. Later

Clark learns that the lecture was very interesting and that

ch. 27] 457 

Davis had greatly enjoyed it and had benefitted from it. 

He therefore contends that Davis did not give up anything of value and that since he did not give any consideration for the contract, the ten dollars does not have to be paid. 

Do you believe that there was a valuable consideration for the contract? 

5. McClure owed Vogel $1,000 which was due on July 1. 

On June 15 McClure told Vogel that he would pay $950 on

July 1 if that would be accepted in settlement of the debt. 

Vogel agreed to this, and on July 1 accepted $950. He then attempted to collect the additional $50, claiming that there was no consideration for the agreement to accept $950 in full payment. Should he be able to collect? 

6. As a joke, Joyce offered Carl ten dollars if he would stand on a certain corner for three hours and wait for a fivepassenger car which fitted a certain description. Carl did not understand that this was a joke and stood on the corner for three hours, waiting for the car to arrive. It did not appear. Later Carl attempted to collect the ten dollars from Joyce, but the latter contended that the whole matter was a joke and that Carl did not render any valuable service which would entitle him, to payment. What is your opinion of this? 

7. Burnett orally agrees to buy a watch from Alms.

He also orally agrees to buy a house and lot. Are both of these agreements enforceable? 

8. Slocum, Ady, and Carson are in a retail store together. Both Ady and Carson wish to purchase a book, but neither has any money. Slocum says to the proprietor, "Give Ady a book and charge it to me." Later he also says, "Give Carson a book and if he does not pay you, I will."

The merchant delivers books to both Ady and Carson. He is unable to collect the cost of the books from these men and attempts to collect from Slocum. Is Slocum liable for the cost of both books? 

9. Chase agreed to erect a building for Durand. The work was postponed from time to time, and was not completed at the end of a year. Durand then tried to avoid the contract, claiming that it was not enforceable since it had not been completed within a year and was not in writing. Do you believe that this made the contract unenforceable?

[ch. 27 

 Section V. AGENCY Law, and Action Through Representatives. Laws aid business by making it possible for a person or group of persons to act through representatives. One who acts as the representative of another is described as an agent. The one for whom the act is performed is known as the principal. With our large organizations, the need for agents is apparent. All of those interested in a great company cannot meet together to act jointly for every transaction completed. If the business is to be carried on, agents must be appointed to have the right to complete transactions. Individuals also frequently find it necessary to appoint agents. 

If your mother wishes to obtain groceries from a neighboring store, she may send you for them rather than go for them herself. If she does, you are her agent in completing this transaction. 

Who May Be a Principal. Anyone who can act for himself can act through an agent. A person who is of age and of sound mind, or a group of such persons can therefore appoint agents to act for them. On the other hand, an insane person or a minor cannot be bound by the acts of an agent. 

Who May Be an Agent. Almost anyone can act as an agent. Since the act of the agent binds the principal and not the agent, the agent does not need to be of age. 

The law protects the principal by preventing an agent's acting for the principal when he has an interest which is adverse to the interest of the principal. Thus, a person cannot act as the agent for the buyer of property when he himself is the seller or when he is acting as the agent of the seller. In such a case his interest is adverse to the interest of the buyer.

Classification of Agents. Agents are usually divided into two groups, special agents and general agents. A special agent is authorized to complete one transaction or a single kind of transaction. A general agent is authorized to perform all kinds of transactions in connection with the operation of a particular business. For example, a sales

ch. 27] 459 

clerk in a retail store is a special agent, for he is authorized only to sell merchandise. The general manager of a retail store is a general agent as he is authorized to complete all of the transactions necessary in the operation of the business.

A distinction between special and general agents is important because of their power to bind the principal. A special agent's power to bind the principal is limited to certain kinds of transactions. A sales clerk in a retail store, for example, cannot bind the principal in the purchase of goods. A general agent, on the other hand, can bind the principal in all transactions necessary in the operation of the business. Even his power, however, is not unlimited, for a general agent who is authorized to operate a grocery business cannot bind his principal in the purchase of investments.

1. The janitor of a certain school is authorized by the school board to order coal from a certain dealer as the coal is needed. Is the janitor the agent of the school board? 

2. Herschel, who is eighteen years of age, appoints Gordon, who is twenty-five years of age, as his agent. He directs Gordon to purchase a typewriter. If Gordon contracts for a typewriter, is Herschel bound by the contract? 

3. Later Gordon appoints Herschel as his agent and directs him to buy a typewriter. Herschel contracts for the typewriter as the agent of Gordon. Is Gordon bound by this contract? 

4. In Problem 3 was Herschel a special agent or a general agent? 

5. A customer purchased a pair of shoes from a clerk for five dollars. He then noticed an office desk which he thought unusually attractive. The clerk offered it to him for twenty-five dollars. This offer was accepted. Later the clerk offered to sell the entire stock and equipment of the store to the customer for three thousand dollars. This offer was also accepted. Which of these agreements do you consider to be enforceable?

[ch. 27 

 Section VI. NEGOTIABLE INSTRUMENTS 

Introduction. Certain forms, some of which have been discussed in earlier chapters, are known as negotiable instruments. It is not necessary for us to consider exactly what a negotiable instrument is. It is sufficient for us to know that checks, notes, drafts, money orders, bonds, and similar unconditional promises or orders to pay definite amounts of money at certain times are in this class. 

To facilitate the use of negotiable instruments, the law gives holders of them rights which are not given to holders of other property. Ordinarily, if property is stolen and sold, the original owner can claim it from the holder. If, however, a negotiable instrument that is payable to bearer is stolen, and if it is transferred to another person under certain conditions, this latter party can collect the amount of the instrument from the maker or the drawer. 

Holder in Due Course. In order to claim the special rights that are given to holders of negotiable instruments, a person must be what the law terms a holder in due course. 

A holder in due course is one who takes the instrument under the following conditions: (1) The instrument must be complete and regular upon its face. This means that the instrument must be written in a complete form and must not show any changes or alterations which would cause a reasonably prudent person to doubt its genuineness. (2) The instrument must not be overdue. If, then, a note dated July 1, due in sixty days, is transferred on October 1, the holder is not a holder in due course. (3) The instrument must be taken in good faith and for value. This means that the instrument must have been received in the ordinary course of business in exchange for a reasonable amount. If a note for $500, made by a responsible business man, was purchased for $5, the holder would not be considered a holder in due course, for the amount paid would not be considered reasonable. The sale of the note for such an amount would be considered suffi-

ch. 27] 461 

cient to indicate to a reasonably prudent person that there was some defect in the title. (4) At the time the instrument is transferred, the one receiving it must not know of any defect in its title. If a person knows that a negotiable instrument, such as a note, was secured by fraud or without consideration, he cannot become a holder in due course of that instrument.

Qualities of Negotiable Instruments. The special quali ties of negotiable instruments make the acceptance of such instruments somewhat safer than it otherwise would be. 

If the instrument is not overdue, and if it appears to be complete and regular, a person needs only to consider whether the instrument is genuine and whether the parties are solvent. He must know that the instrument is genuine, for a forged instrument is worthless in the hands of any holder. Also, if the maker of an instrument is insolvent, the instrument may be worthless, even in the hands of a holder in due course.

The special qualities of negotiable instruments make it necessary for one to guard such instruments with unusual care. For example, if a note made payable to "Bearer" is stolen and is transferred by the thief to a holder in due course, the latter can collect its amount. 

Summary. Throughout the various sections of this chapter we have been observing ways in which law aids and controls business. The ways mentioned in this chapter are not the only ones of importance. In Chapters XVII and 

XVIII we have observed that the law aids business in providing for the formation of companies known as insurance companies which assume risks that individuals or businesses would otherwise have to bear. In Chapter XXVI we observed that the law provides for different types of business organizations, such as the partnership and the corporation, which are useful in carrying on business affairs. 

Many other ways in which law aids and controls business, and a further discussion of the methods presented in this chapter may be obtained through courses in business law such as may be offered in your school.

[ch. 27 

  

1. On August 1 Henning bought a note for five dollars which was supposed to have been signed by a wealthy business man. From the description given below, point out the different reasons why he was not a holder in due course. (a) The note was for one thousand dollars. (b) The note was dated February 2. (c) The time of the note was sixty days. (d) The note was signed with a typewriter. 

2. Two persons offer Gates notes signed by Boyd. The face value of each note is one thousand dollars. Neither note is due. Boyd's reputation for paying his debts is excellent. Gates purchases both notes. For one he pays $950; for the other he pays $25. Is he a holder in due course of both notes? 

3. X stole a note payable to "Bearer" and transferred it to Y, who knew that the note was stolen. Was Y a holder in due course? 

4. X forges Y's name to a note. He then transfers the note to Z, who does not know of the forgery. The time of the note has not yet expired, and the note appears in every way to be complete and regular. Is Z a holder in due course? 

5. X owes one thousand dollars. This debt is evidenced by a note for that amount. Several days before the note is due, X pays Y but does not secure the note. Y immediately transfers the note to Z for a reasonable amount. Can Z collect the amount of the note from X?

CHAPTER XXVIII SOURCES OF INFORMATION Section I. REFERENCE BOOKS FOR TRAVEL 

Introduction. A single library may contain hundreds of thousands of books, in each of which valuable information is printed. One of these books may contain over a million words printed on over a thousand pages. Hundreds of facts may be found on each page. Many of these facts may be of temporary value; that is, they may be true today, but not true tomorrow.

With such a tremendous amount of information available and with much of it temporary in nature, it is obviously impossible for anyone to learn all of it. It is sufficient in most cases if one knows where to find certain information and how to use it when it has been found. 

The number of reference books is so great that we cannot consider all of them here. We shall, however, study a few that are most commonly used. They will illustrate the purpose of reference books and their use. 

Preliminary Study. Before a person begins to search for information, he should, if possible, be sufficiently familiar with reference material to know where he will most likely find the information desired. Before starting to use a particular book, he should study it carefully to acquaint himself with the information contained in it. Many persons are handicapped in their use of common books of reference because they do not realize the different types of information which these books contain. The following study of reference material will suggest the sources which are available and the types of information which each provides. 

Time Tables. In Chapter XII we observed that a time table published by a transportation company contains an 463

[ch. 28 

index to stations and a table showing the cities and towns served by that company. In addition, a time table usually contains a map showing the lines operated and the principal cities served by each. This information is useful when one is planning a trip, especially when it is necessary for him to use the services of more than one company. 

On many pages in time tables of railroads, special services available on different trains are described. Notes about these services are shown in Figure 78 on page 173. 

Official Guide of the Railways. Those of us in the United 

States use this book most frequently as a guide to the railways in this country, as was explained in Chapter XII. In addition, however, it contains various kinds of information, of which the following are the most important: (a) Tables for railroads in other North American countries, and Cuba. (b) Tables for air lines, with some information about fares and train and bus connections. (c) Tables for steamship lines, with dates of sailing and arrival, and information about fares. 

Maps. Useful maps are available for those planning to travel by railroad, bus, airplane, or automobile. The time tables for the different transportation companies usually contain maps showing the lines for the various companies, and sometimes the principal connecting lines. Larger maps and atlases may show all of the railroad and transportation lines in a certain territory. Such maps are helpful to a person who is planning a trip which will require the use of more than one road. He can readily trace the lines serving the cities of departure and destination, and can thus find the reasonable junction point. This aids him in finding the proper tables in the pamphlets of time tables published by the different transportation companies. 

Road maps, published in atlases and separately, are useful to one traveling by automobile. Such maps are exceptionally complete. They commonly show all highways; the quality of the improvements on each highway; the distance

ch. 28] 465 

SOURCES OF INFORMATION 

between each city, town, or village and the nearby communities with which there are highway connections. 

Hotel Directory. This directory contains a list of the principal hotels in the United States and Canada. For many hotels the number of rooms, the rates, and the name of the manager are given.

The symbol following the number of rooms indicates that the hotel is European plan; that is, the rate charged is for the room only. The symbol indicates that the hotel is American plan; that is, the rate charged is for the room and meals. The majority of the hotels in this country are European plan. 

The hotels are classified geographically. They are first divided according to the states of the United States and the provinces of Canada, and then according to cities. The different hotels in one city are listed alphabetically. 

This guide is very useful to a person who wishes to plan a trip in advance and desires to know the names of the hotels at which he will stop, so that he can use these hotels as mailing addresses or can write to them for room reservations. 

Questions for Class Discussion and Laboratory Problems are not given with this chapter as they are to be based on the reference books actually available for study. They will be assigned by the teacher from material published separately.

[ch. 28 

 Section II. REFERENCE BOOKS FOR MAIL AND TRANSPORTATION Official Postal Guide. This book is published by the 

United States Government Printing Office, in Washington, D. C. It contains complete information about all of the services of the postal department, including all classes of domestic and international service. The guide is quite large and contains an immense amount of information, but it is carefully indexed so that any information desired at one time can be located quickly.

The guide also contains lists of post offices classified in several different ways. One list contains the names of post offices classified alphabetically according to states, and then alphabetically according to offices. Another is an alphabetic list of post offices in which the offices are arranged alphabetically without reference to states. When the list was made up, the state name was taken into consideration only when offices with the same name were found in two or more states. 

A third list contains the names of post offices classified, first, according to states; second, according to counties; and, third, according to offices. This list is especially valuable if one wishes to find the names of all post offices in a certain county or group of counties.

The postal guide may be used for many purposes. It is probably most commonly used by the ordinary shipper as a source of information about parcel-post zones. 

Official Directory of the Railway Express Agency. This directory contains several classes of information, but its chief purpose is to provide rates from one express office to express offices in the United States, Canada, and Mexico. 

All express offices in the United States are listed alphabetically under the names of states. A number of offices in other countries are listed in the back of the directory, arranged alphabetically under the names of the countries. After the name of each office, a key number is given which makes it possible for one to refer to other tables and to find the express rates for packages of different weights.

ch. 28] 467 

SOURCES OF INFORMATION Express and Parcel Post Comparative Rate Guide. This is a guide which facilitates the comparing of express and parcel-post rates from one shipping point to various points in the United States and Canada. It also contains general information about express service, parcel-post service, air express service, and foreign mail.

Bullinger's Postal and Shippers' Guide. An immense amount of useful information relating to mail, express, and parcel post is contained in this book. It probably is used most frequently by shippers in finding routes for freight shipments. The guide is very desirable for this purpose since it shows the railroads serving each city, town, or village. For a town or village not on a railroad, it shows the nearest railroad station and the road or roads serving it. 

In Figure 184 the number or numbers after the name of each place refer to the railroad or railroads serving that point. Elsewhere in the book there is a complete list of railroads, numbered consecutively. When one finds a number after the name of a place, he can quickly locate the corresponding railroad name.

[ch. 28 

 Section III. BUSINESS DIRECTORIES 

City Directory. For most cities, a directory is published to assist a person in finding the names and addresses of individuals and businesses. The directory is usually published in several sections, or in several volumes if the city is large. 

One section contains an alphabetic list of names of people and businesses. The occupation or position, the business address, and the home address are given for all adults, usually for those over twenty years of age. This part of the directory is useful when a person's name is known, but his address or occupation is unknown. 

Another section of the directory contains the names of all streets alphabetically arranged, the location of each street, the house numbers, and the names of adults living at each address. This directory is used when the names of those living at a certain address are desired. It is particularly useful when complete lists of names for certain territories are desired.

The third section is the classified business directory. 

This contains a list of the names of business persons, firms, and institutions, arranged according to pursuits. It is, then, similar to the classified section of the telephone directory. 

Directory of Manufacturers. A directory of manufacturers is used largely by those who wish to know where to purchase certain goods. It contains several lists to assist prospective purchasers in finding the information they wish. 

In one list, the names of manufacturers are alphabetically arranged. This list is used when the person knows the name of the company, but does not know the address of the company, or of a branch office.

In another list, the names are classified according to products. Here the names of all manufacturers of one product are given together. This classification is helpful when one wishes to obtain the names of those manufacturing certain goods.

A third list classifies products according to their trade names. Many products are given trade names which are different from the names of the companies manufacturing

Telephone Directory. The two most common telephone directories are the alphabetic directory and the classified business directory, discussed in Chapter VIII. The former contains an alphabetic list of subscribers; the latter, a list classified according to occupations and professions. 

In addition, numeric telephone guides are provided for some cities. Such a guide is divided into sections according to the names of exchanges. The telephone numbers are then listed numerically, and for each number the subscriber's name and address are given. This enables a person to find the name and the address of the subscriber when he knows only the telephone number.

Financial Directories. Two important groups of financial directories are the credit rating books published by R. G. Dun and Company and The Bradstreet Company, and the investment manuals published by Moody's Investors Service and Poor's Publishing Company. The former were discussed in Chapter XXII. Their purpose is to show the financial strength and the credit ratings of businesses.

Poor's and Moody's manuals of investments provide much data about the important businesses, in which investors might be interested. The history of each business, the earnings during past years, the property owned and the debts owed at different times, and many other facts useful to investors are included.

Because of the great number of companies, not all of this information is given in one volume, but it is classified according to services rendered. One volume contains data about public utilities, such as telephone companies and street railways. Other volumes contain information about railroads, banks, financial organizations, and industrial organizations. Still another volume contains information about the securities of governments and municipalities, which may be available for investors.

ch. 28] 471 

SOURCES OF INFORMATION Section IV. GENERAL REFERENCE BOOKS 

Dictionary. A dictionary is most frequently used as a source of information about the meaning, pronunciation, and spelling of words. In addition to the word list, to which most frequent reference is made, an unabridged dictionary contains other valuable information. A partial list of the additional divisions are: (a) A section at the bottom of each page, containing words less commonly used; obsolete words; and other forms, such as the adverbial form, of words listed above. (b) Addenda containing an alphabetic list of new words or words with new meanings. Pronunciation, definitions, etc., are given the same as in the general section. (c) A list of names of persons, arranged alphabetically, with pronunciation, explanation, date of birth, and date of death, if the person is deceased. Most of these individuals are contemporaries. (d) Population of cities in the United States, having more than five thousand inhabitants. (e) A geographic dictionary of the world, listing cities and countries, and containing many facts about them. (f) A pronouncing biographical dictionary containing the names of noteworthy persons, and biographical information.

Encyclopedia. The various standard encyclopedias are a valuable source of information on a tremendous number of subjects. They attempt to include much information about every principal subject and something essential about other subjects. The information is classified under tens of thousands of headings.

World Almanac. This almanac gathers together in one volume a great number of facts on subjects of all kinds. The range of subjects covered is indicated by the clipping from the index for one year, illustrated on the following page.

The almanac is especially useful as a source book since it is printed each year and contains many recent data.
