Government Publications. Bulletins containing much valuable information on a great variety of subjects are issued by the various departments of the national government and are sold at nominal prices by the Superintendent of Documents, Washington, D. C. Price lists containing titles and prices of all publications in each group or general classification are issued by the Superintendent of Documents. 

From an examination of one of these lists, it is possible for a person to find the bulletin or bulletins in which he is most interested.

Summary. The foregoing pages do not contain a complete list of valuable sources of information. Their purpose is rather to describe briefly a few of the most commonly used reference books and to illustrate the different kinds of reference books published and the information that may be found in them. Books without number, magazines, trade journals, catalogs, and printed material of all kinds furnish practically unlimited sources of information.

WORD STUDY 

An understanding of the vocabulary of business greatly aids a person in obtaining business information. The ability to use effectively a broad vocabulary also aids one in satisfactorily filling a business position. 

The vocabulary of business is, in general, the same as any other vocabulary. Any reading and studying which increases your general vocabulary will therefore help you in business. There are, however, certain words and phrases which are frequently used in business, sometimes with special meanings. Some of these words are found in the preceding chapters. In this section of the text these words are briefly defined. No attempt is made to give all possible definitions, but rather those which define the word as it is used in this book. 

Study the following words and definitions carefully. A mastery of them will help you, first, to understand the discussions in this text; second, to understand any business information or instructions given elsewhere; and, third, to fill a position in business satisfactorily. 

CHAPTER I 

barter: an exchange of goods for goods. business: buying and selling, or traffic in general; any particular occupation or employment habitually engaged in, especially for livelihood or gain. cashier: one who has charge of money; specifically, the officer who has charge of the payments and receipts (money, checks, notes) of a bank or a mercantile company. cash receipts: cash which has been received. cash register: a device for recording the amount of cash received, usually having an automatic adding machine and exhibiting the amount of the sale. currency: that which is in circulation, or passes from hand to hand, as a medium of exchange, such as coin. denomination: one of a series of values denoted by special names; as, the denominations of United States money ($1, $2, $5). 473

deposit: that which is placed anywhere, or in any one's hands, for safe-keeping; especially money lodged with a bank or banker, subject to order. To lodge for safe-keeping or as a pledge; especially to put on deposit in a bank. face value: the value stamped on the face; nominal value, as of a stamp, note, etc. income: that gain which proceeds from labor, business, or property. medium of exchange: a means by which one thing is given or taken in return for another regarded as an equivalent. product: anything produced, as by generation, growth, or thought; as, the products of the farm; the products of manufactures. receipt: a writing acknowledging the taking or receiving of goods or money. To give a receipt for; to put a receipt on; as, to receipt a bill. standard of value: that which is made the measure of value in any monetary system. transaction: the doing or performing of any affair; as, the transaction of business; that which is done or in the process of being done; as, many business transactions were completed. 

CHAPTER II 

account: a written or printed statement of business dealings, or of a certain class of them. bank balance: the amount a depositor has left on deposit in a bank after the checks which he has drawn have been paid. bank check: a written order directing a bank or banker to pay money. bearer: one who holds a check, note, draft, or other order for the payment of money. check: see bank check. check register: a book in which entries are made which give detailed information about checks. check-writing machine: a machine which stamps the amount of a check on the proper line in such a way that it cannot be altered easily. depositor: one who makes a deposit, especially of money in a bank. drawer: one who draws or issues an order for payment. overdraw (a bank account): to write checks in excess of the amount on deposit in the bank. payee: the person to whom money is to be, or has been paid; the person named in a note or check as the one to whom, or to whose order, the amount is promised or directed to be paid. post-dated: dated after the real time, as a check.

WORD STUDY 

475 raised check: a check on which the amount has been fraudulently increased. stub (of a check book, receipt book, etc.): a small part of each leaf permanently attached to the back for memoranda of the contents of the part torn away. valid: founded on truth or fact; having legal strength or force. 

CHAPTER III 

forged: made or imitated falsely; counterfeited, as a signature, or a signed document. guarantee: to become responsible for the fulfillment of; to give or furnish security to. guaranty: an agreement by which one undertakes to make sure or certain the existence, continuance, or the like, of something. honor: to accept and pay when due; as, to honor a check. identification: act of proving the same (with something described, claimed, or asserted); act of establishing the identity of. indorse: to write one's name, alone or with other words, on the back of (a paper), to transfer it, or to secure its payment or performance. indorsement: a writing, usually on the back but sometimes on the face, of a negotiable instrument, by which the property therein is assigned and transferred. indorsement in blank: an indorsement which omits the name of the person in whose favor the indorsement is made, usually made by simply writing the name of the indorser on the back of the paper. 

Such an indorsement makes the paper payable to bearer. indorsement in full: an indorsement which includes the name of the person in whose favor the indorsement is made. Such an indorsement makes the paper payable only to the person in whose favor it was indorsed, or on his order. indorser: the person who indorses. restrictive indorsement: an indorsement which prohibits further negotiation, or which gives authority to deal with the paper as directed, but not to transfer ownership.

CHAPTER IV 

A. B. A. numbers: numbers given to each bank in the United States by the American Bankers' Association. bank book: the depositor's book in which a bank enters the amounts of deposits made and of checks written by the depositor.

bank numbers: see A. B. A. numbers. bank statement: a statement given a depositor by the bank, which shows the beginning balance, the deposits made, the checks paid by the bank, and the balance in the bank. canceled check: a check which has been stamped to show that it has been paid. check book: a book containing blank checks upon a bank. deposit slip: a slip or ticket which a depositor leaves with a deposit, containing a memorandum of checks, funds, etc., deposited, with the date, etc. deposit ticket: see deposit slip.

Federal Reserve System: a banking system established in the United 

States by the Federal Reserve Act of December 23, 1913. 

joint bank account: an account in a bank in which two or more persons make deposits and against which they write checks. outstanding check: a check which has been issued, but not paid by the bank. pass book: see bank book. reconciliation of bank balance: comparison of a bank statement with the check stubs to prove the accuracy of both records. signature card: card used in a bank, which contains the signature or signatures of a depositor.

CHAPTER V 

amount (of an interest-bearing note): the sum of the principal and the interest. bank discount: a sum equal to the interest at a given rate on the amount of a note from the time of discounting until it becomes due. date of maturity: date on which the period a note or other obligation has to run, terminates. discount: to lend money upon, deducting a discount or allowance for interest; as, banks discount notes. face of a note: amount for which the note was written. indorsement for collection: the indorsement placed on a note by the owner when he wishes to have it collected by the bank and wants to show that the note is still his property. indorsement for partial payment: an indorsement of a note showing that part of the amount has been paid. indorsement for security: an indorsement of a note by a person other than the maker in order to aid the maker in borrowing money on the note.

WORD STUDY 

477 interest: a rate per cent of money paid for the use of money. interest-bearing note: a note which includes interest in the promise to pay the amount of the loan. loan: that which one lends or borrows, especially a sum of money lent at interest. maker: the person who makes a promissory note. non-interest-bearing note: a note in which the promise to pay does not include interest. note: a written promise to pay. note book: a book containing blank promissory notes. obligation: an agreement or acknowledgment of a liability to pay a certain sum, or do a certain thing. principal: a sum of money placed at interest, due as a debt or used as a fund. proceeds: that which results, proceeds, or accrues from some possession or transaction; as, the proceeds of a note; the proceeds of a sale. promissory note: see note. property: that to which a person has a legal title; thing owned. rate: quantity or amount measured per unit of time; as, the legal rate of interest. security: protection; that which makes safe; something given, deposited, or pledged to make secure or certain the fulfillment of an obligation, the payment of a debt, etc. time (of a note): the years, months, or days before a note is due.

CHAPTER VI 

application for a postal money order: a printed form used in making a request for a postal money order. bank draft: a check which one bank draws on funds deposited in another bank. cashier's check: a check drawn by a bank upon its own funds, signed by the cashier. certified check: a check certified to be good by the bank upon which it is drawn, by the signature of the cashier or paying teller with the word certified or its equivalent across the face of the check. express money order: an order for the payment of money issued at an express office and payable at another express office or a bank. postal money order: a government order for the payment of money issued at one post office and payable at some specified office.

registered mail: a letter or parcel, the addresses of the sender and the consignee of which are, on the payment of a special fee, registered in the post office, and the transmission and delivery of which are attended to with certain formalities for the sake of security. remittance: transmittal of money, bills, etc., especially to a distant place; also the thing, especially money, remitted. unlimited check: an order for the payment of money issued at an express office, which can be used in sending large remittances. 

CHAPTER VII 

charge customer: one who buys on credit. contract: an agreement between two or more persons to do or for bear from doing something, especially such an agreement that is legally enforceable. credit: mercantile reputation entitling one to be trusted; the time given for payment for lands or goods sold on trust. credit association: an organization which furnishes information about the credit ratings of individuals and businesses to merchants. creditor: one who gives credit in business matters; hence, one to whom money is due. credit rating: the classification of a person according to the promptness with which he meets his obligations. credit terms: a space of time granted to a debtor for discharging his obligation. debtor: one who owes a debt. finance company: a company organized for the purpose of financing sales and making collections. installment purchase: a purchase in which the amount is divided into portions that are made payable at different times. installment sale: a sale in which the amount is divided into portions that are made payable at different times. merchant:. anyone making a business of buying and selling commodities. on account: to be paid for later; on credit. on open account: see on account. sales ticket: form on which a merchant records the articles sold to a customer and the prices of the articles. surety bond: an agreement on the part of a surety company to be responsible for any loss caused by an individual in handling money or in doing anything for which he is responsible.

WORD STUDY 

479 surety company: a company which for a fee assumes responsibility for the acts of others. terms of credit: see credit terms.

CHAPTER VIII 

alphabetic: expressed in the order of the letters of the alphabet. appointment call: a long-distance call in which the connection is to be established with a particular person at a specified time. classified: distributed into classes; arranged according to a system. collect call: a telephone call on which the charges are to be billed against the called station. continental telephone: a telephone the receiver and mouthpiece of which are in one unit. desk telephone: a telephone the receiver and mouthpiece of which are separate units. dial telephone: a telephone which has a circular dial which a person uses in obtaining a connection with another telephone. dial tone: a steady humming sound which is heard on a dial telephone when the receiver is removed, and which indicates that the line is ready for use. extension telephone: an extra telephone which is provided by the telephone company for a subscriber and is located in a different part of the building. hand telephone: see continental telephone. local calls: all telephone calls which are made in one city or through one exchange. long-distance calls: all telephone calls which are made to other towns and cities. manual telephone: a telephone in which the number is given to the central operator who makes the connection by hand. messenger call: a telephone call to a person who does not have a telephone, and in which a messenger of the telephone company calls that person to a connecting telephone. party line: a telephone line which is used by two or more subscribers. person-to-person call: a long-distance call in which the connection is made with a particular person whenever he can be located. private branch exchange: a private telephone exchange of a particular business in which a private switchboard operated by an employee of the business is connected with the central office. report charge: a charge which is made by the telephone company when the person desired in an appointment call or a person-toperson call cannot be located by the telephone company.

station-to-station call: a long-distance call in which the person calling will talk to any person at a given business or telephone number. switchboard: an apparatus consisting of panels, bearing a collection of switches so arranged that a number of circuits may be connected. telephone directory: a book containing the names and addresses of persons and businesses with their telephone numbers, and giving general information about telephone services. telephone-to-telephone call: see station-to-station call. transmitter: that portion of a telegraphic or telephonic instrument by means of which a message is sent. 

CHAPTER IX 

cablegram: a message sent by a submarine telegraphic cable. call box: a device installed in a place of business for use in summoning messengers of the telegraph company. central time: the standard time used in the greater part of the Mississippi Valley. code: a system of words or other symbols arbitrarily used to represent words or phrases for brevity or secrecy. day letter: a telegram sent during the day at a rate lower than the standard message rate, with a minimum charge equal to the charge for fifty words. daylight saving time: time one hour faster than standard time. eastern time: the standard time used chiefly in the states along the 

Atlantic Coast, and in adjoining states. fast day telegram: the fastest type of message sent by a telegraph company, for which the minimum rate is the charge for ten words. lettergram: see day letter and night letter. mountain time: the standard time used in almost all of the Rocky 

Mountain district. night letter: a telegram similar to a day letter except that it is sent at night and at a lower rate. night message: a telegram which is similar to a fast day telegram but which is sent at night to be delivered the following morning. night telegram: see night message.

Pacific time: the standard time used in the states bordering on the 

Pacific Ocean, and in adjoining states. radiogram: a message transmitted by a wireless telegraph. telegram: a message sent by telegraph; also, the fastest type of message sent by a telegraph company. See fast day telegram. waived: relinquished; gave up a claim to.

WORD STUDY 

481 

CHAPTER X 

air mail: mail carried by airplanes. 

C. O. D.: abbreviation for collect (or cash) on delivery, meaning that the value of the package will be collected before the package is delivered to the addressee. collect on delivery: see C. O. D. dead letter division: a department of the general post office to which all letters which have lain for a certain fixed time uncalled for at the post office are sent to be opened and either returned to the writer or destroyed. first-class matter: a division of the United States domestic mail comprising, in general, all matter wholly or partly written, sealed or unsealed, and all matter sealed or otherwise closed against ready inspection. fourth-class matter: a division of the United States domestic mail comprising the same matter as that in third-class except that the weight of a package is over one-half pound. indemnity: compensation for loss, damage, or injury sustained. insurance: a contract whereby for a stipulated consideration one party undertakes to indemnify or guarantee another against loss by a certain specified contingency or peril. in transit: during passage. parcel post: that branch of the post office having to do with the collection, transmission, and delivery of parcels. parcel-post mail: see fourth-class matter. second-class matter: a division of the United States domestic mail comprising all newspapers and other periodicals, lawfully bearing the statement, "Entered at the post office as second-class matter." special delivery: prompt delivery of mail matter by special postal messenger. special handling: as prompt delivery of parcel-post matter as of first-class matter. third-class matter: a division of the United States domestic mail comprising mailable matter not first- or second-class and weighing eight ounces or less.

CHAPTER XI 

adjustment: the bringing of a thing or things into proper or exact position or condition. air express: service of express companies in sending packages by airplane.

bill of lading: a written account of goods shipped, signed by the agent of a common carrier, as a railroad, acknowledging receipt of the goods and promising to deliver them safe at the place indicated. charges collect: the sending of a package in such a way that the cost of transportation will not have to be paid by the shipper but will be paid by the one receiving the package. consignee: one to whom something is consigned or shipped. consignor: one who consigns or ships something. damaged: injured or impaired. express: a company or system for the prompt and safe transportation of parcels, money, or goods; also, an office or depot of such a company. To send by means of an express company. freight: compensation for the carriage of goods; that with which anything is laden for transportation; the ordinary transportation of goods directly afforded by a common carrier, especially a railroad company; cargo, especially of a car on a railroad. memorandum: an informal record of something which it is desired to remember or to preserve for future use, especially of the writer; a note to help or jog the memory. prepaid charges: the cost of transportation which is paid by the shipper at the time the shipment is made. transportation: act of conveying or transporting; the state of being conveyed or transported. truck lines: transportation lines over which freight is carried by means of trucks.

CHAPTER XII 

baggage: the trunks, valises, satchels, etc., which one carries along on a journey. baggage car: car in a train in which the trunks, valises, satchels, etc., of the passengers are carried. baggage checks: checks given a traveler to show that his baggage has been accepted for transportation and to identify the baggage when he arrives at his destination. berth: a long box or shelf on the side of a cabin, stateroom, or railway car to sleep in. chair car: a railroad car operated by the Pullman Company for daytime travel. coach passenger: a traveler who rides in an ordinary passenger car, as distinguished from one riding in a drawing-room car, a sleeping car, etc.

WORD STUDY 

483 consul: an official appointed by or with the authority of a government to reside in some foreign country to care for the commercial interests of the citizens of the appointing government. destination: the place set for the end of a journey, or to which someone is going. dining car: a car on a train, containing tables, in which meals are served. document: an original or official paper relied upon as the basis, proof, or support of anything else; in its most extended sense, including any writing, book, or other instrument conveying information. excursion: a special trip offered by a transportation company at a lower rate than that usually in effect. financial: pertaining to finances or money matters. junction: the place or point of union or meeting; specifically, the place where the lines of a railway meet or cross. letter of credit: a letter or notification addressed by a banker to one or more of his correspondents certifying that the person named therein is entitled to draw on him or his credit up to a certain sum. local train: a train which makes a stop at every or practically every station on its line; a train to accommodate a certain limited district.

Official Railway Guide: a book containing the time tables for railroads. pamphlet: a book of a few sheets of printed matter, commonly with a paper cover. parcel room: a room in a railway station in which bags and packages may be temporarily checked. parlor car: see chair car. passport: usually a formal document issued by a state officer to a citizen of the state, certifying his citizenship, authorizing him to leave the state of which he is a subject, and requesting protection for him abroad. passport application: a request for a passport; the printed form on which a request for a passport is made. 

Passport Bureau: a division of the United States Government which takes care of applications for passports.

Pullman car: a car operated by the Pullman Company. 

Pullman reservation: space in a Pullman car reserved for a traveler. railroad station: a regular stopping place in a railroad route for the convenience of passengers, taking in fuel, moving freight, etc.; also, by extension, the building or buildings connected with such a stopping place; a depot.

redeem: to regain possession of by payment of a stipulated price. refund: to give back; to restore; to repay. route: the course or way which is or is to be traveled or passed. sleeping car: a railroad car or carriage arranged with compartments and berths for sleeping. stop-over privilege: the right to interrupt a trip by stopping at some place. through train: a train for long-distance traffic, usually stopping at only the larger cities. time table: a tabular statement of the time at which or within which several things are to take place; as, the time table of trains. tourist sleeper: a railroad car similar to a Pullman car but less elaborate and consequently less expensive. 

Travelers' Aid: an association which maintains offices in large railway depots for the purpose of aiding travelers arriving in the city by giving information relative to hotels, boarding houses, etc. travelers' check: a check issued by a financial company and payable by any of the correspondents of the issuing company. visa (or vise): an indorsement made on a passport by the proper authorities, denoting that it has been examined and that the bearer is permitted to proceed. 

CHAPTER XIII 

capital: the amount of property owned by an individual or corporation at a specified time, as distinct from the income received during a given period; the amount of such property which is used for business purposes. compound interest: interest on both the original principal and the accrued interest. investment: the laying out of money in the purchase of some kind of property, especially a source of income or profit; the amount of money invested or that in which money is invested. 'rate of return: the rate of yield, as from an investment. resources: funds; money or any property that can be converted into supplies. savings: sums saved from time to time and kept unexpended. thrift: careful management of resources. 

CHAPTER XIV 

allowance: a share or portion allotted or granted; a stated quantity. balance: to arrange or prove, as a book of accounts, so that the sum of one side equals the sum of the other side.

WORD STUDY 

485 budget: a plan by following which an individual or business uses resources, such as money, wisely. budget of money: a plan by which the financial income of an individual is divided in definite proportions into allowances for savings and expenses. budget of time: a plan by which the hours of a day are divided in definite proportions into allowances for work, recreation, etc. expenditure: the laying out, as of money; that which is paid out. expense: that which is expended, or consumed; charge; cost. record: that which is written or transcribed to perpetuate a knowledge of acts or events. 

CHAPTER XV 

allotted: distributed or parceled out in parts or portions. dependent: one who is sustained by another or who relies on another for support or favor. standard budget: conditions. a plan or budget which is accurate for certain actual value: real value.

CHAPTER XVI 

availability: quality of being available or ready for use. bond: a writing under seal by which a government or corporation binds itself to pay a certain sum on or before a future day appointed. Such an instrument is made for the purpose of borrowing money. bondholder: a person who holds a bond; especially the holder of a bond of a government or a corporation. building and loan association: a society organized to afford a safe investment for savings and to aid its members in building homes or other edifices, or buying land, especially for improvement. commercial bank: a bank the business of which is to receive deposits and to pay checks which are drawn against these deposits. common stock: ordinary capital stock not sharing the privileges of preferred stock. cooperative organization: an association for the practice of economic cooperation. cooperative savings bank: a bank in which deposits are made as in any other bank, but the ownership of which is divided into shares which may be purchased by depositors. corporation: any group of persons treated by the law as an individual or unity having rights or liabilities, or both, distinct from those of the persons composing it.

dividend: a distributive sum, share, or percentage-applied to the profits as apportioned among shareholders. earnings: money earned; wages or reward gained by work or service. investment security: an evidence of debt or of property, as a bond, stock certificate, or other instrument, which is purchased for the purpose of receiving a return. investor: one who makes an investment. liquid investment: an investment which can be promptly converted into cash. long-term note: a note which is to run for a long period of time. market value: the price at which an article or commodity commonly sells. mortgage: a conveyance of property upon condition, as security for the payment of a debt or the performance of a duty, and to become void upon payment or performance according to the particular terms. par value: face value; nominal value. postal savings: savings which are deposited in a savings bank conducted by the government through the local post offices. postal savings certificate: a written testimony that serves as a receipt, given to a person when he makes a deposit in a postal savings bank. preferred stock: capital stock which has a priority over common stock, especially in the taking of dividends. profits: excess of income over expenditure, as in a business during a given period of time. risk: the chance of loss or the perils to the subject matter of insurance covered by the contract; also, the degree of probability of such loss. sale value: see market value. savings account: a bank account in which deposits are made at compound interest and which are not subject to check. savings bank: a bank the business of which is to receive and invest small deposits, chiefly savings, and to pay compound interest thereon. semi-annually: half yearly, or twice a year. share: any of the equal interests or rights into which the entire capital stock of a corporation is divided. shareholder: one who holds and owns a share or shares in a corporation. share of stock: see share.

WORD STUDY 

487 stock: the capital of a company or corporation in the form of transferable shares, each of a certain amount; (in the plural) funds or property made up of shares in various corporations or in corporations in general. stock certificate: a written testimony of the fact that the holder has an interest in a corporation through the ownership of a share or shares of its stock. stockholder: one who is a holder or proprietor of stock or stocks. trustee: a person to whom property is legally committed in trust; one intrusted with property for another. 

CHAPTER XVII 

automobile insurance: insurance which provides reimbursement for loss by fire, theft, or damage to an automobile, damage to the automobile or property of another, or injury to other persons, caused by the automobile of the insured. coinsurer: a person who carries that type of fire insurance in which the insured is treated as insuring himself to the extent of that part of the risk not covered by his policy. coinsurance clause: the clause in a fire insurance policy which provides that the insured is to be treated as insuring himself to the extent of that part of the risk not covered by his policy. collision insurance: a form of automobile insurance which provides for reimbursement for damage to an automobile by collision with other objects or by an upset. damages: the estimated reparation in money for detriment or injury sustained. depreciation: a falling of value. fire and theft insurance: a form of automobile insurance which provides for reimbursement for loss if an automobile is damaged or destroyed by fire or is stolen. fire insurance: insurance for a given period against loss from injury to specified property by fire. insurable interest: such an interest in the subject of insurance as would suffer legal damage, detriment, or prejudice in the event of the happening of the perils insured against. insurance: a contract whereby, for a stipulated consideration, one party undertakes to indemnify or guarantee another against loss by a specified contingency or peril, the contract being set forth in a document called a policy. insurance company: a company which makes a business of insuring individuals against loss.

insured: a person whose life or property is insured. inventory: an itemized list of goods or values with their estimated worth. law suit: an action or process in a court for the recovery of a right or claim; legal application to a court for justice. negotiate: to treat or discuss with a view to coming to terms upon some matter. negotiations: a treating with another with a view to coming to terms, as for a sale or purchase. policy: the document containing the contract made by an insurance company with a person whose property or life is insured. policyholder: a person to whom an insurance policy has been granted. premium: the consideration paid, whether in money or otherwise, for a contract of insurance. property damage insurance: a form of automobile insurance which protects the insured from claims arising through damage caused to the property of another person by his automobile. public liability insurance: a form of automobile insurance which protects the insured from claims arising through injury caused another person by the insured's automobile. reimburse: to repay; to make restoration or payment of an equivalent to (a person). representative: one who represents others or another in a special capacity.

CHAPTER XVIII 

beneficiary: the person named in a policy of insurance as the one to receive the proceeds or benefits accruing thereunder. cash surrender value: the consideration given by the insurance company when the legal liability of the company is voluntarily canceled by the insured and beneficiary. disability insurance: a form of insurance which protects one from loss caused by sickness, accident, or permanent disability. endowment policy: an insurance policy which provides for the payment of a fixed sum at the expiration of a fixed term of years, or (as is usually stipulated) at prior death. estate: the aggregate of property of all kinds which a person leaves to be divided at his death. life insurance: a form of insurance which insures one person against loss by the death of another. limited-payment life policy: a policy the premiums of which are paid only during a limited period of years and on which the insurance is payable at death only.

WORD STUDY 

489 non-participating insurance policy: a policy which does not entitle the policyholder to participate in the profits. ordinary life insurance policy: a policy the premiums of which are to be paid continuously during the life of the insured and on which the insurance is payable at death only. participating policy: a policy which entitles the policyholder to participate in the profits. term insurance policy: a policy the premiums of which are to be paid only during a limited period of time and at the end of which the policy expires and has no cash value. 

CHAPTER XIX 

addressee: one to whom anything, as a letter, is addressed. body (of a letter): the main, central, or principal part. complimentary close: the complimentary remark which is placed at the close of a letter just before the signature. correspondence: a group of letters, especially those relating to one subject. correspondent: one with whom intercourse is carried on by letter. dictator: one who dictates to a writer. heading (of a letter): that part of a letter which shows the address of the writer and the date, or the date alone. introductory address: the part of a letter which shows the name and the address of the addressee. letterhead: letter paper having a heading printed or engraved thereon. letter-opening machine: a machine which opens letters by cutting a narrow strip off one side of the envelope. mail clerk: an employee who opens incoming mail and sees that it is distributed to the proper departments of the business for handling. reference initials: the initials of the dictator and the typist which are placed on a letter for reference purposes. return address: the name and the address of the writer which are placed on the envelope so that if the letter cannot be delivered to the addressee, it can be returned to the writer. salutation: the beginning words of a letter which are in the nature of a greeting; as, Dear Sir. time stamp: a device for use in stamping on incoming letters the date and time of day the letter is received. typist: a person who operates a typewriting machine. window envelope: an envelope with a window of transparent material through which the address on the letter or inclosure can be read.

CHAPTER XX 

alphabetic filing: a method of filing correspondence in which the guides are arranged according to the letters of the alphabet. bellows file: a device for keeping small collections of letters, the divisions of which expand in the manner of a bellows as they become filled. box file: a boxlike device for keeping small collections of letters, which has a number of cardboard divisions. file clerk: an employee whose duty it is to file letters and other papers and to locate any particular paper or group of papers which is requested. filing: the act of arranging or laying away, as papers, in a methodical manner for preservation and reference. filing department: that department of a business in which the files are kept and the employees of which take care of the correspondence which has been or is to be filed. folder: a heavy sheet of paper folded once, which is used in a file to hold the letters or papers of one classification. folder tab: a slight flap which extends over the edge of a folder and on which the title of the folder is placed. geographic filing: a method of filing correspondence in which the guides are arranged according to states, cities, or any other geographic divisions desired. guide: a heavy sheet of cardboard which is used in the drawer of a file to separate the folders of one classification from those of the preceding or following. miscellaneous folder: a folder in which is filed the correspondence of different persons or businesses which is so small in amount that separate folders are unnecessary. numeric filing: a method of filing correspondence in which the guides are arranged according to numbers. out-guide: a guide which is placed in the file when a folder is removed and which shows the name of the person using the folder and the date on which he received it. 

Shannon file: a device for keeping, usually temporarily, small collections of letters or business papers. It consists of a board, on one end of which is a double arch over which letters and papers are slipped. spindle file: a device consisting of a spindle or spike, usually used as a temporary method of disposing of papers. vertical file: a device for keeping a large amount of correspondence, consisting of one or more drawers in which the letters stand on edge in folders separated by suitable guides.

WORD STUDY 

491 

CHAPTER XXI 

calculating machine: any machine for the mechanical performance of mathematical operations. discount: an allowance upon an account or debt, usually made in consideration of prompt or cash payment. To deduct from an account, debt, charge, or the like. invoice: a written account or itemized statement of merchandise shipped or sent to a purchaser, consignee, etc., with the quantity, value or prices, and charges shown. purchase order: a direction in writing to furnish supplies. purchase requisition: a written request that supplies be purchased. purchases journal: a book in which the purchases of merchandise offered for sale are entered as they are made. purchasing agent: an employee who places purchase orders for a business. purchasing department: the department of a business which is responsible for buying for the entire organization. stock clerk: an employee responsible for the stock of merchandise or supplies.

CHAPTER XXII 

bill: an account of services rendered, or work done, with the price or charge. To charge or enter in a bill. billing clerk: an employee who makes out invoices, especially one who uses a billing machine. billing machine: a machine used in writing invoices, which is similar to a typewriter with an attachment to hold the invoice in place and to facilitate the use of carbon paper. binder: a cover or other device for holding together loose papers, pamphlets, etc. charge sale: a sale which is made with the agreement that the purchaser is to pay for the goods later. credit memorandum: a form which is given to a customer when he returns goods or when he is granted a deduction from an invoice, and which shows the amount credited to his account. credit rating books: books furnished by credit associations which show the credit ratings of individuals and businesses. credit sale: see charge sale. credit standing: the classification of a person or business according to the promptness with which obligations are met. credit ticket: see credit memorandum. on credit: on account; to be paid for later.

retail sale: a sale in which the goods or supplies are sold directly to the consumer. sales journal: a book in which the sales of merchandise on account are entered as they occur. wholesale sale: a sale in which the goods or supplies are sold to a retailer or jobber rather than direct to the consumer. account:

CHAPTER XXIII 

a written or printed statement of business dealings, or debits or credits, or of a certain class of them, or of other things subjected to a reckoning or review. balance: the excess of either side of an account; the equality between the totals of the two sides of an account. To compute the difference, if any, between the debits and credits of an account. bookkeeping machine: a machine which is used in making entries in a ledger from a journal or other book of original entry. book of original entry: a bookkeeping record in which an entry of a transaction is first made. canceled draft: a bank draft which has been canceled to show that payment was made. cash book: a book in which is kept a register of money received or paid out. credit: acknowledgment of payment by entering in an account; the right side of an account. To enter upon the credit side of an account. debit: the left side of an account. 

To charge with, as a debt; to enter on the debit side of an account. financial records: records which show facts about money matters. folio column: the column of a bookkeeping record in which is entered the page on which an account appears in another book. ledger: the final book of record of business transactions, in which all debits and credits from the journal, etc., are placed under appropriate heads. posting: the process of transferring or carrying an entry or item from a book of original entry to a ledger. proving cash: the process of checking the amount of cash on hand with the balance shown by the cash record. receipted: containing a writing acknowledging the taking or receiving of goods or money. statement: an abstract of an account showing the balance due. statement of account: see statement.

WORD STUDY 

493 voucher: a book, paper, or other thing which serves to vouch the truth of accounts, or to confirm and establish facts of any kind; specifically, any receipt or the like showing payment of a debt. 

CHAPTER XXIV 

business executive: any person in business who is charged with administrative or executive work. "order-taker": said of a salesperson who does not practice the principles of salesmanship, but merely takes a customer's order. personality: distinctive personal character; individuality. prospective customer: a person who is expected to become a purchaser. salesmanship: the science of persuading others to exchange money for goods or services, with a resultant satisfaction to both parties. self-confidence: the quality or state of having confidence in one's own strength or powers; self-reliance. trade journals: periodical publications relating to different businesses or trades.

CHAPTER XXV 

ambition: an eager desire for preferment, honor, superiority, power, or the attainment of some thing. "blind alley job": a position which gives an employee no opportunity to increase his ability, and therefore to gain advancement. clerical work: work which involves the keeping of records or accounts, taking care of correspondence, or the like, with or without administrative, executive, or other authority. defraud: to deprive of some right, interest, or property by a deceitful device; to cheat. employment agency: an organization which makes a business of locating positions for individuals. executive: qualified for, concerned with, or pertaining to the conduct of affairs. financial return: the profit from labor, investment, undertaking, etc., measured in money. initiative: energy or aptitude displayed in the initiation of action, especially of action that tends to develop or open out new fields. letter of application: a letter requesting that the writer be considered for a position. personal interview: a meeting face to face; a conference. resourcefulness: ability to meet a situation, to rise to an occasion, or the like. tact: nice discernment of the best course of action under given conditions.

CHAPTER XXVI 

agent: one who acts for or in the place of another by authority from him. agreement: mutual understanding, arrangement, or stipulation; an exchange of promises. articles of copartnership: the written contract of partners, setting forth the terms of the agreement. charter: an instrument in writing from the sovereign power of a state or country, executed in due form, granting or guaranteeing rights, franchises, or privileges. commercial department (of a bank): a department of a bank in which deposits are accepted and checking privileges are allowed the depositors. consumer: one who uses goods and so diminishes or destroys their usefulness. contract: an agreement between two or more persons to do or forbear something, especially such an agreement that is legally enforceable. distribution: the process of conveying commodities from the producers to the consumers. manufacturer: the owner of a factory; one who takes raw materials and makes products from them by hand, machinery, or art. natural resources: the raw materials supplied by nature from which all the products of man are derived. operating expenses: costs or expenses incurred in the regular operation of a business. partner: one of two or more associated as joint principals in carrying on any business with a view to joint profit; a member of a partnership. partnership: the relation existing between two or more persons who have contracted to place their money, effects, labor, skill, or some or all of them, in lawful commerce or business with the understanding that there shall be a division of profit between them. production: act or process of producing or bringing forth. retailer: a person or business which sells goods in small quantities direct to consumers. safety deposit box: a small box rented by a bank for the purpose of safely keeping jewelry, stocks, bonds, or other valuable articles. safety deposit vault: a room in a bank which provides for the safe keeping of deposit boxes containing valuables or important articles.

WORD STUDY 

495 savings department (of a bank): a department of a bank in which deposits are accepted but checking privileges are not granted to the depositors. sole proprietor: the single owner of a business. sole proprietorship: a business operated by one person alone. technical: of or pertaining to the useful or mechanical arts, or any science, business, or the like; as, technical problems, skill, words. wholesaler: a person or business which sells goods by the piece or in large quantities to retailers and jobbers, rather than direct to consumers.

CHAPTER XXVII 

acceptance: an agreeing, either expressly or by conduct, to the act or offer of another so that a contract is concluded and the parties become bound. agency: relation between a principal and his agent. consideration: that which is regarded as the equivalent or return given or suffered by one for the act or promise of another. disaffirm: to refuse to confirm; to refuse to accept as true, just, or rightful authority or obligation, or the like. enforceable: capable of being put into force, or of being made to take effect. false representation: an untrue representation willfully made to deceive another to his damage. fraud: a false representation of a matter of fact which deceives, and is intended to deceive, another so that he shall act upon it to his legal injury. general agent: an agent who is authorized to perform all kinds of transactions in connection with the operation of a particular business. good faith: sincere or honest belief in the truthfulness and authority of something. holder in due course: the person in possession of and legally entitled to receive payment of a bill, note, or check. infant: a person of either sex who has not attained the age at which full civil rights are accorded. insolvent: unable to pay one's debts as they fall due in the ordinary course of business, or having ceased to pay one's debts in the usual course of business; relating to persons unable to pay their debts. minor: see infant.

necessaries: such things in respect to infants, lunatics, and others similarly incompetent or dependent, as are requisite for support suitable to station. negotiable instrument: promissory note, check, etc., transferable by delivery, with or without indorsement, as the case may be, so that the title passes to the transferee who, if a bona fide holder without notice, is not prejudiced in his rights by any defect in the title of prior parties or by any personal defenses available to prior parties among themselves. offer: act of offering, bringing forward, or proposing; a proffer. personal property: estate or property consisting in general of things temporary or movable. principal: one who employs another to act for him; the person from whom an agent's authority is derived. real property: property in houses and land. solvent: able or sufficient to pay all just debts; as, a solvent merchant. special agent: an agent who is authorized to complete one transaction or a single kind of transaction. undue influence: such influence as prevents a person from exercising his own will and substitutes in its place the will of another, as by constraint, persuasion, or otherwise.
